Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa

Homage to that Blessed One, the Worthy One, the Perfectly Enlightened One.

Saṃyuttanikāye

The Connected Discourses

Saḷāyatanavagga-aṭṭhakathā

The Commentary on the Book of the Six Sense Bases

1. Saḷāyatanasaṃyuttaṃ

1. The Connected Discourses on the Six Sense Spheres

1. Aniccavaggo

1. The Chapter on Impermanence

1. Ajjhattāniccasuttavaṇṇanā

1. The Commentary on the Discourse on Internal Impermanence

1. Saḷāyatanavaggassa [V 3.0001][M 3.0001][P 2.0354] paṭhame cakkhunti dve cakkhūni – ñāṇacakkhu ceva maṃsacakkhu ca. Tattha ñāṇacakkhu pañcavidhaṃ – buddhacakkhu, dhammacakkhu, samantacakkhu, dibbacakkhu, paññācakkhūti. Tesu buddhacakkhu nāma āsayānusayañāṇañceva indriyaparopariyattañāṇañca, yaṃ – “buddhacakkhunā lokaṃ volokento”ti (mahāva. 9; ma. ni. 1.283; 2.338) āgataṃ[V 3.0002]. Dhammacakkhu nāma heṭṭhimā tayo maggā tīṇi ca phalāni, yaṃ – “virajaṃ vītamalaṃ dhammacakkhuṃ udapādī”ti (mahāva. 16; ma. ni. 2.395) āgataṃ. Samantacakkhu nāma sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ, yaṃ – “pāsādamāruyha samantacakkhū”ti (mahāva. 8; ma. ni. 1.282; 2.338) āgataṃ. Dibbacakkhu nāma ālokapharaṇena uppannaṃ ñāṇaṃ, yaṃ – “dibbena cakkhunā visuddhenā”ti (pārā. 13; ma. ni. 2.341) āgataṃ. Paññācakkhu nāma catusaccaparicchedakañāṇaṃ, yaṃ – “cakkhuṃ udapādī”ti (sa. ni. 5.1081; mahāva. 15) āgataṃ.

1. In the first sutta of the Chapter on the Six Sense Bases, 'eye' (cakkhu) refers to two eyes: the eye of wisdom and the eye of flesh. Therein, the eye of wisdom is fivefold: the Buddha-eye, the Dhamma-eye, the all-around eye, the divine eye, and the eye of understanding. Among these, the Buddha-eye is the knowledge of dispositions and underlying tendencies, and the knowledge of the maturity of others' faculties, as it is stated: 'surveying the world with the Buddha-eye.' The Dhamma-eye is the three lower paths and three fruits, as it is stated: 'the stainless, immaculate Dhamma-eye arose.' The all-around eye is the knowledge of omniscience, as it is stated: 'having ascended the palace, the one with the all-around eye.' The divine eye is the knowledge that arises through the pervasion of light, as it is stated: 'with the divine eye, which is purified.' The eye of understanding is the knowledge that defines the four truths, as it is stated: 'the eye arose.'

Maṃsacakkhupi [M 3.0002] duvidhaṃ – sasambhāracakkhu, pasādacakkhūti. Tesu yvāyaṃ akkhikūpake akkhipaṭalehi parivārito maṃsapiṇḍo, yattha catasso dhātuyo vaṇṇagandharasojā sambhavo jīvitaṃ bhāvo cakkhupasādo kāyapasādoti saṅkhepato terasa sambhārā honti. Vitthārato pana catasso dhātuyo vaṇṇagandharasojā sambhavoti ime nava catusamuṭṭhānavasena chattiṃsa, jīvitaṃ bhāvo cakkhupasādo kāyapasādoti ime kammasamuṭṭhānā tāva cattāroti cattārīsa sambhārā honti. Idaṃ sasambhāracakkhu [P 2.0355] nāma. Yaṃ panettha setamaṇḍalaparicchinnena kaṇhamaṇḍalena parivārite diṭṭhimaṇḍale sanniviṭṭhaṃ rūpadassanasamatthaṃ pasādamattaṃ, idaṃ pasādacakkhu nāma. Tassa tato paresañca sotādīnaṃ vitthārakathā visuddhimagge vuttāva.

The eye of flesh is also twofold: the eye with its components and the sensitive eye. Among these, there is that lump of flesh in the eye-socket, surrounded by the membranes of the eye, wherein, in brief, there are thirteen components: the four great elements, color, odor, taste, nutritive essence, genesis, life faculty, sex faculty, eye sensitivity, and body sensitivity. In detail, however, these nine—the four great elements, color, odor, taste, nutritive essence, and genesis—by way of the four origins, become thirty-six; and these four—the life faculty, sex faculty, eye sensitivity, and body sensitivity—are originated by kamma. Thus, there are forty components. This is called the eye with its components. As for that which is the mere sensitivity capable of seeing forms, situated in the circle of the pupil, which is surrounded by the black circle and demarcated by the white circle—this is called the sensitive eye. A detailed account of this and of the others, the ear and so on, has already been given in the Visuddhimagga.

Tattha yadidaṃ pasādacakkhu, taṃ gahetvā bhagavā – cakkhuṃ, bhikkhave, aniccantiādimāha. Tattha – “catūhi kāraṇehi aniccaṃ udayabbayavantatāyā”tiādinā nayena vitthārakathā heṭṭhā pakāsitāyeva. Sotampi pasādasotameva adhippetaṃ, tathā ghānajivhākāyā. Manoti tebhūmakasammasanacāracittaṃ. Iti idaṃ suttaṃ chasu ajjhattikāyatanesu tīṇi lakkhaṇāni dassetvā kathite bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ.

Therein, it is with reference to this sensitive eye that the Blessed One said: 'Monks, the eye is impermanent,' and so on. In this context, the detailed explanation in the manner of 'Impermanent for four reasons: because it possesses rise and fall,' etc., has already been shown below. By 'ear' also, only the sensitive ear is intended, and likewise for the nose, tongue, and body. By 'mind' is intended the mind of insight whose sphere is the contemplation of the three planes. Thus this sutta, having shown the three characteristics in the six internal sense bases, was spoken in accordance with the disposition of those who were to understand.

2-3. Ajjhattadukkhasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-3. Commentary on the Discourses on Internal Suffering, etc.

2-3. Dutiyaṃ dve lakkhaṇāni, tatiyaṃ ekalakkhaṇaṃ dassetvā kathite bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ. Sesāni pana tehi sallakkhitāni vā ettakeneva vā sallakkhessantīti.

2-3. The second [discourse] was taught showing two characteristics (suffering and not-self), and the third, one characteristic (not-self), in accordance with the disposition of those who were to understand. As for the remaining characteristics, they were either already well noted by them, or they will understand by just this much.

4-6. Bāhirāniccasuttādivaṇṇanā

4-6. Commentary on the Discourses on External Impermanence, etc.

4-6. Catutthe [V 3.0003] rūpagandharasaphoṭṭhabbā catusamuṭṭhānā, saddo dvisamuṭṭhāno, dhammāti tebhūmakadhammārammaṇaṃ. Idampi bāhiresu chasu āyatanesu tilakkhaṇaṃ dassetvā kathite bujjhanakānaṃ vasena vuttaṃ. Pañcame chaṭṭhe ca dutiyatatiyesu vuttasadisova nayo.

4-6. In the fourth, form, smell, taste, and tactile objects have four causes of arising; sound has two causes of arising; and by 'dhamma' is meant the mental-object pertaining to the three planes. This too was spoken in accordance with those who are to understand, when the three characteristics regarding the six external sense bases were shown and taught. In the fifth and sixth, the method is just like that stated in the second and third.

7-12. Ajjhattāniccātītānāgatasuttādivaṇṇanā

7-12. Commentary on the Discourses beginning with Internal Impermanence (Past and Future), etc.

7-12. Sattamādīni [M 3.0003] atītānāgatesu cakkhādīsu aniccalakkhaṇādīni sallakkhetvā paccuppannesu balavagāhena kilamantānaṃ vasena vuttāni. Sesaṃ sabbattha heṭṭhā vuttanayamevāti.

7-12. The discourses beginning with the seventh were spoken for the sake of those who are weary from strong grasping of present phenomena, after having noted the characteristics of impermanence, etc., in the eye, etc., of the past and future. In all cases, the remainder is just according to the method stated before.

Aniccavaggo paṭhamo.

The First Chapter on Impermanence.

2. Yamakavaggo

2. The Chapter of Pairs

1-4. Paṭhamapubbesambodhasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-4. Commentary on the First Discourse on Before Enlightenment, etc.

13-16. Yamakavaggassa [P 2.0356] paṭhamadutiyesu ajjhattikānanti ajjhattajjhattavasena ajjhattikānaṃ. So pana nesaṃ ajjhattikabhāvo chandarāgassa adhimattabalavatāya veditabbo. Manussānañhi antogharaṃ viya cha ajjhattikāyatanāni, gharūpacāraṃ viya cha bāhirāyatanāni. Yathā nesaṃ puttadāradhanadhaññapuṇṇe antoghare chandarāgo adhimattabalavā hoti, tattha kassaci pavisituṃ na denti, appamattena bhājanasaddamattenāpi “kiṃ eta”nti? Vattāro bhavanti. Evamevaṃ chasu ajjhattikesu āyatanesu adhimattabalavachandarāgoti. Iti imāya chandarāgabalavatāya tāni “ajjhattikānī”ti vuttāni. Gharūpacāre pana no tathā balavā hoti, tattha carante manussepi catuppadānipi na sahasā nivārenti. Kiñcāpi na nivārenti, anicchantā pana pasupacchimattampi gahituṃ na denti. Iti nesaṃ tattha na adhimattabalavachandarāgo hoti. Rūpādīsupi tatheva na adhimattabalavachandarāgo, tasmā tāni “bāhirānī”ti vuttāni. Vitthārato [V 3.0004] pana ajjhattikabāhirakathā visuddhimagge vuttāva. Sesaṃ dvīsupi suttesu heṭṭhā vuttanayameva. Tathā tatiyacatutthesu.

13-16. In the first and second [discourses] of the Chapter of Pairs, 'of the internal' means of that which is internal by way of being truly internal. Their state of being internal should be understood as due to the excessively great strength of sensual desire. For humans, the six internal sense bases are like the interior of a house, and the six external sense bases are like the approach to a house. Just as their sensual desire for a house full of children, wives, wealth, and grain is excessively strong—so much so that they do not allow anyone to enter, and even at the mere sound of a pot they are ones to ask, 'What is that?'—even so, sensual desire is excessively strong in the six internal sense bases. Thus, due to this strength of sensual desire, they are called 'internal.' In the approach to the house, however, it is not so strong; there, they do not hastily prevent people or quadrupeds who are moving about. Though they do not prevent them, if unwilling, they do not allow even a blade of grass to be taken. Thus, their sensual desire there is not excessively strong. Similarly, in regard to forms, etc., sensual desire is not excessively strong; therefore, they are called 'external.' The detailed discussion of internal and external has already been stated in the Visuddhimagga. The rest in both suttas is according to the method explained earlier. The same applies to the third and fourth [discourses].

5-6. Paṭhamanoceassādasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the First Sutta on 'If There Were No Gratification', etc.

17-18. Pañcame nissaṭāti nikkhantā. Visaññuttāti nosaṃyuttā. Vippamuttāti no adhimuttā vimariyādīkatena cetasāti nimmariyādīkatena cetasā. Yañhi kilesajātaṃ vā vaṭṭaṃ vā appahīnaṃ hoti, tena [M 3.0004] sekhānaṃ cittaṃ samariyādīkataṃ nāma. Yaṃ pahīnaṃ, tena vimariyādīkataṃ. Idha pana sabbaso kilesānañceva vaṭṭassa ca pahīnattā vimariyādīkatena [P 2.0357] kilesavaṭṭamariyādaṃ atikkantena cittena vihariṃsūti attho. Chaṭṭhepi eseva nayo. Chasupi panetesu suttesu catusaccameva kathitanti veditabbaṃ.

17-18. In the fifth, 'departed' means having gone forth. 'Disunited' means not united. 'Liberated' means not un-liberated. 'With a mind free from limitations' means with a mind made without boundaries. For when any mass of defilements or the cycle of existence remains unabandoned, by that the mind of trainees is said to be limited. By that which is abandoned, it is made unlimited. Here, however, because all defilements and the cycle are completely abandoned, they dwelled with a mind free from limitations, which has transcended the limitations of defilements and the cycle—this is the meaning. The same method applies to the sixth. It should be understood that in these six suttas, only the Four Truths are discussed.

7-10. Paṭhamābhinandasuttādivaṇṇanā

7-10. Commentary on the First Sutta on Rejoicing, etc.

19-22. Sattamādīsu catūsu vaṭṭavivaṭṭameva kathitaṃ. Anupubbakathā pana nesaṃ heṭṭhā vuttanayeneva veditabbāti.

19-22. In the four suttas beginning with the seventh, only the round of existence and that which is free from the round are taught. Their sequential explanation, however, should be understood according to the method stated below.

Yamakavaggo dutiyo.

The Yamaka Chapter, the second.

3. Sabbavaggo

3. The Chapter on All

1. Sabbasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Sutta on All

23. Sabbavaggassa paṭhame sabbaṃ vo, bhikkhaveti sabbaṃ nāma catubbidhaṃ – sabbasabbaṃ, āyatanasabbaṃ, sakkāyasabbaṃ, padesasabbanti. Tattha –

23. In the first sutta of the Chapter on All, in the phrase 'all, monks,' the term 'all' is of four kinds: the all-encompassing all, the sense-base all, the personality-group all, and the partial all. Among these—

“Na tassa addiṭṭhamidhaatthi kiñci,

Atho aviññātamajānitabbaṃ;

Sabbaṃ [V 3.0005] abhiññāsi yadatthi neyyaṃ,

Tathāgato tena samantacakkhū”ti. (mahāni. 156; cūḷani. dhotakamāṇavapucchāniddeso 32; paṭi. ma. 1.121) –

“Here there is nothing unseen by him, nor anything unknown or unknowable. He has directly known all that can be known; therefore the Tathāgata is the All-Seeing One.”

Idaṃ sabbasabbaṃ nāma. “Sabbaṃ vo, bhikkhave, desessāmi, taṃ suṇāthā”ti (saṃ. ni. 4.24) idaṃ āyatanasabbaṃ nāma. “Sabbadhammamūlapariyāyaṃ vo, bhikkhave, desessāmī”ti (ma. ni. 1.1) idaṃ sakkāyasabbaṃ nāma. “Sabbadhammesu vā pana paṭhamasamannāhāro uppajjati cittaṃ mano mānasaṃ…pe… tajjāmanodhātū”ti idaṃ padesasabbaṃ nāma. Iti pañcārammaṇamattaṃ padesasabbaṃ. Tebhūmakadhammā sakkāyasabbaṃ. Catubhūmakadhammā āyatanasabbaṃ. Yaṃkiñci neyyaṃ sabbasabbaṃ. Padesasabbaṃ sakkāyasabbaṃ [M 3.0005] na pāpuṇāti, sakkāyasabbaṃ āyatanasabbaṃ na pāpuṇāti, āyatanasabbaṃ sabbasabbaṃ na pāpuṇāti. Kasmā? Sabbaññutaññāṇassa ayaṃ nāma dhammo ārammaṇaṃ na hotīti natthitāya. Imasmiṃ pana sutte āyatanasabbaṃ adhippetaṃ.

This is called the all-encompassing all. “Monks, I will teach you the All; listen to it” (SN 35.23)—this is called the sense-base all. “Monks, I will teach you the root of all phenomena” (MN 1)—this is called the personality-group all. “In all phenomena, the first advertence arises—citta, mano, mānasaṃ… the mind-element born thereof”—this is called the partial all. Thus, the partial all is merely the five sense-objects. The phenomena of the three planes are the personality-group all. The phenomena of the four planes are the sense-base all. Whatever is knowable is the all-encompassing all. The partial all does not reach the personality-group all; the personality-group all does not reach the sense-base all; the sense-base all does not reach the all-encompassing all. Why? Because of the non-existence of any phenomenon that could be said, 'This is not an object for the knowledge of omniscience.' In this sutta, however, the sense-base all is intended.

Paccakkhāyāti [P 2.0358] paṭikkhipitvā. Vācāvatthukamevassāti, vācāya vattabbavatthumattakameva bhaveyya. Imāni pana dvādasāyatanāni atikkamitvā ayaṃ nāma añño sabhāvadhammo atthīti dassetuṃ na sakkuṇeyya. Puṭṭho ca na sampāyeyyāti, “katamaṃ aññaṃ sabbaṃ nāmā”ti? Pucchito, “idaṃ nāmā”ti vacanena sampādetuṃ na sakkuṇeyya. Vighātaṃ āpajjeyyāti dukkhaṃ āpajjeyya. Yathā taṃ, bhikkhave, avisayasminti ettha tanti nipātamattaṃ. Yathāti kāraṇavacanaṃ, yasmā avisaye puṭṭhoti attho. Avisayasmiñhi sattānaṃ vighātova hoti, kūṭāgāramattaṃ silaṃ sīsena ukkhipitvā gambhīre udake taraṇaṃ avisayo, tathā candimasūriyānaṃ ākaḍḍhitvā pātanaṃ, tasmiṃ avisaye vāyamanto vighātameva āpajjati, evaṃ imasmimpi avisaye vighātameva āpajjeyyāti adhippāyo.

'Having rejected' means having repudiated. 'It would be a mere matter of speech' means it would be merely a thing to be spoken of by speech. Furthermore, having gone beyond these twelve sense bases, one would not be able to show that 'there exists another natural phenomenon called this.' 'And when questioned, he would not satisfy' means that when asked, “What is that other thing called ‘all’?” he would not be able to satisfy by answering, “It is called this.” 'He would come to distress' means he would come to suffering. In the phrase Yathā taṃ, bhikkhave, avisayasminti, the word taṃ is a mere particle. Yathā is a word indicating reason; the meaning is, 'because one is questioned about what is not one's domain.' For, for beings striving in what is not their domain, only distress occurs. For instance, lifting a stone the size of a gabled building with one's head and crossing deep water is not one's domain; similarly, pulling down the sun and moon is not one's domain. One striving in that which is not one's domain comes only to distress. In the same way, one striving in this matter which is not one's domain would also come only to distress—this is the intended meaning.

2. Pahānasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Discourse on Abandoning

24. Dutiye sabbappahānāyāti sabbassa pahānāya. Cakkhusamphassapaccayā uppajjati vedayitanti cakkhusamphassaṃ mūlapaccayaṃ katvā uppannā sampaṭicchanasantīraṇavoṭṭhabbanajavanavedanā. Cakkhuviññāṇasampayuttāya [V 3.0006] pana vattabbameva natthi. Sotadvārādivedanāpaccayādīsupi eseva nayo. Ettha pana manoti bhavaṅgacittaṃ. Dhammāti ārammaṇaṃ. Manoviññāṇanti sahāvajjanakajavanaṃ. Manosamphassoti bhavaṅgasahajāto samphasso. Vedayitanti sahāvajjanavedanāya javanavedanā. Bhavaṅgasampayuttāya pana vattabbameva natthi. Āvajjanaṃ bhavaṅgato amocetvā manoti sahāvajjanena bhavaṅgaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Dhammāti ārammaṇaṃ. Manoviññāṇanti javanaviññāṇaṃ. Manosamphassoti bhavaṅgasahajāto samphasso. Vedayitanti javanasahajātā vedanā. Sahāvajjanena bhavaṅgasahajātāpi vaṭṭatiyeva. Yā panettha desanā anusiṭṭhiāṇā, ayaṃ paṇṇatti nāmāti.

24. In the second discourse, 'for the abandoning of all' means for the abandoning of everything. 'Dependent on eye-contact, feeling arises' means the feeling of receiving, investigating, determining, and impulsion that has arisen having made eye-contact the root condition. However, regarding the feeling associated with eye-consciousness, nothing need be said. This same method should be understood for feelings at the ear-door and so on. Here, 'mind' is the life-continuum consciousness. 'Mental objects' is the object. 'Mind-consciousness' is the impulsion together with adverting. 'Mind-contact' is contact co-arisen with the life-continuum. 'Feeling' is the feeling of impulsion together with the feeling of adverting. However, regarding the feeling associated with the life-continuum, nothing need be said. Alternatively, without separating adverting from the life-continuum, 'mind' should be understood as the life-continuum together with adverting. 'Mental objects' is the object. 'Mind-consciousness' is the impulsion-consciousness. 'Mind-contact' is contact co-arisen with the life-continuum. 'Feeling' is the feeling co-arisen with impulsion. Together with adverting, that which is co-arisen with the life-continuum is also suitable. Whatever teaching is here is the authoritative instruction; this is called a designation.

3. Abhiññāpariññāpahānasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on Direct Knowledge, Full Comprehension, and Abandonment

25. Tatiye [M 3.0006][P 2.0359] sabbaṃ abhiññā pariññā pahānāyāti sabbaṃ abhijānitvā parijānitvā pajahanatthāya. Abhiññā pariññā pahātabbanti abhijānitvā parijānitvā pahātabbaṃ. Sesaṃ vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ.

25. In the third discourse, 'all for direct knowledge, for full comprehension, for abandonment' means for the purpose of abandoning all, having directly known and fully comprehended it. 'To be directly known, fully comprehended, and abandoned' means that having directly known and fully comprehended, it is to be abandoned. The remainder should be understood in the way already stated.

4. Paṭhamaaparijānanasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the First Discourse on Not Fully Comprehending

26. Catutthe anabhijānaṃ aparijānaṃ avirājayaṃ appajahanti anabhijānanto aparijānanto avirājento appajahanto. Ettha ca avirājentoti avigacchāpento. Iti imasmiṃ sutte tissopi pariññā kathitā honti. “Abhijāna”nti hi vacanena ñātapariññā kathitā, “parijāna”nti vacanena tīraṇapariññā, “virājayaṃ pajaha”nti dvīhi pahānapariññāti.

26. In the fourth discourse, 'not directly knowing, not fully comprehending, not becoming dispassionate toward, not abandoning' means one who is not directly knowing, not fully comprehending, not becoming dispassionate, and not abandoning. Here, 'not becoming dispassionate' means not causing dispassion. Thus, in this discourse, all three kinds of full comprehension are taught. By the word 'directly knowing,' full comprehension as to the known is taught. By the word 'fully comprehending,' full comprehension by investigation is taught. By the two words 'becoming dispassionate toward' and 'abandoning,' full comprehension by abandoning is taught.

5. Dutiyaaparijānanasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Second Discourse on Not Fully Comprehending

27. Pañcame cakkhuviññāṇaviññātabbā dhammāti heṭṭhā gahitarūpameva gahetvā dasseti. Heṭṭhā vā āpāthagataṃ gahitaṃ, idha anāpāthagataṃ. Idaṃ panettha sanniṭṭhānaṃ – heṭṭhā āpāthagatampi anāpāthagatampi gahitameva, idha pana cakkhuviññāṇasampayuttā tayo khandhā. Te hi cakkhuviññāṇena [V 3.0007] saha viññātabbattā “cakkhuviññāṇaviññātabbā”ti vuttā. Sesapadesupi eseva nayo.

27. In the fifth discourse, by the phrase 'phenomena to be known by eye-consciousness,' he shows the very form-object that was taken up previously. Alternatively, previously the form-object that had come into range was taken up, whereas here that which has not come into range is taken up. This is the conclusion here: previously, both that which had come into range and that which had not come into range were indeed taken up, but here it refers to the three aggregates associated with eye-consciousness. For, because they are to be known together with eye-consciousness, they are called 'phenomena to be known by eye-consciousness.' This same method should be understood in the remaining passages as well.

6. Ādittasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Fire Sermon

28. Chaṭṭhe gayāsīseti gayāgāmassa hi avidūre gayāti ekā pokkharaṇīpi atthi nadīpi, gayāsīsanāmako hatthikumbhasadiso piṭṭhipāsāṇopi, yattha bhikkhusahassassapi okāso pahoti, bhagavā tattha viharati. Tena vuttaṃ “gayāsīse”ti. Bhikkhū āmantesīti tesaṃ sappāyadhammadesanaṃ vicinitvā taṃ desessāmīti āmantesi.

6. In the sixth discourse, the phrase “at Gayāsīsa” is to be understood thus: Indeed, not far from the village of Gayā there is a pond named Gayā and also a river; there is also a large rock-platform named Gayāsīsa, which is like an elephant’s forehead, where there is room for a thousand monks. The Blessed One was dwelling there. Therefore, it was said, “at Gayāsīsa.” The phrase “He addressed the monks” means that having considered a suitable Dhamma teaching for them, he addressed them, intending, “I will teach it.”

Tatrāyaṃ anupubbikathā – ito kira dvānavutikappe mahindo nāma rājā ahosi. Tassa jeṭṭhaputto phusso nāma. So pūritapāramī pacchimabhavikasatto, paripākagate ñāṇe bodhimaṇḍaṃ āruyha [P 2.0360] sabbaññutaṃ paṭivijjhi[M 3.0007]. Rañño kaniṭṭhaputto tassa aggasāvako ahosi, purohitaputto dutiyasāvako. Rājā cintesi – “mayhaṃ jeṭṭhaputto nikkhamitvā buddho jāto, kaniṭṭhaputto aggasāvako, purohitaputto dutiyasāvako”ti. So “amhākaṃyeva buddho, amhākaṃ dhammo, amhākaṃ saṅgho”ti vihāraṃ kāretvā vihāradvārakoṭṭhakato yāva attano gharadvārā ubhato veḷubhittikuṭikāhi parikkhipitvā matthake suvaṇṇatārakakhacitasamosaritagandhadāmamālādāmavitānaṃ bandhāpetvā heṭṭhā rajatavaṇṇaṃ vālukaṃ santharitvā pupphāni vikirāpetvā tena maggena bhagavato āgamanaṃ kāresi.

28. Herein is the sequential narrative: It is said that ninety-two eons ago, there was a king named Mahinda. His eldest son was named Phussa. He, having fulfilled the perfections and being a being in his final existence, ascended the seat of enlightenment when his knowledge had matured and penetrated to omniscience. The king’s youngest son became his chief disciple, and the purohita’s son became the second disciple. The king thought: “My eldest son has gone forth and become a Buddha, my youngest son is the chief disciple, and the purohita’s son is the second disciple.” Thinking, “The Buddha is ours alone, the Dhamma is ours, the Sangha is ours,” he had a monastery built. From the monastery’s gatehouse to his own palace door, he had it enclosed on both sides with huts having bamboo-wattle walls. Above, he had a canopy fastened, adorned with golden stars and hung with garlands of incense and flowers. Below, he had silver-colored sand spread and flowers scattered, and by that path, he arranged for the Blessed One’s arrival.

Satthā vihārasmiṃyeva ṭhito cīvaraṃ pārupitvā antosāṇiyāva saddhiṃ bhikkhusaṅghena rājagehaṃ āgacchati, katabhattakicco antosāṇiyāva gacchati. Koci kaṭacchubhikkhāmattampi dātuṃ na labhati. Tato nāgarā ujjhāyiṃsu, “buddho loke uppanno, na ca mayaṃ puññāni kātuṃ labhāma. Yathā hi candimasūriyā sabbesaṃ ālokaṃ karonti, evaṃ buddhā nāma sabbesaṃ hitatthāya uppajjanti, ayaṃ pana rājā sabbesaṃ puññacetanaṃ attanoyeva anto pavesetī”ti.

The Teacher, standing right in the monastery, would put on his robe and go to the royal residence with the community of monks, staying within the curtained enclosure. Having finished his meal, he would return, staying within the curtained enclosure. No one could give even a ladleful of alms. Therefore, the townspeople complained: “A Buddha has arisen in the world, yet we do not get to make merit. For just as the sun and moon provide light for all, so too do Buddhas arise for the welfare of all. But this king directs all meritorious intentions only toward himself.”

Tassa ca rañño aññe tayo puttā atthi. Nāgarā tehi saddhiṃ ekato hutvā sammantayiṃsu, “rājakulehi saddhiṃ aṭṭo nāma natthi, ekaṃ upāyaṃ karomā”ti. Te paccante core [V 3.0008] uṭṭhāpetvā, “katipayā gāmā pahaṭā”ti sāsanaṃ āharāpetvā rañño ārocayiṃsu. Rājā putte pakkosāpetvā“tātā, ahaṃ mahallako, gacchatha core vūpasamethā”ti pesesi. Payuttacorā ito cito ca avippakiritvā tesaṃ santikameva āgacchiṃsu. Te anāvāse gāme vāsetvā “vūpasamitā corā”ti āgantvā rājānaṃ vanditvā aṭṭhaṃsu.

Now, that king had three other sons. The townspeople, having joined together with them, consulted, saying: “With the royal family, there is no legal recourse; let us devise a plan.” They instigated thieves on the border and had a message brought to the king, saying, “Several villages have been plundered.” The king summoned his sons and sent them, saying, “My dears, I am old. Go and pacify the thieves.” The commissioned thieves, rather than scattering, came directly to their presence. The princes settled them in uninhabited villages and, returning to the king, paid homage, stood, and announced, “The thieves have been pacified.”

Rājā tuṭṭho “tātā, varaṃ vo demī”ti āha. Te adhivāsetvā gantvā nāgarehi saddhiṃ mantayiṃsu, “raññā amhākaṃ varo dinno. Kiṃ gaṇhāmā”ti? Ayyaputtā, tumhākaṃ hatthiassādayo na dullabhā[P 2.0361], buddharatanaṃ pana dullabhaṃ, na sabbakālaṃ uppajjati, tumhākaṃ jeṭṭhabhātikassa phussabuddhassa paṭijagganavaraṃ gaṇhathāti. Te “evaṃ karissāmā”ti nāgarānaṃ paṭissuṇitvā [M 3.0008] katamassukammā sunhātā suvilittā rañño santikaṃ gantvā, “deva, no varaṃ dethā”ti yāciṃsu. Kiṃ gaṇhissatha tātāti? Deva, amhākaṃ hatthiassādīhi attho natthi, jeṭṭhabhātikassa no phussabuddhassa paṭijagganavaraṃ dethāti. “Ayaṃ varo na sakkā mayā jīvamānena dātu”nti dve kaṇṇe pidahi. “Deva, na tumhe amhehi balakkārena varaṃ dāpitā, tumhehi attano ruciyā tuṭṭhehi dinno. Kiṃ, deva, rājakulassa dve kathā vaṭṭantī”ti? Saccavāditāya bhaṇiṃsu.

The pleased king said, “My dears, I grant you a boon.” They accepted and went to consult with the townspeople, saying, “The king has granted us a boon. What should we take?” The townspeople replied: “Noble sons, elephants, horses, and the like are not difficult for you to obtain, but the Buddha-jewel is difficult to obtain; it does not arise at all times. Take the boon of attending to your eldest brother, the Buddha Phussa.” Agreeing to the townspeople’s words, saying, “We will do so,” they had their hair and beards trimmed, bathed well, and were well-anointed. Then they went to the king’s presence and requested, “Your Majesty, grant us our boon.” “What will you take, my dears?” he asked. “Your Majesty, we have no need for elephants, horses, and the like. Grant us the boon of attending to our eldest brother, the Buddha Phussa.” “This boon cannot be given by me while I am alive,” he said, and covered both his ears. “Your Majesty, you were not forced by us to grant the boon; you gave it, pleased by your own wish. Is it fitting, Your Majesty, for the royal family to have two words?” Thus they spoke, appealing to the king's truthfulness.

Rājā vinivattituṃ alabhanto – “tātā, satta saṃvacchare satta māse satta ca divase upaṭṭhahitvā tumhākaṃ dassāmī”ti āha. “Sundaraṃ, deva, pāṭibhogaṃ dethā”ti. “Kissa pāṭibhogaṃ tātā”ti? “Ettakaṃ kālaṃ amaraṇapāṭibhogaṃ devā”ti. “Tātā, ayuttaṃ pāṭibhogaṃ dāpetha, na sakkā evaṃ pāṭibhogaṃ dātuṃ, tiṇagge ussāvabindusadisaṃ sattānaṃ jīvita”nti. “No ce, deva, pāṭibhogaṃ detha, mayaṃ antarā matā kiṃ kusalaṃ karissāmā”ti? “Tena hi, tātā, cha saṃvaccharāni dethā”ti. “Na sakkā, devā”ti. “Tena hi pañca, cattāri, tīṇi, dve, ekaṃ saṃvaccharaṃ detha”. “Satta, cha māse detha…pe… māsaḍḍhamattaṃ dethā”ti. “Na sakkā, devā”ti. “Tena hi sattadivasamattaṃ dethā”ti. “Sādhu, devāti satta divase sampaṭicchiṃsu”. Rājā satta saṃvacchare satta māse satta divase kattabbasakkāraṃ sattasuyeva divasesu akāsi.

The king, unable to go back on his word, said: “My dears, after I have attended to him for seven years, seven months, and seven days, I will give him to you.” “Excellent, Your Majesty. Give us a guarantee.” “A guarantee for what, my dears?” “Your Majesty, a guarantee of not dying for that length of time.” “My dears, you ask for an improper guarantee. It is not possible to give such a guarantee; the life of beings is like a dewdrop on the tip of a blade of grass.” “Your Majesty, if you do not give a guarantee and we die in the meantime, what wholesome deed can we do?” “In that case, my dears, give me six years.” “That is not possible, Your Majesty.” “Then five, four, three, two, one year.” “Give seven, six months… give half a month.” “That is not possible, Your Majesty.” “Then give just seven days.” “Very well, Your Majesty,” they said, and accepted the seven days. The king, within those seven days, performed all the honor that was to be done in seven years, seven months, and seven days.

Tato puttānaṃ vasanaṭṭhānaṃ satthāraṃ pesetuṃ aṭṭhausabhavitthataṃ maggaṃ alaṅkārāpesi, majjhaṭṭhāne catuusabhappamāṇaṃ [V 3.0009] padesaṃ hatthīhi maddāpetvā kasiṇamaṇḍalasadisaṃ katvā vālukāya santharāpetvā pupphābhikiṇṇamakāsi, tattha tattha kadaliyo ca puṇṇaghaṭe ca ṭhapāpetvā dhajapaṭākā [P 2.0362] ukkhipāpesi. Usabhe usabhe pokkharaṇiṃ khaṇāpesi, aparabhāge dvīsu passesu gandhamālāpupphāpaṇe pasārāpesi. Majjhaṭṭhāne catuusabhavitthārassa alaṅkatamaggassa ubhosu passesu dve dve usabhavitthāre magge khāṇukaṇṭake harāpetvā daṇḍadīpikāyo kārāpesi. Rājaputtāpi attano āṇāpavattiṭṭhāne soḷasausabhamaggaṃ tatheva alaṅkārāpesuṃ.

Then, in order to send the Teacher to the dwelling place of his sons, he had a royal road eight usabhas in width adorned. In the middle, he had an area measuring four usabhas trampled by elephants, made like an earth kasiṇa disc, spread with sand, and strewn with flowers. Here and there, he had plantain trees and full water pots placed, and banners and flags raised. At every usabha, he had a square pond dug. In the outer section, on both sides, he had shops selling perfumes, garlands, and flowers set up. In the middle, on both sides of the adorned royal road four usabhas in width, he had paths, each two usabhas wide, cleared of stumps and thorns, and had rows of lamp posts erected. The princes, too, in the area where their authority extended, had a road sixteen usabhas in width adorned in that very same way.

Rājā [M 3.0009] attano āṇāpavattiṭṭhānassa kedārasīmaṃ gantvā satthāraṃ vanditvā paridevamāno, “tātā, mayhaṃ dakkhiṇakkhiṃ uppāṭetvā gaṇhantā viya gacchatha, evaṃ gaṇhitvā gatā pana buddhānaṃ anucchavikaṃ kareyyātha. Mā surāsoṇḍā viya pamattā vicaritthā”ti āha. Te “jānissāma mayaṃ, devā”ti satthāraṃ gahetvā gatā, vihāraṃ kāretvā satthu niyyātetvā tattha satthāraṃ paṭijaggantā kālena therāsane, kālena majjhimāsane, kālena saṅghanavakāsane tiṭṭhanti. Dānaṃ upaparikkhamānānaṃ tiṇṇampi janānaṃ ekasadisameva ahosi. Te upakaṭṭhāya vassūpanāyikāya cintayiṃsu – “kathaṃ nu kho satthu ajjhāsayaṃ gaṇheyyāmā”ti? Atha nesaṃ etadahosi – “buddhā nāma dhammagaruno, na āmisagaruno, sīle patiṭṭhamānā mayaṃ satthu ajjhāsayaṃ gahetuṃ sakkhissāmā”ti dānasaṃvidhāyake manusse pakkosāpetvā, “tātā, imināva nīhārena yāgubhattakhādanīyādīni sampādentā dānaṃ pavattethā”ti vatvā dānasaṃvidahanapalibodhaṃ chindiṃsu.

The king went to the boundary of his jurisdiction, which was like a field-dike, paid homage to the Teacher, and lamenting, said: “Dear sons, you go as if tearing out and taking my right eye. Having taken him, may you act in a manner befitting the Buddhas. Do not wander heedlessly like drunkards.” They replied, “We shall understand, Your Majesty,” and taking the Teacher, they departed. Having had a monastery built and having offered it to the Teacher, they attended to the Teacher there, at times standing at the elder’s seat, at times at the middle-thera’s seat, and at times at the seat of the junior-most monk. When they observed the alms-giving, the offering of all three persons was exactly the same. As the time for the rains-residence drew near, they thought: “How indeed might we grasp the Teacher’s inclination?” Then this thought occurred to them: “Buddhas, by nature, hold the Dhamma in high esteem, not material things. By establishing ourselves in virtue, we shall be able to grasp the Teacher’s inclination.” Having summoned the people who arrange the alms, they said: “Dears, preparing rice-gruel, meals, edibles, and so forth in this very same manner, continue the alms-giving.” Having said this, they cut off the obstruction of arranging the alms-giving.

Atha nesaṃ jeṭṭhabhātā pañcasate purise ādāya dasasu sīlesu patiṭṭhāya dve kāsāyāni acchādetvā kappiyaṃ udakaṃ paribhuñjamāno vāsaṃ kappesi. Majjhimo tīhi, kaniṭṭho dvīhi purisasatehi saddhiṃ tatheva paṭipajji. Te yāvajīvaṃ satthāraṃ upaṭṭhahiṃsu. Satthā tesaṃyeva santike parinibbāyi.

Then, their eldest brother, taking five hundred men, having established himself in the ten precepts, donned two ochre-dyed robes and, consuming only allowable water, took up his dwelling. The middle brother with three hundred men, and the youngest with two hundred men, practiced in that very same way. They attended upon the Teacher for as long as they lived. The Teacher attained Parinibbāna in their very presence.

Tepi kālaṃ katvā tato paṭṭhāya dvānavutikappe manussalokato devalokaṃ, devalokato ca manussalokaṃ saṃsarantā amhākaṃ satthukāle devalokā cavitvā manussaloke nibbattiṃsu. Tesaṃ dānagge byāvaṭo mahāamacco aṅgamagadhānaṃ rājā bimbisāro hutvā nibbatti. Te tasseva rañño raṭṭhe [P 2.0363] brāhmaṇamahāsālakule nibbattiṃsu. Jeṭṭhabhātā jeṭṭhova jāto, majjhimakaniṭṭhā majjhimakaniṭṭhāyeva. Yepi tesaṃ parivāramanussā, te parivāramanussāva jātā. Te vuddhimanvāya tayopi [V 3.0010] janā taṃ purisasahassaṃ ādāya nikkhamitvā tāpasā hutvā uruvelāyaṃ nadītīreyeva vasiṃsu. Aṅgamagadhavāsino māse māse tesaṃ mahāsakkāraṃ abhiharanti.

They too, having passed away, from that time onwards for ninety-two kappas, wandered from the human world to the deva world, and from the deva world to the human world. In the time of our Teacher, having passed away from the deva world, they were reborn in the human world. The great minister who was devoted to their alms-giving was reborn as King Bimbisāra of Aṅga and Magadha. They themselves were reborn in a wealthy brahmin family in that very king's realm. The eldest brother was born as the eldest; the middle and youngest were born as the middle and youngest respectively. Those who were their retinue were also born as their retinue. Having reached maturity, the three of them, taking those one thousand men, went forth and, having become ascetics, lived on the riverbank at Uruvelā. The inhabitants of Aṅga and Magadha brought them great offerings every month.

Atha [M 3.0010] amhākaṃ bodhisatto katābhinikkhamano anupubbena sabbaññutaṃ patvā pavattitavaradhammacakko yasādayo kulaputte vinetvā saṭṭhi arahante dhammadesanatthāya disāsu uyyojetvā sayaṃ pattacīvaramādāya – “te tayo jaṭilabhātike damessāmī”ti uruvelaṃ gantvā anekehi pāṭihāriyasatehi tesaṃ diṭṭhiṃ bhinditvā te pabbājesi. So taṃ iddhimayapattacīvaradharaṃ samaṇasahassaṃ ādāya gayāsīsaṃ gantvā tehi parivārito nisīditvā, – “katarā nu kho etesaṃ dhammakathā sappāyā”ti cintento, “ime sāyaṃpātaṃ aggiṃ paricaranti. Imesaṃ dvādasāyatanāni ādittāni sampajjalitāni viya katvā desessāmi, evaṃ ime arahattaṃ pāpuṇituṃ sakkhissantī”ti sanniṭṭhānamakāsi. Atha nesaṃ tathā dhammaṃ desetuṃ imaṃ ādittapariyāyaṃ abhāsi. Tena vuttaṃ – “bhikkhū āmantesīti tesaṃ sappāyadhammadesanaṃ vicinitvā taṃ desessāmīti āmantesī”ti. Tattha ādittanti padittaṃ sampajjalitaṃ. Sesaṃ vuttanayameva. Iti imasmiṃ sutte dukkhalakkhaṇaṃ kathitaṃ.

Then our Bodhisatta, having made the great renunciation, gradually attained omniscience and set rolling the supreme Wheel of the Dhamma. Having trained noble sons such as Yasa, he sent out sixty Arahants to various directions for the purpose of teaching the Dhamma. Taking his bowl and robe himself, thinking, “I will tame those three matted-hair ascetic brothers,” he went to Uruvelā. There, with many hundreds of miracles, he shattered their wrong view and gave them the going forth. That Blessed One, taking that thousand monks who possessed bowls and robes created by psychic power, went to Gayāsīsa. Surrounded by them, he sat down and, pondering, “What Dhamma talk would be suitable for them?” he reflected: “These monks attend to the fire in the evening and morning. I shall teach by presenting the twelve sense-bases as if they are burning and blazing all around. In this way, they will be able to attain Arahantship.” Having made this determination, he then delivered this Discourse on Burning to teach them the Dhamma accordingly. Therefore it was said: ‘He addressed the monks,’ meaning, having sought out a suitable Dhamma teaching for them, he addressed them in order to teach it. Therein, ‘burning’ means kindled, blazing all around. The remainder is in the manner already stated. Thus, in this sutta, the characteristic of suffering is taught.

7. Addhabhūtasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Afflicted Discourse

29. Sattame addhabhūtanti adhibhūtaṃ ajjhotthaṭaṃ, upaddutanti attho. Imasmimpi sutte dukkhalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ.

29. In the seventh, 'addhabhūta' means afflicted, overwhelmed, and oppressed. In this discourse too, only the characteristic of suffering is explained.

8. Samugghātasāruppasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Discourse on Suitability for Uprooting

30. Aṭṭhame sabbamaññitasamugghātasāruppanti sabbesaṃ taṇhāmānadiṭṭhimaññitānaṃ samugghātāya anucchavikaṃ. Idhāti imasmiṃ sāsane. Cakkhuṃ na maññatīti cakkhuṃ [P 2.0364] ahanti vā mamanti vā paroti vā parassāti vā na maññati. Cakkhusmiṃ na maññatīti ahaṃ cakkhusmiṃ, mama kiñcanapalibodho cakkhusmiṃ paro cakkhusmiṃ, parassa kiñcanapalibodho cakkhusminti na maññati. Cakkhuto na maññatīti ahaṃ cakkhuto niggato, mama kiñcanapalibodho cakkhuto niggato, paro cakkhuto niggato, parassa kiñcanapalibodho cakkhuto [V 3.0011] niggatoti evampi na maññati, taṇhāmānadiṭṭhimaññanānaṃ ekampi na uppādetīti attho. Cakkhuṃ meti na maññatīti mama cakkhūti na maññati, mamattabhūtaṃ taṇhāmaññanaṃ na uppādetīti attho. Sesaṃ uttānamevāti. Imasmiṃ sutte catucattālīsāya ṭhānesu arahattaṃ pāpetvā vipassanā kathitā.

30. In the eighth, “suitable for uprooting all conceiving” means suitable for the uprooting of all conceivings of craving, pride, and views. “Here” means in this dispensation. “He does not conceive the eye” means he does not conceive the eye as “I,” “mine,” “another,” or “belonging to another.” “He does not conceive in the eye” means he does not conceive “I am in the eye,” “my impediment is in the eye,” “another is in the eye,” or “another’s impediment is in the eye.” “He does not conceive from the eye” means he does not conceive “I have emerged from the eye,” “my impediment has emerged from the eye,” “another has emerged from the eye,” or “another’s impediment has emerged from the eye”—the meaning is he does not give rise to even one of the conceivings of craving, pride, or views. “He does not conceive ‘the eye is mine’” means he does not conceive “the eye is mine”—the meaning is he does not give rise to the craving-conceiving of possessiveness. The rest is clear. In this sutta, insight leading to arahantship is explained in forty-four places.

9. Paṭhamasamugghātasappāyasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the First Discourse Conducive to Complete Uprooting

31. Navame [M 3.0011] samugghātasappāyāti samugghātassa upakārabhūtā. Tato taṃ hoti aññathāti tato taṃ aññenākārena hoti. Aññathābhāvī bhavasatto loko bhavamevābhinandatīti aññathābhāvaṃ vipariṇāmaṃ upagamanena aññathābhāvī hutvāpi bhavesu satto laggo lagito palibuddho ayaṃ loko bhavaṃyeva abhinandati. Yāvatā, bhikkhave, khandhadhātuāyatananti, bhikkhave, yattakaṃ idaṃ khandhā ca dhātuyo ca āyatanāni cāti khandhadhātuāyatanaṃ. Tampi na maññatīti sabbampi na maññatīti heṭṭhā gahitameva saṃkaḍḍhitvā puna dasseti. Imasmiṃ sutte aṭṭhacattālīsāya ṭhānesu arahattaṃ pāpetvā vipassanā kathitā.

31. In the ninth, ‘suitable for uprooting’ means conducive to uprooting. ‘From that, it becomes otherwise’ means from that, it becomes in another manner. ‘The world, becoming otherwise, clings to existence, delighting only in existence’ means, undergoing change and transformation, even having become otherwise, this world, attached, clinging, and bound to existences, delights only in existence. ‘Monks, as far as aggregates, elements, and sense bases’ means: ‘Monks, whatever aggregates, elements, and sense bases there are.’ ‘He does not conceive that’ means he does not conceive any of it—having drawn together what was stated below, he shows it again. In this discourse, insight leading to arahantship is explained in forty-eight instances.

10. Dutiyasamugghātasappāyasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Second Discourse Conducive to Complete Uprooting

32. Dasame etaṃ mamātiādīhi tīhi tīhi padehi taṇhāmānadiṭṭhigāhe dassetvā tiparivaṭṭanayena desanā katā. Paṭipāṭiyā pana tīsupi imesu suttesu saha vipassanāya cattāropi maggā kathitāti.

32. In the tenth discourse, the graspings of craving, conceit, and wrong view were shown by means of the three phrases beginning with ‘This is mine,’ and the teaching was given with its three turnings. However, in sequence, in these three discourses, the four paths along with insight were taught.

Sabbavaggo tatiyo.

The third, the Chapter on the All, is concluded.

4. Jātidhammavaggavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Jātidhammavagga (Chapter on Things Subject to Birth)

33-42. Jātidhammavagge [P 2.0365] jātidhammanti jāyanadhammaṃ nibbattanasabhāvaṃ. Jarādhammanti jīraṇasabhāvaṃ. Byādhidhammanti byādhino uppattipaccayabhāvena byādhisabhāvaṃ. Maraṇadhammanti maraṇasabhāvaṃ[V 3.0012]. Sokadhammanti sokassa uppattipaccayabhāvena sokasabhāvaṃ. Saṃkilesikadhammanti saṃkilesikasabhāvaṃ. Khayadhammanti khayagamanasabhāvaṃ. Vayadhammādīsupi eseva nayoti.

In the Jātidhammavagga, ‘subject to birth’ means having the nature of arising, the state of coming into existence. ‘Subject to aging’ means having the nature of aging. ‘Subject to illness’ means having the nature of illness, due to being a condition for the arising of illness. ‘Subject to death’ means having the nature of death. ‘Subject to sorrow’ means having the nature of sorrow, due to being a condition for the arising of sorrow. ‘Subject to defilement’ means having the nature of being defiled. ‘Subject to destruction’ means having the nature of going to destruction. This same method should be understood for ‘subject to vanishing’ and so on.

Jātidhammavaggo catuttho.

The fourth, the Chapter on Things Subject to Birth, is concluded.

5. Sabbaaniccavaggavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Aniccavagga (Chapter on Impermanence)

43-52. Aniccavagge [M 3.0012] abhiññeyyanti pade ñātapariññā āgatā, itarā pana dve gahitāyevāti veditabbā. Pariññeyyapahātabbapadesupi tīraṇapahānapariññāva āgatā, itarāpi pana dve gahitāyevāti veditabbā. Sacchikātabbanti paccakkhaṃ kātabbaṃ. Abhiññāpariññeyyanti idhāpi pahānapariññā avuttāpi gahitāyevāti veditabbā. Upaddutanti anekaggaṭṭhena. Upassaṭṭhanti upahataṭṭhena. Sesaṃ uttānamevāti.

In the Aniccavagga, in the phrase ‘to be directly known,’ comprehension as known is stated; however, it should be understood that the other two are also included. Also in the phrases ‘to be fully comprehended’ and ‘to be abandoned,’ comprehension by scrutinizing and comprehension by abandoning are stated; however, it should be understood that the other forms of comprehension are also included. ‘To be realized’ means to be made directly evident. Here also, in the phrase ‘to be directly known and fully comprehended,’ it should be understood that comprehension by abandoning, though not stated, is included. ‘Oppressed’ is in the sense of not being single-pointed. ‘Assailed’ is in the sense of being afflicted. The rest is clear.

Sabbaaniccavaggo pañcamo.

The fifth, the Chapter on Impermanence, is concluded.

Paṭhamo paṇṇāsako.

The first group of fifty is concluded.

6. Avijjāvaggavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Avijjāvagga (Chapter on Ignorance)

53-62. Avijjāvagge avijjāti catūsu saccesu aññāṇaṃ. Vijjāti arahattamaggavijjā. Aniccato jānato passatoti dukkhānattavasenāpi jānato passato [P 2.0366] pahīyatiyeva, idaṃ pana aniccavasena kathite bujjhanakapuggalassa ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ. Eseva nayo sabbattha. Api cettha saṃyojanāti dasa saṃyojanāni. Āsavāti cattāro āsavā. Anusayāti satta anusayā. Sabbupādānapariññāyāti sabbesaṃ catunnampi upādānānaṃ tīhi pariññāhi parijānanatthāya. Pariyādānāyāti khepanatthāya. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

In the Avijjāvagga, ‘ignorance’ is ignorance concerning the four truths. ‘Knowledge’ is the knowledge of the path of Arahantship. Regarding ‘for one who knows and sees as impermanent’: ignorance is indeed abandoned also for one who knows and sees by way of suffering and not-self; however, this was spoken by way of impermanence according to the inclination of the person to be awakened. This same method should be understood throughout. Moreover, here ‘fetters’ means the ten fetters. ‘Taints’ means the four taints. ‘Latent tendencies’ means the seven latent tendencies. ‘For the full comprehension of all clinging’ means for the purpose of fully knowing all four kinds of clinging with the three kinds of comprehension. ‘For complete removal’ means for the purpose of exhaustion. The rest is clear throughout.

Avijjāvaggo chaṭṭho.

The sixth, the Chapter on Ignorance, is concluded.

7. Migajālavaggo

7. The Migajāla Chapter

1. Paṭhamamigajālasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the First Migajāla Sutta

63. Migajālavaggassa paṭhame [V 3.0013] cakkhuviññeyyāti cakkhuviññāṇena passitabbā. Sotaviññeyyādīsupi eseva nayo. Iṭṭhāti pariyiṭṭhā vā hontu [M 3.0013] mā vā, iṭṭhārammaṇabhūtāti attho. Kantāti kamanīyā. Manāpāti manavaḍḍhanakā. Piyarūpāti piyajātikā. Kāmūpasaṃhitāti ārammaṇaṃ katvā uppajjamānena kāmena upasaṃhitā rajanīyāti rañjanīyā, rāguppattikāraṇabhūtāti attho. Nandīti taṇhānandī. Saṃyogoti saṃyojanaṃ. Nandisaṃyojanasaṃyuttoti nandībandhanena baddho. Araññavanapatthānīti araññāni ca vanapatthāni ca. Tattha kiñcāpi abhidhamme nippariyāyena “nikkhamitvā bahi indakhīlā sabbametaṃ arañña”nti (vibha. 529) vuttaṃ, tathāpi yaṃ taṃ “pañcadhanusatikaṃ pacchima”nti (pārā. 654) araññakaṅganipphādakaṃ senāsanaṃ vuttaṃ, tadeva adhippetanti veditabbaṃ. Vanapatthanti gāmantaṃ atikkamitvā manussānaṃ anupacāraṭṭhānaṃ, yattha na kasīyati na vapīyati. Vuttampi cetaṃ –

63. In the first sutta of the Migajāla Chapter, ‘cognizable by the eye’ means to be seen by eye-consciousness. This same method should be understood for ‘cognizable by the ear,’ and so on. ‘Pleasing’ means: whether they are sought after or not, the meaning is that they have become desirable objects. ‘Desirable’ means attractive. ‘Agreeable’ means gladdening the mind. ‘Of a lovely form’ means of a dear nature. ‘Connected with sensual pleasure’ means connected with the sensual pleasure that arises from taking them as an object. ‘Provoking lust’ means fit to be lusted after; the meaning is that they have become a cause for the arising of lust. ‘Delight’ means the delight of craving. ‘Yoke’ means fetter. ‘Yoked to the fetter of delight’ means bound by the bond of delight. ‘Forests and wildernesses’ means forests and also wildernesses. Therein, although in the Abhidhamma it is said directly, ‘Having gone out beyond the city-post, all of that is a forest,’ nevertheless, it should be understood that what is intended here is that dwelling which fulfills the forest-dweller’s practice, described as ‘at least five hundred bow-lengths away.’ ‘Wilderness’ is a place beyond the vicinity of a village, a place not frequented by humans, where there is no plowing or sowing. And this has been said:

“Vanapatthanti dūrānametaṃ senāsanānaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Vanapatthanti vanasaṇḍānametaṃ, vanapatthanti bhiṃsanakānametaṃ, vanapatthanti salomahaṃsānametaṃ, vanapatthanti pariyantānametaṃ, vanapatthanti amanussūpacārānaṃ senāsanānametaṃ [P 2.0367] adhivacana”nti (vibha. 531).

‘Wilderness’ is a designation for distant dwellings. ‘Wilderness’ is a designation for dwellings in a forest grove. ‘Wilderness’ is a designation for frightening dwellings. ‘Wilderness’ is a designation for dwellings that cause one’s hair to stand on end. ‘Wilderness’ is a designation for borderland dwellings. ‘Wilderness’ is a designation for dwellings not frequented by humans.

Ettha ca pariyantānanti imaṃ ekaṃ pariyāyaṃ ṭhapetvā sesapariyāyehi vanapatthāni veditabbāni. Pantānīti pariyantāni atidūrāni. Appasaddānīti udukkhalamusaladārakasaddādīnaṃ abhāvena appasaddāni. Appanigghosānīti tesaṃ tesaṃ ninnādamahānigghosassa abhāvena appanigghosāni. Vijanavātānīti sañcaraṇajanassa sarīravātavirahitāni. Manussarāhasseyyakānīti manussānaṃ rahokammassa anucchavikāni. Paṭisallānasāruppānīti nilīyanasāruppāni.

And here, setting aside this one meaning, ‘borderland,’ wildernesses are to be understood by the remaining meanings. ‘Remote’ means on the border, very far. ‘With little sound’ means having little sound due to the absence of the sounds of mortars, pestles, children, and so on. ‘With little clamor’ means having little clamor due to the absence of the echoing, great clamor of various beings. ‘Without the wind of people’ means devoid of the wind from the bodies of people moving about. ‘Suitable for human privacy’ means suitable for the private actions of humans. ‘Suitable for seclusion’ means suitable for concealment.

2. Dutiyamigajālasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Second Migajāla Sutta

64. Dutiye [V 3.0014] nandinirodhā dukkhanirodhoti taṇhānandiyā nirodhena vaṭṭadukkhassa nirodho.

64. In the second sutta, the phrase ‘with the cessation of delight, there is the cessation of suffering’ means that with the cessation of delight, which is craving, there is the cessation of the suffering of the round of existence.

3-5. Paṭhamasamiddhimārapañhāsuttādivaṇṇanā

3-5. Commentary on the First Discourse on Samiddhi’s Questions to Māra, etc.

65-67. Tatiye [M 3.0014] samiddhīti attabhāvassa samiddhatāya evaṃ laddhanāmo. Tassa kira therassa attabhāvo abhirūpo ahosi pāsādiko, ukkhittamālāpuṭo viya alaṅkatamālāgabbho viya ca sabbākārapāripūriyā samiddho. Tasmā samiddhitveva saṅkhaṃ gato. Māroti maraṇaṃ pucchati. Mārapaññattīti māroti paññatti nāmaṃ nāmadheyyaṃ. Atthi tattha māro vā mārapaññatti vāti tattha maraṇaṃ vā maraṇanti idaṃ nāmaṃ vā atthīti dasseti. Catutthaṃ uttānameva, tathā pañcamaṃ.

In the third sutta, ‘Samiddhi’ is a name thus obtained because of the accomplishment of his personality. It is said that the Elder’s person was beautiful and pleasing, like a lifted bouquet of flowers and like a decorated flower-chamber, accomplished with completeness in all aspects. Therefore, he was reckoned only as ‘Samiddhi.’ Death is called ‘Māra.’ ‘The designation of Māra’ means the designation, name, or term is ‘Māra.’ The question ‘Is there Māra or a designation of Māra there?’ shows this meaning: ‘In that personality, is there death, or is there this name “death”?’ The fourth sutta is self-evident, and likewise the fifth.

6. Samiddhilokapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Samiddhi World-Question Discourse

68. Chaṭṭhe [P 2.0368] lokoti lujjanapalujjanaṭṭhena loko. Iti migajālattherassa āyācanasuttato paṭṭhāya pañcasupi suttesu vaṭṭavivaṭṭameva kathitaṃ.

68. In the sixth sutta, ‘loka’ (world) is so called in the sense of breaking up and dissolving. Thus, starting from the sutta requested by the Elder Migajāla, in the five suttas as well, only the round of existence and its cessation have been taught.

7. Upasenaāsīvisasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Upasena Viper Sutta

69. Sattame sītavaneti evaṃnāmake susānavane. Sappasoṇḍikapabbhāreti sappaphaṇasadisatāya evaṃladdhanāme pabbhāre. Upasenassāti dhammasenāpatino kaniṭṭhabhātikaupasenattherassa. Āsīviso patito hotīti thero kira katabhattakicco mahācīvaraṃ gahetvā leṇacchāyāya mandamandena vātapānavātena bījiyamāno nisīditvā dupaṭṭanivāsane sūcikammaṃ karoti. Tasmiṃ khaṇe leṇacchadane dve āsīvisapotakā kīḷanti. Tesu eko patitvā therassa aṃsakūṭe avatthāsi. So ca phuṭṭhaviso hoti. Tasmā patitaṭṭhānato paṭṭhāya therassa kāye dīpasikhā viya vaṭṭiṃ pariyādiyamānamevassa visaṃ otiṇṇaṃ. Thero visassa tathāgamanaṃ disvā kiñcāpi taṃ patitamattameva yathāparicchedena gataṃ, attano pana iddhibalena “ayaṃ attabhāvo leṇe mā vinassatū”ti [V 3.0015] adhiṭṭhahitvā bhikkhū āmantesi. Purāyaṃ kāyo idheva vikiratīti yāva na vikirati, tāva naṃ bahiddhā nīharathāti attho. Aññathattanti aññathābhāvaṃ. Indriyānaṃ vā vipariṇāmanti cakkhusotādīnaṃ indriyānaṃ [M 3.0015] pakativijahanabhāvaṃ. Tattheva vikirīti bahi nīharitvā ṭhapitaṭṭhāne mañcakasmiṃyeva vikiri.

69. In the seventh sutta, ‘Sītavana’ refers to a charnel-ground forest so named. ‘Sappasoṇḍikapabbhāra’ refers to a mountain slope so named because of its resemblance to a snake’s hood. ‘Of Upasena’ refers to the Elder Upasena, the younger brother of the General of the Dhamma. ‘A viper fell’ means: it is said that the Elder, having completed his meal duties and taken his great robe, sat down in the shade of the cave, being fanned by a gentle breeze from an opening, while doing needlework on his two-layered lower garment. At that moment, two young vipers were playing on the cave’s ceiling. One of them fell and landed on the Elder’s shoulder-tip. And it was a snake with touch-venom. Therefore, starting from the place where it fell, its venom penetrated and consumed the Elder’s body just as a lamp flame consumes a wick. The Elder, seeing the venom spreading thus, although the venom had already spread to its limit from the very moment it fell, nevertheless by his own psychic power made the determination, ‘May this personality not perish in the cave,’ and addressed the monks. ‘Before this body disintegrates right here’ means: ‘So long as this body has not disintegrated, take it outside.’ ‘Alteration’ means a state of otherness. ‘Or the alteration of the faculties’ means the abandoning of the natural state of the faculties such as the eye and ear. ‘It disintegrated right there’ means: having been taken outside, it disintegrated right on the couch in the place where it was put.

8. Upavāṇasandiṭṭhikasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Upavāṇa Sandiṭṭhika Sutta

70. Aṭṭhame rūpappaṭisaṃvedīti nīlapītādibhedaṃ ārammaṇaṃ vavatthāpento rūpaṃ paṭisaṃviditaṃ karoti, tasmā rūpappaṭisaṃvedī nāma hoti. Rūparāgappaṭisaṃvedīti kilesassa atthibhāveneva pana rūparāgaṃ paṭisaṃviditaṃ karoti nāma, tasmā rūparāgappaṭisaṃvedīti vuccati. Sandiṭṭhikotiādīni visuddhimagge vuttatthāneva. No [P 2.0369] ca rūparāgappaṭisaṃvedīti kilesassa natthibhāveneva na rūparāgaṃ paṭisaṃviditaṃ karoti nāma, tasmā “no ca rūparāgappaṭisaṃvedī”ti vuccati. Imasmiṃ sutte sekhāsekhānaṃ paccavekkhaṇā kathitā.

70. In the eighth, ‘rūpappaṭisaṃvedī’ (perceiving forms) means determining an object with distinctions such as blue, yellow, etc.; thus, one makes form perceived, and is therefore called ‘one who perceives forms.’ ‘Rūparāgappaṭisaṃvedī’ (perceiving lust for forms) means that because of the very existence of defilement, one makes lust for form perceived; therefore, one is called ‘one who perceives lust for forms.’ ‘Sandiṭṭhika’ and so on have the meaning already stated in the Visuddhimagga. ‘No ca rūparāgappaṭisaṃvedī’ (but not perceiving lust for forms) means that because of the very non-existence of defilement, one does not make lust for form perceived; therefore, one is called ‘one who does not perceive lust for forms.’ In this discourse, the reflection of trainees and adepts is expounded.

9. Paṭhamachaphassāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the First Chaphassāyatana Sutta

71. Navame phassāyatanānanti phassākarānaṃ. Avusitanti avuṭṭhaṃ. Ārakāti dūre. Etthāhaṃ, bhante, anassasanti, bhante, ahaṃ ettha anassasiṃ, naṭṭho nāma ahanti vadati. Bhagavā – “ayaṃ bhikkhu ‘ahaṃ nāma imasmiṃ sāsane naṭṭho’ti vadati, kinnu khvassa aññesu dhātukammaṭṭhāna-kasiṇakammaṭṭhānādīsu abhiyogo atthī”ti cintetvā, tampi apassanto – “kataraṃ nu kho kammaṭṭhānaṃ imassa sappāyaṃ bhavissatī”ti cintesi. Tato “āyatanakammaṭṭhānameva sappāya”nti disvā taṃ kathento taṃ kiṃ maññasi bhikkhūtiādimāha. Sādhūti tassa byākaraṇe sampahaṃsanaṃ. Esevanto dukkhassāti ayameva vaṭṭadukkhassanto paricchedo, nibbānanti attho.

71. In the ninth, ‘of the sense bases of contact’ (phassāyatanānaṃ) means ‘of the sources of contact.’ ‘Avusitaṃ’ means ‘unpracticed.’ ‘Ārakā’ means ‘far away.’ ‘Here, venerable sir, I am undone’ (etthāhaṃ, bhante, anassasaṃ) means he says: ‘Venerable sir, here I am undone; I am indeed lost.’ The Blessed One, thinking, ‘This bhikkhu says, “I am indeed lost in this Dispensation”—I wonder, does he have application to other meditation subjects such as the element meditation subject or the kasiṇa meditation subject?’ and not seeing that either, he considered: ‘Which meditation subject, I wonder, would be suitable for him?’ Then, seeing that the sense-base meditation subject was suitable, while teaching it he said the words beginning with, ‘What do you think, bhikkhu?’ ‘Sādhu’ (good) is an expression of delight at his answer. ‘This is the end of suffering’ means: this itself is the end, the limit of the suffering of the round—that is, Nibbāna.

10. Dutiyachaphassāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Second Chaphassāyatana Sutta

72. Dasame anassasanti nassasiṃ, naṭṭho nāmamhi icceva attho. Āyatiṃ apunabbhavāyāti [V 3.0016] ettha āyatiṃ apunabbhavo nāma nibbānaṃ, nibbānatthāya pahīnaṃ bhavissatīti attho.

72. In the tenth, ‘anassasaṃ’ means ‘I am lost,’ ‘I am indeed lost.’ ‘For the future, for no further becoming’ (āyatiṃ apunabbhavāya) here means that ‘no further becoming’ in the future is Nibbāna; the meaning is that it will be abandoned for the sake of Nibbāna.

11. Tatiyachaphassāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Third Chaphassāyatana Sutta

73. Ekādasame [M 3.0016] anassasanti naṭṭho, panassasanti atinaṭṭho. Sesaṃ vuttanayeneva veditabbanti.

73. In the eleventh, ‘anassasaṃ’ means ‘lost,’ and ‘panassasaṃ’ means ‘utterly lost.’ The rest should be understood according to the previously stated method.

Migajālavaggo sattamo.

The Migajāla Vagga, the Seventh.

8. Gilānavaggo

8. The Chapter on the Sick

1-5. Paṭhamagilānasuttādivaṇṇanā

1–5. Commentary on the First Sutta on the Sick, and so forth

74-78. Gilānavaggassa [P 2.0370] paṭhame amukasminti asukasmiṃ. Ayameva vā pāṭho. Appaññātoti aññāto apākaṭo. Navopi hi koci paññāto hoti rāhulatthero viya sumanasāmaṇero viya ca, ayaṃ pana navo ceva apaññāto ca. Sesamettha vuttanayamevāti. Tathā ito paresu catūsu.

In the first sutta of the Chapter on the Sick, 'amukasmiṃ' means 'in such and such a place.' Or this is the reading itself. 'Appaññāta' means 'unknown, not manifest.' For even a new monk may be known, like the Elder Rāhula or the novice Sumana, but this monk is both new and unknown. The rest here is just as has been explained. Similarly in the four that follow this one.

6. Paṭhamaavijjāpahānasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the First Sutta on the Abandoning of Ignorance

79. Chaṭṭhe aniccato jānatoti dukkhānattavasena jānatopi pahīyatiyeva, idaṃ pana aniccalakkhaṇaṃ dassetvā vutte bujjhanakassa ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ.

79. In the sixth sutta, regarding 'knowing as impermanent': even when one knows by means of suffering and non-self, ignorance is indeed abandoned. However, this sutta, having shown the characteristic of impermanence, was taught according to the inclination of the person who is to understand the Truths.

7. Dutiyaavijjāpahānasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Second Sutta on the Abandoning of Ignorance

80. Sattame sabbe dhammāti sabbe tebhūmakadhammā. Nālaṃ abhinivesāyāti abhinivesaparāmāsaggāhena [V 3.0017] gaṇhituṃ na yuttā. Sabbanimittānīti sabbāni saṅkhāranimittāni. Aññato passatīti yathā apariññātābhiniveso jano passati, tato aññato passati. Apariññātābhiniveso hi jano sabbanimittānipi attato passati. Pariññātābhiniveso pana anattato passati, no attatoti evaṃ imasmiṃ sutte anattalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ.

80. In the seventh sutta, 'all phenomena' means all phenomena of the three realms. 'Not fit for adherence' means they are not suitable to be grasped with the grasping of adherence and misapprehension. 'All signs' means all signs that are formations. 'Sees otherwise' means one sees in a way that is different from how a person with uncomprehended adherence sees. For a person with uncomprehended adherence sees all signs as self. But one with comprehended adherence sees them as non-self, not as self. Thus, in this sutta, only the characteristic of non-self was taught.

8. Sambahulabhikkhusuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Sutta on Many Monks

81. Aṭṭhame [M 3.0017] idha noti ettha no-kāro nipātamattameva. Sesaṃ uttānatthameva. Kevalaṃ idha dukkhalakkhaṇaṃ kathitanti veditabbaṃ.

81. In the eighth sutta, in 'idha no', the 'no' is merely a particle. The rest has an obvious meaning. It should be known that here only the characteristic of suffering was taught.

9. Lokapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Sutta on the Question about the World

82. Navame lujjatīti palujjati bhijjati. Idha aniccalakkhaṇaṃ kathitaṃ.

82. In the ninth sutta, 'lujjati' means 'it perishes, it breaks up.' Here, the characteristic of impermanence was taught.

10. Phaggunapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Sutta on Phagguna's Question

83. Dasame chinnapapañceti taṇhāpapañcassa chinnattā chinnapapañce. Chinnavaṭumeti taṇhāvaṭumasseva chinnattā chinnavaṭume. Kiṃ pucchāmīti pucchati? Atikkantabuddhehi pariharitāni cakkhusotādīni pucchāmīti pucchati. Atha vā sace magge bhāvitepi anāgate cakkhusotādivaṭṭaṃ vaḍḍheyya, taṃ pucchāmīti pucchatīti.

83. In the tenth sutta, ‘with proliferation cut off’ means that because the proliferation of craving has been cut off, one is ‘with proliferation cut off.’ ‘With the round cut off’ means that because the round of craving itself has been cut off, one is ‘with the round cut off.’ ‘What shall I ask?’ he asks. ‘Shall I ask about the eye, ear, and so on, that were used by past Buddhas?’ he asks. Or, ‘Even if the path has been developed, if the round of the eye, ear, and so on, were to continue in the future, shall I ask about that?’ he asks.

Gilānavaggo aṭṭhamo.

The Gilāna Vagga, the Eighth.

9. Channavaggo

9. The Channa Chapter

1. Palokadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on the Nature of Perishing

84. Channavaggassa [V 3.0018][P 2.0371] paṭhame palokadhammanti bhijjanakasabhāvaṃ. Evamettha aniccalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ.

84. In the first discourse of the Channa Chapter, ‘palokadhamma’ means the nature of disintegration. Thus, here only the characteristic of impermanence is stated.

2. Suññatalokasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Discourse on the Empty World

85. Dutiye attaniyenāti attano santakena parikkhārena. Evamettha anattalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ.

85. In the second discourse, ‘attaniyena’ means with one’s own possessions. Thus, here only the characteristic of not-self is stated.

3. Saṃkhittadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

3. The Commentary on the Discourse on the Dhamma in Brief

86. Tatiyaṃ khandhiyavagge ānandovāde (saṃ. ni. 3.83) vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ.

86. The third discourse should be understood in the same way as stated in the Ānandovāda Sutta of the Khandha Chapter (SN 3.83).

4. Channasuttavaṇṇanā

4. The Commentary to the Channa Sutta

87. Catutthe [M 3.0018] channoti evaṃnāmako thero, na abhinikkhamanaṃ nikkhantathero. Paṭisallānāti phalasamāpattito. Gilānapucchakāti gilānupaṭṭhākā. Gilānupaṭṭhānaṃ nāma buddhapasatthaṃ buddhavaṇṇitaṃ, tasmā evamāha. Sīsaveṭhaṃ dadeyyāti sīse veṭhanaṃ sīsaveṭhaṃ, tañca dadeyya. Satthanti jīvitahārakasatthaṃ. Nāvakaṅkhāmīti na icchāmi. Pariciṇṇoti paricarito. Manāpenāti manavaḍḍhanakena kāyakammādinā. Ettha ca satta sekhā paricaranti nāma, arahā paricārī nāma, bhagavā pariciṇṇo nāma.

87. In the fourth discourse, ‘Channa’ refers to the elder of that name, not the elder who went forth with him at the Great Renunciation. ‘Seclusion’ means from fruition attainment. ‘Those who inquire about the sick’ means attendants of the sick. Attending to the sick is praised and commended by the Buddha; therefore, he spoke thus. ‘He should give a headband’ means a wrapping for the head, and that he should give. ‘A knife’ refers to a life-taking knife. ‘I do not desire it’ means ‘I do not want it.’ ‘Served’ means attended to. ‘Pleasingly’ means with bodily action and so on that increases the mind. Here, seven trainees are called ‘those who attend,’ an arahant is called ‘one who attends,’ and the Blessed One is called ‘one who has been attended to.’

Etañhi, āvuso, sāvakassa patirūpanti, āvuso, sāvakassa nāma etaṃ anucchavikaṃ. Anupavajjanti appavattikaṃ appaṭisandhikaṃ. Pucchāvuso sāriputta, sutvā vedissāmāti ayaṃ sāvakapavāraṇā [V 3.0019] nāma. Etaṃ [P 2.0372] mamātiādīni taṇhāmānadiṭṭhiggāhavasena vuttāni. Nirodhaṃ disvāti khayavayaṃ ñatvā. Netaṃ mama, nesohamasmi, na meso attāti samanupassāmīti aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ anattāti samanupassāmi. Ettakesu ṭhānesu channatthero sāriputtattherena pucchitaṃ pañhaṃ arahatte pakkhipitvā kathesi. Sāriputtatthero pana tassa puthujjanabhāvaṃ ñatvāpi taṃ “puthujjano”ti vā “khīṇāsavo”ti vā avatvā tuṇhīyeva ahosi. Cundatthero panassa puthujjanabhāvaṃ saññāpessāmīti cintetvā ovādaṃ adāsi.

‘Indeed, friend, this is fitting for a disciple’—indeed, friend, this is appropriate for a disciple. ‘Blameless’ means without recurrence, without rebirth-linking. ‘Ask, friend Sāriputta; having heard, we will understand’—this is called the disciple’s invitation to speak. ‘This is mine,’ etc., are stated by way of grasping at craving, conceit, and views. ‘Having seen cessation’ means knowing destruction and passing away. ‘This is not mine, I am not this, this is not my self’—thus I contemplate it as impermanent, suffering, and not-self. In these instances, the elder Channa, questioned by the elder Sāriputta, answered by including the question within the scope of Arahantship. The elder Sāriputta, however, even though he knew his state as an ordinary person, did not call him ‘an ordinary person’ or ‘one with cankers destroyed,’ but remained silent. The elder Cunda, however, thinking, ‘I will make his ordinary state known to him,’ gave him advice.

Tattha tasmāti yasmā māraṇantikaṃ vedanaṃ adhivāsetuṃ asakkonto satthaṃ āharāmīti vadati, tasmā puthujjano āyasmā, tena idampi manasikarohīti dīpeti. Yasmā vā channaṃ āyatanānaṃ nirodhaṃ disvā cakkhādīni tiṇṇaṃ gāhānaṃ vasena na samanupassāmīti vadasi. Tasmā idampi tassa bhagavato sāsanaṃ āyasmatā manasikātabbantipi puthujjanabhāvameva dīpento vadati. Niccakappanti niccakālaṃ. Nissitassāti taṇhāmānadiṭṭhīhi nissitassa. Calitanti vipphanditaṃ hoti. Yathayidaṃ āyasmato uppannaṃ vedanaṃ adhivāsetuṃ asakkontassa “ahaṃ vedayāmi, mama vedanā”ti appahīnaggāhassa idāni vipphanditaṃ hoti, imināpi naṃ “puthujjanova tva”nti vadati.

Here, ‘Therefore’ means: ‘Because, unable to endure the feeling that is near to death, you say, “I will bring a weapon,” therefore, venerable sir, you are an ordinary person.’ By this, he indicates, ‘Attend to this also.’ Or, ‘Because you say, “Having seen the cessation of the six sense bases, I do not contemplate the eye, etc., by way of the three kinds of grasping,” therefore, this teaching of the Blessed One should also be attended to by the venerable one’—thus, revealing his ordinary state, he speaks. ‘Perpetually’ means at all times. ‘For one who is dependent’ means for one who is dependent on craving, conceit, and views. ‘Agitated’ means it is trembling. Just as now, for the venerable one who is unable to endure the arisen feeling, and who, due to unabandoned grasping, thinks, ‘I feel, this feeling is mine,’ there is now trembling—by this too, he says of him, ‘You are indeed an ordinary person.’

Passaddhīti [M 3.0019] kāyacittapassaddhi, kilesapassaddhi nāma hotīti attho. Natiyāti taṇhānatiyā. Asatīti bhavatthāya ālayanikantipariyuṭṭhāne asati. Āgatigati na hotīti paṭisandhivasena āgati nāma, cutivasena gamanaṃ nāma na hoti. Cutūpapātoti cavanavasena cuti, upapajjanavasena upapāto. Nevidha na huraṃ na ubhayamantarenāti na idhaloke na paraloke na ubhayattha hoti. Esevanto dukkhassāti vaṭṭadukkhakilesadukkhassa [P 2.0373] ayameva anto ayaṃ paricchedo parivaṭumabhāvo hoti. Ayameva hi ettha attho. Ye pana “ubhayamantarenā”ti vacanaṃ gahetvā antarābhavaṃ icchanti, tesaṃ vacanaṃ niratthakaṃ. Antarābhavassa hi bhāvo abhidhamme paṭikkhittoyeva. “Antarenā”ti vacanaṃ pana vikappantaradīpanaṃ. Tasmā ayamettha attho – neva idha na huraṃ, aparo vikappo na ubhayanti.

‘Tranquility’ (passaddhi) means tranquility of body and mind, that is, tranquility from defilements. ‘Inclination’ (nati) refers to the inclination of craving. ‘When there is no’ (asati) means when there is no clinging, delight, or obsession for the sake of existence. ‘There is no coming and going’ (āgatigati na hoti) means there is no coming by way of rebirth-linking, and no going by way of passing away. ‘Death and rebirth’ (cutūpapāta) refers to death in the sense of passing away, and rebirth in the sense of arising. ‘Neither here nor beyond nor in between the two’ (nevidha na huraṃ na ubhayamantarenā) means it is not in this world, not in the next world, and not in both places. ‘This is the end of suffering’ (esevanto dukkhassa) means this itself is the end of the suffering of the cycle and the suffering of defilements—this is the limit, the state of the cycle having ceased. This indeed is the meaning here. But the statement of those who, taking the phrase ‘in between the two’ (ubhayamantarenā), wish for an intermediate existence is meaningless, for the existence of an intermediate state has indeed been rejected in the Abhidhamma. The phrase ‘in between’ (antarenā), however, indicates another alternative. Therefore, the meaning here is: neither here nor hereafter; a further alternative is, not in both.

Satthaṃ āharesīti jīvitahārakasatthaṃ āhari, āharitvā kaṇṭhanāḷaṃ chindi. Athassa tasmiṃ khaṇe maraṇabhayaṃ okkami, gatinimittaṃ upaṭṭhāsi. So attano puthujjanabhāvaṃ ñatvā, saṃviggacitto [V 3.0020] vipassanaṃ paṭṭhapetvā, saṅkhāre pariggaṇhanto arahattaṃ patvā, samasīsī hutvā parinibbuto. Sammukhāyeva anupavajjatā byākatāti kiñcāpi idaṃ therassa puthujjanakāle byākaraṇaṃ hoti; etena pana byākaraṇena anantarāyamassa parinibbānaṃ ahosi. Tasmā bhagavā tadeva byākaraṇaṃ gahetvā kathesi.

‘He brought forth a knife’ (satthaṃ āharesi) means he brought forth a life-taking knife; having brought it forth, he cut his windpipe. Then, at that very moment, fear of death descended upon him, and the sign of his future destination appeared. Knowing his state as an ordinary person, with a sense of urgency, he established insight, comprehended the formations, attained Arahantship, and, becoming a ‘samasīsī,’ he attained final Nibbāna. ‘It was declared in his presence that he was blameless’ (sammukhāyeva anupavajjatā byākatā) means that although this declaration was made concerning the elder while he was still an ordinary person, by this declaration his final Nibbāna occurred without obstacle. Therefore, the Blessed One, taking up that very declaration, spoke.

Upavajjakulānīti upasaṅkamitabbakulāni. Iminā thero, “bhante, evaṃ upaṭṭhākesu ca upaṭṭhāyikāsu ca vijjamānāsu so bhikkhu tumhākaṃ sāsane parinibbāyissatī”ti pubbabhāgapaṭipattiyaṃ kulasaṃsaggadosaṃ dassento pucchati. Athassa bhagavā kulesu saṃsaggābhāvaṃ dīpento honti hete sāriputtātiādimāha. Imasmiṃ kira ṭhāne therassa kulesu asaṃsaṭṭhabhāvo pākaṭo ahosi. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānameva.

‘Families that should be approached’ (upavajjakulāni) means families that should be visited. By this, the elder, showing the fault of association with families in the preliminary part of the practice, asks, ‘Venerable sir, with such male and female supporters present, how will that bhikkhu attain final Nibbāna in your Dispensation?’ Then the Blessed One, showing his absence of association with families, said, ‘These exist, Sāriputta…’ and so forth. It seems that on this occasion, the elder’s lack of association with families became evident. The rest is clear everywhere.

5-6. Puṇṇasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the Puṇṇa Sutta, etc.

88-89. Pañcame tañceti taṃ cakkhuñceva rūpañca. Nandisamudayā dukkhasamudayoti taṇhāya samodhānena pañcakkhandhadukkhassa samodhānaṃ hoti. Iti [M 3.0020] chasu dvāresu “nandisamudayā dukkhasamudayo”ti iminā dvinnaṃ saccānaṃ vasena [P 2.0374] vaṭṭaṃ matthakaṃ pāpetvā dassesi. Dutiyanaye nirodho maggoti dvinnaṃ saccānaṃ vasena vivaṭṭaṃ matthakaṃ pāpetvā dassesi. Iminā tvaṃ puṇṇāti pāṭiyekko anusandhi. Evaṃ tāva vaṭṭavivaṭṭavasena desanaṃ arahatte pakkhipitvā idāni puṇṇattheraṃ sattasu ṭhānesu sīhanādaṃ nadāpetuṃ iminā tvantiādimāha.

In the fifth, 'tañca' means that eye and that form. 'From the arising of delight is the arising of suffering' means: through conjunction with craving, there is a conjunction of the suffering of the five aggregates. Thus, in the six doors, with the phrase ‘from the arising of delight is the arising of suffering,’ the Blessed One taught, having brought the cycle of existence to its culmination by means of the two truths. In the second method, by means of the two truths, cessation and the path, He taught, having brought that which is free from the cycle to its culmination. 'By this, you, Puṇṇa...' is a separate connection. Thus, having first placed the teaching concerning the cycle and that which is free from the cycle within Arahantship, now, in order to have the Elder Puṇṇa utter a lion's roar in seven instances, the Blessed One said, 'By this, you...' and so on.

Caṇḍāti duṭṭhā kibbisā. Pharusāti kakkhaḷā akkosissantīti dasahi akkosavatthūhi akkosissanti. Paribhāsissantīti “kiṃ samaṇo nāma tvaṃ, idañcidañca te karissāmā”ti tajjessanti. Evametthāti evaṃ mayhaṃ ettha bhavissati. Daṇḍenāti catuhatthadaṇḍena vā khadiradaṇḍena vā ghaṭikamuggarena vā. Satthenāti ekatodhārādinā satthena. Satthahārakaṃ pariyesantīti jīvitahārakasatthaṃ pariyesanti. Idaṃ thero tatiyapārājikavatthusmiṃ asubhakathaṃ sutvā attabhāvena jigucchantānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ satthahārakapariyesanaṃ sandhāyāha. Damūpasamenāti ettha damoti indriyasaṃvarādīnaṃ etaṃ nāmaṃ.

‘Fierce’ means wicked and sinful. ‘Harsh’ means rough. ‘They will abuse’ means they will abuse with the ten grounds for abuse. ‘They will revile’ means they will threaten, saying, “What kind of ascetic are you? We will do this and that to you.” ‘Thus it will be for me here’ means this is how it will be for me in this matter. ‘With a stick’ means with a four-cubit stick, a khadira-wood stick, or a club. ‘With a weapon’ means with a single-edged weapon or the like. ‘They seek a life-taking weapon’ means they seek a weapon that takes away life. The Elder said this referring to the seeking of a life-taking weapon by the monks who, having heard the discourse on foulness in the context of the third pārājika offense, were disgusted with their own bodies. ‘By taming and calming’—here, 'taming' (dama) is a name for sense-restraint and so on.

“Saccena [V 3.0021] danto damasā upeto,

Vedantagū vusitabrahmacariyo”ti. (saṃ. ni. 1.195; su. ni. 467) –

“Tamed by truth, endowed with self-restraint, one who has reached the goal through supreme knowledge, who has lived the holy life.”

Ettha hi indriyasaṃvaro damoti vutto. “Yadi saccā damā cāgā, khantyā bhiyyodha vijjatī”ti (su. ni. 191; saṃ. ni. 1.246) ettha paññā damoti vuttā. “Dānena damena saṃyamena saccavajjenā”ti (dī. ni. 1.165; ma. ni. 2.226) ettha uposathakammaṃ damoti vuttaṃ. Imasmiṃ pana sutte khanti damoti veditabbo. Upasamoti tasseva vevacanaṃ.

For here, in the previous quote, sense-restraint is called 'dama.' In the verse, ‘If there is nothing found here better than truth, self-control (dama), generosity, and patience,’ 'dama' refers to wisdom. In the phrase, ‘By giving, by self-control (dama), by restraint, and by truthful speech,’ 'dama' refers to the Uposatha observance. But in this Sutta, patience should be understood as 'dama.' 'Upasama' (calm) is a synonym for it.

Atha kho āyasmā puṇṇoti ko panesa puṇṇo, kasmā ca panettha gantukāmo ahosīti? Sunāparantavāsiko eva esa, sāvatthiyaṃ pana asappāyavihāraṃ sallakkhetvā tattha gantukāmo ahosi.

Now, concerning the venerable Puṇṇa: Who is this Puṇṇa, and why did he wish to go there? He was indeed a resident of Sunāparanta, but having noted the unsuitable dwelling in Sāvatthī, he wished to go there.

Tatrāyaṃ [M 3.0021] anuppubbikathā – sunāparantaraṭṭhe kira ekasmiṃ vāṇijagāme ete dve bhātaro. Tesu kadāci jeṭṭho pañca sakaṭasatāni gahetvā janapadaṃ [P 2.0375] gantvā bhaṇḍaṃ āharati, kadāci kaniṭṭho. Imasmiṃ pana samaye kaniṭṭhaṃ ghare ṭhapetvā, jeṭṭhabhātiko pañca sakaṭasatāni gahetvā, janapadacārikaṃ caranto anupubbena sāvatthiṃ patvā, jetavanassa nātidūre sakaṭasatthaṃ nivesetvā bhuttapātarāso parijanaparivuto phāsukaṭṭhāne nisīdi.

Here is the sequential narrative: In the Sunāparanta country, it is said, there were two brothers in a certain merchant village. At times the elder brother would take five hundred carts and go into the country to procure goods, and at times the younger brother. At this time, leaving the younger brother at home, the elder brother took five hundred carts and, journeying through the country, gradually arrived at Sāvatthī. He halted his train of carts not far from Jetavana and, after taking his morning meal, sat at ease in a pleasant place, attended by his retinue.

Tena ca samayena sāvatthivāsino bhuttapātarāsā uposathaṅgāni adhiṭṭhāya suddhuttarāsaṅgā gandhapupphādihatthā yena buddho, yena dhammo, yena saṅgho, tanninnā tappoṇā tappabbhārā hutvā, dakkhiṇadvārena nikkhamitvā jetavanaṃ gacchanti. So te disvā “kahaṃ ime gacchantī”ti ekaṃ manussaṃ pucchi. Kiṃ tvaṃ, ayyo, na jānāsi? Loke buddhadhammasaṅgharatanāni nāma uppannāni, icceso mahājano satthu santikaṃ dhammakathaṃ sotuṃ gacchatīti. Tassa “buddho”ti vacanaṃ chavicammādīni chinditvā aṭṭhimiñjaṃ āhacca aṭṭhāsi. So attano parijanaparivuto tāya parisāya saddhiṃ vihāraṃ gantvā, satthu madhurassarena dhammaṃ desentassa parisapariyante ṭhito, dhammaṃ sutvā pabbajjāya cittaṃ uppādesi. Atha tathāgatena kālaṃ viditvā parisāya uyyojitāya satthāraṃ [V 3.0022] upasaṅkamitvā, vanditvā, svātanāya nimantetvā, dutiyadivase maṇḍapaṃ kāretvā, āsanāni paññāpetvā, buddhappamukhassa saṅghassa mahādānaṃ datvā, bhuttapātarāso uposathaṅgāni adhiṭṭhāya bhaṇḍāgārikaṃ pakkosāpetvā, “ettakaṃ dhanaṃ vissajjitaṃ, ettakaṃ dhanaṃ na vissajjita”nti sabbaṃ ācikkhitvā, “imaṃ sāpateyyaṃ mayhaṃ kaniṭṭhassa dehī”ti sabbaṃ niyyātetvā, satthu santike pabbajitvā, kammaṭṭhānaparāyaṇo ahosi.

At that time, the residents of Sāvatthī, having taken their morning meal and undertaken the Uposatha precepts, wearing clean upper robes and with perfumes and flowers in hand, were inclined, bent, and sloped towards the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Saṅgha.
