Leaving by the southern gate, they went to Jetavana. Seeing them, he asked a man, “Where are these people going?” “Sir, do you not know? In the world, the gems named Buddha, Dhamma, and Saṅgha have arisen. Therefore, this great crowd is going to the Teacher’s presence to hear the Dhamma talk.” For him, the word ‘Buddha’ cut through his skin, flesh, and so on, and stood, having struck the marrow of his bones. Surrounded by his retinue, he went with that crowd to the monastery. Standing at the edge of the assembly while the Teacher was teaching the Dhamma with a sweet voice, he heard the Dhamma and the thought to go forth arose in him. Then, when the Tathāgata, knowing the time, had dismissed the assembly, he approached the Teacher, paid homage, and invited him for the next day. On the second day, having had a pavilion made and seats prepared, he gave a great offering of alms to the Saṅgha with the Buddha at its head. After he had eaten his morning meal and undertaken the Uposatha precepts, he summoned the treasurer, explained everything—“this much wealth has been spent, this much has not”—and handed over everything, saying, “Give this property to my younger brother.” Then, having gone forth in the presence of the Teacher, he became devoted to his meditation subject.

Athassa kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikarontassa kammaṭṭhānaṃ na upaṭṭhāti. Tato cintesi – “ayaṃ janapado mayhaṃ asappāyo, yaṃnūnāhaṃ satthu santike kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā sakaraṭṭhameva gaccheyya”nti. Atha pubbaṇhasamaye piṇḍāya caritvā, sāyanhe paṭisallānā vuṭṭhahitvā, bhagavantaṃ upasaṅkamitvā, kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathāpetvā, satta [P 2.0376] sīhanāde naditvā, pakkāmi. Tena vuttaṃ, “atha kho āyasmā puṇṇo…pe… viharatī”ti.

Then, as he was attending to the meditation subject, the meditation subject did not become manifest for him. He thought, “This district is unsuitable for me. What if I were to take a meditation subject from the Teacher and return to my own country?” Then, in the morning, after going for alms, and rising from seclusion in the evening, he approached the Blessed One, had him teach the meditation subject, roared seven lion’s roars, and departed. Therefore it is said, “Then the venerable Puṇṇa… dwelt…”

Kattha [M 3.0022] panāyaṃ vihāsīti? Catūsu ṭhānesu vihāsi. Sunāparantaraṭṭhaṃ tāva pavisitvā ca abbuhatthapabbataṃ nāma patvā vāṇijagāmaṃ piṇḍāya pāvisi. Atha naṃ kaniṭṭhabhātā sañjānitvā bhikkhaṃ datvā, “bhante, aññattha agantvā idheva vasathā”ti paṭiññaṃ kāretvā tattheva vasāpesi.

Where then did he dwell? He dwelt in four places. First, having entered the country of Sunāparanta and reached a mountain called Abbuhattha, he entered a merchant village for alms. Then his younger brother recognized him, offered him alms, and having made him promise, “Venerable sir, do not go elsewhere, dwell right here,” he had him stay there.

Tato samuddagirivihāraṃ nāma agamāsi. Tattha ayakantapāsāṇehi paricchinditvā katacaṅkamo atthi, koci taṃ caṅkamituṃ samattho nāma natthi. Tattha samuddavīciyo āgantvā ayakantapāsāṇesu paharitvā mahāsaddaṃ karonti. Thero “kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikarontānaṃ phāsuvihāro hotū”ti samuddaṃ nissaddaṃ katvā adhiṭṭhāsi.

From there, he went to a monastery named Samuddagiri. There, a walking path had been made, enclosed by lodestone rocks. There, ocean waves would come and strike the lodestone rocks, making a great noise. The Elder, thinking, 'May there be a comfortable dwelling for those contemplating the meditation subject,' made a resolution and rendered the ocean silent.

Tato mātulagiriṃ nāma agamāsi. Tatthapi sakuṇasaṅgho ussanno rattiñca divā ca saddo ekābaddhova ahosi. Thero “idaṃ ṭhānaṃ na phāsuka”nti tato makulakārāmavihāraṃ nāma gato. So vāṇijagāmassa nātidūro naccāsanno gamanāgamanasampanno vivitto appasaddo. Thero “imaṃ ṭhānaṃ phāsuka”nti tattha rattiṭṭhānadivāṭṭhānacaṅkamanādīni kāretvā vassaṃ upagacchi. Evaṃ catūsu ṭhānesu vihāsi.

From there, he went to a monastery named Mātulagiri. There, too, the community of birds was numerous, and day and night the sound was continuous, as if a single clamor. The Elder, thinking, 'This place is not comfortable,' went from there to a monastery named Makulakārāma. It was not too far from a merchant village, nor too close, suitable for coming and going, secluded, and with little sound. The Elder, thinking, 'This place is comfortable,' had places for the night, places for the day, walking paths, and so on, built there and entered the rains-residence. Thus, he dwelt in four places.

Athekadivasaṃ [V 3.0023] tasmiṃyeva antovasse pañca vāṇijasatāni “parasamuddaṃ gacchāmā”ti nāvāya bhaṇḍaṃ pakkhipiṃsu. Nāvārohanadivase therassa kaniṭṭhabhātā theraṃ bhojetvā, therassa santike sikkhāpadāni gahetvā, vanditvā, “bhante, samuddo nāma asaddheyyo anekantarāyo, amhe āvajjeyyāthā”ti vatvā nāvaṃ āruhi. Nāvā uttamajavena gacchamānā aññataraṃ dīpakaṃ pāpuṇi. Manussā “pātarāsaṃ karissāmā”ti dīpake uttiṇṇā. Tasmiṃ pana dīpake aññaṃ kiñci natthi, candanavanameva ahosi.

Then one day, during that same rains-residence, five hundred merchants loaded goods onto a ship, saying, 'We will go to the further shore of the ocean.' On the day of boarding the ship, the Elder’s younger brother fed the Elder, received the training rules from the Elder, paid homage, and said, 'Venerable sir, the ocean is untrustworthy and has many dangers; may you be mindful of us,' and then boarded the ship. The ship, moving at great speed, reached a certain small island. The men disembarked onto the small island, thinking, 'We will make our morning meal.' On that small island, however, there was nothing else; there was only a sandalwood forest.

Atheko vāsiyā rukkhaṃ ākoṭetvā lohitacandanabhāvaṃ ñatvā āha – “bho, mayaṃ lābhatthāya parasamuddaṃ [P 2.0377] gacchāma, ito ca uttari lābho nāma natthi, caturaṅgulamattā ghaṭikā satasahassaṃ agghati. Hāretabbayuttakaṃ bhaṇḍaṃ hāretvā candanassa pūressāmā”ti te tathā kariṃsu[M 3.0023]. Candanavane adhivatthā amanussā kujjhitvā, “imehi amhākaṃ candanavanaṃ nāsitaṃ ghātessāma ne”ti cintetvā – “idheva ghātitesu sabbaṃ ekakuṇapaṃ bhavissati, samuddamajjhe nesaṃ nāvaṃ osīdessāmā”ti āhaṃsu. Atha nesaṃ nāvaṃ āruyha muhuttaṃ gatakāleyeva uppātikaṃ uṭṭhapetvā sayampi te amanussā bhayānakāni rūpāni dassayiṃsu. Bhītā manussā attano attano devatānaṃ namassanti. Therassa kaniṭṭho cūḷapuṇṇakuṭumbiko “mayhaṃ bhātā avassayo hotū”ti therassa namassamāno aṭṭhāsi.

Then one man struck a tree with an adze and, recognizing it as red sandalwood, said: 'Friends, we are going to the further shore of the ocean for profit, but there is no greater profit than this. A piece just four finger-breadths in size is worth a hundred thousand. Let us discard the goods that should be discarded and fill the ship with sandalwood.' They did so. The non-humans who dwelt in the sandalwood forest became angry and thought, 'These men have destroyed our sandalwood forest; we will kill them.' They said, 'If they are killed right here, the whole island will become a single mass of corpses. We will sink their ship in the middle of the ocean.' Then, having boarded their ship, just as it had gone for a moment, those non-humans raised a calamitous great wave and they themselves displayed terrifying forms. The frightened men paid homage to their own respective deities. The Elder’s younger brother, the householder Cūḷapuṇṇa, stood paying homage to the Elder, thinking, 'May my brother be my refuge.'

Theropi kira tasmiṃyeva khaṇe āvajjetvā, tesaṃ byasanuppattiṃ ñatvā, vehāsaṃ uppatitvā, abhimukho aṭṭhāsi. Amanussā theraṃ disvāva “ayyo puṇṇatthero etī”ti apakkamiṃsu, uppātikaṃ sannisīdi. Thero “mā bhāyathā”ti te assāsetvā “kahaṃ gantukāmatthā”ti pucchi. Bhante, amhākaṃ sakaṭṭhānameva gacchāmāti. Thero nāvaṅgaṇe akkamitvā “etesaṃ icchitaṭṭhānaṃ gacchatū”ti adhiṭṭhāsi. Vāṇijā sakaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā, taṃ pavattiṃ puttadārassa ārocetvā, “etha theraṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmā”ti pañcasatāpi attano pañcahi mātugāmasatehi saddhiṃ tīsu saraṇesu patiṭṭhāya upāsakattaṃ paṭivedesuṃ. Tato nāvāya bhaṇḍaṃ otāretvā therassa ekaṃ koṭṭhāsaṃ katvā, “ayaṃ, bhante, tumhākaṃ koṭṭhāso”ti āhaṃsu. Thero mayhaṃ visuṃ koṭṭhāsakiccaṃ natthi. Satthā pana tumhehi diṭṭhapubboti. Na diṭṭhapubbo, bhanteti. Tena hi iminā satthu maṇḍalamāḷaṃ karotha. Evaṃ satthāraṃ passissathāti. Te sādhu, bhanteti. Tena ca koṭṭhāsena attano ca koṭṭhāsehi maṇḍalamāḷaṃ kātuṃ ārabhiṃsu.

The Elder too, it is said, at that very moment reflected, and knowing their impending destruction, he rose into the sky and stood facing them. As soon as the non-humans saw the Elder, they fled, thinking, 'The noble Elder Puṇṇa is coming!' The great wave subsided. The Elder reassured them, saying, 'Do not be afraid,' and asked, 'Where do you wish to go?' 'Venerable sir, we are going to our own place.' The Elder, stepping onto the ship's deck, made a resolution: 'May this ship go to their desired place.' The merchants, having arrived at their own place, related the incident to their children and wives, and saying, 'Come, let us go for refuge to the Elder,' all five hundred of them, together with their five hundred womenfolk, established themselves in the Three Refuges and declared themselves lay followers. Then, unloading the goods from the ship, they made a portion for the Elder and said, 'Venerable sir, this is your portion.' The Elder said, 'I have no need for a separate portion. But have you ever seen the Teacher?' 'We have never seen him, venerable sir.' 'In that case, with this, build a circular pavilion for the Teacher. In this way, you will see the Teacher.' 'Very well, venerable sir,' they said. And with his portion and their own portions, they began to build the circular pavilion.

Satthāpi [V 3.0024] kira taṃ āraddhakālato paṭṭhāya paribhogaṃ akāsi. Ārakkhamanussā rattiṃ obhāsaṃ disvā, “mahesakkhā devatā atthī”ti saññaṃ kariṃsu. Upāsakā maṇḍalamāḷañca bhikkhusaṅghassa [P 2.0378] ca senāsanāni niṭṭhāpetvā dānasambhāraṃ sajjetvā, “kataṃ, bhante, amhehi attano kiccaṃ, satthāraṃ pakkosathā”ti therassa ārocesuṃ. Thero sāyanhasamaye iddhiyā sāvatthiṃ gantvā, “bhante, vāṇijagāmavāsino tumhe daṭṭhukāmā[M 3.0024], tesaṃ anukampaṃ karothā”ti bhagavantaṃ yāci. Bhagavā adhivāsesi. Thero sakaṭṭhānameva paccāgato.

The Teacher also, it is said, from the time its construction began, made use of it by entering meditative attainment there. The guards, seeing a radiance at night, formed the perception, 'There is a deity of great power.' The lay followers, having completed the circular pavilion and the lodgings for the community of monks, and having prepared the requisites for the offering, informed the Elder, 'Venerable sir, our task has been done. Please invite the Teacher.' In the evening, the Elder went to Sāvatthī by psychic power and requested the Blessed One, 'Venerable sir, the inhabitants of the merchant village wish to see you; please show compassion to them.' The Blessed One consented. The Elder returned to his own place.

Bhagavāpi ānandattheraṃ āmantesi, “ānanda, sve sunāparante vāṇijagāme piṇḍāya carissāma, tvaṃ ekūnapañcasatānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ salākaṃ dehī”ti. Thero “sādhu, bhante”ti bhikkhusaṅghassa tamatthaṃ ārocetvā, “ākāsacārī bhikkhū salākaṃ gaṇhantū”ti āha. Taṃdivasaṃ kuṇḍadhānatthero paṭhamaṃ salākaṃ aggahesi. Vāṇijagāmavāsinopi “sve kira satthā āgamissatī”ti gāmamajjhe maṇḍapaṃ katvā dānaggaṃ sajjayiṃsu. Bhagavā pātova sarīrapaṭijagganaṃ katvā, gandhakuṭiṃ pavisitvā, phalasamāpattiṃ appetvā, nisīdi. Sakkassa paṇḍukambalasilāsanaṃ uṇhaṃ ahosi. So “kiṃ ida”nti āvajjetvā satthu sunāparantagamanaṃ disvā, vissakammaṃ āmantesi, “tāta, ajja bhagavā tiṃsamattāni yojanasatāni piṇḍacāraṃ gamissati. Pañca kūṭāgārasatāni māpetvā jetavanadvārakoṭṭhakamatthake gamanasajjāni katvā ṭhapehī”ti. So tathā akāsi. Bhagavato kūṭāgāraṃ catumukhaṃ ahosi, dvinnaṃ aggasāvakānaṃ dvimukhāni, sesāni ekamukhāni, satthā gandhakuṭito nikkhamitvā paṭipāṭiyā ṭhapitakūṭāgāresu dhurakūṭāgāraṃ pāvisi. Dve aggasāvake ādiṃ katvā ekūnapañcabhikkhusatānipi kūṭāgāragatāni ahesuṃ. Ekaṃ tucchaṃ kūṭāgāraṃ ahosi, pañcapi kūṭāgārasatāni ākāse uppattiṃsu.

The Blessed One also addressed the Elder Ānanda, saying, “Ānanda, tomorrow we will go for alms in the merchant village of Sunāparanta. You should give the lot to the four hundred and ninety-nine monks.” The Elder replied, “Very well, venerable sir,” and having informed the Saṅgha of monks about the matter, said, “Let the monks who travel through the sky take the lot.” On that day, the Elder Kuṇḍadhāna was the first to take a lot. The dwellers of the merchant village, hearing that the Teacher would come the next day, prepared a pavilion in the middle of the village and arranged the alms-offering. The Blessed One, having attended to his bodily needs early in the morning, entered the fragrant dwelling, entered upon fruition-attainment, and sat down. Sakka's Paṇḍukambala stone throne became hot. He reflected, “What is this?” and seeing the Teacher's journey to Sunāparanta, addressed Vissakamma, saying, “Dear one, today the Blessed One will travel about three thousand yojanas for his alms-round. Create five hundred peaked houses, and having made them ready for departure, place them above the gate-tower of Jetavana.” He did so. The Blessed One's peaked house had four entrances, those of the two chief disciples had two entrances each, and the rest had one entrance each. The Teacher, having left the fragrant dwelling, entered the foremost peaked house among those arranged in order. Beginning with the two chief disciples, the four hundred and ninety-nine monks also went into their respective peaked houses. One peaked house was empty, and all five hundred peaked houses rose into the sky.

Satthā saccabandhapabbataṃ nāma patvā kūṭāgāraṃ ākāse ṭhapesi. Tasmiṃ pabbate saccabandho nāma micchādiṭṭhikatāpaso mahājanaṃ micchādiṭṭhiṃ uggaṇhāpento lābhaggayasaggappatto [P 2.0379] hutvā vasati, abbhantare cassa antocāṭiyaṃ padīpo viya arahattaphalassa upanissayo jalati. Taṃ disvā “dhammamassa kathessāmī”ti gantvā dhammaṃ desesi. Tāpaso desanāpariyosāne arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. Maggenevassa abhiññā āgatā. So ehibhikkhu hutvā iddhimayapattacīvaradharo tucchakūṭāgāraṃ pāvisi.

The Teacher reached a mountain named Saccabandha and placed the peaked house in the sky. On that mountain lived an ascetic with wrong views named Saccabandha, who, while teaching wrong views to the great multitude, dwelt there having attained the pinnacle of gain and honor. Within him, however, the supporting condition for the fruit of arahantship burned like a lamp inside a pot. Seeing him, the Teacher thought, “I will teach him the Dhamma,” and having gone to him, he taught the Dhamma. At the end of the discourse, the ascetic attained arahantship. The higher knowledges came to him by means of the Path itself. He became an ehi-bhikkhu, and bearing a bowl and robes created by psychic power, he entered the empty peaked house.

Bhagavā [V 3.0025][M 3.0025] kūṭāgāragatehi pañcahi bhikkhusatehi saddhiṃ vāṇijagāmaṃ gantvā, kūṭāgārāni adissamānakāni katvā, vāṇijagāmaṃ pāvisi. Vāṇijā buddhappamukhassa saṅghassa mahādānaṃ datvā satthāraṃ makulakārāmaṃ nayiṃsu. Satthā maṇḍalamāḷaṃ pāvisi. Mahājano yāva satthā bhattadarathaṃ paṭippassambheti, tāva pātarāsaṃ katvā uposathaṅgāni samādāya bahuṃ gandhañca pupphañca ādāya dhammassavanatthāya ārāmaṃ paccāgamāsi. Satthā dhammaṃ desesi. Mahājanassa bandhanamokkho jāto, mahantaṃ buddhakolāhalaṃ ahosi.

The Blessed One, together with the five hundred monks who were in the peaked houses, went to the merchant village. Having made the peaked houses invisible, he entered the merchant village. The merchants offered a great alms-giving to the Saṅgha headed by the Buddha and led the Teacher to the Makulakārāma. The Teacher entered the circular pavilion. While the Teacher was alleviating his bodily fatigue, the great multitude, having taken their morning meal and undertaken the Uposatha observances, took much incense and flowers and returned to the monastery to hear the Dhamma. The Teacher taught the Dhamma. For the great multitude, release from bondage occurred, and a great Buddha-uproar arose.

Satthā mahājanassa saṅgahatthāya sattāhaṃ tattheva vasi, aruṇaṃ pana mahāgandhakuṭiyaṃyeva uṭṭhapesi. Sattāhampi dhammadesanāpariyosāne caturāsītiyā pāṇasahassānaṃ dhammābhisamayo ahosi. Tattha sattāhaṃ vasitvā, vāṇijagāme piṇḍāya caritvā, “tvaṃ idheva vasāhī”ti puṇṇattheraṃ nivattetvā antare nammadānadī nāma atthi, tassā tīraṃ agamāsi. Nammadā nāgarājā satthu paccuggamanaṃ katvā, nāgabhavanaṃ pavesetvā, tiṇṇaṃ ratanānaṃ sakkāraṃ akāsi. Satthā tassa dhammaṃ kathetvā nāgabhavanā nikkhami. So “mayhaṃ, bhante, paricaritabbaṃ dethā”ti yāci. Bhagavā nammadānadītīre padacetiyaṃ dassesi. Taṃ vīcīsu āgatāsu pidhīyati, gatāsu vivarīyati. Mahāsakkārapattaṃ ahosi. Satthā tato nikkhamitvā saccabandhapabbataṃ gantvā saccabandhaṃ āha – “tayā mahājano apāyamagge otārito. Tvaṃ idheva vasitvā, etesaṃ laddhiṃ vissajjāpetvā, nibbānamagge patiṭṭhāpehī”ti. Sopi paricaritabbaṃ yāci. Satthā ghanapiṭṭhipāsāṇe allamattikapiṇḍamhi lañchanaṃ viya padacetiyaṃ dassesi. Tato jetavanameva gato. Etamatthaṃ sandhāya teneva antaravassenātiādi vuttaṃ.

The Teacher, for the sake of benefiting the great multitude, stayed right there for seven days, but he caused the dawn to rise in the Great Fragrant Dwelling itself. For seven days, at the conclusion of the Dhamma discourse, eighty-four thousand beings attained the realization of the Dhamma. After staying there for seven days and having gone for alms in the merchant village, he sent the Elder Puṇṇa back, saying, “You stay right here.” In between, there is a river named Nammadā, and he went to its bank. The Nāga king Nammadā came out to welcome the Teacher, led him into the Nāga palace, and rendered homage to the Three Jewels. The Teacher taught him the Dhamma and then left the Nāga palace. He requested, “Venerable sir, please give me something to venerate.” The Blessed One showed him a footprint shrine on the bank of the Nammadā River. When the waves came, it was covered; when they receded, it was revealed. It became a place of great veneration. The Teacher then departed from there, went to the Saccabandha Mountain, and said to Saccabandha: “You have led the great multitude onto the path to the lower realms. You should stay right here, cause them to abandon this view, and establish them on the path to Nibbāna.” He too requested something to venerate. The Teacher showed him a footprint shrine on a solid stone slab, like a seal stamped on a lump of wet clay. From there, he went directly to Jetavana. It is with reference to this matter that the words beginning with “during that same rains-retreat” were spoken.

Parinibbāyīti [P 2.0380] anupādisesāya nibbānadhātuyā parinibbāyi. Mahājano therassa satta divasāni sarīrapūjaṃ katvā, bahūni gandhakaṭṭhāni samodhānetvā, sarīraṃ jhāpetvā dhātuyo ādāya cetiyaṃ akāsi. Sambahulā bhikkhūti therassa āḷāhanaṭṭhāne ṭhitabhikkhū. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānameva. Chaṭṭhaṃ uttānameva.

He attained Parinibbāna with the Nibbāna-element without residue remaining. The great multitude, having honored the elder’s body for seven days, gathered much fragrant firewood, cremated the body, took the relics, and built a shrine. “Several monks” refers to the monks standing at the elder’s cremation place. The rest is clear throughout. The sixth is also clear.

7-8. Paṭhamaejāsuttādivaṇṇanā

7-8. Commentary on the First Ejā Sutta, etc.

90-91. Sattame [M 3.0026] ejāti taṇhā. Sā hi calanaṭṭhena ejāti vuccati. Sāva ābādhanaṭṭhena [V 3.0026] rogo, anto dussanaṭṭhena gaṇḍo, nikantanaṭṭhena sallaṃ. Tasmāti yasmā ejā rogo ceva gaṇḍo ca sallañca, tasmā. Cakkhuṃ na maññeyyātiādi vuttanayameva, idhāpi sabbaṃ heṭṭhā gahitameva saṃkaḍḍhitvā dassitaṃ. Aṭṭhamaṃ vuttanayameva.

90-91. In the seventh, “ejā” is craving. It is called “ejā” because of its characteristic of agitation. It is also a “disease” (rogo) because of its afflicting nature, a “boil” (gaṇḍo) because of its internal corruption, and an “arrow” (sallaṃ) because of its piercing nature. “Therefore” means: since ejā is a disease, a boil, and an arrow, therefore… “Should not conceive the eye,” etc., is by the same method as stated. Here too, everything taken up below is gathered together and shown. The eighth is by the same method as stated.

9-10. Paṭhamadvayasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the First Dvaya Sutta, etc.

92-93. Navame dvayanti dve dve koṭṭhāse. Dasame itthetaṃ dvayanti evametaṃ dvayaṃ. Calañceva byathañcāti attano sabhāvena asaṇṭhahanato calati ceva byathati ca. Yopi hetu yopi paccayoti cakkhuviññāṇassa vatthārammaṇaṃ hetu ceva paccayo ca. Kuto niccaṃ bhavissatīti kena kāraṇena niccaṃ bhavissati. Yathā pana dāsassa dāsiyā kucchismiṃ jāto putto parova dāso hoti, evaṃ aniccameva hotīti attho. Saṅgatīti sahagati. Sannipātoti ekato sannipatanaṃ. Samavāyoti ekato samāgamo. Ayaṃ vuccati cakkhusamphassoti iminā saṅgatisannipātasamavāyasaṅkhātena paccayena uppannattā paccayanāmeneva saṅgati sannipāto samavāyoti ayaṃ vuccati cakkhusamphasso.

92-93. In the ninth, “dvaya” means two parts, two portions. In the tenth, “itthetaṃ dvayaṃ” means “thus this pair.” “Calañceva byathañca” means it trembles and is afflicted because it does not stand firm by its own nature. “Yopi hetu yopi paccayo” means the base and object are both the cause and the condition for eye-consciousness. “Kuto niccaṃ bhavissati” means “By what reason will it be permanent?” Just as a son born in the womb of a female slave is indeed a slave, so too it is simply impermanent—this is the meaning. “Saṅgatī” means coming together. “Sannipāto” means confluence. “Samavāyoti” means concurrence. Regarding “This is called eye-contact”: because it arises from a condition designated as coming together, confluence, and concurrence, contact itself is called 'coming together,' 'confluence,' and 'concurrence' by the name of its condition; thus this is called 'eye-contact'.

Sopi hetūti phassassa vatthu ārammaṇaṃ sahajātā tayo khandhāti ayaṃ hetu. Phuṭṭhoti upayogatthe paccattaṃ, phassena phuṭṭhameva gocaraṃ vedanā vedeti, cetanā ceteti, saññā sañjānātīti attho. Phuṭṭhoti vā phassasamaṅgīpuggalo[P 2.0381], phassena phuṭṭhārammaṇameva vedanādīhi vedeti ceteti sañjānātītipi vuttaṃ hoti. Iti imasmiṃ sutte samatiṃsakkhandhā kathitā honti. Kathaṃ? Cakkhudvāre tāva vatthu ceva ārammaṇañca rūpakkhandho, phuṭṭho vedetīti vedanākkhandho, cetetīti saṅkhārakkhandho, sañjānātīti saññākkhandho, vijānātīti viññāṇakkhandhoti. Sesadvāresupi eseva nayo. Manodvārepi hi vatthurūpaṃ ekantato rūpakkhandho, rūpe pana ārammaṇe sati ārammaṇampi rūpakkhandhoti cha pañcakātiṃsa honti. Saṅkhepena panete chasupi dvāresu pañceva [M 3.0027] khandhāti sapaccaye pañcakkhandhe aniccāti vitthāretvā vuccamāne bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena idaṃ suttaṃ desitanti.

“This too is the cause” means: for contact, the base, the object, and the three co-nascent aggregates—this is the cause. “Phuṭṭho” (contacted) is in the nominative case, here used in the sense of experience; through contact, feeling feels only the object that is contacted, intention intends, and perception perceives—this is the meaning. Alternatively, “phuṭṭho” refers to a person endowed with contact; by means of contact, only the contacted object is felt, intended, and perceived by feeling and so on—this is also said. Thus, in this discourse, thirty aggregates are taught. How? At the eye-door, for instance, the base and the object are the form aggregate; “contacted, one feels” is the feeling aggregate; “one intends” is the formations aggregate; “one perceives” is the perception aggregate; and “one cognizes” is the consciousness aggregate. The same method applies to the remaining doors. At the mind-door too, the physical base is definitely the form aggregate; however, when the object is form, the object too is the form aggregate. Thus, six sets of five make thirty. In brief, however, in all six doors there are just these five aggregates. When the five aggregates with their conditions are explained in detail as impermanent, this discourse is taught according to the disposition of those who are ready to understand.

Channavaggo navamo.

The Channa Chapter is the ninth.

10. Saḷavaggo

10. The Chapter of Six

1. Adantaaguttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Untamed and Unguarded

94. Saḷavaggassa [V 3.0027] paṭhame adantāti adamitā. Aguttāti agopitā. Arakkhitāti na rakkhitā. Asaṃvutāti apihitā. Dukkhādhivāhā hontīti nerayikādibhedaṃ adhikadukkhaṃ āvahanakā honti. Sukhādhivāhā hontīti jhānamaggaphalapabhedaṃ adhikasukhaṃ āvahanakā honti. Adhivahātipi pāṭho, esevattho.

94. In the first of the Chapter of Six, `adantā` means untamed. `Aguttā` means unguarded. `Arakkhitā` means unprotected. `Asaṃvutā` means unclosed. `Dukkhādhivāhā honti` means they are bringers of excessive suffering, of various kinds such as that of the hells. `Sukhādhivāhā honti` means they are bringers of excessive happiness, of various kinds such as that of the jhānas, paths, and fruits. The reading `adhivahā` is also found, and the meaning is the same.

Saḷevāti cha eva. Asaṃvuto yattha dukkhaṃ nigacchatīti yesu āyatanesu saṃvaravirahito dukkhaṃ pāpuṇāti. Tesañca ye saṃvaraṇaṃ avedisunti ye tesaṃ āyatanānaṃ saṃvaraṃ vindiṃsu paṭilabhiṃsu. Viharantānavassutāti viharanti anavassutā atintā.

“Saḷeva” means six indeed. “Asaṃvuto yattha dukkhaṃ nigacchati” means: in those sense bases where one is devoid of restraint, one encounters suffering. “Tesañca ye saṃvaraṇaṃ avedisuṃ” means: those who have found and attained restraint over those sense bases. “Viharantānavassutā” means: they dwell without outflows, unstained.

Asāditañca sādunti assādavantañca madhurañca. Phassadvayaṃ sukhadukkhe upekkheti sukhaphassañca dukkhaphassañcāti idaṃ phassadvayaṃ upekkhe, upekkhāmevettha uppādeyyāti attho. Phassadvayaṃ sukhadukkhaṃ upekkhoti vā pāṭho, phassahetukaṃ sukhadukkhaṃ upekkho, sukhe anurodhaṃ dukkhe ca virodhaṃ anuppādento upekkhako bhaveyyātipi attho. Anānuruddho [P 2.0382] aviruddho kenacīti kenaci saddhiṃ neva anuruddho na viruddho bhaveyya.

`Asāditañca sādunti` means that which is savory and sweet. `Phassadvayaṃ sukhadukkhe upekkhe` means one should regard this pair of contacts—pleasant contact and painful contact—with equanimity; the meaning is that one should generate equanimity therein. Alternatively, the reading is `phassadvayaṃ sukhadukkhaṃ upekkhoti`: one should be equanimous towards pleasure and pain that have contact as their cause; the meaning is also that one should be equanimous by not generating attraction towards pleasure and aversion towards pain. `Anānuruddho aviruddho kenacīti` means one should be neither attracted to nor in conflict with anyone.

Papañcasaññāti kilesasaññāya papañcasaññā nāma hutvā. Itarītarā narāti lāmakasattā papañcayantā upayantīti papañcayamānā vaṭṭaṃ upagacchanti. Saññinoti sasaññā sattā. Manomayaṃ gehasitañca sabbanti sabbameva pañcakāmaguṇagehanissitaṃ manomayaṃ vitakkaṃ. Panujjāti panuditvā nīharitvā. Nekkhammasitaṃ irīyatīti dabbajātiko bhikkhu nekkhammasitaṃ iriyena irīyati.

`Papañcasaññāti` means perceptions of proliferation, so called because they are perceptions associated with defilements. `Itarītarā narāti ... papañcayantā upayanti` means inferior beings, by creating proliferation, approach the round of rebirth. `Saññinoti` means beings possessing perception. `Manomayaṃ gehasitañca sabbanti` means all mind-made thought dependent on the 'house' of the five strands of sensual pleasure. `Panujjāti` means having dispelled, having removed. `Nekkhammasitaṃ irīyatīti` means a monk of noble birth behaves with conduct based on renunciation.

Chassu yadā [M 3.0028] subhāvitoti chasu ārammaṇesu yadā suṭṭhu bhāvito. Phuṭṭhassa cittaṃ na vikampate [V 3.0028] kvacīti sukhaphassena vā dukkhaphassena vā phuṭṭhassa kismiñci cittaṃ na kampati na vedhati. Bhavattha jātimaraṇassa pāragāti jātimaraṇānaṃ pāraṃ nibbānaṃ gamakā hotha.

`Chassu yadā subhāvitoti` means when, in regard to the six objects, it is thoroughly cultivated. `Phuṭṭhassa cittaṃ na vikampate kvacīti` means for one who is touched by either pleasant or painful contact, the mind does not tremble or shake in regard to anything. `Bhavattha jātimaraṇassa pāragāti` means be those who go to Nibbāna, the far shore of birth and death.

2. Mālukyaputtasuttavaṇṇanā

2. The Explanation of the Mālukyaputta Sutta

95. Dutiye mālukyaputtoti mālukyabrāhmaṇiyā putto. Etthāti etasmiṃ tava ovādāyācane. Iminā theraṃ apasādetipi ussādetipi. Kathaṃ? Ayaṃ kira daharakāle rūpādīsu pamajjitvā pacchā mahallakakāle araññavāsaṃ patthento kammaṭṭhānaṃ yācati. Atha bhagavā “ettha dahare kiṃ vakkhāma? Mālukyaputto viya tumhepi taruṇakāle pamajjitvā mahallakakāle araññaṃ pavisitvā samaṇadhammaṃ kareyyāthā”ti iminā adhippāyena bhaṇanto theraṃ apasādeti nāma.

95. In the second sutta, `mālukyaputtoti` means the son of the brahmin woman Mālukyā. `Etthāti` means: in this your request for instruction. By this, the Blessed One both rebukes and encourages the elder. How? It is said that this elder, having been heedless regarding forms and so on in his youth, later in his old age, desiring to dwell in the forest, asks for a meditation subject. Then the Blessed One, speaking with this intention, ‘In this case, what shall we say to the young monks? “Like Mālukyaputta, you too, having been heedless in your youth, might enter the forest in your old age and practice the duty of a recluse”’—thus he is said to rebuke the elder.

Yasmā pana thero mahallakakālepi araññaṃ pavisitvā samaṇadhammaṃ kātukāmo, tasmā bhagavā “ettha dahare kiṃ vakkhāma? Ayaṃ amhākaṃ mālukyaputto [P 2.0383] mahallakakālepi araññaṃ pavisitvā samaṇadhammaṃ kattukāmo kammaṭṭhānaṃ yācati, tumhe nāma taruṇakālepi vīriyaṃ na karothā”ti iminā adhippāyena bhaṇanto theraṃ ussādeti nāma.

However, since the elder, even in his old age, is desirous of entering the forest and practicing the duty of a recluse, therefore the Blessed One, speaking with this intention, is said to encourage the elder: ‘In this case, what shall we say to the young monks? “This Mālukyaputta of ours, even in his old age, wishing to enter the forest to practice the duty of a recluse, asks for a meditation subject; but you, even in your youth, do not make an effort!”’

Yatra hi nāmāti yo nāma. Kiñcāpāhanti kiñcāpi “ahaṃ mahallako”ti ñātaṃ. Yadi ahaṃ mahallako, mahallako samānopi sakkhissāmi samaṇadhammaṃ kātuṃ, desetu me, bhante, bhagavāti adhippāyena mahallakabhāvaṃ anuggaṇhanto ovādañca pasaṃsanto evamāha.

`Yatra hi nāmāti` means: whoever. `Kiñcāpāhanti` means: although it is known, “I am old.” With this intention, “Even if I am old, being old, I will be able to practice the duty of a recluse; Venerable Sir, let the Blessed One teach me,” not dwelling on his state of being old and praising the instruction, he spoke thus.

Adiṭṭhā adiṭṭhapubbāti imasmiṃ attabhāve adiṭṭhā atītepi adiṭṭhapubbā. Na ca passasīti etarahipi na passasi. Na ca te hoti passeyyanti evaṃ samannāhāropi te yattha natthi, api nu te tattha chandādayo uppajjeyyunti pucchati.

`Adiṭṭhā adiṭṭhapubbāti` means: unseen in this existence, and not seen before in the past. `Na ca passasīti` means: even now you do not see. `Na ca te hoti passeyyanti` means: where even such a mental advertence is not present for you, could desire and so on arise for you there? Thus He asks.

Diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattanti rūpāyatane cakkhuviññāṇena diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattaṃ bhavissati. Cakkhuviññāṇañhi rūpe rūpamattameva passati, na niccādisabhāvaṃ, iti sesaviññāṇehipi me ettha diṭṭhamattameva cittaṃ bhavissatīti attho. Atha vā [M 3.0029] diṭṭhe diṭṭhaṃ nāma cakkhuviññāṇaṃ, rūpe rūpavijānananti [V 3.0029] attho. Mattāti pamāṇaṃ, diṭṭhaṃ mattā assāti diṭṭhamattaṃ, cittaṃ, cakkhuviññāṇamattameva me cittaṃ bhavissatīti attho. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yathā āpāthagatarūpe cakkhuviññāṇaṃ na rajjati na dussati na muyhati, evaṃ rāgādivirahena cakkhuviññāṇamattameva javanaṃ bhavissati, cakkhuviññāṇapamāṇeneva javanaṃ ṭhapessāmīti. Atha vā diṭṭhaṃ nāma cakkhuviññāṇena diṭṭharūpaṃ, diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattaṃ nāma tattheva uppannaṃ sampaṭicchanasantīraṇavoṭṭhabbanasaṅkhātaṃ cittattayaṃ. Yathā taṃ na rajjati, na dussati, na muyhati, evaṃ āpāthagate rūpe teneva sampaṭicchanādippamāṇena javanaṃ uppādessāmi, nāhaṃ taṃ pamāṇaṃ atikkamitvā [P 2.0384] rajjanādivasena uppajjituṃ dassāmīti ayamettha attho. Eseva nayo sutamutesu.

`Diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattanti` means: in the seen, there will be only the seen. When a form-object is seen by eye-consciousness, there will be just the seen. For eye-consciousness sees only the mere form in a form-object, not its nature as permanent, etc. The meaning is: thus, even with the remaining consciousnesses, my mind regarding this will be one of mere seeing. Or alternatively: in the seen, the ‘seeing’ is eye-consciousness; the meaning is the knowing of form in a form-object. `Mattāti` means measure. `Diṭṭhamattaṃ` means that for which the seeing is the measure, referring to the mind. The meaning is: my mind will be one of mere eye-consciousness. This is what is meant: just as eye-consciousness, with regard to a form that has come into range, is not impassioned, is not averse, and is not deluded, so too, being free from passion and so on, the impulsion will be one of mere eye-consciousness; I will establish the impulsion only within the measure of eye-consciousness. Or alternatively: the ‘seen’ is the form seen by eye-consciousness. `Diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattaṃ` is the triad of mind-moments called receiving, investigating, and determining, which arises right there. Just as that triad is not impassioned, is not averse, and is not deluded, so too, with regard to a form that has come into range, I will cause the impulsion to arise with that same measure of receiving and so on. I will not, having transgressed that measure, allow it to arise through the power of passion and so on. This is the meaning here. This same method applies to the heard and the sensed.

Viññāte viññātamattanti ettha pana viññātaṃ nāma manodvārāvajjanena viññātārammaṇaṃ, tasmiṃ viññāte viññātamattanti āvajjanapamāṇaṃ. Yathā āvajjanena na rajjati na dussati na muyhati, evaṃ rajjanādivasena uppajjituṃ adatvā āvajjanapamāṇeneva cittaṃ ṭhapessāmīti ayamettha attho.

In `viññāte viññātamattanti`, the ‘cognized’ is the object cognized by mind-door adverting. In that cognized, `viññātamattanti` means the measure of the adverting. The meaning here is: just as by adverting one is not impassioned, is not averse, and is not deluded, so too, not allowing it to arise through the power of passion and so on, I will establish the mind only within the measure of adverting.

Yatoti yadā. Tatoti tadā. Na tenāti tena rāgena vā ratto, dosena vā duṭṭho, mohena vā mūḷho na bhavissati. Tato tvaṃ mālukyaputta na tatthāti yadā tvaṃ tena rāgena vā dosamohehi vā ratto vā duṭṭho vā mūḷho vā na bhavissasi, tadā tvaṃ na tattha tasmiṃ diṭṭhe vā sutamutaviññāte vā paṭibaddho allīno patiṭṭhito nāma bhavissasi. Nevidhātiādi vuttatthameva.

`Yatoti` means: when. `Tatoti` means: then. `Na tenāti` means: you will not be impassioned by that passion, corrupted by that aversion, or deluded by that delusion. `Tato tvaṃ mālukyaputta na tatthāti` means: when you are not impassioned by that passion, or corrupted and deluded by that aversion and delusion, then you will not be bound to, clinging to, or established in that seen, heard, sensed, or cognized object. The passage beginning `Nevidhāti` has the meaning already explained.

Sati muṭṭhāti sati naṭṭhā. Tañca ajjhosāti taṃ ārammaṇaṃ gilitvā. Abhijjhā ca vihesā cāti abhijjhāya ca vihiṃsāya ca. Atha vā “tassa vaḍḍhantī”ti padenāpi saddhiṃ yojetabbaṃ, abhijjhā ca vihesā cāti imepi dve dhammā tassa vaḍḍhantīti attho.

`Sati muṭṭhā` means: mindfulness is lost. `Tañca ajjhosāya` means: having swallowed that object. `Abhijjhā ca vihesā ca` means by covetousness and by vexation. Or alternatively, it should be connected with the words `tassa vaḍḍhanti`; the meaning is: these two states, covetousness and vexation, also increase for him.

Cittamassūpahaññatīti abhijjhāvihesāhi assa cittaṃ upahaññati. Ācinatoti ācinantassa. Ārā nibbāna vuccatīti evarūpassa puggalassa nibbānaṃ nāma dūre pavuccati. Ghatvāti ghāyitvā. Bhotvāti bhutvā sāyitvā [M 3.0030] lehitvā. Phussāti phusitvā. Paṭissatoti paṭissatisaṅkhātāya [V 3.0030] satiyā yutto. Sevato cāpi vedananti catumaggasampayuttaṃ nibbattitalokuttaravedanaṃ sevantassa. Khīyatīti khayaṃ gacchati. Kiṃ taṃ? Dukkhampi kilesajātampi. Aññataroti asītiyā mahāsāvakānaṃ abbhantaro eko. Iti imasmiṃ sutte gāthāhipi vaṭṭavivaṭṭameva kathitaṃ.

`Cittamassūpahaññati` means: his mind is afflicted by covetousness and vexation. `Ācinato` means: for one who is accumulating. `Ārā nibbānaṃ vuccati` means: for a person of such a nature, Nibbāna is said to be far. `Ghatvā` means: having smelled. `Bhotvā` means: having eaten, having tasted, having licked. `Phussa` means: having touched. `Paṭissato` means: endowed with the mindfulness called recollection. `Sevato cāpi vedanaṃ` means: for one who attends to the supramundane feeling associated with the four paths, which has been brought forth. `Khīyati` means: it goes to destruction. What is that? Both suffering and the host of defilements. `Aññataro` means: one included among the eighty great disciples. Thus, in this discourse, even in the verses, only the round of existence and its cessation are taught.

3. Parihānadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on the State of Decline

96. Tatiye parihānadhammanti parihānasabhāvaṃ. Abhibhāyatanānīti abhibhavitāni āyatanāni. Sarasaṅkappāti ettha sarantīti sarā, dhāvantīti attho. Sarā ca [P 2.0385] te saṅkappā ca sarasaṅkappā. Saṃyojaniyāti bandhaniyā bandhanassa paccayabhūtā. Tañce bhikkhūti taṃ evaṃ uppannaṃ kilesajātaṃ, taṃ vā ārammaṇaṃ. Adhivāsetīti citte āropetvā vāseti. Nappajahatīti chandarāgappahānena na pajahati. Evaṃ sabbapadehi yojetabbaṃ. Abhibhāyatanañhetaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatāti etaṃ buddhena bhagavatā abhibhavitaṃ āyatananti kathitaṃ. Idha dhammaṃ pucchitvā vibhajantena puggalena dhammo dassito.

96. In the third discourse, `parihānadhamma` means a state of decline. `Abhibhāyatanāni` means the spheres that are overwhelmed. Regarding `sarasaṅkappā`: here, because they run (`saranti`), they are `sarā`; the meaning is that they rush about. They are both `sarā` and `saṅkappā`, thus they are `sarasaṅkappā`. `Saṃyojaniyā` means things to be bound, which are a condition for bondage. `Tañce bhikkhu` refers to that group of defilements thus arisen, or to that object. `Adhivāseti` means he lets it dwell, having placed it in the mind. `Nappajahati` means he does not abandon it by abandoning desire and lust. In this way, it should be connected with all the terms. `Abhibhāyatanañhetaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā` means: this was taught by the Buddha, the Blessed One, as a sphere that is overwhelmed. Here, having been asked about the Dhamma, the one analyzing it has shown the Dhamma by way of a person.

4. Pamādavihārīsuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Discourse on One Who Dwells in Heedlessness

97. Catutthe asaṃvutassāti apihitassa na pidahitvā sañchāditvā ṭhapitassa. Byāsiñcatīti viāsiñcati, kilesatintaṃ hutvā vattati. Pāmojjanti dubbalapīti. Pītīti balavapīti. Passaddhīti darathapassaddhi. Dhammā na pātubhavantīti samathavipassanādhammā na uppajjanti. Imasmiṃ sutte puggalaṃ pucchitvā vibhajantena dhammena puggalo dassito.

97. In the fourth discourse, `asaṃvutassa` means of one who is uncovered, not having been closed, not having been covered, having been left open. `Byāsiñcati` means it thoroughly drenches; it exists, having become the moisture of defilements. `Pāmojja` means weak rapture. `Pīti` means strong rapture. `Passaddhi` means the tranquility of distress. `Dhammā na pātubhavanti` means the dhammas of serenity and insight do not arise. In this discourse, having been asked about the person, the one analyzing has shown the person by way of the Dhamma.

5. Saṃvarasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Discourse on Restraint

98. Pañcame kathañca, bhikkhave, asaṃvaroti idaṃ maggakusalassa vāmaṃ muñcitvā dakkhiṇaṃ gaṇheyyāsīti paṭhamaṃ pahātabbamaggakkhānaṃ viya uddesakkamena avatvā desanākusalatāya paṭhamaṃ pahātabbadhammakkhānavasena vuttanti veditabbaṃ. Idha dhammaṃ pucchitvā dhammova vibhatto.

98. In the fifth discourse, this statement, 'And how, monks, is there lack of restraint?' should be understood as not having been spoken in the order of a summary—like an explanation for one skilled in the path of the way to be abandoned first, saying, 'Having abandoned the left, you should take the right'—but rather, due to skill in teaching, it was spoken by way of explaining first the Dhamma to be abandoned. Here, having been asked about the Dhamma, the Dhamma itself was analyzed.

6. Samādhisuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Discourse on Concentration

99. Chaṭṭhe [V 3.0031][M 3.0031] samādhinti cittekaggataṃ. Idañhi suttaṃ cittekaggatāya parihāyamāne disvā, “imesaṃ cittekaggataṃ labhantānaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ phātiṃ gamissatī”ti ñatvā kathitaṃ.

99. In the sixth discourse, `samādhi` means one-pointedness of mind. Indeed, this discourse was taught after seeing monks declining from one-pointedness of mind and knowing, 'For these who are attaining one-pointedness of mind, their meditation subject will prosper.'

7. Paṭisallānasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Discourse on Seclusion

100. Sattame [P 2.0386] paṭisallānanti kāyavivekaṃ. Idañhi suttaṃ kāyavivekena parihāyamāne disvā, “imesaṃ kāyavivekaṃ labhantānaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ phātiṃ gamissatī”ti ñatvā kathitaṃ.

100. In the seventh discourse, `paṭisallāna` means bodily seclusion. Indeed, this discourse was taught after seeing monks declining in bodily seclusion and knowing, 'For these who are attaining bodily seclusion, their meditation subject will prosper.'

8-9. Paṭhamanatumhākaṃsuttādivaṇṇanā

8-9. Commentary on the First 'Not Yours' Discourse and Others

101-102. Aṭṭhamaṃ upamāya parivāretvā kathite bujjhanakānaṃ, navamaṃ suddhikavaseneva bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayavasena vuttaṃ. Attho pana ubhayatthāpi khandhiyavagge vuttanayeneva veditabbo.

The eighth discourse, when taught surrounded by simile, was spoken according to the inclination of those who are to understand; the ninth was spoken purely, according to the inclination of those who are to understand. The meaning in both cases, however, should be understood only in the way explained in the Khandhiyavagga.

10. Udakasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Udaka Discourse

103. Dasame udako sudanti ettha sudanti nipātamattaṃ. Udakoti tassa nāmaṃ. Idaṃ jātu vedagūti ettha idanti nipātamattaṃ. Atha vā idaṃ mama vacanaṃ suṇāthāti dīpento evamāha. Jātu vedagūti ahaṃ ekaṃseneva vedagū, vedasaṅkhātena ñāṇena neyyesu gato, vedaṃ vā gato adhigato, paṇḍitohamasmīti attho. Sabbajīti ekaṃsena sabbavaṭṭaṃ jinitvā abhibhavitvā ṭhitosmīti vadati. Apalikhataṃ gaṇḍamūlanti apalikhataṃ dukkhamūlaṃ. Palikhaṇinti palikhataṃ mayā, khanitvā ṭhitosmīti dīpeti.

103. In the tenth discourse, regarding `udako sudaṃ`, here `sudaṃ` is merely a particle. `Udako` is his name. Regarding `idaṃ jātu vedagū`, here `idaṃ` is merely a particle. Alternatively, explaining thus, 'Listen to this word of mine!', he spoke in this way. `Jātu vedagū` means: 'I am certainly a `vedagū`, one who has gone to the knowable through the knowledge called `veda`, or one who has gone to and attained `veda`; I am wise.' This is the meaning. `Sabbajī`: he says, 'I stand, having certainly conquered and overcome the entire round of existence.' `Apalikhataṃ gaṇḍamūlaṃ` means the unexcavated root of suffering. `Palikhaṇiṃ`: he shows, 'It has been dug up by me; I stand, having dug it up.'

Mātāpettikasambhavassāti mātito ca pitito ca nibbattena mātāpettikena sukkasoṇitena sambhūtassa. Odanakummāsūpacayassāti odanena ceva kummāsena ca upacitassa vaḍḍhitassa. Aniccucchādanaparimaddanabhedanaviddhaṃsanadhammassāti ettha ayaṃ kāyo hutvā abhāvaṭṭhena aniccadhammo[V 3.0032], duggandhavighātatthāya tanuvilepanena ucchādanadhammo, aṅgapaccaṅgābādhavinodanatthāya khuddakasambāhanena [P 2.0387] parimaddanadhammo, daharakāle [M 3.0032] vā ūrūsu sayāpetvā gabbhavāsena dussaṇṭhitānaṃ tesaṃ tesaṃ aṅgānaṃ saṇṭhānasampādanatthaṃ añchanapīḷanādīnaṃ vasena parimaddanadhammo, evaṃ pariharitopi ca bhedanaviddhaṃsanadhammo bhijjati ceva vikirati ca, evaṃ sabhāvoti attho.

`Mātāpettikasambhava` means born from mother and father, produced from the semen and blood of both parents. `Odanakummāsūpacaya` means grown and increased by rice and gruel. `Aniccucchādanaparimaddanabhedanaviddhaṃsanadhamma`—here, this body is of an impermanent nature by reason of ceasing to be after having come into being; it is of a nature to be anointed with fine ointments to counteract its foul smell; it is of a nature to be massaged with gentle rubbing to relieve pains in its limbs, or, in infancy, to be massaged by laying it on the thighs and by means of pulling, pressing, and so on, to properly shape those various limbs that had become malformed from dwelling in the womb; and even when cared for in this way, it is of a nature to break up and be destroyed—that is, it breaks up and scatters. Such is its nature—this is the meaning.

Tattha mātāpettikasambhavaodanakummāsūpacayaparimaddanapadehi vaḍḍhi kathitā, aniccabhedanaviddhaṃsanapadehi parihāni. Purimehi vā tīhi samudayo, pacchimehi atthaṅgamoti. Evaṃ cātumahābhūtikassa kāyassa vaḍḍhiparihāninibbattibhedā dassitā. Sesaṃ uttānatthamevāti.

Here, by the terms `mātāpettikasambhava`, `odanakummāsūpacaya`, and `parimaddana`, growth is taught; by the terms `anicca`, `bhedana`, and `viddhaṃsana`, decline is taught. Alternatively, by the former three terms, arising is taught; by the latter, passing away. In this way, the growth, decline, arising, and perishing of the body composed of the four great elements are shown. The rest has an obvious meaning.

Saḷavaggo dasamo.

The Chapter of Six is the tenth.

Dutiyo paṇṇāsako.

The Second Fifty.

11. Yogakkhemivaggo

11. The Chapter on Security from Bondage

1. Yogakkhemisuttavaṇṇanā

1. Explanation of the Discourse on Security from Bondage

104. Yogakkhemivaggassa paṭhame yogakkhemipariyāyanti catūhi yogehi khemino kāraṇabhūtaṃ. Dhammapariyāyanti dhammakāraṇaṃ. Akkhāsi yoganti yuttiṃ kathesi. Tasmāti kasmā? Kiṃ akkhātattā, udāhu pahīnattāti? Pahīnattā. Na hi akkhānena yogakkhemi nāma hoti.

104. In the first discourse of the Chapter on Security from Bondage, 'the exposition on security from bondage' means that which is the cause of security from the four bonds. 'The exposition of the Dhamma' means the cause for knowing the Dhamma. 'He taught the method' means he taught the suitable means. 'Therefore'—why does one become one named 'secure from bondage'? Is it because it was taught, or is it because it was abandoned? It is because it was abandoned. For one does not become 'secure from bondage' merely by means of teaching.

2-10. Upādāyasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-10. Commentary on the Upādāya Sutta and Others

105-113. Dutiye vedanāsukhadukkhaṃ kathitaṃ, taṃ pana vipākasukhadukkhaṃ vaṭṭati. Tatiye dukkhassāti vaṭṭadukkhassa. Catutthe lokassāti saṅkhāralokassa. Pañcamādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ siyā, taṃ khandhiyavagge vuttanayameva.

In the second discourse, the pleasure and pain that is feeling is discussed; that, however, is properly the pleasure and pain that is resultant. In the third, 'of suffering' means of the suffering of the round. In the fourth, 'of the world' means of the world of formations. In the fifth and subsequent discourses, whatever should be said, that has the very same method as stated in the Khandhiyavagga.

Yogakkhemivaggo ekādasamo.

The Chapter on Security from Bondage is the eleventh.

12. Lokakāmaguṇavaggo

12. The Chapter on Worldly Sense Pleasures

1-2. Paṭhamamārapāsasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-2. Commentary on the First Mārapāsa Sutta and Others

114-115. Lokakāmaguṇavaggassa [V 3.0033][M 3.0033][P 2.0388] paṭhame āvāsagatoti vasanaṭṭhānaṃ gato. Mārassa vasaṃ gatoti tividhassāpi mārassa vasaṃ gato. Paṭimukka’ssa mārapāsoti assa gīvāya mārapāso paṭimukko pavesito. Dutiyaṃ uttānameva.

In the first discourse of the Chapter on Worldly Sense Pleasures, 'gone to the abode' means gone to the abode of Māra. 'Fallen under Māra’s control' means fallen under the control of the threefold Māra. 'The snare of Māra is fastened to him' means the snare of Māra is fastened on his neck, is inserted. The second discourse is self-evident.

3. Lokantagamanasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Lokantagamana Sutta

116. Tatiye lokassāti cakkavāḷalokassa. Lokassa antanti saṅkhāralokassa antaṃ. Vihāraṃ pāvisīti “mayi vihāraṃ paviṭṭhe ime bhikkhū, imaṃ uddesaṃ ānandaṃ pucchissanti, so ca tesaṃ mama sabbaññutaññāṇena saṃsanditvā kathessati. Tato naṃ thomessāmi, mama thomanaṃ sutvā bhikkhū ānandaṃ upasaṅkamitabbaṃ, vacanañcassa sotabbaṃ saddhātabbaṃ maññissanti, taṃ nesaṃ bhavissati dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāyā”ti cintetvā saṃkhittena bhāsitassa vitthārena atthaṃ avibhajitvāva nisinnāsane antarahito gandhakuṭiyaṃ pāturahosi. Tena vuttaṃ “uṭṭhāyāsanā vihāraṃ pāvisī”ti.

116. In the third discourse, 'of the world' means of the world-system. 'The end of the world' means the end of the world of formations. 'He entered the dwelling' means: thinking, 'When I have entered the dwelling, these monks will ask Ānanda about this summary, and he will explain it to them after comparing it with my omniscient knowledge. Then I will praise him. Having heard my praise, the monks will consider Ānanda as one who should be approached, his words as what should be listened to, and as what should be trusted. That will be for their welfare and happiness for a long time,' without having analyzed in detail the meaning of what was spoken in brief, he disappeared from the seat where he was sitting and reappeared in the perfumed chamber. Therefore it was said, 'Rising from his seat, he entered the dwelling.'

Satthu ceva saṃvaṇṇitoti satthārā ca pasattho. Viññūnanti idampi karaṇatthe sāmivacanaṃ, paṇḍitehi sabrahmacārīhi ca sambhāvitoti attho. Pahotīti sakkoti. Atikkammeva mūlaṃ atikkammeva khandhanti sāro nāma mūle vā khandhe vā bhaveyya, tampi atikkamitvāti attho. Evaṃsampadamidanti evaṃsampattikaṃ, īdisanti attho. Atisitvāti atikkamitvā. Jānaṃ jānātīti jānitabbameva jānāti. Passaṃ passatīti passitabbameva passati. Yathā vā ekacco viparītaṃ gaṇhanto jānantopi na jānāti, passantopi na passati, na evaṃ bhagavā. Bhagavā pana jānanto jānāti, passanto passatiyeva. Svāyaṃ dassanapariṇāyakaṭṭhena cakkhubhūto. Viditakaraṇaṭṭhena ñāṇabhūto[P 2.0389]. Aviparītasabhāvaṭṭhena pariyattidhammapavattanato vā hadayena cintetvā vācāya nicchāritadhammamayoti dhammabhūto. Seṭṭhaṭṭhena brahmabhūto. Atha vā cakkhu [V 3.0034] viya bhūtoti cakkhubhūto. Evametesu padesu attho veditabbo. Svāyaṃ dhammassa [M 3.0034] vattanato vattā. Pavattanato pavattā. Atthaṃ nīharitvā nīharitvā dassanasamatthatāya atthassa ninnetā. Amatādhigamāya paṭipattiṃ desetīti amatassa dātā.

'Praised by the Teacher' means praised by the Teacher. 'By the wise' is a genitive in the sense of the instrumental; the meaning is: esteemed by wise fellow practitioners. 'Is capable' means is able. 'Having gone beyond the root, having gone beyond the trunk' means: essence may be found in the root or the trunk, but he has gone beyond even that. 'Such is this attainment' means endowed with such a state, of such a kind. 'Having transcended' means having gone beyond. 'Knowing, he knows' means he knows only what should be known. 'Seeing, he sees' means he sees only what should be seen. Or, it is not that the Blessed One is like some who, grasping pervertedly, do not know even when knowing, and do not see even when seeing. Rather, the Blessed One, knowing, truly knows; seeing, he truly sees. He is the Eye (cakkhubhūto) because he is the guide to vision. He is Knowledge (ñāṇabhūto) because he is the one who makes things known. He is Dhamma (dhammabhūto) because of his unperverted nature, or because he sets the scriptural Dhamma in motion, or because he is made of the Dhamma uttered by his speech after reflecting with his heart. He is Brahmā (brahmabhūto) because he is the best. Alternatively, he is the Eye (cakkhubhūto) because he has become like an eye. Thus the meaning of these terms should be understood. He is the Speaker (vattā) because he speaks the Dhamma. He is the Proclaimer (pavattā) because he sets it in motion. He is the Guide to the meaning (atthassa ninnetā) because of his ability to bring forth the meaning and show it. He is the Giver of the Deathless (amatassa dātā) because he teaches the practice for the attainment of the Deathless.

Agaruṃ karitvāti punappunaṃ yācāpentopi hi garuṃ karoti nāma. Attano sekkhapaṭisambhidāñāṇe ṭhatvā sinerupādato vālikaṃ uddharamāno viya dubbiññeyyaṃ katvā kathentopi garuṃ karotiyeva nāma. Evaṃ akatvā amhe punappunaṃ ayācāpetvā suviññeyyampi no katvā kathehīti vuttaṃ hoti.

'Without making it burdensome' means: for one who makes others request again and again is indeed making it burdensome. One who, established in one's own knowledge of the analytical insights of a trainee, teaches after making it difficult to understand—like one trying to lift a measure of gold dust from the foot of Mount Sineru—also indeed makes it burdensome. Thus, it is said: 'Without doing so, without making us request again and again, but having made it easy for us to understand, please teach.'

Yaṃ kho voti yaṃ kho tumhākaṃ. Cakkhunā kho, āvuso, lokasmiṃ lokasaññī hoti lokamānīti cakkhuñhi loke appahīnadiṭṭhi puthujjano sattalokavasena lokoti sañjānāti ceva maññati ca, tathā cakkavāḷalokavasena. Na hi aññatra cakkhādīhi dvādasāyatanehi tassa sā saññā vā māno vā uppajjati. Tena vuttaṃ, “cakkhunā kho, āvuso, lokasmiṃ lokasaññī hoti lokamānī”ti. Imassa ca lokassa gamanena anto nāma ñātuṃ vā daṭṭhuṃ vā pattuṃ vā na sakkā. Lujjanaṭṭhena pana tasseva cakkhādibhedassa lokassa nibbānasaṅkhātaṃ antaṃ appatvā vaṭṭadukkhassa antakiriyā nāma natthīti veditabbā.

'What is yours' means what is indeed yours. 'Friends, with the eye, in the world, one is a perceiver of the world, a conceiver of the world.' For an ordinary person whose wrong view is unabandoned perceives and conceives the eye as 'world' by way of the world of beings, and likewise by way of the world-system. For apart from the twelve sense bases beginning with the eye, that perception or conceit of his does not arise. Therefore it was said, 'Friends, with the eye, in the world, one is a perceiver of the world, a conceiver of the world.' And the end of this world cannot be known, seen, or reached by traveling. However, it should be understood that without reaching the end called Nibbāna of that very world—differentiated as the eye and so on, which has the nature of perishing—there is no making an end of the suffering of the round.

Evaṃ pañhaṃ vissajjetvā idāni “sāvakena pañho kathitoti mā nikkaṅkhā ahuvattha, ayaṃ bhagavā sabbaññutaññāṇatulaṃ gahetvā nisinno. Icchamānā tameva upasaṅkamitvā nikkaṅkhā hothā”ti uyyojento ākaṅkhamānā panātiādimāha.

Having thus answered the question, he now urged them, saying, 'Do not be satisfied and free from doubt thinking, “The question has been answered by a disciple.” This Blessed One is seated, having taken up the scale of omniscient knowledge. If you wish, approach him and become free from doubt.' Thus urging them, he spoke the words beginning with 'ākaṅkhamānā pana...'

Imehi ākārehīti imehi kāraṇehi cakkavāḷalokassa antābhāvakāraṇehi ceva saṅkhāralokassa antāpattikāraṇehi ca. Imehi padehīti imehi akkharasampiṇḍanehi. Byañjanehīti pāṭiyekkaakkharehi.

'By these modes' means by these reasons—both the reasons for the non-existence of an end to the world-system and the reasons for the attainment of the end of the world of formations. 'By these terms' means by these collections of letters. 'By these letters' means by the individual letters.

Paṇḍitoti [P 2.0390] paṇḍiccena samannāgato. Catūhi kāraṇehi paṇḍito dhātukusalo āyatanakusalo paccayākārakusalo kāraṇākāraṇakusaloti. Mahāpaññoti mahante atthe mahante dhamme mahantā [M 3.0035] niruttiyo mahantāni paṭibhānāni paṭiggaṇhanasamatthatāya mahāpaññāya samannāgato. Yathā [V 3.0035] taṃ ānandenāti yathā ānandena byākataṃ, taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Yathā ānandena taṃ byākataṃ, ahampi taṃ evameva byākareyyanti attho.

‘A wise one’ means endowed with wisdom. A wise one is so for four reasons: skilled in the elements, skilled in the sense bases, skilled in causal relations, and skilled in causes and non-causes. ‘One with great wisdom’ means endowed with great wisdom because of being capable of grasping great meanings, great doctrines, great expressions, and great insights. ‘As by Ānanda’—this is said with reference to how it was explained by Ānanda. ‘Just as Ānanda explained it, I too would explain it in just that way’—is the meaning.

4. Kāmaguṇasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Discourse on Sensual Pleasures

117. Catutthe ye meti ye mama. Cetaso samphuṭṭhapubbāti cittena anubhūtapubbā. Tatra me cittaṃ bahulaṃ gacchamānaṃ gaccheyyāti tesu pāsādattayatividhanāṭakādibhedasampattivasena anubhūtapubbesu pañcasu kāmaguṇesu bahūsu vāresu uppajjamānaṃ uppajjeyyāti dīpeti. Paccuppannesu vāti idha padhānacariyakāle chabbassāni supupphitavanasaṇḍajātānaṃ dijagaṇādīnaṃ vasena diṭṭhasutādibhedaṃ manoramārammaṇaṃ kāmaguṇaṃ katvā dassento “evarūpesu paccuppannesu vā bahulaṃ uppajjeyyā”ti dasseti. Appaṃ vā anāgatesūti anāgate “metteyyo nāma buddho bhavissati, saṅkho nāma rājā, ketumatī nāma rājadhānī”tiādivasena (dī. ni. 3.106) parittakameva anāgatesu kāmaguṇesu uppajjeyyāti dasseti. Tatra me attarūpenāti tatra mayā attano hitakāmajātikena. Appamādoti sātaccakiriyā pañcasu kāmaguṇesu cittassa avossaggo. Satīti ārammaṇapariggahitasati. Ārakkhoti ayaṃ appamādo ca sati ca cetaso ārakkho karaṇīyo, evaṃ me ahosīti dasseti, ārakkhatthāya ime dve dhammā kātabbāti vuttaṃ hoti.

117. In the fourth sutta, ‘those of mine’ (ye me) means ‘those of mine’ (ye mama). ‘Previously touched by the mind’ (cetaso samphuṭṭhapubbā) means ‘previously experienced by the mind’ (cittena anubhūtapubbā). ‘There my mind would frequently go’ (tatra me cittaṃ bahulaṃ gacchamānaṃ gaccheyyā) indicates that it would arise repeatedly towards those five strands of sensual pleasure that had been previously experienced through various forms of prosperity, such as the three kinds of palaces, the three kinds of dancers, and the like. ‘Or in the present’ (paccuppannesu vā) means that here, during the time of striving, for six years, having made a delightful object of the senses—seen, heard, and so on—by way of flocks of birds and so on in fully blossomed forest groves, he shows, ‘In such present objects, it may frequently arise.’ ‘Or little in the future’ (appaṃ vā anāgatesū) indicates that in the future, when ‘Metteyya will be the Buddha, Saṅkha the king, Ketumatī the capital,’ and so on, the mind would arise only a little toward future strands of sensual pleasure. ‘There, by my own nature’ (tatra me attarūpena) means there, by me, who was by nature desirous of my own welfare. ‘Vigilance’ (appamādo) is continuous action and non-release of the mind towards the five strands of sensual pleasure. ‘Mindfulness’ (satī) is mindfulness that grasps the object. ‘Guardianship’ (ārakkho) means that this vigilance and mindfulness should be made a guard for the mind—thus, he shows, ‘This was how it was for me.’ It is said that these two states should be cultivated for the sake of guarding.

Tasmātiha, bhikkhave, se āyatane veditabbeti yasmā cetaso ārakkhatthāya appamādo ca sati ca kātabbā, yasmā tasmiṃ āyatane vidite appamādena vā satiyā vā kātabbaṃ natthi, tasmā [P 2.0391] se āyatane veditabbe, taṃ kāraṇaṃ jānitabbanti attho. Saḷāyatananirodhanti saḷāyatananirodho vuccati nibbānaṃ, taṃ sandhāya bhāsitanti attho. Nibbānasmiñhi cakkhuādīni ceva nirujjhanti rūpasaññādayo ca nirujjhantīti. Sesaṃ vuttanayameva.

‘Therefore, bhikkhus, that sphere should be understood’ (tasmātiha, bhikkhave, se āyatane veditabbe) means that because vigilance and mindfulness should be cultivated for the sake of guarding the mind, and because when that sphere is known, there is nothing to be done with vigilance or mindfulness, therefore that sphere should be understood, and that reason should be known. ‘The cessation of the six sense bases’ (saḷāyatananirodhaṃ) means that the cessation of the six sense bases is called Nibbāna, and it is with reference to that that it was spoken. For in Nibbāna, the eye and so on cease, and perceptions of forms and so on cease. The rest is as has been said.

5-6. Sakkapañhasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the Discourse on Sakka's Questions, etc.

118-119. Pañcame [M 3.0036] diṭṭheva dhammeti imasmiṃyeva attabhāve. Parinibbāyantīti kilesaparinibbānena parinibbāyanti. Tannissitaṃ viññāṇaṃ hotīti taṇhānissitaṃ kammaviññāṇaṃ hoti[V 3.0036]. Tadupādānanti taṃgahaṇaṃ, taṇhāgahaṇena sahagataṃ viññāṇaṃ hotīti attho. Chaṭṭhaṃ uttānameva.

118-119. In the fifth [sutta], ‘in this very life’ (diṭṭheva dhamme) means in this very existence. ‘They attain final Nibbāna’ (parinibbāyanti) means they attain final Nibbāna through the quenching of defilements. ‘Consciousness is dependent on that’ (tannissitaṃ viññāṇaṃ hoti) means kamma-consciousness dependent on craving arises. ‘With that as fuel’ (tadupādānaṃ) means that grasping; the meaning is that consciousness arises accompanied by the grasping of craving. The sixth is clear.

7. Sāriputtasaddhivihārikasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Discourse on Sāriputta's Co-resident Monk

120. Sattame santānessatīti ghaṭessati, yogavicchedamassa pāpuṇituṃ na dassati.

120. In the seventh [sutta], ‘he will continue’ (santānessati) means he will connect; he will not allow its connection to be broken.

8. Rāhulovādasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Discourse on the Advice to Rāhula

121. Aṭṭhame vimuttiparipācaniyāti vimuttiṃ paripācentīti vimuttiparipācaniyā. Dhammāti pannarasa dhammā, te saddhindriyādīnaṃ visuddhikaraṇavasena veditabbā. Vuttañhetaṃ –

121. In the eighth [sutta], ‘ripening for liberation’ (vimuttiparipācaniyā) means they cause liberation to ripen; therefore, they are called ‘ripening for liberation’. ‘Dhammas’ (dhammā) refers to fifteen dhammas; they should be understood by way of being the cause for the purification of the faith faculty and so on. For it was said:

“Assaddhe puggale parivajjayato, saddhe puggale sevato bhajato payirupāsato, pasādanīye suttante paccavekkhato, imehi tīhākārehi saddhindriyaṃ visujjhati. Kusīte puggale parivajjayato, āraddhavīriye puggale sevato bhajato payirupāsato, sammappadhāne paccavekkhato, imehi tīhākārehi vīriyindriyaṃ visujjhati. Muṭṭhassatī puggale parivajjayato, upaṭṭhitassatī puggale sevato bhajato payirupāsato[P 2.0392], satipaṭṭhāne paccavekkhato, imehi tīhākārehi satindriyaṃ visujjhati. Asamāhite puggale parivajjayato, samāhite puggale sevato bhajato payirupāsato, jhānavimokkhe paccavekkhato, imehi tīhākārehi samādhindriyaṃ visujjhati. Duppaññe puggale parivajjayato, paññavante puggale sevato bhajato payirupāsato, gambhīrañāṇacariyaṃ paccavekkhato, imehi tīhākārehi paññindriyaṃ visujjhati. Iti ime pañca puggale parivajjayato, pañca puggale sevato bhajato payirupāsato[M 3.0037], pañca suttante paccavekkhato, imehi pannarasahi ākārehi imāni pañcindriyāni visujjhantī”ti (paṭi. ma. 1.184).

“By avoiding faithless individuals, associating with, attending on, and waiting upon faithful individuals, and reflecting on inspiring discourses, the faculty of faith is purified in these three ways. By avoiding lazy individuals, associating with, attending on, and waiting upon energetic individuals, and reflecting on the right efforts, the faculty of energy is purified in these three ways. By avoiding those who are forgetful, associating with, attending on, and waiting upon those who are mindful, and reflecting on the foundations of mindfulness, the faculty of mindfulness is purified in these three ways. By avoiding those who are unconcentrated, associating with, attending on, and waiting upon those who are concentrated, and reflecting on the jhānas and liberations, the faculty of concentration is purified in these three ways. By avoiding those who are unwise, associating with, attending on, and waiting upon those who are wise, and reflecting on the domain of profound knowledge, the faculty of wisdom is purified in these three ways. Thus, by avoiding these five kinds of individuals, associating with, attending on, and waiting upon these five kinds of individuals, and reflecting on these five kinds of discourses, these five faculties are purified through these fifteen aspects.” (Paṭis. Ma. 1.184)

Aparepi pannarasa dhammā vimuttiparipācaniyā – saddhāpañcamāni indriyāni, aniccasaññā, anicce dukkhasaññā, dukkhe anattasaññā, pahānasaññā, virāgasaññāti imā [V 3.0037] pañca nibbedhabhāgiyā saññā, meghiyattherassa kathitā kalyāṇamittatādayo pañca dhammāti (udā. 31). Kāya pana velāya bhagavato etadahosīti? Paccūsasamaye lokaṃ volokentassa.

Furthermore, there are fifteen other dhammas ripening for liberation: the five faculties with faith as the fifth; the perception of impermanence, the perception of suffering in the impermanent, the perception of not-self in suffering, the perception of abandoning, and the perception of dispassion—these five perceptions are conducive to penetration; and the five dhammas beginning with good friendship taught to the Elder Meghiya (Udāna 31). But at what time did this occur to the Blessed One? While surveying the world at dawn.

Anekāni devatāsahassānīti āyasmatā rāhulena padumuttarassa bhagavato pādamūle pālitanāgarājakāle patthanaṃ paṭṭhapentena saddhiṃ patthanaṃ paṭṭhapitadevatāsu pana kāci bhūmaṭṭhakā devatā, kāci antalikkhaṭṭhakā, kāci cātumahārājikā, kāci devaloke, kāci brahmaloke nibbattā. Imasmiṃ pana divase sabbāpi tā ekaṭṭhāne andhavanasmiṃyeva sannipatitā, tā sandhāyāha – “anekāni devatāsahassānī”ti. Dhammacakkhunti imasmiṃ sutte cattāro ca maggā cattāri ca phalāni dhammacakkhunti veditabbāni. Tattha hi kāci devatā sotāpannā ahesuṃ, kāci sakadāgāmī, anāgāmī, khīṇāsavā. Tāsañca pana devatānaṃ ettakāti gaṇanavasena paricchedo natthi. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānameva.

‘Many thousands of devas’: Among the devas who made an aspiration together with the Venerable Rāhula when he made his aspiration at the feet of the Blessed One Padumuttara at the time he was the Nāga king Pālita, some were reborn as earth-bound devas, some as devas dwelling in the sky, some as Cātumahārājika devas, some in a deva world, and some in a Brahmā world. But on this day, all of them gathered in one place, in the Andhavana itself. Referring to them, he said, ‘many thousands of devas.’ ‘The eye of the Dhamma’ in this discourse should be understood as the four paths and the four fruits. For there, some devas became stream-enterers, some once-returners, non-returners, or arahants. And of those devas, there is no limit by way of counting. The rest is clear everywhere.

9-10. Saṃyojaniyadhammasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Discourses Concerning Things that Fetter, and so forth.

122-123. Navamadasamāni iṭṭhārammaṇavasena kathiyamāne bujjhanakānaṃ vasena vuttānīti.

The ninth and tenth discourses were spoken by way of agreeable objects, for the sake of those who were to understand.

Lokakāmaguṇavaggo dvādasamo.

The Twelfth Chapter on the Strands of Worldly Sensual Pleasure.

13. Gahapativaggo

13. The Chapter on Householders

1-3. Vesālīsuttādivaṇṇanā

1-3. Commentary on the Vesālī Sutta and so forth.

124-126. Gahapativaggassa [P 2.0393] paṭhame uggoti paṇītadāyakānaṃ aggauggo, so bhagavatā “etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, mama sāvakānaṃ paṇītadāyakānaṃ yadidaṃ [M 3.0038] uggo gahapatī”ti evaṃ etadagge ṭhapito. Sesametesu ceva dvīsu, tatiye ca vuttatthameva.

In the first discourse of the Chapter on Householders, as for Ugga, he was the foremost among the givers of excellent things. He was placed in that foremost position by the Blessed One, who said: “Monks, among my disciples who are givers of excellent things, the householder Ugga is the foremost.” In these two discourses and in the third, the remainder has the meaning already stated.

4-5. Bhāradvājasuttādivaṇṇanā

4-5. Commentary on the Bhāradvāja Sutta and so forth.

127-128. Catutthe [V 3.0038] piṇḍaṃ ulamāno pariyesamāno pabbajitoti piṇḍolo. So kira parijiṇṇabhogo brāhmaṇo ahosi. Atha bhikkhusaṅghassa lābhasakkāraṃ disvā piṇḍatthāya nikkhamitvā pabbajito. So mahantaṃ kapallapattaṃ gahetvā carati, tena kapallapūraṃ yāguṃ pivati, kapallapūre pūve khādati, kapallapūraṃ bhattaṃ bhuñjati. Athassa mahagghasabhāvaṃ satthu ārocayiṃsu. Satthā tassa pattatthavikaṃ nānujāni. Heṭṭhāmañce pattaṃ nikkujjitvā ṭhapeti. So ṭhapentopi ghaṃsantova paṇāmetvā ṭhapeti, gaṇhantopi ghaṃsantova ākaḍḍhitvā gaṇhāti. Taṃ gacchante kāle dhaṃsanena parikkhīṇaṃ nāḷikodanamattameva gaṇhanakaṃ jātaṃ. Tato satthu ārocesuṃ, athassa satthā pattatthavikaṃ anujāni. Thero aparena samayena indriyabhāvanaṃ bhāvetvā aggaphale arahatte patiṭṭhāsi. Iti so piṇḍatthāya pabbajitattā piṇḍolo, gottena pana bhāradvājoti ubhayaṃ ekato katvā piṇḍolabhāradvājoti vuccati.

In the fourth discourse, he was called Piṇḍola because he went forth seeking alms. It is said that he was a brahmin whose possessions were exhausted. Then, having seen the gain and honor of the community of monks, he went forth from home for the sake of alms. He would wander about carrying a large bowl; with it, he would drink a bowlful of gruel, eat a bowlful of cakes, and eat a bowlful of rice. Then they informed the Teacher of his gluttonous nature. The Teacher did not allow him a bowl-bag. He would place the bowl upside down under a low couch. Even when placing it, he would set it down by scraping it, and when taking it, he would take it by dragging it. As time went on, it became worn out from scraping and could only hold about a measure of cooked rice. Then they informed the Teacher, and the Teacher allowed him a bowl-bag. At a later time, the elder, having developed the cultivation of the faculties, became established in the highest fruit, Arahantship. Thus, because he had gone forth for the sake of alms, he was called Piṇḍola, and by clan, he was Bhāradvāja. Combining both, he is called Piṇḍolabhāradvāja.

Upasaṅkamīti uggatuggatehi mahāamaccehi parivuto upasaṅkami. Thero kira ekadivasaṃ sāvatthiyaṃ piṇḍāya caritvā katabhattakicco nidāghasamaye sītaṭhāne divāvihāraṃ nisīdissāmīti ākāsena gantvā gaṅgātīre udenassa rañño udapānaṃ nāma uyyānaṃ atthi, tattha pavisitvā aññatarasmiṃ rukkhamūle divāvihāraṃ nisīdi sītena udakavātena [P 2.0394] bījiyamāno.

“He approached” means he approached surrounded by very distinguished great ministers. It is said that one day, the elder, having wandered for alms in Sāvatthī and finished his meal, thinking, “In the hot season, I will sit for the day’s abiding in a cool place,” went through the sky. On the bank of the Ganges, there is a park belonging to King Udena named Udapāna. Having entered there, he sat for the day’s abiding at the foot of a certain tree, being fanned by a cool, water-bearing breeze.

Udenopi kho nāma rājā sattāhaṃ mahāpānaṃ pivitvā sattame divase uyyānaṃ paṭijaggāpetvā mahājanaparivāro uyyānaṃ gantvā maṅgalasilāpaṭṭe atthatāya seyyāya nipajji. Tassa ekā paricārikā pāde sambāhamānā nisinnā. Rājā kamena niddaṃ okkami. Tasmiṃ niddaṃ okkante nāṭakitthiyo “yassatthāya mayaṃ gītādīni payojeyyāma, so niddaṃ upagato, na ca niddākāle [M 3.0039] mahāsaddaṃ kātuṃ vaṭṭatī”ti attano attano tūriyāni ṭhapetvā uyyānaṃ pakkantā. Tā tattha tattha phalāphalāni khādamānā pupphāni piḷandhamānā vicarantiyo theraṃ disvā “mā saddaṃ karitthā”ti aññamaññaṃ nivārayamānā vanditvā nisīdiṃsu. Thero “issā pahātabbā, maccheraṃ vinodetabba”ntiādinā nayena tāsaṃ anurūpaṃ dhammakathaṃ kathesi.

King Udena, having drunk liquor at a great festival for seven days, on the seventh day had the park prepared and, surrounded by a great retinue, went to the park and lay down on a bed spread on the auspicious stone slab. One of his female attendants sat massaging his feet. The king gradually fell asleep. When he had fallen asleep, the female dancers, thinking, “He for whose sake we would perform songs and so on has fallen asleep, and it is not proper to make a loud noise while he is sleeping,” set aside their respective musical instruments and went off into the park. As they were wandering about here and there, eating various fruits and adorning themselves with flowers, they saw the elder. Restraining one another, saying, “Do not make a noise,” they paid homage and sat down. The elder gave them a suitable Dhamma talk in the manner of, “Envy should be abandoned, stinginess should be dispelled,” and so on.

Sāpi [V 3.0039] kho rañño pāde sambāhamānā nisinnā itthī pāde cāletvā rājānaṃ pabodhesi. So “kahaṃ tā gatā”ti pucchi. Kiṃ tāsaṃ tumhehi? Tā ekaṃ samaṇaṃ parivāretvā nisinnāti. Rājā kuddho uddhane pakkhittaloṇaṃ viya taṭataṭāyamāno uṭṭhahitvā “tambakipillikāhi naṃ khādāpessāmī”ti gacchanto ekasmiṃ asokarukkhe tambakipillikānaṃ puṭaṃ disvā hatthenākaḍḍhitvā sākhaṃ gaṇhituṃ nāsakkhi. Kipillikapuṭo chijjitvā rañño sīse pati, sakalasarīraṃ sālithusehi parikiṇṇaṃ viya daṇḍadīpikāhi ḍayhamānaṃ viya ca ahosi. Thero rañño paduṭṭhabhāvaṃ ñatvā iddhiyā ākāsaṃ pakkhandi. Tāpi itthiyo uṭṭhāya rañño santikaṃ gantvā sarīraṃ puñchantiyo viya bhūmiyaṃ patitapatitā kipillikāyo gahetvā sarīre khipamānā sabbā mukhasattīhi vijjhiṃsu – “kiṃ nāmetaṃ, aññe rājāno pabbajite disvā vandanti, pañhaṃ pucchanti, ayaṃ pana rājā kipillikapuṭaṃ sīse bhinditukāmo jāto”ti.

That woman who was sitting massaging the king’s feet moved his feet and woke the king. He asked, “Where have they gone?” She spoke slanderously, saying, “What concern are they to you? They are sitting surrounding a certain ascetic.” The king, becoming angry, got up crackling like salt thrown into a furnace and, as he was going, said, “I will have him bitten by red ants.” Seeing a nest of red ants on an asoka tree, he pulled at it with his hand but was unable to get the branch. The ant nest broke and fell on the king’s head. His entire body became as if sprinkled with red paddy husks and as if being burned by torches. The elder, knowing the king’s malicious state, flew into the sky by his psychic power. Those women, too, got up and went to the king. As if wiping his body, they picked up the ants that had fallen on the ground and threw them on his body. They all pierced him with the spears of their mouths, saying: “What is this? Other kings, upon seeing ascetics, pay homage and ask questions. But this king has become desirous of breaking an ant nest on an ascetic’s head!”

Rājā attano aparādhaṃ disvā uyyānapālaṃ pakkosāpetvā pucchi – “kiṃ esa pabbajito? Aññesupi divasesu idha āgacchatī”ti? Āma, devāti. Idha tvaṃ āgatadivase mayhaṃ āroceyyāsīti. Theropi [P 2.0395] katipāheneva puna āgantvā rukkhamūle nisīdi. Uyyānapālo disvā – “mahanto me ayaṃ paṇṇākāro”ti vegena gantvā rañño ārocesi. Rājā uṭṭhahitvā saṅkhapaṇavādisaddaṃ nivāretvā uggatuggatehi amaccehi saddhiṃ uyyānaṃ agamāsi. Tena vuttaṃ “upasaṅkamī”ti.

The king, seeing his own fault, had the park keeper summoned and asked, “Does this ascetic come here on other days too?” “Yes, Your Majesty.” “On the day he comes here, you should inform me.” After a few days, the elder came again and sat at the foot of a tree. The park keeper, seeing him, thought, “This is a great gift for me,” and quickly went and informed the king. The king got up, stopped the sound of conches, drums, and so on, and went to the park with his most distinguished ministers. Therefore, it is said, “he approached.”

Anikīḷitāvino kāmesūti yā kāmesu kāmakīḷā, taṃ akīḷitapubbā, aparibhuttakāmāti attho. Addhānañca āpādentīti paveṇiṃ paṭipādenti, dīgharattaṃ anubandhāpenti. Mātumattīsūti mātupamāṇāsu. Lokasmiñhi mātā bhaginī dhītāti idaṃ tividhaṃ garukārammaṇaṃ nāma[M 3.0040], iti garukārammaṇe upanibandhaṃ cittaṃ vimocetuṃ na labhatīti dassento evamāha. Athassa tena pañhena cittaṃ anotarantaṃ disvā bhagavatā paṭikūlamanasikāravasena cittūpanibandhanatthaṃ vuttaṃ dvattiṃsākārakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathesi.

‘Not having sported in sensual pleasures’ means: they have not previously sported in the sport of sensual pleasures; they have not consumed sensual pleasures—this is the meaning. ‘And they cause the long course to continue’ means they establish a tradition and cause it to be continued for a long time. ‘Like a mother’ means those of the measure of a mother. For in the world, mother, sister, and daughter are the threefold objects of reverence; thus, showing that it is not possible to free a mind bound to a revered object, he spoke thus. Then, seeing his mind was not settling on account of that question, the Elder taught the meditation subject on the thirty-two parts of the body, spoken by the Blessed One for the purpose of binding the mind by means of attention to the repulsive.

Abhāvitakāyāti abhāvitapañcadvārikakāyā. Tesaṃ taṃ dukkaraṃ hotīti tesaṃ taṃ asubhakammaṭṭhānaṃ bhāvetuṃ dukkaraṃ hoti. Itissa imināpi cittaṃ anotarantaṃ disvā indriyasaṃvarasīlaṃ kathesi[V 3.0040]. Indriyasaṃvarasmiñhi upanibandhacittaṃ viheṭhetuṃ na labhati. Rājā taṃ sutvā tattha otiṇṇacitto acchariyaṃ, bho bhāradvājātiādimāha.

‘Undeveloped body’ means a body with undeveloped five sense-doors. ‘For them, that is difficult’ means it is difficult for them to develop that meditation subject of foulness. Thus, seeing his mind was not settling on account of this either, he taught the virtue of restraint of the sense-faculties. For a mind bound up in the restraint of the sense-faculties cannot be afflicted. Hearing this, the king, with a mind that descended into it, said: ‘Wonderful, Master Bhāradvāja!’ and so on.

Arakkhiteneva kāyenātiādīsu hatthapāde kīḷāpento gīvaṃ parivattento kāyaṃ na rakkhati nāma, nānappakāraṃ duṭṭhullaṃ kathento vacanaṃ na rakkhati nāma, kāmavitakkādayo vitakkento cittaṃ na rakkhati nāma. Rakkhiteneva kāyenātiādīsu vuttavipariyāyena attho veditabbo.

In passages such as ‘With an unguarded body’: one who plays with their hands and feet and turns their neck is said not to guard the body; one who speaks various kinds of coarse speech is said not to guard speech; one who thinks thoughts of sensual desire and so on is said not to guard the mind. In passages such as ‘With a guarded body’, the meaning should be understood in the opposite way to what has been stated.

Ativiya maṃ tasmiṃ samaye lobhadhammā parisahantīti maṃ tasmiṃ samaye atikkamitvā lobho adhibhavatīti attho. Upaṭṭhitāya satiyāti kāyagatāya satiyā supaṭṭhitāya. Na maṃ tathā tasmiṃ samayeti tasmiṃ samaye maṃ yathā pubbe, na tathā lobho atikkamitvā uppajjatīti attho. Parisahantīti padassa uppajjantītipi atthoyeva. Iti imasmiṃ sutte tayo kāyā kathitā. Kathaṃ[P 2.0396]? “Imameva kāya”nti ettha hi karajakāyo kathito, “bhāvitakāyo”ti ettha pañcadvārikakāyo, “rakkhiteneva kāyenā”ti ettha copanakāyo, kāyaviññattīti attho. Pañcamaṃ uttānameva.

‘States of greed greatly overwhelm me at that time’ means: at that time, greed, having transgressed, overpowers me. ‘With mindfulness established’ means: with mindfulness related to the body well established. ‘Not so for me at that time’ means: at that time, greed does not arise and, having transgressed, overpower me as it did before. The word ‘parisahanti’ (they overwhelm) also has the meaning ‘uppajjanti’ (they arise). Thus, in this discourse, three bodies are taught. How? In the phrase ‘this very body’, the physical body is taught. In ‘developed body’, the body of the five sense-doors is taught. In ‘with a guarded body’, the motivating body is taught, meaning bodily intimation. The fifth is self-evident.

6. Ghositasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Ghosita Sutta

129. Chaṭṭhe rūpā ca manāpāti rūpā ca manāpā saṃvijjanti. Cakkhuviññāṇañcāti cakkhuviññāṇañca saṃvijjati. Sukhavedaniyaṃ phassanti cakkhuviññāṇasampayuttaṃ upanissayavasena javanakāle sukhavedanāya paccayabhūtaṃ phassaṃ. Sukhā vedanāti ekaṃ phassaṃ paṭicca javanavasena sukhavedanā uppajjati. Sesapadesupi eseva nayo.

6.
