In the sixth sutta, ‘pleasing forms’ means: pleasing forms exist. ‘And eye-consciousness’ means: eye-consciousness exists. ‘Contact productive of pleasant feeling’ means: contact associated with eye-consciousness, which, by way of decisive support at the time of the javanas, is a condition for pleasant feeling. ‘Pleasant feeling’ means: pleasant feeling arises by way of the javanas, dependent on one contact. This same method should be understood for the remaining phrases.

Iti [M 3.0041] imasmiṃ sutte tevīsati dhātuyo kathitā. Kathaṃ? Ettha hi cakkhupasādo cakkhudhātu, tassa ārammaṇaṃ rūpadhātu, cakkhuviññāṇaṃ viññāṇadhātu, cakkhuviññāṇadhātuyā sahajātā tayo khandhā dhammadhātu, evaṃ pañcasu dvāresu catunnaṃ catunnaṃ vasena vīsati. Manodvāre “manodhātū”ti āvajjanacittaṃ gahitaṃ, ārammaṇañceva hadayavatthu ca dhammadhātu, vatthunissitaṃ manoviññāṇadhātūti [V 3.0041] evaṃ tevīsati honti. Evaṃ tevīsatiyā dhātūnaṃ vasena dhātunānattaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatāti dasseti.

129. Thus, in this sutta, twenty-three elements are taught. How? Herein, eye-sensitivity is the eye-element; its object is the form-element; eye-consciousness is the consciousness-element; the three aggregates co-arisen with the eye-consciousness-element are the dhamma-element. In this way, in the five sense-doors, by way of four each, there are twenty elements. In the mind-door, the adverting consciousness is taken as the ‘mind-element’; both its object and the heart-base are the dhamma-element; the consciousness dependent on the base is the mind-consciousness-element. Thus there are twenty-three. In this way, he shows that the diversity of elements was taught by the Blessed One by means of these twenty-three elements.

7-8. Hāliddikānisuttādivaṇṇanā

7-8. Commentary on the Hāliddikāni Sutta, etc.

130-131. Sattame manāpaṃ itthetanti pajānātīti yaṃ anena manāpaṃ rūpaṃ diṭṭhaṃ, taṃ itthetanti evametaṃ manāpameva tanti pajānāti. Cakkhuviññāṇaṃ sukhavedaniyañca phassaṃ paṭiccāti cakkhuviññāṇañceva, yo ca upanissayakoṭiyā vā anantarakoṭiyā vā samanantarakoṭiyā vā sampayuttakoṭiyā vā sukhavedanāya paccayo phasso, taṃ sukhavedaniyaṃ phassañca paṭicca uppajjati sukhavedanāti. Esa nayo sabbattha. Iti imesu dvīsu suttesu kiriyāmanoviññāṇadhātu āvajjanakiccā, manodhātuyeva vā samānā manodhātunāmena vuttāti veditabbā. Aṭṭhamaṃ [P 2.0397] uttānameva.

In the seventh discourse, ‘he understands it as pleasant, as desirable’ means: whatever pleasant form is seen by him, he understands it as ‘desirable’—that is, he understands, ‘thus, this is indeed pleasant.’ ‘Dependent on eye-consciousness and contact productive of pleasant feeling’ means that pleasant feeling arises dependent on eye-consciousness itself, and on whatever contact is a condition for pleasant feeling—whether by way of decisive support, or contiguity, or immediate contiguity, or association—that is, on that contact productive of pleasant feeling. This is the method in all cases. Thus, in these two discourses, it should be understood that the functional mind-consciousness element, which has the function of adverting, is spoken of by the name ‘mind-element,’ being equivalent to the mind-element. The eighth discourse is self-explanatory.

9. Lohiccasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Lohicca Sutta

132. Navame makkarakateti evaṃnāmake nagare araññakuṭikāyanti araññe katāya pāṭiyekkāya kuṭiyaṃ, na vihārapaccantakuṭiyaṃ. Māṇavakāti yepi tattha mahallakā, te mahallakakālepi antevāsikatāya māṇavakātveva vuttā. Tenupasaṅkamiṃsūti pāto sippaṃ uggaṇhitvā sāyaṃ “ācariyassa kaṭṭhāni āharissāmā”ti araññaṃ pavisitvā vicarantā yena sā kuṭikā, tenupasaṅkamiṃsu. Parito parito kuṭikāyāti tassā kuṭikāya samantato samantato. Seleyyakānīti aññamaññassa piṭṭhiṃ gahetvā laṅghitvā ito cito caṅkamanakīḷanāni.

In the ninth discourse, ‘in the town named Makkarakata, in a wilderness hut’ means in a solitary hut built in the wilderness, not a hut on the outskirts of the monastery. ‘Young men’ means that even those who were elderly there were called ‘young men’ because they were resident pupils even in their old age. ‘They approached’ means that having learned their craft in the morning, in the evening they thought, ‘We will fetch firewood for the teacher,’ and while wandering in the forest, they approached that hut. ‘All around the hut’ means all around that hut. ‘Playful activities’ means games of holding one another's backs and jumping over, and games of pacing to and fro.

Muṇḍakātiādīsu [M 3.0042] muṇḍe muṇḍāti, samaṇe ca samaṇāti vattuṃ vaṭṭeyya, ime pana hīḷentā “muṇḍakā samaṇakā”ti āhaṃsu. Ibbhāti gahapatikā. Kaṇhāti kaṇhā, kāḷakāti attho. Bandhupādāpaccāti ettha bandhūti brahmā adhippeto. Tañhi brāhmaṇā pitāmahoti voharanti. Pādānaṃ apaccā pādāpaccā, brahmuno piṭṭhipādato jātāti adhippāyo. Tesaṃ kira ayaṃ laddhi “brāhmaṇā brahmuno mukhato nikkhantā, khattiyā urato, vessā [V 3.0042] nābhito, suddā jāṇuto, samaṇā piṭṭhipādato”ti. Bharatakānanti kuṭimbikānaṃ. Kuṭimbikā hi yasmā raṭṭhaṃ bharanti, tasmā bharatāti vuccanti. Ime pana paribhavaṃ katvā vadamānā “bharatakāna”nti āhaṃsu.

Regarding 'bald-pates', etc.: for a bald person it would be proper to say 'bald', and for an ascetic, 'ascetic'. But these youths, speaking with contempt, said 'bald-pates, petty ascetics'. By 'wealthy ones' (ibbhā) is meant householders. By 'dark ones' (kaṇhā) is meant those of dark birth; the meaning is black. Regarding 'offspring from the kinsman's feet' (bandhupādāpaccā): here, by 'kinsman' (bandhu), Brahmā is intended. For brahmins call him 'grandfather' (pitāmaha). 'Offspring of the feet' (pādāpaccā) means born from the top of Brahmā's foot; this is the intention. It seems this is their doctrine: 'Brahmins issued from Brahmā's mouth, khattiyas from his chest, vessas from his navel, suddas from his knees, and ascetics from the top of his foot.' By 'Bharatakas' (bharatakānaṃ) is meant 'of householders'. Because householders (kuṭimbikā) support (bharanti) the country, they are called 'Bharatas'. But these youths, speaking contemptuously, said 'Bharatakas'.

Vihārā nikkhamitvāti “rattiṭṭhakāparicchanne rajatapaṭṭasannibhasamavippakiṇṇavālike ramaṇīye pariveṇe kaṭṭhakalāpe bandhitvā khipamānā vālikaṃ āluḷetvā, hatthena hatthaṃ ādāya paṇṇakuṭiṃ pariyāyantā ‘ime imesaṃ bharatakānaṃ [P 2.0398] sakkatā, ime imesaṃ bharatakānaṃ sakkatā’ti punappunaṃ viravantā ativiya ime māṇavakā kīḷaṃ karonti, vihāre bhikkhūnaṃ atthibhāvampi na jānanti, dassessāmi nesaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ atthibhāva”nti cintetvā paṇṇakuṭito nikkhami.

Regarding 'having come out of the dwelling' (vihārā nikkhamitvā): he thought, 'In this delightful precinct, enclosed by red bricks, with sand evenly scattered like a silver sheet, these youths are playing excessively. They are bundling and throwing faggots of wood, stirring up the sand, holding hands and circling the leaf-hut, shouting again and again, "These fellows are honored by these base householders! These fellows are honored by these base householders!" They do not even know of the presence of monks in the monastery. I will show them that there are monks here!' Thinking thus, he came out of the leaf-hut.

Sīluttamā pubbatarā ahesunti guṇavantānaṃ guṇe kathite nigguṇānaṃ guṇābhāvo pākaṭova bhavissatīti porāṇakabrāhmaṇānaṃ guṇe kathento evamāha. Tattha sīluttamāti sīlajeṭṭhakā. Sīlañhi tesaṃ uttamaṃ, na jātigottaṃ. Ye purāṇaṃ sarantīti ye porāṇakaṃ brāhmaṇadhammaṃ saranti. Abhibhuyya kodhanti kodhaṃ abhibhavitvā tesaṃ dvārāni suguttāni surakkhitāni ahesuṃ. Dhamme ca jhāne ca ratāti dasavidhe kusalakammapathadhamme aṭṭhasamāpattijhānesu ca ratā.

Regarding 'Virtue was supreme, foremost in ancient times' (sīluttamā pubbatarā ahesuṃ): he spoke thus, thinking, 'When the virtues of the virtuous are spoken of, the lack of virtue in the unvirtuous will become evident,' and so he spoke of the qualities of the ancient brahmins. Here, 'virtue was supreme' (sīluttamā) means that virtue was their chief quality (sīlajeṭṭhakā). For them, virtue (sīla) was supreme, not birth or lineage. 'Those who remember the ancient ways' (ye purāṇaṃ saranti) means those who recall the noble way of the ancient brahmins (brāhmaṇadhammaṃ). 'Having overcome anger' (abhibhuyya kodhaṃ) means that having subdued anger, their sense-doors were well-guarded and well-protected. 'Delighting in Dhamma and jhāna' (dhamme ca jhāne ca ratā) means they delighted in the Dhamma of the ten wholesome courses of action and in the jhānas of the eight meditative attainments.

Evaṃ porāṇānaṃ guṇaṃ kathetvā athetarahi brāhmaṇānaṃ mānaṃ nimmaddento ime ca vokkamma japāmasetiādimāha. Tattha vokkammāti etehi guṇehi apakkamitvā. Japāmaseti mayaṃ japāma sajjhāyāmāti ettakeneva brāhmaṇamhāti maññamānā brāhmaṇā mayanti iminā gottena mattā hutvā visamaṃ caranti, visamāni kāyakammādīni karontīti attho. Puthuattadaṇḍāti [M 3.0043] puthu attā daṇḍā etehīti puthuattadaṇḍā, gahitanānāvidhadaṇḍāti attho. Sataṇhātaṇhesūti sataṇhanittaṇhesu. Aguttadvārassa bhavanti moghāti asaṃvutadvārassa sabbepi vatasamādānā moghā bhavantīti dīpeti. Yathā kinti? Supineva laddhaṃ purisassa vittanti yathā supine purisassa laddhaṃ maṇimuttādinānāvidhaṃ vittaṃ moghaṃ hoti, pabujjhitvā kiñci na passati, evaṃ moghā bhavantīti attho.

Having thus described the virtues of the ancients, then, to crush the conceit of the brahmins of the present, He said, beginning with 'ime ca vokkamma japāmase...'. Here, 'vokkamma' means having departed from these virtues. 'Japāmase' means 'we mutter, we recite,' thinking 'By this much alone, we are brahmins,' and so, intoxicated by this lineage, they behave unrighteously, meaning they perform unrighteous bodily actions and so on. 'Puthuattadaṇḍā' means 'those who have taken up various punishments'; the meaning is 'having taken up various kinds of punishments'. 'Sataṇhātaṇhesūti' means among those with craving and those without craving. 'Aguttadvārassa bhavanti moghā' shows that for one with unguarded sense doors, all undertakings of vows are in vain. How so? 'Like wealth gained in a dream:' Just as the various kinds of wealth like gems and pearls gained in a dream by a person are in vain, for upon waking, he sees nothing, so too are they in vain; this is the meaning.

Anāsakāti ekāhadvīhādivasena anāhārakā. Thaṇḍilasāyikā cāti haritakusasanthate bhūmibhāge sayanaṃ, pāto sinānañca [P 2.0399] tayo ca vedāti pātova udakaṃ pavisitvā nhānañceva tayo [V 3.0043] ca vedā. Kharājinaṃ jaṭā paṅkoti kharasamphassaṃ ajinacammañceva jaṭākalāpo ca paṅko ca, paṅko nāma dantamalaṃ. Mantā sīlabbataṃ tapoti mantā ca ajasīlagosīlasaṅkhātaṃ sīlaṃ ajavatagovatasaṅkhātaṃ vatañca. Ayaṃ idāni brāhmaṇānaṃ tapoti vadati. Kuhanā vaṅkadaṇḍā cāti paṭicchannakūpo viya paṭicchannadosaṃ kohaññañceva vaṅkadaṇḍo, ca udumbarapalāsabeḷuvarukkhānaṃ aññatarato gahitaṃ vaṅkadaṇḍañcāti attho. Udakācamanāni cāti udakena mukhaparimajjanāni. Vaṇṇā ete brāhmaṇānanti ete brāhmaṇānaṃ parikkhārabhaṇḍakavaṇṇāti dasseti. Kata kiñcikkhabhāvanāti katā kiñcikkhabhāvanā. Ayameva vā pāṭho, āmisakiñcikkhassa vaḍḍhanatthāya katanti attho.

'Anāsakā' means being without food for one, two, or more days. 'Thaṇḍilasāyikā' means lying on a plot of ground spread with green kusa grass. 'Pāto sinānañca tayo ca vedā' means entering the water early to bathe, and also the three Vedas. 'Kharājinaṃ jaṭā paṅko' means a rough-feeling deerskin, a topknot of matted hair, and filth; 'paṅko' is the name for tooth-dirt. 'Mantā sīlabbataṃ tapo' means chants, the practice known as the goat-practice and the cow-practice, and the vow known as the goat-vow and the cow-vow. He says, 'This is now the austerity of the brahmins.' 'Kuhanā vaṅkadaṇḍā ca' means deceit with concealed faults like a covered pit, and a crooked staff; the meaning is a crooked staff taken from one of the udumbara, palāsa, or beḷuva trees. 'Udakācamanāni ca' means rinsing the mouth with water. 'Vaṇṇā ete brāhmaṇānaṃ' shows that these are the characteristic marks of the brahmins' accoutrements. 'Kata kiñcikkhabhāvanā' means the cultivation of something trifling has been done. Or, this is the reading itself, meaning it is done for the purpose of increasing some trifling material gain.

Evaṃ etarahi brāhmaṇānaṃ mānaṃ nimmadditvā puna porāṇakabrāhmaṇānaṃ vaṇṇaṃ kathento cittañca susamāhitantiādimāha. Tattha susamāhitanti tesaṃ brāhmaṇānaṃ cittaṃ upacārappanāsamādhīhi susamāhitaṃ ahosīti dasseti. Akhilanti mudu athaddhaṃ. So maggo brahmapattiyāti so seṭṭhapattiyā maggo, tumhe pana kiṃ brāhmaṇā nāmāti dīpento evamāha.

Having thus shattered the pride of the present-day brahmins, and again speaking of the virtues of the ancient brahmins, He spoke the words beginning with 'cittañca susamāhitaṃ'. Therein, 'susamāhitaṃ' shows that the minds of those brahmins were well-concentrated by means of access concentration and absorption concentration. 'Akhilaṃ' means gentle and not harsh. 'So maggo brahmapattiyā' means that is the path to the highest attainment. Wishing to show, 'But as for you, what sort of brahmins are you?', He spoke thus.

Āgamaṃsu nu khvidhāti āgamaṃsu nu kho idha. Adhimuccatīti kilesavasena adhimutto giddho hoti. Byāpajjatīti byāpādavasena pūticittaṃ hoti. Parittacetasoti anupaṭṭhitasatitāya saṃkilesacittena parittacitto. Cetovimuttinti phalasamādhiṃ. Paññāvimuttinti phalapaññaṃ. Appamāṇacetasoti upaṭṭhitasatitāya nikkilesacittena appamāṇacitto.

The phrase 'Āgamaṃsu nu khvidha' is to be parsed as 'āgamaṃsu nu kho idha'. 'Adhimuccati' means that by way of defilements, one becomes intent and greedy. 'Byāpajjati' means that by way of ill will, the mind becomes corrupt. 'Parittacetaso' means having a limited mind due to unestablished mindfulness and a defiled mind. 'Cetovimuttiṃ' means the concentration of fruition. 'Paññāvimuttiṃ' means the wisdom of fruition. 'Appamāṇacetaso' means having an immeasurable mind due to established mindfulness and a mind free from defilements.

10. Verahaccānisuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Verahaccāni Sutta

133. Dasame [M 3.0044] kāmaṇḍāyanti evaṃnāmake nagare. Yaggheti codanatthe nipāto. Sesaṃ uttānamevāti.

10. In the tenth sutta, 'Kāmaṇḍā' is the name of a city. 'Yagghe' is a particle in the sense of urging. The rest is clear.

Gahapativaggo terasamo.

133. The Thirteenth Householder Chapter.

14. Devadahavaggo

The Devadaha Chapter

1. Devadahasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Devadaha Sutta

134. Devadahavaggassa [V 3.0044][P 2.0400] paṭhame devadahanti napuṃsakaliṅgena laddhanāmo nigamo. Manoramāti manaṃ ramayantā, manāpāti attho. Amanoramāti amanāpā.

14. In the first sutta of the Devadaha Chapter, 'Devadaha' is the name of a town, which is in the neuter gender. 'Manoramā' means delighting the mind; the meaning is pleasing. 'Amanoramā' means unpleasing.

2. Khaṇasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Khaṇa Sutta

135. Dutiye chaphassāyatanikā nāmāti visuṃ chaphassāyatanikā nāma nirayā natthi. Sabbesupi hi ekatiṃsamahānirayesu chadvāraphassāyatanapaññatti hotiyeva. Idaṃ pana avīcimahānirayaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Saggāti idhāpi tāvatiṃsapurameva adhippetaṃ. Kāmāvacaradevaloke pana ekasmimpi chaphassāyatanapaññattiyā abhāvo nāma natthi. Iminā kiṃ dīpeti? Niraye ekantadukkhasamappitabhāvena, devaloke ca ekantasukhasamappitattā ekantakhiḍḍārativasena uppannapamādena maggabrahmacariyavāsaṃ vasituṃ na sakkā. Manussaloko pana vokiṇṇasukhadukkho, idheva apāyopi saggopi paññāyati. Ayaṃ maggabrahmacariyassa kammabhūmi nāma, sā tumhehi laddhā. Tasmā ye vo ime mānussakā khandhā laddhā, te vo lābhā. Yañca vo idaṃ manussattaṃ laddhaṃ, paṭiladdho vo brahmacariyavāsassa khaṇo samayoti. Vuttampi hetaṃ porāṇehi –

135. In the second sutta, 'of the six sense-bases' means that there are no hells separately named 'of the six sense-bases.' For in all thirty-one great hells, cognition through contact at the six doors indeed occurs. This, however, was said with reference to the Avīci great hell. Here 'heaven' also refers specifically to the Tāvatiṃsa celestial city. In the desire-realm deva worlds, there is no such thing as an absence of cognition through contact at the six doors in any of them. What does this show? In hell, because of being completely given over to suffering, and in the deva world, because of being completely endowed with happiness and due to negligence arising from constant play and delight, it is not possible to live the noble life of the path. The human world, however, has mixed pleasure and suffering; here itself the plane of misery and heaven are also discerned. This is called the ground of action for the noble life of the path, which you have obtained. Therefore, these human aggregates that you have obtained are your great gain. And this human existence that you have obtained—the moment, the right occasion for living the noble life has been obtained by you. This was also said by the ancients:

“Ayaṃ kammabhūmi idha maggabhāvanā,

Ṭhānāni saṃvejaniyā bahū idha;

Saṃvegasaṃvejaniyesu vatthusu,

Saṃvegajātova payuñca yoniso”ti.

“This is the ground of action; here is the development of the path. Many are the grounds for spiritual urgency here. In matters that cause spiritual urgency, with a sense of urgency having arisen, apply yourself with wise attention.”

3. Paṭhamarūpārāmasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the First Rūpārāma Sutta

136. Tatiye [M 3.0045] rūpasammuditāti rūpe sammuditā pamoditā. Dukkhāti dukkhitā. Sukhoti [V 3.0045] nibbānasukhena sukhito. Kevalāti [P 2.0401] sakalā. Yāvatatthīti vuccatīti yattakā atthīti vuccati. Ete voti ettha vo-kāro nipātamattaṃ. Paccanīkamidaṃ hoti, sabbalokena passatanti yaṃ idaṃ passantānaṃ paṇḍitānaṃ dassanaṃ, taṃ sabbalokena paccanīkaṃ hoti viruddhaṃ. Loko hi pañcakkhandhe niccā sukhā attā subhāti maññati, paṇḍitā aniccā dukkhā anattā asubhāti. Sukhato āhūti sukhanti kathenti. Sukhato vidūti sukhanti jānanti. Sabbametaṃ nibbānameva sandhāya vuttaṃ.

136. In the third sutta, 'delighted in form' means delighted and joyful in form. 'Suffering' means afflicted with suffering. 'Happy' means made happy by the happiness of Nibbāna. 'Entire' means all. 'Whatever is said to exist' means 'whatever phenomena are said to exist.' In 'ete vo,' the particle 'vo' is merely an expletive. 'This is contrary to what the whole world sees' means that this vision of the wise who see is contrary to and opposed to the whole world. For the world considers the five aggregates as permanent, pleasant, self, and beautiful, while the wise say they are impermanent, suffering, non-self, and unattractive. 'They speak of it as happiness' means they call it happiness. 'They know it as happiness' means they know it as happiness. All this is said with reference only to Nibbāna.

Sammūḷhetthāti ettha nibbāne sammūḷhā. Aviddasūti bālā. Sabbepi hi channavutipāsaṇḍino “nibbānaṃ pāpuṇissāmā”ti saññino honti, te pana “nibbānaṃ nāma ida”ntipi na jānanti. Nivutānanti kilesanīvaraṇena nivutānaṃ pariyonaddhānaṃ. Andhakāro apassatanti apassantānaṃ andhakāro hoti. Kiṃ taṃ evaṃ hoti? Nibbānaṃ vā nibbānadassanaṃ vā apassantānañhi bālānaṃ nibbānampi nibbānadassanampi kāḷameghaavacchāditaṃ viya candamaṇḍalaṃ kaṭāhena paṭikujjitapatto viya ca niccakālaṃ tamo ceva andhakāro ca sampajjati.

'Deluded here' means deluded regarding Nibbāna. 'The unwise' means fools. Indeed, all ninety-six kinds of sectarians have the perception, 'We will attain Nibbāna,' but they do not even know, 'This is what is called Nibbāna.' 'Of the veiled' means of those who are veiled, enveloped by the hindrances which are defilements. 'Darkness for those who do not see' means for those not seeing, there is darkness. What is it that is so? For fools who do not see Nibbāna or the vision of Nibbāna, both Nibbāna and the vision of Nibbāna become perpetual darkness and gloom, like the disk of the moon covered by a dark cloud, or like a bowl overturned by a kiln-pot.

Satañca vivaṭaṃ hoti, āloko passatāmivāti satañca sappurisānaṃ paññādassanena passantānaṃ nibbānaṃ āloko viya vivaṭaṃ hoti. Santike na vijānanti, magā dhammassa akovidāti yaṃ attano sarīre kese vā lomādīsu vā aññatarakoṭṭhāsaṃ paricchinditvā anantarameva adhigantabbato attano vā khandhānaṃ nirodhamaggato santike nibbānaṃ. Taṃ evaṃ santike samānampi maggabhūtā janā maggāmaggadhammassa catusaccadhammassa vā akovidā na jānanti.

'But for the virtuous it is open, like light for those who see' means: for the virtuous, the good persons who see with the vision of wisdom, Nibbāna is open like light. 'Being unskilled in the Dhamma, they do not know what is near' means: Nibbāna is near, since it is to be attained immediately after having demarcated a certain part in one's own body, such as the hairs of the head or the hairs of the body, or because it is the path to the cessation of one's own aggregates. Even though it is thus near, people who are like wild deer, being unskilled in the Dhamma of the path and the non-path or the Dhamma of the Four Truths, do not know it.

Māradheyyānupannehīti tebhūmakavaṭṭaṃ mārassa nivāsaṭṭhānaṃ anupannehi. Ko [P 2.0402] nu aññatra ariyebhīti ṭhapetvā ariye ko nu añño nibbānapadaṃ jānituṃ arahati. Sammadaññāya parinibbantīti arahattapaññāya sammā jānitvā anantarameva anāsavā hutvā kilesaparinibbānena parinibbanti. Atha vā sammadaññāya anāsavā hutvā ante khandhaparinibbānena parinibbāyanti.

'By those who have not entered Māra's domain' means by those who have not entered the dwelling place of Māra, which is the round of existence in the three realms. 'Who else but the noble ones?' means: setting aside the noble ones, who else is worthy to know the state of Nibbāna? 'Having rightly understood, they are fully extinguished' means: having rightly known with the wisdom of Arahantship, immediately becoming free from taints, they are fully extinguished by the extinction of defilements. Or, having rightly understood and become free from taints, at the end they are fully extinguished by the extinction of the aggregates.

4-12. Dutiyarūpārāmasuttādivaṇṇanā

4-12. Commentary on the Suttas beginning with the Second Rūpārāma Sutta

137-145. Catutthaṃ [V 3.0046][M 3.0046] suddhikaṃ katvā desiyamāne bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ. Pañcamādīni tathā tathā bujjhantānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttāni. Attho pana tesaṃ pākaṭoyevāti.

137-145. The fourth discourse, being taught as prose only, was spoken according to the inclination of those who were to understand. The fifth and the following discourses were spoken according to the inclination of those who were to understand in those respective ways. But their meaning is indeed evident.

Devadahavaggo cuddasamo.

The Devadaha Chapter, the fourteenth.

15. Navapurāṇavaggo

15. The Chapter on the New and the Old

1. Kammanirodhasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on the Cessation of Kamma

146. Navapurāṇavaggassa paṭhame navapurāṇānīti navāni ca purāṇāni ca. Cakkhu, bhikkhave, purāṇakammanti na cakkhu purāṇaṃ, kammameva purāṇaṃ, kammato pana nibbattattā paccayanāmena evaṃ vuttaṃ. Abhisaṅkhatanti paccayehi abhisamāgantvā kataṃ. Abhisañcetayitanti cetanāya pakappitaṃ. Vedaniyaṃ daṭṭhabbanti vedanāya vatthūti passitabbaṃ. Nirodhā vimuttiṃ phusatīti imassa tividhassa kammassa nirodhena vimuttiṃ phusati. Ayaṃ vuccatīti ayaṃ tassā vimuttiyā ārammaṇabhūto nirodho kammanirodhoti vuccati. Iti imasmiṃ sutte pubbabhāgavipassanā kathitā.

146. In the first discourse of the Chapter on the New and the Old, 'the new and the old' means both new kamma and old kamma. 'The eye, bhikkhus, is old kamma'—not that the eye is old, but only the kamma is old; however, because it is produced from kamma, it is so called by the name of its condition. 'Formed' means made by conditions having come together. 'Intended' means determined by volition. 'To be felt' means it should be seen as a basis for feeling. 'Through cessation, one touches liberation' means through the cessation of this threefold kamma, one touches liberation. 'This is called' means this cessation, which has become the object of that liberation, is called the cessation of kamma. Thus, in this discourse, preliminary-part insight is taught.

2-5. Aniccanibbānasappāyasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-5. Commentary on the Suttas beginning with the Anicca Sutta and the Nibbānasappāya Sutta

147-150. Dutiye nibbānasappāyanti nibbānassa sappāyaṃ upakārapaṭipadaṃ. Tatiyādīsupi eseva nayo. Paṭipāṭiyā pana catūsupi etesu suttesu saha vipassanāya cattāro maggā kathitā.

147-150. In the second discourse, 'conducive to Nibbāna' means the practice that is suitable and beneficial for attaining Nibbāna. This same method should be understood for the third and following discourses. Furthermore, in these four discourses, in sequence, the four paths are taught together with insight.

6-7. Antevāsikasuttādivaṇṇanā

6-7. Commentary on the Suttas beginning with the Antevāsika Sutta

151-152. Chaṭṭhe [P 2.0403] anantevāsikanti anto vasanakakilesavirahitaṃ. Anācariyakanti ācaraṇakakilesavirahitaṃ[V 3.0047]. Antassa vasantīti anto assa vasanti. Te naṃ samudācarantīti te etaṃ adhibhavanti ajjhottharanti sikkhāpenti vā. “Evaṃ vejjakammaṃ karohi, evaṃ dūtakamma”nti iti sikkhāpanasaṅkhātena samudācaraṇatthenassa te ācariyā nāma honti, tehi [M 3.0047] ācariyehi sācariyakoti vuccati. Sesamettha vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ. Sattamaṃ heṭṭhā kathitanayameva.

In the sixth discourse, anantevāsika means free from the defilement that dwells within, which is like a bad pupil. Anācariyaka means free from the defilement that acts as a teacher, which is like a bad teacher. Antassa vasanti means: they dwell inside him. Te naṃ samudācaranti means: those defilements overpower and overwhelm him, or they instruct him, saying, ‘Do medical work like this, do messenger work like this.’ Thus, because of this ‘instructing,’ which is the meaning of samudācaraṇa, those defilements are called his teachers. Because of those teachers, he is called sācariyaka (one with such a teacher). The rest herein should be understood in the way already stated. The seventh discourse has the same method as was stated below.

8. Atthinukhopariyāyasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Atthinukhopariyāya Sutta.

153. Aṭṭhame yaṃ pariyāyaṃ āgammāti yaṃ kāraṇaṃ āgamma. Aññatreva saddhāyāti vinā saddhāya saddhaṃ apanetvā. Ettha ca saddhāti na paccakkhā saddhā. Yo pana parassa evaṃ kira evaṃ kirāti kathentassa sutvā uppanno saddahanākāro, taṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Ruciādīsupi rucāpetvā khamāpetvā atthetanti gahaṇākāro ruci nāma, evaṃ kira bhavissatīti anussavanaṃ anussavo, nisīditvā ekaṃ kāraṇaṃ cintentassa kāraṇaṃ upaṭṭhāti, evaṃ upaṭṭhitassa atthetanti gahaṇaṃ ākāraparivitakko nāma, kāraṇavitakkoti attho. Kāraṇaṃ cintentassa pāpikā laddhi uppajjati, taṃ atthesāti gahaṇākāro diṭṭhinijjhānakkhanti nāma. Aññaṃ byākareyyāti imāni pañca ṭhānāni muñcitvā arahattaṃ byākareyya. Imasmiṃ sutte sekhāsekhānaṃ paccavekkhaṇā kathitā.

153. In the eighth discourse, yaṃ pariyāyaṃ āgamma means: by means of which reason. Aññatreva saddhāya means: without faith, having removed faith. And herein, saddhā is not direct faith. Rather, this is said with reference to the manner of believing that arises upon hearing another say, ‘So it is said, so it is said.’ In the case of liking and so on: the manner of grasping—having been pleased and satisfied, thinking, ‘This exists’—is called ruci (liking). Hearing along, thinking, ‘Thus it will be, it is said,’ is anussavo (hearsay). For one who sits down and reflects on a reason, a reason presents itself; for one to whom it has thus presented itself, the grasping, thinking, ‘This exists,’ is called ākāraparivitakko (reasoned reflection); the meaning is reflection on a reason. For one who reflects on a reason, a wrong view arises; the manner of grasping it, thinking, ‘This view is mine,’ is called diṭṭhinijjhānakkhanti (approval through contemplation of a view). Aññaṃ byākareyya means: one would declare arahantship, having abandoned these five grounds. In this discourse, the reviewing of trainees and non-trainees is discussed.

9-10. Indriyasampannasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Indriyasampanna Sutta and the Like

154-155. Navame indriyasampannoti paripuṇṇindriyo. Tattha yena cha indriyāni sammasitvā arahattaṃ pattaṃ, so tehi nibbisevanehi indriyehi samannāgatattā, cakkhādīni vā cha indriyāni [P 2.0404] sammasantassa uppannehi saddhādīhi indriyehi samannāgatattā paripuṇṇindriyo nāma hoti, taṃ sandhāya bhagavā cakkhundriye cetiādinā nayena desanaṃ vitthāretvā ettāvatā kho bhikkhu indriyasampanno hotīti āha. Dasamaṃ heṭṭhā vuttanayamevāti.

In the ninth discourse, indriyasampanno means one with complete faculties (paripuṇṇindriyo). Herein, one who has attained arahantship by contemplating the six faculties is called ‘one with complete faculties’ because of being endowed with those faculties that are free from indulgence. Alternatively, one is called ‘one with complete faculties’ because of being endowed with the faculties of faith and so on that arise for one who contemplates the six faculties beginning with the eye. With reference to this, the Blessed One, having elaborated the teaching in the manner beginning with ‘in the eye faculty…,’ said: ‘It is to this extent, bhikkhu, that one is accomplished in the faculties.’ The tenth discourse is in accordance with the method previously stated.

Navapurāṇavaggo pañcadasamo.

The Fifteenth Chapter on the New and Old.

Tatiyo paṇṇāsako.

The Third Fifty.

16. Nandikkhayavaggo

16. The Chapter on the Destruction of Delight

1-4. Ajjhattanandikkhayasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-4. Commentary on the Ajjhattanandikkhaya Sutta and Related Discourses

156-159. Nandikkhayavaggassa [V 3.0048][M 3.0048][P 3.0001] paṭhame nandikkhayā rāgakkhayo, rāgakkhayā nandikkhayoti nandiyā ca rāgassa ca atthato ekattā vuttaṃ. Suvimuttanti arahattaphalavimuttivasena suṭṭhu vimuttaṃ. Sesamettha dutiyādīsu ca uttānameva.

In the first discourse of the Nandikkhaya Vagga, ‘the destruction of delight is the destruction of lust, and the destruction of lust is the destruction of delight’ is stated because delight and lust are one in meaning. Suvimuttaṃ means well-liberated by way of the liberation that is the fruit of arahantship. The remainder here and in the second discourse and so forth is self-evident.

5-6. Jīvakambavanasamādhisuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the Jīvakambavanasamādhi Sutta and Related Discourses.

160-161. Pañcamaṃ samādhivikalānaṃ, chaṭṭhaṃ paṭisallānavikalānaṃ cittekaggatañca kāyavivekañca labhantānaṃ etesaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ phātiṃ gamissatīti ñatvā kathitaṃ. Tattha okkhāyatīti (paccakkhāyati) paññāyati pākaṭaṃ hoti. Iti dvīsupi etesu saha vipassanāya cattāro maggā kathitā.

The fifth discourse was taught for those deficient in concentration, and the sixth for those deficient in seclusion, it being known that their meditation subject will flourish when they obtain both one-pointedness of mind and bodily seclusion. Therein, okkhāyati means it appears directly (paccakkhāyati), it appears, it becomes manifest. Thus, in both these discourses, the four paths together with insight were taught.

7-9. Koṭṭhikaaniccasuttādivaṇṇanā

7-9. Commentary on the Koṭṭhikaanicca Sutta and Related Discourses

162-164. Sattamādīsu [P 3.0002] tīsu therassa vimuttiparipācaniyā dhammāva kathitā.

In the three discourses beginning with the seventh, the teachings conducive to the ripening of the elder's liberation were taught.

10-12. Micchādiṭṭhipahānasuttādivaṇṇanā

10-12. Commentary on the Micchādiṭṭhipahāna Sutta and Related Discourses

165-167. Dasamādīni tīṇi pāṭiyekkena puggalajjhāsayavasena vuttāni. Tesaṃ attho vuttanayeneva veditabboti.

The three discourses beginning with the tenth were taught individually according to the disposition of the individual. Their meaning should be understood in the manner already stated.

Nandikkhayavaggo solasamo.

The Chapter on the Destruction of Delight is the sixteenth.

17. Saṭṭhipeyyālavaggo

17. The Chapter of the Sixty Repetitions

1-60. Ajjhattaaniccachandasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-60. Commentary on the Ajjhattaaniccachanda Sutta and Related Discourses

168-227. Tadanantaro [V 3.0049] saṭṭhipeyyālo nāma hoti, so uttānatthova. Yāni panettha saṭṭhi suttāni vuttāni, tāni “chando pahātabbo”ti [M 3.0049] evaṃ tassa tasseva padassa vasena bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayavasena vuttāni. Iti sabbāni tāni pāṭiyekkena puggalajjhāsayavasena kathitāni. Ekekasuttapariyosāne cettha saṭṭhi saṭṭhi bhikkhū arahattaṃ pattāti.

Immediately following that is what is called the Sixty Repetitions, which is of plain meaning. The sixty discourses taught herein were taught according to the disposition of those who would understand by way of that very phrase, ‘desire is to be abandoned.’ Thus, all of them were taught individually according to the disposition of the individual. And at the conclusion of each and every discourse here, sixty by sixty bhikkhus attained Arahantship.

Saṭṭhipeyyālavaggo.

The Chapter of the Sixty Repetitions.

18. Samuddavaggo

18. The Chapter on the Ocean

1. Paṭhamasamuddasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the First Sutta on the Ocean

228. Samuddavaggassa paṭhame cakkhu, bhikkhave, purisassa samuddoti yadi duppūraṇaṭṭhena yadi vā samuddanaṭṭhena samuddo, cakkhumeva samuddo. Tassa hi pathavito yāva akaniṭṭhabrahmalokā nīlādiārammaṇaṃ samosarantaṃ paripuṇṇabhāvaṃ kātuṃ na sakkoti, evaṃ duppūraṇaṭṭhenapi samuddo. Cakkhu ca tesu tesu nīlādīsu ārammaṇesu samuddati, asaṃvutaṃ hutvā osaramānaṃ kilesuppattiyā kāraṇabhāvena sadosagamanena gacchatīti samuddanaṭṭhenapi samuddo. Tassa rūpamayo vegoti samuddassa appamāṇo ūmimayo vego viya tassāpi cakkhusamuddassa samosarantassa nīlādibhedassa ārammaṇassa vasena appameyyo rūpamayo vego veditabbo. Yo taṃ rūpamayaṃ vegaṃ sahatīti yo taṃ cakkhusamudde samosaṭaṃ rūpamayaṃ vegaṃ, manāpe rūpe rāgaṃ, amanāpe dosaṃ, asamapekkhite mohanti evaṃ rāgādikilese [P 3.0003] anuppādento upekkhakabhāvena sahati.

228. In the first discourse of the Samudda Vagga, regarding ‘The eye, bhikkhus, is a person’s ocean’: whether ‘ocean’ is used in the sense of being difficult to fill or in the sense of assailing, the eye itself is the ocean. For even when sense-objects such as blue, from the earth up to the Akaniṭṭha Brahma realm, enter its range, it cannot be brought to a state of fullness; thus, it is an ocean in the sense of being difficult to fill. And the eye assails those various sense-objects such as blue; being unrestrained and rushing forth, it goes with a corrupt movement due to being a cause for the arising of defilements. Thus, it is an ocean also in the sense of assailing. Regarding ‘Its surge is made of forms’: just as the ocean’s immeasurable surge is made of waves, so too for this eye-ocean, when sense-objects of various kinds such as blue enter its range, its immeasurable surge made of forms should be understood. Regarding ‘One who withstands that surge made of forms’: this means a bhikkhu who endures with equanimity that surge made of forms that has entered the eye-ocean, by not giving rise to defilements such as lust for a pleasant form, aversion for an unpleasant form, and delusion for a form that is met with indifference.

Saūmintiādīsu kilesaūmīhi saūmiṃ. Kilesāvaṭṭehi sāvaṭṭaṃ. Kilesagāhehi sagāhaṃ[V 3.0050]. Kilesarakkhasehi sarakkhasaṃ. Kodhūpāyāsassa ca vasena saūmiṃ. Vutañhetaṃ “ūmibhayanti kho, bhikkhave, kodhūpāyāsassetaṃ adhivacana”nti (itivu. 109; ma. ni. 2.162; a. ni. 4.122). Kāmaguṇavasena sāvaṭṭaṃ. Vutañhetaṃ “āvaṭṭaggāhoti kho, bhikkhave, pañcannetaṃ kāmaguṇānaṃ adhivacana”nti (saṃ. ni. 4.241). Mātugāmavasena sagāhaṃ sarakkhasaṃ. Vuttañhetaṃ “gāharakkhasoti kho, bhikkhave, mātugāmassetaṃ adhivacana”nti (itivu. 109). Sesavāresupi eseva [M 3.0050] nayo. Sabhayaṃ duttaraṃ accatarīti ūmibhayena sabhayaṃ duratikkamaṃ atikkami. Lokantagūti saṅkhāralokassa antaṃ gato. Pāragatoti vuccatīti nibbānaṃ gatoti kathīyati.

In the phrases beginning with `saūmiṃ` (with waves): `saūmiṃ` means 'with the waves of defilements'; `sāvaṭṭaṃ` (with whirlpools) means 'with the whirlpools of defilements'; `sagāhaṃ` (with sea-monsters) means 'with the sea-monsters of defilements'; `sarakkhasaṃ` (with demons) means 'with the demons of defilements'. And `saūmiṃ` is also understood by way of anger and despair. For this was stated: '"Fear of waves," monks, is a term for anger and despair.' `Sāvaṭṭaṃ` is understood by way of sensual pleasures. For this was stated: '"Whirlpool and snare," monks, is a term for the five strands of sensual pleasure.' `Sagāhaṃ` and `sarakkhasaṃ` are understood by way of womenfolk. For this was stated: '"Sea-monster and demon," monks, is a term for womenfolk.' This same method applies in the remaining passages. Regarding 'He crossed over that which is fearful and hard to cross': he crossed over that which is fearful and difficult to traverse due to the fear of waves and so on. Regarding 'gone to the world's end': he has gone to the end of the world of formations. Regarding 'is called one who has gone to the far shore': it is said that he has gone to Nibbāna.

2-3. Dutiyasamuddasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-3. Commentary on the Discourses Beginning with the Second One on the Ocean

229-230. Dutiye samuddoti samuddanaṭṭhena samuddo, kiledanaṭṭhena temanaṭṭhenāti vuttaṃ hoti. Yebhuyyenāti ṭhapetvā ariyasāvake. Samunnāti kilinnā tintā nimuggā. Tantākulakajātātiādi heṭṭhā vitthāritameva. Maccujahoti tayo maccū jahitvā ṭhito. Nirupadhīti tīhi upadhīhi anupadhi. Apunabbhavāyāti nibbānatthāya. Amohayī maccurājanti yathā tassa gatiṃ na jānāti, evaṃ maccurājānaṃ mohetvā gato. Tatiyaṃ vuttanayameva.

In the second discourse: `samudda` (ocean) is so called in the sense of accumulating (samuddana), and it is said to be in the sense of moistening (kiledana) and wetting (temana). 'For the most part' (yebhuyyena) means excluding the noble disciples. 'Moistened' (samunna) means soiled, wet, and submerged. The passage beginning 'Tantākulakajātā' has already been explained in detail below. 'One who has abandoned death' (maccujaho) means one who stands having abandoned the three kinds of death. 'Without acquisitions' (nirupadhi) means without the three acquisitions. 'For no future existence' (apunabbhavāya) means for the sake of Nibbāna. 'He bewildered the King of Death' (amohayī maccurājaṃ) means he departed having bewildered the King of Death so that he would not know his destination. The third discourse is in the same way as has been stated.

4-6. Khīrarukkhopamasuttādivaṇṇanā

4-6. Commentary on the Discourses Beginning with the Simile of the Milk-Tree

231-233. Catutthe appahīnaṭṭhena atthi, tenevāha so appahīnoti. Parittāti, pabbatamattampi rūpaṃ aniṭṭhaṃ arajanīyaṃ parittaṃ nāma hoti, evarūpāpissa rūpā cittaṃ pariyādiyantīti dasseti. Ko pana vādo adhimattānanti [P 3.0004] iṭṭhārammaṇaṃ panassa rajanīyaṃ vatthu cittaṃ pariyādiyatīti ettha kā kathā? Ettha ca nakhapiṭṭhipamāṇampi maṇimuttādi rajanīyaṃ vatthu adhimattārammaṇamevāti veditabbaṃ. Daharotiādīni tīṇipi aññamaññavevacanāneva. Ābhindeyyāti pahareyya padāleyya vā. Pañcame tadubhayanti taṃ ubhayaṃ. Chaṭṭhaṃ uttānameva.

In the fourth discourse: 'There is' (atthi) in the sense of not having been abandoned; therefore, He said, 'it has not been abandoned by him' (so appahīno). Regarding 'trifling' (paritta): even a form the size of a mountain, if it is undesirable and unenticing, is called 'trifling'. It is shown that even such forms can overwhelm the mind of one whose defilements are not abandoned. What then is to be said of excessive things? What is there to say here that a desirable and enticing object overwhelms his mind? And here, even an object the size of a fingernail, like a gem or pearl, if it is enticing, should be understood as an excessively captivating object. The three terms beginning with 'daharo' are synonyms for one another. 'Ābhindeyyāti' means one should strike or break apart. In the fifth discourse, 'tadubhayanti' means those two. The sixth discourse is self-explanatory.

7. Udāyīsuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Udāyī Discourse

234. Sattame [V 3.0051] anekapariyāyenāti anekehi kāraṇehi. Itipāyanti itipi ayaṃ. Imasmiṃ sutte aniccena anattalakkhaṇaṃ kathitaṃ.

234. In the seventh discourse: 'In many ways' (anekapariyāyena) means for many reasons. 'Itipāyaṃ' is a contraction of 'iti pi ayaṃ' (for this reason also, this...). In this discourse, the characteristic of not-self is taught by means of impermanence.

8. Ādittapariyāyasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Fire Sermon

235. Aṭṭhame anubyañjanaso nimittaggāhoti “hatthā sobhanā pādā sobhanā”ti evaṃ anubyañjanavasena nimittaggāho. Nimittaggāhoti hi [M 3.0051] saṃsandetvā gahaṇaṃ, anubyañjanaggāhoti vibhattigahaṇaṃ. Nimittaggāho kumbhīlasadiso sabbameva gaṇhāti, anubyañjanaggāho rattapāsadiso vibhajitvā hatthapādādīsu taṃ taṃ koṭṭhāsaṃ. Ime pana dve gāhā ekajavanavārepi labbhanti, nānājavanavāre vattabbameva natthi.

235. In the eighth discourse: 'Grasping the sign by way of its details' (anubyañjanaso nimittaggāho) is grasping the sign by way of its details, as in, 'The hands are beautiful, the feet are beautiful.' 'Grasping the sign' (nimittaggāho) is grasping by combining, while 'grasping the details' (anubyañjanaggāho) is grasping by distinguishing. Grasping the sign is like a crocodile, which seizes everything at once. Grasping the details is like a leech, which, having distinguished them, takes this or that part among the hands, feet, and so on. These two kinds of grasping can be obtained even in a single javana-process; there is nothing to be said about them occurring in different javana-processes.

Nimittassādagathitanti nimittassādena ganthitaṃ baddhaṃ. Viññāṇanti kammaviññāṇaṃ. Tasmiṃ ce samaye kālaṃ kareyyāti na koci saṃkiliṭṭhena cittena kālaṃ karonto nāma atthi. Sabbasattānañhi bhavaṅgeneva kālakiriyā hoti. Kilesabhayaṃ pana dassento evamāha. Samayavasena vā evaṃ vuttaṃ. Cakkhudvārasmiñhi āpāthagate ārammaṇe rattacittaṃ vā duṭṭhacittaṃ vā mūḷhacittaṃ vā ārammaṇarasaṃ anubhavitvā bhavaṅgaṃ otarati, bhavaṅge ṭhatvā kālakiriyaṃ karoti. Tasmiṃ samaye kālaṃ [P 3.0005] karontassa dveva gatiyo pāṭikaṅkhā, imassa samayassa vasenetaṃ vuttaṃ.

'Bound by delight in the sign' (nimittassādagathitaṃ) means fettered and bound by delight in the sign. 'Consciousness' (viññāṇaṃ) means kamma-consciousness. Regarding the phrase, 'If one were to die at that time' (tasmiṃ ce samaye kālaṃ kareyya): there is no one who dies with a defiled mind. For the death of all beings occurs only with the life-continuum (bhavaṅga). However, He said this to show the danger of the defilements. Alternatively, it was said this way with regard to the occasion. For when an object comes into range at the eye-door, a mind with lust, hate, or delusion, having experienced the taste of the object, descends into the life-continuum. Having stood in the life-continuum, the act of dying occurs. For one dying at that time, only two destinations are to be expected. This was said with reference to this occasion.

Imaṃ khvāhaṃ, bhikkhave, ādīnavanti imaṃ anekāni vassasatasahassāni niraye anubhavitabbaṃ dukkhaṃ sampassamāno evaṃ vadāmi tattāya ayosalākāya akkhīni añjāpetukāmoti. Iminā nayena sabbattha attho veditabbo. Ayosaṅkunāti ayasūlena. Sampalimaṭṭhanti dvepi kaṇṇacchiddāni vinivijjhitvā pathaviyaṃ ākoṭanavasena sampalimaṭṭhaṃ.

Regarding 'Seeing this danger, monks, I...' (Imaṃ khvāhaṃ, bhikkhave, ādīnavaṃ): Seeing this suffering to be experienced in hell for many hundreds of thousands of years, I speak thus. It should be understood as, 'wishing to have the eyes seared by a heated iron rod.' The meaning should be understood in this way throughout. 'With an iron stake' (ayosaṅkunā) means with an iron spike. 'Crushed' (sampalimaṭṭhaṃ) means having pierced both ear-holes, it is crushed by means of being hammered into the ground.

Tatiyavāre sampalimaṭṭhanti nakhacchedanaṃ pavesetvā ukkhipitvā sahadhunaṭṭhena chinditvā pātanavasena sampalimaṭṭhaṃ. Catutthavāre sampalimaṭṭhanti bandhanamūlaṃ chetvā pātanavasena sampalimaṭṭhaṃ. Pañcamavāre [V 3.0052] sampalimaṭṭhanti tikhiṇāya sattiyā kāyapasādaṃ uppāṭetvā patanavasena sampalimaṭṭhaṃ. Sattiyāti ettha mahatī daṇḍakavāsi veditabbā. Sottanti nipajjitvā niddokkamanaṃ. Yathārūpānaṃ vitakkānaṃ vasaṃ gato saṅghaṃ bhindeyyāti iminā vitakkānaṃ yāva saṅghabhedā pāpakammāvahanatā dassitā. Sesamettha uttānameva.

In the third instance, 'crushed' (sampalimaṭṭhaṃ) means it is crushed by means of being thrown down, after inserting a nail-cutter, lifting it, and cutting it together with the septum of the nose. In the fourth instance, 'crushed' (sampalimaṭṭhaṃ) means it is crushed by means of being thrown down, after cutting the root of the tongue. In the fifth instance, 'crushed' (sampalimaṭṭhaṃ) means it is crushed by means of being thrown down, after flaying the body's sensitivity with a sharp spear. Here, 'sattiyā' should be understood as a large pole-axe. 'Sotthiṃ' means entering into sleep after lying down. By the passage, 'Having come under the power of such thoughts, one might split the Saṅgha,' the capacity of wrong thoughts to lead to evil deeds, up to the point of causing a schism in the Saṅgha, is shown. The rest here is self-explanatory.

9-10. Paṭhamahatthapādopamasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Discourses Beginning with the First Simile of the Hand and Foot.

236-237. Navame [M 3.0052] hatthesu, bhikkhave, satīti hatthesu vijjamānesu. Dasame na hotīti vuccamāne bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayavasena vuttaṃ. Dvīsupi cetesu vipākasukhadukkhameva dassetvā vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitanti.

In the ninth discourse, the phrase 'monks, when there are hands' means 'when hands exist'. In the tenth, when it is said 'it is not', this was spoken according to the disposition of those who are to understand. In both these discourses, having shown only the pleasure and pain that are results, the round of existence and that which is free from the round were taught.

Samuddavaggo niṭṭhito.

The Chapter on the Ocean is concluded.

19. Āsīvisavaggo

19. The Chapter on the Viper

1. Āsīvisopamasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on the Simile of the Viper

238. Āsīvisavaggassa paṭhame bhikkhū āmantesīti ekacārikadvicārikaticārikacatucārikapañcacārike sabhāgavuttino kārake yuttapayutte sabbepi dukkhalakkhaṇakammaṭṭhānike parivāretvā nisinne yogāvacare bhikkhū āmantesi. Idañhi [P 3.0006] suttaṃ puggalajjhāsayena vuttaṃ. Puggalesupi vipañcitaññūnaṃ disāvāsikānaṃ dukkhalakkhaṇakammaṭṭhānikānaṃ upaṭṭhānavelāya āgantvā satthāraṃ parivāretvā nisinnānaṃ vasena vuttaṃ. Evaṃ santepi ugghaṭitaññūādīnaṃ catunnampi puggalānaṃ paccayabhūtamevetaṃ. Ugghaṭitaññū puggalo hi imassa suttassa mātikānikkhepeneva arahattaṃ pāpuṇissati, vipañcitaññū mātikāya vitthārabhājanena, neyyapuggalo imameva suttaṃ sajjhāyanto paripucchanto yoniso manasikaronto kalyāṇamitte sevanto bhajanto payirupāsanto arahattaṃ pāpuṇissati. Padaparamassetaṃ suttaṃ anāgate vāsanā bhavissatīti evaṃ sabbesampi upakārabhāvaṃ ñatvā bhagavā sineruṃ ukkhipanto viya ākāsaṃ vitthārento viya cakkavāḷapabbataṃ [V 3.0053] kampento viya ca mahantena ussāhena seyyathāpi, bhikkhaveti imaṃ āsīvisopamasuttaṃ ārabhi.

238. In the first [sutta] of the Chapter on the Viper, [the phrase] 'he addressed the monks' [means]: he addressed the yogāvacara monks who were seated surrounding him—practitioners who were solitary, in pairs, in threes, in fours, or in fives, who had a similar practice, who were diligent and resolute, all of whom were meditators on the characteristic of suffering. For this discourse was spoken according to the disposition of individuals. And among individuals, it was spoken on account of those who understand through detailed explanation, who were residing in various regions, meditators on the characteristic of suffering, who had come at the time of attendance and were seated surrounding the Teacher. Even so, it is a supporting condition for all four types of individuals, beginning with those who understand quickly. Indeed, an individual who understands quickly will attain Arahantship merely by the laying down of the mātikā of this sutta; a person who is to be guided, by reciting this very sutta, questioning, reflecting wisely, attending, frequenting, and waiting upon good friends, will attain Arahantship. For the person for whom the words are the highest attainment, this sutta will become a latent tendency in the future. Thus, knowing it to be beneficial for all, the Blessed One, with great effort—as if lifting Mount Sineru, as if expanding the sky, as if shaking the Cakkavāḷa mountain range—began this Discourse on the Simile of the Viper with the words, 'Just as, monks...'

Tattha cattāro āsīvisāti kaṭṭhamukho, pūtimukho, aggimukho, satthamukhoti ime cattāro. Tesu kaṭṭhamukhena daṭṭhassa sakalasarīraṃ sukkhakaṭṭhaṃ viya thaddhaṃ hoti, sandhipabbesu adhimattaṃ ayasūlasamappitaṃ viya tiṭṭhati. Pūtimukhena daṭṭhassa pakkapūtipanasaṃ viya vipubbakabhāvaṃ āpajjitvā paggharati[M 3.0053], caṅgavāre pakkhittaudakaṃ viya hoti. Aggimukhena daṭṭhassa sakalasarīraṃ jhāyitvā bhasmamuṭṭhi viya thusamuṭṭhi viya ca vippakirīyati. Sattamukhena daṭṭhassa sakalasarīraṃ bhijjati, asanipātaṭṭhānaṃ viya mahānikhādanena khatasandhimukhaṃ viya ca hoti. Evaṃ visavasena vibhattā cattāro āsīvisā.

Here, the four vipers are: the wooden-mouthed, the putrid-mouthed, the fire-mouthed, and the weapon-mouthed. When bitten by the wooden-mouthed viper, the entire body becomes stiff like dry wood, and at the joints, it is exceedingly fixed as if pierced by an iron spike. When bitten by the putrid-mouthed viper, the body reaches a state of putrefaction like a ripe, putrid jackfruit and oozes, becoming like water poured into a filter cloth. When bitten by the fire-mouthed viper, the entire body is burnt up and scatters like a handful of ashes or a handful of husks. When bitten by the weapon-mouthed viper, the whole body splits apart, like a place struck by lightning or like a joint-opening carved out by a large chisel. Thus, the four vipers are distinguished by their venom.

Visavegavikārena panete soḷasa honti. Kaṭṭhamukho hi [P 3.0007] daṭṭhaviso, diṭṭhaviso, phuṭṭhaviso, vātavisoti catubbidho hoti. Tena hi daṭṭhampi diṭṭhampi phuṭṭhampi tassa vātena pahaṭampi sarīraṃ vuttappakārena thaddhaṃ hoti. Sesesupi eseva nayoti. Evaṃ visavegavikāravasena soḷasa honti.

Moreover, due to the variations in venom potency, these become sixteen. For the wooden-mouthed viper is fourfold: that with bite-venom, sight-venom, touch-venom, and wind-venom. Thus, whether one is bitten, seen, touched, or struck by its wind, the body becomes rigid as described above. The same principle applies to the rest. In this way, due to the variations in venom potency, there are sixteen.

Puna puggalapaṇṇattivasena catusaṭṭhi honti. Kathaṃ? Kaṭṭhamukhesu tāva daṭṭhaviso ca āgataviso no ghoraviso, ghoraviso no āgataviso, āgataviso ceva ghoraviso ca, nevāgataviso na ghoravisoti catubbidho hoti. Tattha yassa visaṃ sampajjalitatiṇukkāya aggi viya sīghaṃ abhiruhitvā akkhīni gahetvā khandhaṃ gahetvā sīsaṃ gahetvā ṭhitanti vattabbataṃ āpajjati maṇisappādīnaṃ visaṃ viya, mantaṃ pana parivattetvā kaṇṇavātaṃ datvā daṇḍakena pahaṭamatte otaritvā daṭṭhaṭṭhāneyeva tiṭṭhati, ayaṃ āgataviso no ghoraviso nāma. Yassa pana visaṃ saṇikaṃ abhiruhati, āruḷhāruḷhaṭṭhāne pana ayaṃ sītaudakaṃ viya hoti udakasappādīnaṃ visaṃ viya, dvādasavassaccayenāpi kaṇṇapiṭṭhikhandhapiṭṭhikādīsu gaṇḍapiḷakādivasena paññāyati, mantaparivattanādīsu ca kayiramānāsu sīghaṃ na otarati, ayaṃ ghoraviso no āgataviso nāma. Yassa pana visaṃ sīghaṃ abhiruhati, na sīghaṃ otarati aneḷakasappādīnaṃ visaṃ viya, ayaṃ āgataviso ceva ghoraviso ca. Yassa pana visaṃ mandaṃ hoti, otāriyamānampi sukheneva otarati nīlasappadhammanisappādīnaṃ visaṃ viya, ayaṃ nevāgataviso na [V 3.0054] ghoraviso nāma. Iminā upāyena kaṭṭhamukhe daṭṭhavisādayo pūtimukhādīsu ca daṭṭhavisādayo veditabbāti. Evaṃ puggalapaṇṇattivasena catusaṭṭhi.

Furthermore, by way of individual designation, they become sixty-four. How? In the case of the wooden-mouthed vipers, the bite-venom, for instance, is fourfold: that with swift but not fierce venom; that with fierce but not swift venom; that with venom that is both swift and fierce; and that with venom that is neither swift nor fierce. Here, the venom of a snake that ascends swiftly like the fire of a blazing grass torch, seizing the eyes, shoulders, and head—like the venom of jeweled snakes, etc.—but which, when a mantra is repeatedly applied, 'ear-wind' is given, and it is merely struck with a stick, subsides and remains only at the site of the bite: this is called 'swift but not fierce venom'. The venom of a snake that ascends slowly, but in every place it has ascended is like cold water—like the venom of water snakes, etc.—and which may manifest even after twelve years as boils, sores, etc., on the back of the ears, the back of the neck, etc., and does not quickly subside even when mantras are applied: this is called 'fierce but not swift venom'. The venom of a snake that ascends swiftly but does not subside swiftly—like the venom of aneḷaka snakes, etc.: this is called 'venom that is both swift and fierce'. The venom of a snake that is slow and, when treated, subsides with ease—like the venom of blue snakes, dhammanī snakes, etc.: this is called 'venom that is neither swift nor fierce'. By this method, the types beginning with bite-venom for the wooden-mouthed viper, and the types beginning with bite-venom for the putrid-mouthed vipers and others, should be understood. Thus, by way of individual designation, there are sixty-four.

Tesu [M 3.0054] “aṇḍajā nāgā”tiādinā (saṃ. ni. 3.342-344) yonivasena ekekaṃ catudhā vibhajitvā chapaṇṇāsādhikāni dve satāni honti. Te jalajāthalajāti dviguṇitā [P 3.0008] dvādasādhikāni pañcasatāni honti, te kāmarūpaakāmarūpānaṃ vasena dviguṇitā catuvīsādhikasahassasaṅkhā honti. Puna gatamaggassa paṭilomato saṃkhippamānā kaṭṭhamukhādivasena cattārova hontīti. Te sandhāya bhagavā “seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, cattāro āsīvisā”ti āha. Kulavasena hi ete gahitā.

Among these [sixty-four], by dividing each one into four based on origin, according to the text beginning 'egg-born nāgas', they become two hundred and fifty-six. When these are doubled for water-born and land-born, they become five hundred and twelve. When these are doubled by way of those who can create forms at will and those who cannot, they are numbered as one thousand and twenty-four. Again, when condensed by reversing the path taken, they become just four, by way of the wooden-mouthed and so on. It was with reference to these that the Blessed One said, 'Just as, monks, there are four vipers.' For these were taken by way of their clan.

Tattha āsīvisāti āsittavisātipi āsīvisā, asitavisātipi āsīvisā, asisadisavisātipi āsīvisā. Āsittavisāti sakalakāye āsiñcitvā viya ṭhapitavisā, parassa ca attano sarīre ca āsiñcanavisāti attho. Asitavisāti yaṃ yaṃ etehi asitaṃ hoti paribhuttaṃ, taṃ taṃ visameva sampajjati, tasmā asitaṃ visaṃ hoti etesanti āsīvisā. Asisadisavisāti asiviya tikhiṇaṃ paramammacchedanasamatthaṃ visaṃ etesanti āsīvisāti evamettha vacanattho veditabbo. Uggatejāti uggatatejā balavatejā. Ghoravisāti dunnimmaddanavisā.

Herein, regarding `āsīvisa`: they are called `āsīvisa` because they have 'poured venom' (`āsittavisā`); they are also called `āsīvisa` because they have 'eaten venom' (`asitavisā`); and they are also called `āsīvisa` because they have 'sword-like venom' (`asisadisavisā`). 'Poured venom' means having venom established as if poured over the entire body; the meaning is having venom for pouring onto another's body and one's own. 'Eaten venom' means that whatever is eaten or consumed by them becomes venom itself; therefore, for them, what is eaten becomes venom, hence they are `āsīvisa`. 'Sword-like venom' means they have venom that is sharp like a sword, capable of severing the most vital parts of the body, hence they are `āsīvisa`. In this way, the meaning of the word should be understood here. `Uggatejā` means having arisen power, having mighty power. `Ghoravisā` means having venom that is difficult to suppress.

Evaṃ vadeyyunti paṭijaggāpanatthaṃ evaṃ vadeyyuṃ. Rājāno hi āsīvise gāhāpetvā – “tathārūpe core vā etehi ḍaṃsāpetvā māressāma, nagarūparodhakāle parasenāya vā taṃ khipissāma, parabalaṃ nimmaddetuṃ asakkontā subhojanaṃ bhuñjitvā varasayanaṃ āruyha etehi attānaṃ ḍaṃsāpetvā sattūnaṃ vasaṃ anāgacchantā attano ruciyā marissāmā”ti āsīvise jaggāpenti. Te yaṃ coraṃ sahasāva māretuṃ na icchanti, “evamete dīgharattaṃ dukkhappatto hutvā marissantī”ti icchantā taṃ purisaṃ evaṃ vadanti ime te ambho purisa cattāro āsīvisāti.

‘Thus they would speak’ means they would speak thus for the purpose of making him tend to them. Indeed, kings, having had vipers caught, rear them, thinking: ‘We will have thieves who deserve such a fate bitten by these and killed; or, during a city siege, we will hurl them at the enemy army; or, being unable to crush the enemy’s force, after enjoying a fine meal and ascending a luxurious bed, we will have ourselves bitten by these and, not falling under the control of our enemies, we will die according to our own wish.’ Those kings who do not wish to kill a certain thief immediately, but desire, ‘Thus, having experienced suffering for a long time, he will die,’ they say to that man: ‘Friend man, these are your four vipers.’

Tattha kālena kālanti kāle kāle. Saṃvesetabbāti nipajjāpetabbā. Aññataro vā aññataro vāti kaṭṭhamukhādīsu yo koci. Yaṃ te ambho purisa karaṇīyaṃ, taṃ karohīti idaṃ atthacarakassa vacanaṃ veditabbaṃ. Tassa kira purisassa evaṃ āsīvise paṭipādetvā ‘ayaṃ vo upaṭṭhāko’ti [V 3.0055] catūsu peḷāsu ṭhapitānaṃ āsīvisānaṃ ārocenti. Atheko nikkhamitvā āgamma tassa purisassa dakkhiṇapādānusārena abhiruhitvā [M 3.0055] dakkhiṇahatthaṃ maṇibandhato paṭṭhāya [P 3.0009] veṭhetvā dakkhiṇakaṇṇasotamūle phaṇaṃ katvā susūti karonto nipajji. Aparo vāmapādānusārena abhiruhitvā tatheva vāmahatthaṃ veṭhetvā vāmakaṇṇasotamūle phaṇaṃ katvā susūti karonto nipajji, tatiyo nikkhamitvā abhimukhaṃ abhiruhitvā kucchiṃ veṭhetvā galavāṭakamūle phaṇaṃ katvā susūti karonto nipajji, catuttho piṭṭhibhāgena abhiruhitvā gīvaṃ veṭhetvā uparimuddhani phaṇaṃ ṭhapetvā susūti karonto nipajji.

Herein, ‘from time to time’ means on occasion. ‘They should be made to lie down’ means they should be made to sleep. ‘One or another’ means any one among those such as the `kaṭṭhamukha` snakes. ‘Friend man, do what needs to be done by you’—this should be understood as the words of one who acts for his welfare. It is said that having thus handed over the vipers to that man, they announce to the vipers placed in four baskets, ‘This is your attendant.’ Then one snake, coming out and approaching, climbs up along the man’s right leg, coils around his right hand starting from the wrist, and, making its hood at the base of his right ear, lies down hissing. Another climbs up along his left leg, coils around his left hand in the same way, and, making its hood at the base of his left ear, lies down hissing. A third, coming out, climbs up from the front, coils around his belly, and, making its hood at the base of his throat, lies down hissing. A fourth climbs up his back, coils around his neck, and, placing its hood on top of his head, lies down hissing.

Evaṃ catūsu āsīvisesu sarīraṭṭhakesuyeva jātesu eko tassa purisassa atthacarakapuriso taṃ disvā “kiṃ te, bho purisa, laddha”nti, pucchi. Tato tena “ime me, bho, hatthesu hatthakaṭakaṃ viya bāhāsu keyūraṃ viya kucchimhi kucchiveṭhanasāṭako viya kaṇṇesu kaṇṇacūḷikā viya gale muttāvaliyo viya sīse sīsapasādhanaṃ viya keci alaṅkāravisesā raññā dinnā”ti vutte so āha – “bho andhabāla, mā evaṃ maññittha ‘raññā me tuṭṭhenetaṃ pasādhanaṃ dinna’nti. Tvaṃ rañño āgucārī coro, ime ca cattāro āsīvisā durupaṭṭhāhā duppaṭijaggiyā, ekasmiṃ uṭṭhātukāme eko nhāyitukāmo hoti, ekasmiṃ nhāyitukāme eko bhuñjitukāmo, ekasmiṃ bhuñjitukāme eko nipajjitukāmo. Tesu yasseva icchā na pūrati, so tattheva ḍaṃsitvā māretī”ti. Atthi pana, bho, evaṃ sante koci sotthimaggoti? Āma, rājapurisānaṃ vikkhittabhāvaṃ ñatvā palāyanaṃ sotthibhāvoti vatvā “yaṃ te karaṇīyaṃ, taṃ karohī”ti vadeyya.

When the four vipers were thus established on his body, a certain man who acted for his welfare saw him and asked, ‘Friend man, what have you received?’ When he replied, ‘Friend, these are certain special ornaments given by the king: like bracelets on my hands, like armlets on my arms, like a decorative waist-cloth on my belly, like ear-pendants in my ears, like strings of pearls on my neck, and like a head-ornament on my head,’ the other said: ‘O blind fool, do not think, “The king, being pleased with me, has given me this adornment.” You are a thief, a wrongdoer against the king, and these four vipers are difficult to attend to and difficult to look after. When one wishes to get up, another wishes to bathe; when one wishes to bathe, another wishes to eat; when one wishes to eat, another wishes to lie down. Among them, whichever one’s desire is not fulfilled, that one will bite you right there and kill you.’ ‘This being so, sir, is there any path to safety?’ ‘Yes, knowing that the king’s men are distracted, fleeing is the way to safety.’ Having said this, he would add, ‘Do what you must do.’

Taṃ sutvā itaro catunnaṃ āsīvisānaṃ pamādakkhaṇaṃ rājapurisehi ca pavivittaṃ disvā, vāmahatthena dakkhiṇahatthaṃ veṭhetvā, dakkhiṇakaṇṇacūḷikāya phaṇaṃ ṭhapetvā, sayitāsīvisassa sarīraṃ parimajjanto viya saṇikaṃ taṃ apanetvā, eteneva upāyena sesepi apanetvā tesaṃ bhīto palāyeyya. Atha naṃ te āsīvisā “ayaṃ amhākaṃ raññā upaṭṭhāko dinno”ti anubandhamānā āgaccheyyuṃ. Idaṃ sandhāya atha kho so, bhikkhave, puriso bhīto catunnaṃ āsīvisānaṃ…pe… palāyethāti vuttaṃ.

Hearing this, the other man, seeing a moment of heedlessness on the part of the four vipers and a place secluded from the king’s men, gently removed with his left hand the sleeping viper—the one that had coiled around his right hand and placed its hood at his right ear-pendant—as if stroking its body. By this same method, he removed the remaining ones as well and, frightened of them, he would flee. Then those vipers, pursuing him, would come after him, thinking, ‘This attendant was given to us by the king.’ With reference to this, it was said: ‘Then, monks, that man, frightened of the four vipers… would flee.’

Tasmiṃ pana purise evaṃ āgatamaggaṃ oloketvā oloketvā palāyante [P 3.0010] rājā “palāto [V 3.0056] so puriso”ti sutvā “ko nu kho taṃ anubandhitvā ghātetuṃ sakkhissatī”ti vicinanto tasseva paccatthike pañca [M 3.0056] jane labhitvā “gacchatha naṃ anubandhitvā ghātethā”ti peseyya. Athassa atthacarā purisā taṃ pavattiṃ ñatvā āroceyyuṃ. So bhiyyosomattāya bhīto palāyetha. Imamatthaṃ sandhāya tamenaṃ evaṃ vadeyyuntiādi vuttaṃ.

But while that man was fleeing, looking back again and again at the path he had come, the king, hearing, ‘That man has fled,’ would search, thinking, ‘Who now will be able to pursue and kill him?’ Finding five enemies of that very man, he would send them, saying, ‘Go, pursue and kill him.’ Then, men acting for his welfare, learning of that event, would inform him. He, frightened beyond measure, would flee. With reference to this meaning, the passage beginning ‘They would speak to him thus…’ was said.

Chaṭṭho antaracaro vadhakoti “paṭhamaṃ āsīvisehi anubaddho ito cito ca te vañcento palāyi, idāni pañcahi paccatthikehi anubaddho suṭṭhutaraṃ palāyati, na sakkā so evaṃ gahetuṃ, upalāḷanāya pana sakkā, tasmā daharakālato paṭṭhāya ekato khāditvā ca pivitvā ca santhavaṃ antaracaraṃ vadhakamassa pesethā”ti amaccehi vuttena raññā pariyesitvā pesito antaracaro vadhako.

‘The sixth is an internal killer’ refers to the internal killer sent by the king after a search, based on what was said by his ministers: ‘First, pursued by the vipers, he fled here and there, deceiving them. Now, pursued by five enemies, he flees even more desperately. He cannot be caught in this way, but he can be caught through enticement. Therefore, send to him an internal killer, an intimate acquaintance who has eaten and drunk together with him since they were young.’

So passeyya suññaṃ gāmanti nivattitvā olokento padaṃ ghāyitvā ghāyitvā vegenāgacchante cattāro āsīvise pañca vadhake paccatthike chaṭṭhañca antaracaraṃ vadhakaṃ “nivatta bho, mā palāyi, puttadārena saddhiṃ kāme paribhuñjanto sukhaṃ vasissasī”ti vatvā āgacchantaṃ disvā, bhiyyosomattāya yena vā tena vā palāyanto paccantaraṭṭhe abhimukhagataṃ ekaṃ chakuṭikaṃ suññaṃ gāmaṃ passeyya. Rittakaññeva paviseyyāti dhanadhaññamañcapīṭhādīhi virahitattā rittakaññeva paviseyya. Tucchakaṃ suññakanti etasseva vevacanaṃ. Parimaseyyāti “sace pānīyaṃ bhavissati, pivissāmi, sace bhattaṃ bhavissati, bhuñjissāmī”ti bhājanaṃ vivaritvā hatthaṃ anto pavesetvā parimaseyya.

He would see an empty village means that, while fleeing, turning back and looking, smelling the tracks again and again, and seeing four venomous snakes, five executioner-enemies, and a sixth, an internal executioner, coming with speed and approaching saying, “Turn back, friend, do not flee! Enjoying sensual pleasures with your wife and children, you will live happily,” he would flee to an even greater degree in any direction whatsoever and would see a deserted village with six huts that he had come upon in a border region. He would enter it, it being truly empty means that because it was devoid of wealth, grain, beds, seats, and so on, he would enter it, it being truly empty. Void and empty are synonyms for this. He would feel around means that thinking, “If there is water, I will drink; if there is food, I will eat,” he would open a vessel, put his hand inside, and feel around.

Tamenaṃ [P 3.0011] evaṃ vadeyyunti channaṃ gharānaṃ ekagharepi kiñci alabhitvā gāmamajjhe eko sandacchāyo rukkho atthi, tattha vaṅkaphalakaṃ atthataṃ disvā, “idha tāva nisīdissāmī”ti gantvā, tattha nisinnaṃ mandamandena vātena bījiyamānaṃ tattakamattampi sukhaṃ santato assādayamānaṃ, tamenaṃ purisaṃ kecideva atthacarakā bahi pavattiṃ ñatvā āgatā evaṃ vadeyyuṃ. Idāni ambho purisāti ambho, purisa, idāni. Corā gāmaghātakāti “yadevettha labhissāma, taṃ gaṇhissāma vā ghātessāma vā”ti āgatā cha gāmaghātakacorā.

Then they would say to him thus means that, not having found anything in even one of the six houses, he sees a tree with cool shade in the middle of the village. Seeing a well-shaped plank spread out there, he goes, thinking, “I will sit here for a while.” While he is sitting there, being fanned by a gentle breeze and continuously savoring even that much pleasure, some well-wishers, having learned of the events outside, come and would speak thus to that man. Now, O man means: O man, now. Village-destroying robbers means the six village-destroying robbers who have come thinking, ‘Whatever we find here, we will either take it or we will kill.’”

Udakaṇṇavanti [M 3.0057] gambhīraṃ puthulaṃ udakaṃ. Gambhīrampi hi aputhulaṃ, puthulaṃ vā agambhīraṃ, na aṇṇavoti [V 3.0057] vuccati, yampana gambhīrañca puthulañca, tassevetaṃ nāmaṃ. Sāsaṅkaṃ sappaṭibhayanti catunnaṃ āsīvisānaṃ pañcannaṃ vadhakānaṃ chaṭṭhassa antaracarassa channañca gāmaghātakacorānaṃ vasena sāsaṅkaṃ sappaṭibhayaṃ. Khemaṃ appaṭibhayanti tesaṃyeva āsīvisādīnaṃ abhāvena khemañca nibbhayañca vicitrauyyānavaraṃ bahvannapānaṃ devanagarasadisaṃ. Na cassa nāvā santāraṇīti “imāya nāvāya orimatīrato paratīraṃ gamissantī”ti evaṃ ṭhapitā ca santāraṇī nāvā na bhaveyya. Uttarasetu vāti rukkhasetu-jaṅghasetu-sakaṭasetūnaṃ aññataro uttarasetu vā na bhaveyya. Tiṭṭhati brāhmaṇoti na kho esa brāhmaṇo. Kasmā naṃ brāhmaṇoti āha? Ettakānaṃ paccatthikānaṃ bāhitattā, desanaṃ vā vinivattento ekaṃ khīṇāsavabrāhmaṇaṃ dassetumpi evamāha.

A great body of water means deep and wide water. For what is deep but not wide, or wide but not deep, is not called a great body of water; but that which is both deep and wide, that is its name. Perilous and fearful means that on account of the four venomous snakes, the five executioners, the sixth internal enemy, and the six village-destroying robbers, it is perilous and fearful. Safe and without fear means that due to the absence of those very snakes and so on, it is safe and without fear, with delightful gardens and forests, with abundant food and drink, like a city of the devas. And there was no ferryboat for crossing means there would be no ferryboat placed there for crossing with the thought, “With this boat they will go from this shore to the other shore.” Or a bridge for crossing over means there would not be any kind of bridge for crossing over, among tree-bridges, foot-bridges, or cart-bridges. A brahmin stands: this is not a brahmin in the ultimate sense. Why then is he called a brahmin? Because he has warded off so many enemies, he is called a brahmin. Or, concluding the discourse, the Blessed One also speaks thus to point out a single brahmin who is an Arahant.

Tasmiṃ pana evaṃ uttiṇṇe cattāro āsīvisā “na laddho vatāsi amhehi, ajja te murumurāya jīvitaṃ khāditvā chaḍḍeyyāma”. Pañca paccatthikā “na laddho vatāsi amhehi, ajja te parivāretvā aṅgamaṅgāni chinditvā rañño santikaṃ [P 3.0012] gatā sataṃ vā sahassaṃ vā labheyyāma”. Chaṭṭho antaracaro “na laddho vatāsi mayā, ajja te phalikavaṇṇena asinā sīsaṃ chinditvā, senāpatiṭṭhānaṃ labhitvā sampattiṃ anubhaveyyaṃ”. Cha corā “na laddho vatāsi amhehi, ajja te vividhāni kammakāraṇāni kāretvā bahudhanaṃ āharāpessāmā”ti cintetvā, udakaṇṇavaṃ otarituṃ asakkontā rañño āṇāya kopitattā parato gantumpi avisahantā tattheva sussitvā mareyyuṃ.

But when he had thus crossed over, the four venomous snakes would think: “Alas, you were not caught by us! Today we would have devoured your life with a crunching sound and discarded it.” The five enemies would think: “Alas, you were not caught by us! Today, having surrounded you and cut off your major and minor limbs, we would have gone to the king and received a reward of a hundred or a thousand.” The sixth, the internal enemy, would think: “Alas, you were not caught by me! Today, having cut off your head with a crystal-colored sword, I would have obtained the position of a general and enjoyed its fortune.” The six robbers, thinking, “Alas, you were not caught by us! Today, having made you perform various acts of torment, we would have brought in much wealth,” and being unable to enter the great body of water and not daring to go further because of the king’s command, would dry up from lack of sustenance and die right there.

Upamā kho myāyanti ettha evaṃ ādito paṭṭhāya opammasaṃsandanaṃ veditabbaṃ – rājā viya hi kammaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ, rājāparādhikapuriso viya vaṭṭanissito puthujjano. Cattāro āsīvisā viya cattāri mahābhūtāni, rañño tassa cattāro āsīvise paṭicchāpitakālo viya kammunā puthujjanassa paṭisandhikkhaṇeyeva catunnaṃ mahābhūtānaṃ dinnakālo. “Imesaṃ āsīvisānaṃ pamādakkhaṇe rājapurisānañca vivittakkhaṇe nikkhamitvā yaṃ te ambho, purisa, karaṇīyaṃ, taṃ karohī”ti vacanena “palāyassū”ti vuttakālo viya satthārā imassa bhikkhuno mahābhūtakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetvā [M 3.0058] “imesu catūsu mahābhūtesu nibbinda virajja, evaṃ vaṭṭato parimuccissasī”ti kathitakālo, tassa purisassa atthacarakavacanaṃ sutvā catunnaṃ āsīvisānaṃ pamādakkhaṇe rājapurisānañca vivittakkhaṇe nikkhamitvā yena vā tena vā palāyanaṃ viya imassa [V 3.0058] bhikkhuno satthu santike kammaṭṭhānaṃ labhitvā mahābhūtāsīvisehi parimuccanatthāya ñāṇapalāyanena palāyanaṃ.

This is an analogy: here, the correlation of the analogy should be understood from the beginning in this way: Kamma should be seen as the king; the ordinary person who is dependent on the round of existence, as the man who offended the king. The four great elements should be seen as the four venomous snakes. The time when the four great elements are given by kamma to the ordinary person at the very moment of rebirth-linking should be seen as the time when the king entrusted that man with the four venomous snakes. The time when it was said by a well-wisher, “Flee!” with the words, “O man, at a moment when these snakes are heedless and the king’s men are absent, depart and do what you must do,” is like the time when the Teacher, having taught this monk the meditation subject of the great elements, said, “Become disenchanted with these four great elements, become dispassionate; in this way you will be freed from the round of existence.” The fleeing of that man in any direction whatsoever—having heard the words of the well-wisher and departed at a moment when the four snakes were heedless and the king’s men were absent—is like the fleeing of this monk with the flight of knowledge for the sake of being freed from the great-element-snakes, after receiving the meditation subject in the presence of the Teacher.

Idāni catunnetaṃ mahābhūtānaṃ adhivacanaṃ pathavīdhātuyā āpodhātuyātiādīsu catumahābhūtakathā ca pañcupādānakkhandhakathā ca āyatanakathā ca visuddhimagge vitthāritanayeneva veditabbā. Ettha ca kaṭṭhamukhaāsīviso viya pathavīdhātu daṭṭhabbā, pūtimukhaaggimukhasatthamukhā viya sesadhātuyo. Yatheva hi kaṭṭhamukhena daṭṭhassa sakalakāyo thaddho [P 3.0013] hoti, evaṃ pathavīdhātupakopenāpi. Yathā ca pūtimukhādīhi daṭṭhassa paggharati ceva jhāyati ca chijjati ca, evaṃ āpodhātutejodhātuvāyodhātupakopenāpīti. Tenāhu aṭṭhakathācariyā –

Now, the designation of the four great elements in such texts as 'the earth element, the water element,' and so on, the discourse on the four great elements, the discourse on the five aggregates of clinging, and the discourse on the sense bases should be known only by the method that has been explained in detail in the Visuddhimagga. Furthermore, here, the earth element should be regarded like a kaṭṭhamukha viper, and the remaining elements like the pūtimukha, aggimukha, and satthamukha vipers. For just as the entire body of one bitten by a kaṭṭhamukha viper becomes stiff, so too with the derangement of the earth element. And just as for one bitten by the pūtimukha vipers and so on, the body oozes, burns, and is cut to pieces, so too with the derangement of the water element, fire element, and air element. Therefore, the commentary teachers said:

“Patthaddho bhavatī kāyo, daṭṭho kaṭṭhamukhena vā;

Pathavīdhātupakopena, hoti kaṭṭhamukheva so.

The body, bitten by a kaṭṭhamukha snake, becomes stiff; similarly, through the derangement of the earth element, that body becomes stiff, as though it were in the mouth of a kaṭṭhamukha snake.

“Pūtiko bhavatī kāyo, daṭṭho pūtimukhena vā;

Āpodhātupakopena, hoti pūtimukheva so.

The body, bitten by a pūtimukha snake, becomes putrid, swelling and festering like an overripe jackfruit; similarly, through the derangement of the water element, that body becomes putrid, as though it were in the mouth of a pūtimukha snake.

“Santatto bhavatī kāyo, daṭṭho aggimukhena vā;

Tejodhātupakopena, hoti aggimukheva so.

The body, bitten by an aggimukha snake, becomes scorched; similarly, through the derangement of the fire element, that body becomes scorched, as though it were in the mouth of an aggimukha snake.

“Sañchinno bhavatī kāyo, daṭṭho satthamukhena vā;

Vāyodhātupakopena, hoti satthamukheva so”ti. –

The body, bitten by a satthamukha snake, is cut to pieces; similarly, through the derangement of the air element, that body is cut to pieces, as though it were in the mouth of a satthamukha snake.

Evaṃ tāvettha visesato sadisabhāvo veditabbo.

Thus, first of all, the similarity in a specific sense should be understood herein.

Avisesato pana āsayato visavegavikārato anatthaggahaṇato durupaṭṭhānato durāsadato akataññutato avisesakārito anantadosūpaddavatoti imehi kāraṇehi etesaṃ āsīvisasadisatā veditabbā. Tattha āsayatoti āsīvisānañhi vammiko āsayo, tattheva te vasanti. Mahābhūtānampi kāyavammiko āsayo[M 3.0059]. Āsīvisānañca rukkhasusiratiṇapaṇṇagahanasaṅkāraṭṭhānānipi [V 3.0059] āsayo. Etesupi hi te vasanti. Mahābhūtānampi kāyasusiraṃ kāyagahanaṃ kāyasaṅkāraṭṭhānaṃ āsayoti. Evaṃ tāva āsayato sadisatā veditabbā.

Furthermore, in a general sense, their similarity to vipers should be understood for these reasons: by way of dwelling place, by way of the derangement from the venom's force, by way of taking what is unbeneficial, by way of being difficult to attend to, by way of being difficult to approach, by way of ungratefulness, by way of not making distinctions, and by way of endless faults and calamities. Therein, regarding 'by way of dwelling place': for vipers, an anthill is their dwelling place; they live right there. For the great elements, too, the body-anthill is their dwelling place. And for vipers, hollow trees, thickets of grass and leaves, and places where sweepings are discarded are also dwelling places; for they live in these too. For the great elements, too, the body which is like a hollow tree, the body which is like a thicket, and the body which is like a place where sweepings are discarded are their dwelling place. Thus, first of all, the similarity by way of dwelling place should be understood.

Visavegavikāratoti āsīvisā hi kulavasena kaṭṭhamukhādibhedato cattāro. Tattha ekeko visavikārato vibhajjamāno daṭṭhavisādivasena catubbidho hoti. Mahābhūtānipi paccattalakkhaṇavasena pathavīādibhedato cattāri. Ettha ekekaṃ kammasamuṭṭhānādivasena catubbidhaṃ hoti. Evaṃ visavegavikārato sadisatā veditabbā.

Regarding 'by way of the derangement from the venom's force': Indeed, vipers are of four kinds by way of lineage, with the distinctions of kaṭṭhamukha, etc. Therein, each one, when being divided by way of the derangement from the venom, becomes fourfold by way of bite-venom, sight-venom, touch-venom, and air-venom. The great elements, too, are fourfold by way of their individual characteristics, with the distinctions of earth, etc. Herein, each one becomes fourfold by way of being originated by kamma, etc. Thus, the similarity by way of the derangement from the venom's force should be understood.

Anatthaggahaṇatoti āsīvise gaṇhantā pañca anatthe gaṇhanti – duggandhaṃ gaṇhanti, asuciṃ gaṇhanti, byādhiṃ gaṇhanti, visaṃ gaṇhanti, maraṇaṃ gaṇhanti. Mahābhūtānipi gaṇhantā pañca anatthe gaṇhanti – duggandhaṃ gaṇhanti, asuciṃ gaṇhanti, byādhiṃ gaṇhanti, jaraṃ gaṇhanti, maraṇaṃ gaṇhanti. Tenāhu porāṇā –

Regarding 'by way of taking what is unbeneficial': those who take hold of vipers take five unbeneficial things—they take foul odor; they take impurity; they take disease; they take poison; they take death. Those who take hold of the great elements also take five unbeneficial things—they take foul odor; they take impurity; they take disease; they take aging; they take death. Therefore, the ancients said:

“Yekeci [P 3.0014] sappaṃ gaṇhanti, mīḷhalittaṃ mahāvisaṃ;

Pañca gaṇhantunatthāni, loke sappābhinandino.

Whoever takes hold of a snake smeared with filth and possessing great venom—those in the world who delight in snakes take five unbeneficial things.

“Duggandhaṃ asuciṃ byādhiṃ, visaṃ maraṇapañcamaṃ;

Anatthā honti pañcete, mīḷhalitte bhujaṅgame.

Foul odor, impurity, disease, poison, and death as the fifth—these five unbeneficial things exist in a snake smeared with filth.

“Evamevaṃ akusalā, andhabālaputhujjanā;

Pañca gaṇhantunatthāni, bhave jātābhinandino.

Just so, the unskillful, blind, foolish ordinary people, those who delight in birth in existence, take five unbeneficial things.

“Duggandhaṃ asuciṃ byādhiṃ, jaraṃ maraṇapañcamaṃ;

Anatthā honti pañcete, mīḷhalitteva pannage”ti. –

Foul odor, impurity, disease, aging, and death as the fifth—these five unbeneficial things exist, just as in a snake smeared with filth.

Evaṃ anatthaggahaṇato sadisatā veditabbā.

Thus, the similarity by way of taking what is unbeneficial should be understood.

Durupaṭṭhānatoti te āsīvisā durupaṭṭhānā, ekasmiṃ uṭṭhātukāme eko nhāyitukāmo hoti[V 3.0060], tasmiṃ nhāyitukāme aparo bhuñjitukāmo, tasmiṃ bhuñjitukāme añño nipajjitukāmo hoti. Tesu yassa yasseva ajjhāsayo na pūrati, so tattheva ḍaṃsitvā māreti. Imehi pana āsīvisehi bhūtāneva durupaṭṭhānatarāni. Pathavīdhātuyā hi bhesajje kayiramāne āpodhātu kuppati, tasseva bhesajjaṃ karontassa tejodhātūti [M 3.0060] evaṃ ekissā bhesajje kayiramāne aparā kuppantīti. Evaṃ durupaṭṭhānato sadisatā veditabbā.

Regarding 'by way of being difficult to attend to': Those vipers are difficult to attend to.
