When one wants to get up, another wants to bathe; when that one wants to bathe, another wants to eat; when that one wants to eat, another wants to lie down. Whichever snake's inclination is not fulfilled, that very snake bites and causes death right there. But the elements are even more difficult to attend to than these vipers. Indeed, when medicine is being administered for the earth element, the water element becomes deranged; while making medicine for that, the fire element becomes deranged. Thus, when medicine is being administered for one element, another becomes deranged. In this way, the similarity by way of being difficult to attend to should be understood.

Durāsadatoti durāsadā hi āsīvisā, gehassa purimabhāge āsīvisaṃ disvā pacchimabhāgena palāyanti, pacchimabhāge disvā purimabhāgena, gehamajjhe disvā gabbhaṃ pavisanti, gabbhe disvā mañcapīṭhaṃ abhiruhanti. Mahābhūtāni tatopi durāsadatarāni. Tathārūpena hi kuṭṭharogena phuṭṭhassa kaṇṇanāsādīni chinditvā patanti, sarīraṃ samphuṭati nīlamakkhikā parivārenti, sarīragandho dūratova ubbāhati. Taṃ purisaṃ akkosamānampi paridevamānampi neva rosavasena, na kāruññena, upasaṅkamituṃ sakkonti, nāsikaṃ pidahitvā kheḷaṃ pātentā dūratova naṃ vivajjenti. Evaṃ aññesampi bhagandarakucchirogavātarogādīnaṃ bībhacchajegucchabhāvakarānañca rogānaṃ vasena ayamevattho vibhāvetabboti. Evaṃ durāsadato sadisatā veditabbā.

Regarding 'by way of being difficult to approach': Indeed, vipers are difficult to approach. Seeing a viper in the front part of the house, people flee through the back part; seeing one in the back part, they flee through the front part; seeing one in the middle of the house, they enter a chamber; seeing one in the chamber, they climb onto a bed or chair. The great elements are even more difficult to approach than those. Indeed, for one afflicted by such a disease as leprosy, the ears, nose, and so on are cut off and fall down; the disease severely afflicts the body; dark blue flies swarm them; the body's odor afflicts even from afar. People are unable to approach that person, even if he is cursing or lamenting, neither out of anger nor out of compassion. Covering their noses and spitting, they shun him from afar. In this way, this same meaning should be made clear by way of other diseases as well, such as fistula, stomach disease, and wind disease, which cause a state of repulsiveness and disgust. Thus, the similarity by way of being difficult to approach should be understood.

Akataññutatoti [P 3.0015] āsīvisā hi akataññuno honti, nhāpiyamānāpi bhojiyamānāpi gandhamālādīhi pūjiyamānāpi peḷāyaṃ pakkhipitvā parihariyamānāpi otārameva gavesanti. Yattha otāraṃ labhanti, tattheva naṃ ḍaṃsitvā mārenti. Āsīvisehipi mahābhūtāneva akataññutarāni. Etesañhi kataṃ nāma natthi, sītena vā uṇhena vā nimmalena jalena nhāpiyamānānipi gandhamālādīhi sakkariyamānānipi muduvatthamudusayanamuduyānādīhi parihariyamānānipi, varabhojanaṃ bhojiyamānānipi, varapānaṃ pāyāpiyamānānipi otārameva gavesanti. Yattha otāraṃ labhanti, tattheva kuppitvā anayabyasanaṃ pāpentīti. Evaṃ akataññutato sadisatā veditabbā.

From the aspect of ungratefulness: Indeed, venomous snakes are ungrateful. Even when bathed, fed, honored with perfumes and garlands, and carried around in a basket, they seek only an opportunity to bite. Wherever they find an opportunity, they bite and kill their benefactor. The great elements are even more ungrateful than venomous snakes. For them, there is no such thing as a favor done. Even when bathed with cool, warm, or clear water, honored with perfumes and garlands, attended to with soft clothes, soft beds, and soft vehicles, fed the finest food, and given the finest drinks—they seek only an opportunity for destruction. Wherever they find an opportunity, they become disturbed and bring about ruin and disaster. In this way, the similarity should be understood from the standpoint of ungratefulness.

Avisesakāritoti āsīvisā hi “ayaṃ khattiyo vā brāhmaṇo vā vesso vā suddo vā gahaṭṭho vā pabbajito vā”ti visesaṃ na karonti, sampattasampattameva ḍaṃsitvā mārenti. Mahābhūtānipi “ayaṃ khattiyo vā brāhmaṇo vā vesso vā suddo vā gahaṭṭho vā pabbajito vā devo vā manusso vā māro vā brahmā vā nigguṇo vā saguṇo vā”ti visesaṃ na karonti[V 3.0061]. Yadi hi nesaṃ “ayaṃ guṇavā”ti lajjā [M 3.0061] uppajjeyya, sadevake loke aggapuggale tathāgate lajjaṃ uppādeyyuṃ. Athāpi nesaṃ “ayaṃ mahāpañño ayaṃ mahiddhiko ayaṃ dhutavādo”tiādinā nayena lajjā uppajjeyya, dhammasenāpatisāriputtattherādīsu lajjaṃ uppādeyyuṃ. Athāpi nesaṃ “ayaṃ nigguṇo dāruṇo thaddho”ti bhayaṃ uppajjeyya, sadevake loke nigguṇathaddhadāruṇānaṃ aggassa devadattassa channaṃ vā satthārānaṃ bhāyeyyuṃ, na ca lajjanti na ca bhāyanti, kuppitvā yaṃkiñci anayabyasanaṃ āpādentiyeva. Evaṃ avisesakārito sadisatā veditabbā.

From the aspect of being indiscriminate: Indeed, venomous snakes make no distinction, thinking, ‘This is a khattiya, a brahmin, a vessa, a sudda, a householder, or a renunciant.’ They bite and kill whomever they encounter. The great elements also make no distinction, thinking, ‘This is a khattiya, a brahmin, a vessa, a sudda, a householder, a renunciant, a deva, a human, Māra, a brahmā, one without virtue, or one with virtue.’ For if shame were to arise in them, thinking, ‘This one is virtuous,’ they would feel shame toward the Tathāgata, the supreme person in the world with its devas. Or if shame were to arise in them through the method beginning with, ‘This one is of great wisdom, this one is of great power, this one is an observer of ascetic practices,’ they would feel shame toward the Elder Sāriputta, the General of the Dhamma, and others. Or if fear were to arise in them, thinking, ‘This one is without virtue, harsh, and stubborn,’ they would fear Devadatta, the foremost among the virtueless, stubborn, and harsh in the world with its devas, or the six heretical teachers. But they neither feel shame nor do they fear; being disturbed, they only bring about any and all ruin and disaster. In this way, the similarity should be understood from the standpoint of being indiscriminate.

Anantadosūpaddavatoti āsīvise nissāya uppajjanakānañhi dosūpaddavānaṃ pamāṇaṃ natthi. Tathā hete ḍaṃsitvā kāṇampi karonti khujjampi pīṭhasappimpi ekapakkhalampīti evaṃ aparimāṇaṃ vippakāraṃ dassenti. Bhūtānipi kuppitāni na kāṇādibhāvesu na kiñci vippakāraṃ na karonti, appamāṇo etesaṃ dosūpaddavoti. Evaṃ anantadosūpaddavato sadisatā veditabbā.

From the aspect of endless faults and calamities: Indeed, there is no measure to the faults and calamities that arise dependent on venomous snakes. For, having bitten, they make one blind, hunchbacked, crippled, or paralyzed on one side—thus they display immeasurable affliction. When the great elements are disturbed, it is not that they do not cause any affliction among states such as blindness; their fault and calamity are immeasurable. In this way, the similarity should be understood from the standpoint of endless faults and calamities.

Idānettha [P 3.0016] catumahābhūtavasena yāva arahattā kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetabbaṃ siyā, taṃ visuddhimagge catudhātuvavatthānaniddese kathitameva.

Now, at this point, the meditation subject concerning the four great elements up to Arahantship should be explained. But that has already been explained in the Visuddhimagga, in the Exposition of the Analysis of the Four Elements.

Pañca vadhakā paccatthikāti kho bhikkhave pañcannetaṃ upādānakkhandhānaṃ adhivacananti ettha dvīhi ākārehi khandhānaṃ vadhakapaccatthikasadisatā veditabbā. Khandhā hi aññamaññañca vadhenti, tesu ca santesu vadho nāma paññāyati. Kathaṃ? Rūpaṃ tāva rūpampi vadheti arūpampi, tathā arūpaṃ arūpampi vadheti rūpampi. Kathaṃ? Ayañhi pathavīdhātu bhijjamānā itarā tisso dhātuyo gahetvāva bhijjati, āpodhātuādīsupi eseva nayo, evaṃ tāva rūpaṃ rūpameva vadheti. Rūpakkhandho pana bhijjamāno cattāro arūpakkhandhe gahetvāva bhijjati, evaṃ rūpaṃ arūpampi vadheti. Vedanākkhandhopi bhijjamāno saññāsaṅkhāraviññāṇakkhandhe gahetvāva bhijjati. Saññākkhandhādīsupi eseva nayo. Evaṃ arūpaṃ arūpameva vadheti. Cutikkhaṇe pana cattāro arūpakkhandhā bhijjamānā vatthurūpampi gahetvāva bhijjanti, evaṃ arūpaṃ rūpampi vadheti. Evaṃ tāva aññamaññaṃ vadhentīti vadhakā. Yattha pana khandhā atthi, tattha chedanabhedanavadhabandhanādayo honti, na aññatthāti. Evaṃ khandhesu santesu vadho paññāyatītipi vadhakā.

Regarding the passage, '“Five murderers and enemies,” O bhikkhus, is a designation for these five aggregates of clinging': Herein, the similarity of the aggregates to murderous enemies should be understood in two ways. For the aggregates kill one another, and when they exist, that which is called 'killing' is apparent. How? First, form kills both form and non-form; likewise, non-form kills both non-form and form. How? When this earth element perishes, it perishes only by taking the other three elements with it; this same principle should be understood for the water element and so on. In this way, first, form kills form itself. Furthermore, when the form aggregate disintegrates, it disintegrates only by taking the four non-form aggregates with it. In this way, form also kills non-form. When the feeling aggregate disintegrates, it disintegrates only by taking the perception, formations, and consciousness aggregates with it. This same principle should be understood for the perception aggregate and the others. In this way, non-form kills non-form itself. Furthermore, at the moment of death, when the four non-form aggregates disintegrate, they disintegrate only by taking the heart-base matter with them. In this way, non-form also kills form. Thus, because they kill one another, they are called 'murderers.' Furthermore, wherever the aggregates exist, there occur cutting, breaking, killing, binding, and so on, but not elsewhere. In this way also, because killing is apparent when the aggregates exist, they are called 'murderers.'

Idāni [V 3.0062][M 3.0062] pañcakkhandhe rūpārūpavasena dve koṭṭhāse katvā, rūpavasena vā nāmavasena vā rūpapariggahaṃ ādiṃ katvā, yāva arahattā kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetabbaṃ siyā tampi visuddhimagge kathitameva.

Now, having divided the five aggregates into two sections—form and non-form—and beginning with the comprehension of form, either by way of form or by way of name, the meditation subject that leads up to Arahantship should be explained. But this too has already been explained in the Visuddhimagga.

Chaṭṭho antaracaro vadhako ukkhittāsikoti kho, bhikkhave, nandīrāgassetaṃ adhivacananti ettha dvīhākārehi nandīrāgassa ukkhittāsikavadhakasadisatā veditabbā paññāsirapātanato [P 3.0017] ca yonisampaṭipādanato ca. Kathaṃ? Cakkhudvārasmiñhi iṭṭhārammaṇe āpāthagate taṃ ārammaṇaṃ nissāya lobho uppajjati, ettāvatā paññāsīsaṃ patitaṃ nāma hoti, sotadvārādīsupi eseva nayo. Evaṃ tāva paññāsirapātanato sadisatā veditabbā. Nandīrāgo panesa aṇḍajādibhedā catasso yoniyo upaneti. Tassa yoniupagamanamūlakāni pañcavīsati mahābhayāni dvattiṃsa kammakāraṇāni ca āgatāneva hontīti evaṃ yonisampaṭipādanatopissa ukkhittāsikavadhakasadisatā veditabbā.

Regarding the passage, '“The sixth, an internal prowler, a murderer with an upraised sword,” O bhikkhus, is a designation for delight and lust': Herein, the similarity of delight and lust to a murderer with an upraised sword should be understood in two ways: from its felling of the head of wisdom and from its causing one to fall repeatedly into a womb. How? When a desirable object comes into range at the eye-door, greed arises dependent on that object. Merely by this much, the head of wisdom is said to have fallen. This same principle should be understood for the ear-door and the others. In this way, first, the similarity should be understood from its felling of the head of wisdom. Furthermore, this delight and lust leads to the four kinds of wombs, which are divided into egg-born and so on. For such a person, the twenty-five great fears and the thirty-two karmic torments that are rooted in entering a womb inevitably come to be. In this way also, its similarity to a murderer with an upraised sword should be understood from its causing one to fall repeatedly into a womb.

Iti nandīrāgavasenāpi ekassa bhikkhuno kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathitameva hoti. Kathaṃ? Ayañhi nandīrāgo saṅkhārakkhandho, taṃ saṅkhārakkhandhoti vavatthapetvā taṃsampayuttā vedanā vedanākkhandho, saññā saññākkhandho, cittaṃ viññāṇakkhandho, tesaṃ vatthārammaṇaṃ rūpakkhandhoti, evaṃ pañcakkhandhe vavatthapeti. Idāni te pañcakkhandhe nāmarūpavasena vavatthapetvā, tesaṃ paccayapariyesanato paṭṭhāya vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā, anupubbena eko arahattaṃ pāpuṇātīti evaṃ nandīrāgavasena kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathitaṃ hoti.

Thus, by way of delight and lust, the meditation subject for a single bhikkhu is indeed explained. How so? This delight and lust is the aggregate of formations. Having determined it as the aggregate of formations, the feeling associated with it is the aggregate of feeling, perception is the aggregate of perception, the mind is the aggregate of consciousness, and their physical base and object is the aggregate of form. In this way, one determines the five aggregates. Now, having determined these five aggregates by way of name-and-form, beginning from the investigation of their conditions, one develops insight and, by sequence, a certain person attains Arahantship. Thus, by way of delight and lust, the meditation subject is explained.

Channaṃ ajjhattikāyatanānaṃ suññagāmena sadisatā pāḷiyaṃyeva āgatā. Ayaṃ panettha kammaṭṭhānanayo – yathā ca te cha corā chakuṭikaṃ suññaṃ gāmaṃ pavisitvā aparāparaṃ vicarantā kiñci alabhitvā gāmena anatthikā honti, evamevaṃ bhikkhu chasu ajjhattikāyatanesu abhinivisitvā vicinanto “aha”nti vā “mama”nti vā gahetabbaṃ kiñci adisvā tehi anatthiko hoti. So “vipassanaṃ paṭṭhapessāmī”ti upādārūpakammaṭṭhānavasena cakkhupasādādayo pariggahetvā “ayaṃ rūpakkhandho”ti vavatthapeti, manāyatanaṃ “arūpakkhandho”ti. Iti sabbānipetāni nāmañceva rūpañcāti nāmarūpavasena vavatthapetvā, tesaṃ paccayaṃ pariyesitvā vipassanaṃ [V 3.0063] vaḍḍhetvā[M 3.0063], saṅkhāre sammasanto anupubbena arahatte patiṭṭhāti. Idaṃ ekassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathitaṃ hoti.

The similarity of the six internal sense bases to an empty village is found in the Pāli canon itself. Herein, this is the method of meditation: Just as those six thieves, having entered an empty village with six huts and wandering from one to another, find nothing and thus have no desire for the village, in the very same way, a bhikkhu, having applied his mind and investigating the six internal sense bases, sees nothing to be grasped as 'I' or 'mine' and thus has no desire for them. Thinking, 'I will establish insight,' by way of the meditation subject of derived matter, he comprehends eye-sensitivity and so on, and determines, 'This is the aggregate of form,' and the mind-base as 'the immaterial aggregates.' Thus, having determined all these by way of name-and-form as 'name' and 'form,' and having investigated their condition, developed insight, and contemplated the formations, he is, by sequence, established in Arahantship. This is the meditation subject for a single bhikkhu up to Arahantship that is explained.

Idāni bāhirānaṃ gāmaghātakacorehi sadisataṃ dassento corā gāmaghātakāti khotiādimāha. Tattha [P 3.0018] manāpāmanāpesūti karaṇatthe bhummaṃ, manāpāmanāpehīti attho. Tattha coresu gāmaṃ hanantesu pañca kiccāni vattanti – corā tāva gāmaṃ parivāretvā ṭhitā aggiṃ datvā kaṭakaṭasaddaṃ uṭṭhāpenti, tato manussā hatthasāraṃ gahetvā bahi nikkhamanti. Tato tehi saddhiṃ bhaṇḍakassa kāraṇā hatthaparāmāsaṃ karonti. Keci panettha pahāraṃ pāpuṇanti, keci pahāraṭṭhāne patanti, avasese pana arogajane bandhitvā attano vasanaṭṭhānaṃ netvā rajjubandhanādīhi bandhitvā dāsaparibhogena paribhuñjanti.

Now, wishing to show the similarity of the external sense bases with village-destroying thieves, the Teacher said the passage beginning, 'Thieves, village-destroyers...' Therein, regarding the phrase 'manāpāmanāpesu,' the locative case is used in the sense of the instrumental; the meaning is 'by means of the agreeable and disagreeable.' In this context, when thieves destroy a village, five actions occur: First, the thieves, having surrounded the village, stand there, set it on fire, and raise a 'kaṭakaṭa' sound. Then, the people, taking their valuable possessions in hand, exit. Then, for the sake of the goods, they engage in physical conflict with them. Some among them receive blows; some fall in the place where they are struck. As for the remaining uninjured people, the thieves bind them, lead them to their own dwelling place, tie them with ropes and the like, and make use of them as slaves.

Tattha gāmaghātakacorānaṃ gāmaṃ parivāretvā aggidānaṃ viya chasu dvāresu ārammaṇe āpāthagate kilesapariḷāhuppatti veditabbā, hatthasāraṃ ādāya bahi nikkhamanaṃ viya. Taṅkhaṇe kusaladhammaṃ pahāya akusalasamaṅgitā, bhaṇḍakassa kāraṇā hatthaparāmasanāpajjanaṃ viya dukkaṭadubbhāsitapācittiyathullaccayānaṃ āpajjanakālo, pahāraladdhakālo viya saṅghādisesaṃ āpajjanakālo, pahāraṃ laddhā pana pahāraṭṭhāne patitakālo viya pārājikaṃ āpajjitvā assamaṇakālo, avasesajanassa bandhitvā vasanaṭṭhānaṃ netvā dāsaparibhogena paribhuñjanakālo viya tameva ārammaṇaṃ nissāya sabbesaṃ passantānaṃyeva cūḷasīlamajjhimasīlamahāsīlāni bhinditvā sikkhaṃ paccakkhāya gihibhāvaṃ āpajjanakālo. Tatrassa puttadāraṃ posentassa sandiṭṭhiko dukkhakkhandho veditabbo, kālaṃ katvā apāye nibbattassa samparāyiko.

Therein, like the village-destroying thieves surrounding the village and setting it on fire, the arising of the blaze of defilements when an object comes into range at the six sense doors should be understood. Like taking one's valuable possessions and exiting, at that moment the abandoning of wholesome states and becoming endowed with unwholesome states should be understood. Like incurring physical conflict for the sake of the goods, the time of incurring offenses of wrong-doing, wrong-speech, expiation, and grave transgression should be understood. Like the time of receiving a blow, the time of incurring a Saṅghādisesa offense should be understood. Like the time of falling in the place where the blow was received, the time of becoming a non-monk after incurring a Pārājika offense should be understood. Like the time of binding the remaining people, leading them to their dwelling place, and making use of them as slaves, the time of reverting to the lay state—having broken the minor, middle, and major moral precepts in full view of everyone, renounced the training, all depending on that very sense object—should be understood. For such a one who then supports a wife and children, the mass of suffering visible in this life is to be understood; and for one who, after dying, is reborn in a state of woe, the mass of suffering in the next life is to be understood.

Imānipi bāhirāyatanāni ekassa bhikkhuno kammaṭṭhānavaseneva kathitāni. Ettha hi rūpādīni cattāri upādārūpāni, phoṭṭhabbāyatanaṃ tisso dhātuyo, dhammāyatane āpodhātuyā saddhiṃ tā catassoti imāni cattāri bhūtāni, tesaṃ paricchedavasena ākāsadhātu, lahutādivasena lahutādayoti evamidaṃ sabbampi bhūtupādāyarūpaṃ rūpakkhandho, tadārammaṇā vedanādayo cattāro arūpakkhandhā. Tattha [P 3.0019] “rūpakkhandho rūpaṃ, cattāro arūpino [M 3.0064] khandhā nāma”nti. Nāmarūpaṃ vavatthapetvā purimanayeneva paṭipajjantassa yāva arahattā kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathitaṃ hoti.

These external sense bases have also been explained solely by way of the meditation subject for a single bhikkhu. Herein, the four beginning with the form base are derived matter. The tangible base is the three elements. In the mental-object base, there is the water element; together with it, those three become four elements. Thus, these four are the primary elements. By way of delimiting them, there is the space element; by way of lightness and so on, there are the qualities of lightness and so on. Thus, all this primary and derived matter is the aggregate of form. The four aggregates beginning with feeling, which have that form as their object, are the immaterial aggregates. Therein, 'the aggregate of form is form; the four immaterial aggregates are name.' Having thus determined name-and-form, for one who practices according to the previous method, the meditation subject is explained up to Arahantship.

Oghānanti [V 3.0064] ettha duruttaraṇaṭṭho oghaṭṭho. Ete hi “sīlasaṃvaraṃ pūretvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇissāmī”ti ajjhāsayaṃ samuṭṭhāpetvā kalyāṇamitte nissāya sammā vāyamantena taritabbā, yena vā tena vā duruttarā. Iminā duruttaraṇaṭṭhena oghāti vuccanti. Tepi ekassa bhikkhuno kammaṭṭhānavasena kathitā. Cattāropi hi ete eko saṅkhārakkhandho vāti. Sesaṃ nandīrāge vuttanayeneva yojetvā vitthāretabbaṃ.

Regarding the word 'floods' (ogha), the meaning of 'flood' is 'that which is difficult to cross.' For these floods must be crossed by one who strives rightly, relying on good spiritual friends, having generated the aspiration, 'Having fulfilled moral restraint, I will attain Arahantship.' Yet, by any means whatsoever, they are difficult to cross. Because of this meaning of 'difficult to cross,' they are called 'floods.' These too are explained by way of the meditation subject for a single bhikkhu. For all four of these floods are but a single aggregate of formations. The remainder should be connected and elaborated according to the method stated in the section on delight and lust.

Sakkāyassetaṃ adhivacananti, sakkāyopi hi āsīvisādīhi udakaṇṇavassa orimatīraṃ viya catumahābhūtādīhi sāsaṅko sappaṭibhayo, sopi ekassa bhikkhuno kammaṭṭhānavaseneva kathito. Sakkāyo hi tebhūmakapañcakkhandhā, te ca samāsato nāmarūpamevāti. Evamettha nāmarūpavavatthānaṃ ādiṃ katvā yāva arahattā kammaṭṭhānaṃ vitthāretabbanti.

This is a designation for the existing body. For the existing body, like the near shore of a great body of water infested with venomous snakes and so forth, is perilous and fearful on account of the four great elements and so on. That too has been explained solely by way of the meditation subject for a single bhikkhu. For the existing body is the five aggregates belonging to the three planes of existence, and these, in brief, are merely name-and-form. Thus, herein, beginning with the determination of name-and-form, the meditation subject should be elaborated up to Arahantship.

Nibbānassetaṃ adhivacananti nibbānañhi udakaṇṇavassa pārimatīraṃ viya catumahābhūtādīhi khemaṃ appaṭibhayaṃ. Vīriyārambhassetaṃ adhivacananti ettha cittakiriyadassanatthaṃ heṭṭhā vuttavāyāmameva vīriyanti gaṇhitvā dasseti. Tiṇṇo pāraṅgatoti taritvā pāraṃ gato.

This is a designation for Nibbāna; for Nibbāna, like the far shore of a great river, is safe and free from fear of the four great elements and so on. This is a designation for the undertaking of effort; here, to show the action of the mind, the exertion spoken of below is taken as 'effort' and shown. 'Having crossed over, gone to the far shore' means having crossed over and gone to the far shore.

Tattha yathā sāsaṅkaorimatīre ṭhitena udakaṇṇavaṃ taritukāmena katipāhaṃ vasitvā saṇikaṃ nāvaṃ sajjetvā udakakīḷaṃ kīḷantena viya na nāvā abhiruhitabbā. Evaṃ karonto hi anāruḷhova byasanaṃ pāpuṇāti. Evameva kilesaṇṇavaṃ taritukāmena “taruṇo tāvamhi, mahallakakāle aṭṭhaṅgikamaggakullaṃ bandhissāmī”ti papañco na kātabbo[P 3.0020]. Evaṃ karonto hi mahallakakālaṃ apatvāpi vināsaṃ pāpuṇāti, patvāpi kātuṃ na sakkoti. Bhaddekarattādīni pana anussaritvā vegeneva ayaṃ ariyamaggakullo bandhitabbo.

Therein, just as one standing on the dangerous near shore, wishing to cross the great river, should not, after dwelling for some days and slowly preparing a boat, board it as if playing a water-game. For one who does so meets with disaster even before boarding. In the very same way, one wishing to cross the great river of defilements should not procrastinate, thinking, 'I am still young; in old age I will build the raft of the Noble Eightfold Path.' For one who does so meets with destruction even without reaching old age, and even if they reach it, they are unable to do it. But, having recollected the Bhaddekaratta Sutta and others, this raft of the Noble Path should be built with speed.

Yathā ca kullaṃ bandhantassa hatthapādapāripūri icchitabbā. Kuṇṭhapādo hi khañjapādo vā patiṭṭhātuṃ na sakkoti, phaṇahatthakādayo tiṇapaṇṇādīni gahetuṃ na sakkonti. Evamimampi ariyamaggakullaṃ bandhantassa sīlapādānañceva saddhāhatthassa [M 3.0065] ca pāripūri icchitabbā. Na hi dussīlo assaddho sāsane appatiṭṭhito paṭipattiṃ assaddahanto ariyamaggakullaṃ bandhituṃ sakkoti. Yathā ca paripuṇṇahatthapādopi dubbalo byādhipīḷito kullaṃ bandhituṃ na sakkoti, thāmasampannova sakkoti, evaṃ sīlavā saddhopi alaso kusīto imaṃ maggakullaṃ bandhituṃ na sakkoti[V 3.0065], āraddhavīriyova sakkotīti imaṃ bandhitukāmena āraddhavīriyena bhavitabbaṃ. Yathā so puriso kullaṃ bandhitvā tīre ṭhatvā yojanavitthāraṃ udakaṇṇavaṃ “ayaṃ mayā paccattapurisakāraṃ nissāya nittharitabbo”ti mānasaṃ bandhati, evaṃ yogināpi caṅkamā oruyha “ajja mayā catumaggavajjhaṃ kilesaṇṇavaṃ taritvā arahatte patiṭṭhātabba”nti mānasaṃ bandhitabbaṃ.

And just as one building a raft should wish for the completeness of hands and feet. For one with a maimed or lame foot cannot stand firm, and those with useless hands and the like cannot grasp grass, leaves, and so on. So too, one building this raft of the Noble Path should wish for the completeness of the feet of virtue and the hand of faith. For an immoral, faithless person, unestablished in the Dispensation and not having faith in the practice, cannot build the raft of the Noble Path. And just as one with complete hands and feet who is weak and afflicted by illness cannot build a raft—only one endowed with strength can—so too, a person who is virtuous and faithful but lazy and indolent cannot build this raft of the Path; only one with aroused effort can. Therefore, one wishing to build it must be one with aroused effort. Just as that person, having built the raft and standing on the shore, resolves in his mind, 'This great river, a yojana wide, must be crossed by me by relying on my own individual effort,' so too the yogi, having descended from his walking meditation path, should resolve in his mind, 'Today, having crossed the great river of defilements that are to be destroyed by the four paths, I must establish myself in Arahantship.'

Yathā ca so puriso kullaṃ nissāya udakaṇṇavaṃ taranto gāvutamattaṃ gantvā nivattitvā olokento “ekakoṭṭhāsaṃ atikkantomhi, aññe tayo sesā”ti jānāti, aparampi gāvutamattaṃ gantvā nivattitvā olokento “dve atikkantomhi, dve sesā”ti jānāti, aparampi gāvutamattaṃ gantvā nivattitvā olokento “tayo atikkantomhi, eko seso”ti jānāti, tampi atikkamma nivattitvā olokento “cattāropi me koṭṭhasā atikkantā”ti jānāti, tañca kullaṃ pādena akkamitvā sotābhimukhaṃ khipitvā uttaritvā tīre tiṭṭhati. Evaṃ ayampi bhikkhu ariyamaggakullaṃ nissāya kilesaṇṇavaṃ taranto sotāpattimaggena paṭhamamaggavajjhe kilese taritvā maggānantare phale ṭhito paccavekkhaṇañāṇena nivattitvā olokento “catumaggavajjhānaṃ me kilesānaṃ eko koṭṭhāso pahīno[P 3.0021], itare tayo sesā”ti jānāti. Puna tatheva indriyabalabojjhaṅgāni samodhānetvā saṅkhāre sammasanto sakadāgāmimaggena dutiyamaggavajjhe kilese taritvā maggānantare phale ṭhito paccavekkhaṇañāṇena nivattitvā, olokento “catumaggavajjhānaṃ me kilesānaṃ dve koṭṭhāsā pahīnā[M 3.0066], itare dve sesā”ti jānāti. Puna tatheva indriyabalabojjhaṅgāni samodhānetvā saṅkhāre sammasanto anāgāmimaggena tatiyamaggavajjhe kilese taritvā maggānantare phale ṭhito paccavekkhaṇañāṇena nivattitvā olokento “catumaggavajjhānaṃ me kilesānaṃ tayo koṭṭhāsā pahīnā, eko seso”ti jānāti. Puna tatheva indriyabalabojjhaṅgāni samodhānetvā saṅkhāre sammasanto arahattamaggena catutthamaggavajjhe kilese taritvā maggānantare phale ṭhito paccavekkhaṇañāṇena nivattitvā olokento “sabbakilesā me pahīnā”ti jānāti.

And just as that man, relying on a raft to cross the great river, having gone a distance of one gāvuta, turns back and looks, and knows, 'I have crossed one part; three others remain'; and having gone another gāvuta, turns back and looks, and knows, 'I have crossed two parts; two remain'; and having gone yet another gāvuta, turns back and looks, and knows, 'I have crossed three parts; one remains'; and having crossed that too, turns back and looks, and knows, 'All four parts have been crossed by me'; and then, pressing the raft with his foot, he casts it to face the current, gets out, and stands on the shore. In the very same way, this bhikkhu, relying on the raft of the Noble Path to cross the great river of defilements, having with the path of stream-entry crossed the defilements to be destroyed by the first path, stands in the fruition immediately following the path. With the knowledge of review, he turns back and looks, and knows, 'Of the defilements to be destroyed by the four paths, one part has been abandoned by me; the other three remain.' Again, in the same way, having harmonized the faculties, powers, and enlightenment factors, and contemplating the formations, he, with the path of the once-returner, crosses the defilements to be destroyed by the second path and stands in the fruition immediately following the path. With the knowledge of review, he turns back and looks, and knows, 'Of the defilements to be destroyed by the four paths, two parts have been abandoned by me; the other two remain.' Again, in the same way, having harmonized the faculties, powers, and enlightenment factors, and contemplating the formations, he, with the path of the non-returner, crosses the defilements to be destroyed by the third path and stands in the fruition immediately following the path. With the knowledge of review, he turns back and looks, and knows, 'Of the defilements to be destroyed by the four paths, three parts have been abandoned by me; one remains.' Again, in the same way, having harmonized the faculties, powers, and enlightenment factors, and contemplating the formations, he, with the path of Arahantship, crosses the defilements to be destroyed by the fourth path and stands in the fruition immediately following the path. With the knowledge of review, he turns back and looks, and knows, 'All my defilements have been abandoned.'

Yathā so puriso taṃ kullaṃ sote pavāhetvā uttaritvā thale ṭhito nagaraṃ pavisitvā uparipāsādavaragato “ettakena vatamhi anatthena mutto”ti ekaggacitto tuṭṭhamānaso nisīdati, evaṃ tasmiṃyeva vā āsane aññesu vā rattiṭṭhānadivāṭṭhānādīsu yattha katthaci nisinno [V 3.0066] “ettakena vatamhi anatthena mutto”ti nibbānārammaṇaṃ phalasamāpattiṃ appetvā ekaggacitto tuṭṭhamānaso nisīdati. Idaṃ vā sandhāya vuttaṃ tiṇṇo pāraṅgato thale tiṭṭhati brāhmaṇoti kho, bhikkhave, arahato etaṃ adhivacananti. Evaṃ tāvettha nānākammaṭṭhānāni kathitāni, samodhānetvā pana sabbānipi ekameva katvā dassetabbāni. Ekaṃ katvā dassentenāpi pañcakkhandhavaseneva vinivattetabbāni.

Just as that man, having let the raft float away in the current, crossed over, stood on dry land, entered the city, ascended to a fine palace, and sat down with a concentrated mind and a delighted heart, thinking, 'Indeed, I am freed from such great misfortune,' so too, whether in that very seat or in other places for staying at night or during the day, wherever one sits, having entered upon the fruition attainment which has Nibbāna as its object, thinking, 'Indeed, I am freed from such great misfortune,' one sits with a concentrated mind and a delighted heart. It was with reference to this that it was said: 'Bhikkhus, “Having crossed over, gone to the far shore, he stands on dry land, a brahmin”—this is a designation for an Arahant.' Thus, various meditation subjects have been taught here; but, having brought them all together, they should be shown as just one. And even by one showing them as one, they should be resolved by way of the five aggregates.

Kathaṃ? Ettha hi cattāri mahābhūtāni ajjhattikāni pañcāyatanāni bāhirāni pañcāyatanāni dhammāyatane pannarasa sukhumarūpāni sakkāyassa ekadesoti ayaṃ rūpakkhandho, manāyatanaṃ viññāṇakkhandho dhammāyatanekadeso cattāro oghā sakkāyekadesoti ime cattāro arūpino khandhā. Tattha rūpakkhandho rūpaṃ, cattāro arūpino khandhā nāmanti idaṃ nāmarūpaṃ. Tassa nandīrāgo kāmogho bhavogho dhammāyatanekadeso sakkāyekadesoti [P 3.0022] ime paccayā. Iti sappaccayaṃ nāmarūpaṃ vavatthapeti nāma. Sappaccayaṃ nāmarūpaṃ vavatthapetvā tilakkhaṇaṃ āropetvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā saṅkhāre sammasanto arahattaṃ pāpuṇātīti idaṃ ekassa bhikkhuno niyyānamukhaṃ.

How? Herein, the four great elements, the five internal sense bases, the five external sense bases, the fifteen subtle forms within the dhamma-base, and a part of the existing body—this is the form aggregate. The mind-base is the consciousness aggregate. A part of the dhamma-base, the four floods, and a part of the existing body—these are the four immaterial aggregates. Therein, the form aggregate is form, and the four immaterial aggregates are name; this is name-and-form. Its conditions are these: delight and lust, the flood of sensuality, the flood of existence, a part of the dhamma-base, and a part of the existing body. Thus is name-and-form with its conditions defined. Having defined name-and-form with its conditions, having applied the three characteristics, having developed insight, and contemplating the formations, one attains Arahantship. This is the means of escape for one bhikkhu.

Tattha cattāro mahābhūtā pañcupādānakkhandhā ajjhattikabāhirāni ekādasāyatanāni dhammāyatanekadeso diṭṭhogho avijjogho sakkāyekadesoti idaṃ dukkhasaccaṃ, nandīrāgo dhammāyatanekadeso kāmogho bhavogho sakkāyekadesoti idaṃ samudayasaccaṃ, pārimatīrasaṅkhātaṃ [M 3.0067] nibbānaṃ nirodhasaccaṃ, ariyamaggo maggasaccaṃ. Tattha dve saccāni vaṭṭaṃ, dve vivaṭṭaṃ, dve lokiyāni, dve lokuttarānīti cattāri saccāni soḷasahākārehi saṭṭhinayasahassehi vibhajitvā dassetabbānīti. Desanāpariyosāne vipañcitaññū pañcasatā bhikkhū arahatte patiṭṭhahiṃsu. Suttaṃ pana dukkhalakkhaṇavasena kathitaṃ.

Herein, the four great elements, the five aggregates of clinging, the eleven internal and external sense bases, a portion of the dhamma-base, the flood of views, the flood of ignorance, and a portion of the existing body—this is the truth of suffering. Delight and lust, a portion of the dhamma-base, the flood of sensuality, the flood of existence, and a portion of the existing body—this is the truth of the origin. Nibbāna, known as the further shore, is the truth of cessation. The noble path is the truth of the path. Herein, two truths are of the round of existence, two are of the escape from the round; two are mundane, two are supramundane. Thus, the four truths should be taught by analyzing them with sixteen aspects and sixty thousand methods. At the end of the discourse, five hundred bhikkhus of penetrative understanding were established in Arahantship. The Sutta, however, was taught by way of the characteristic of suffering.

2. Rathopamasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Sutta of the Chariot Simile.

239. Dutiye sukhasomanassabahuloti kāyikasukhañceva cetasikasomanassañca bahulaṃ assāti sukhasomanassabahulo. Yoni cassa āraddhā hotīti kāraṇañcassa paripuṇṇaṃ hoti. Āsavānaṃ khayāyāti idha āsavakkhayoti arahattamaggo adhippeto, tadatthāyāti attho. Odhastapatodoti [V 3.0067] rathamajjhe tiriyaṃ ṭhapitapatodo. Yenicchakanti yena disābhāgena icchati. Yadicchakanti yaṃ yaṃ gamanaṃ icchati. Sāreyyāti peseyya. Paccāsāreyyāti paṭivinivatteyya. Ārakkhāyāti rakkhaṇatthāya. Saṃyamāyāti veganiggahaṇatthāya. Damāyāti nibbisevanatthāya. Upasamāyāti kilesūpasamatthāya.

239. In the second sutta, 'abundant in pleasure and joy' means that for him bodily pleasure and mental joy are abundant; thus, he is abundant in pleasure and joy. 'And his foundation is well established' means his cause is complete. 'For the destruction of the taints': here, 'destruction of the taints' is intended to mean the path of Arahantship; 'for the sake of that' is the meaning. 'With goad at rest' means a goad placed crosswise in the middle of the chariot. 'As he wishes' means by whichever direction he wishes. 'Whatever he wishes' means whatever movement he wishes. 'He would drive' means he would send forth. 'He would draw back' means he would turn back. 'For protection' means for the sake of guarding. 'For restraint' means for the sake of suppressing momentum. 'For taming' means for the sake of non-indulgence. 'For calming' means for the sake of pacifying the defilements.

Evameva khoti ettha yathā akusalassa sārathino adante sindhave yojetvā visamamaggena rathaṃ pesentassa cakkānipi bhijjanti, akkhopi sindhavānañca khurā, attanāpi anayabyasanaṃ pāpuṇāti, na ca icchiticchitena gamanena sāretuṃ sakkoti[P 3.0023]; evaṃ chasu indriyesu aguttadvāro bhikkhu na icchiticchitaṃ samaṇaratiṃ anubhavituṃ sakkoti. Yathā pana cheko sārathi dante sindhave yojetvā, same bhūmibhāge rathaṃ otāretvā rasmiyo gahetvā, sindhavānaṃ khuresu satiṃ ṭhapetvā, patodaṃ ādāya nibbisevane katvā, pesento icchiticchitena gamanena sāreti. Evameva chasu indriyesu guttadvāro bhikkhu imasmiṃ sāsane icchiticchitaṃ samaṇaratiṃ anubhoti, sace aniccānupassanābhimukhaṃ ñāṇaṃ pesetukāmo hoti, tadabhimukhaṃ ñāṇaṃ gacchati. Dukkhānupassanādīsupi eseva nayo.

Just so, herein, just as when an unskilled charioteer yokes untamed horses and drives the chariot along an uneven path, the wheels break, the axle breaks, and the horses' hooves break, and he himself meets with ruin and disaster, and he cannot drive as he wishes; in the same way, a bhikkhu with unguarded doors in his six senses cannot experience the recluse's delight as he wishes. But just as a skilled charioteer, having yoked well-trained horses, having brought the chariot onto level ground, having taken the reins, having established mindfulness on the horses' hooves, having taken the goad, and having made them non-indulgent, drives as he wishes; in the same way, a bhikkhu with guarded doors in his six senses experiences the recluse's delight in this Dispensation as he wishes. If he wishes to direct his knowledge towards the contemplation of impermanence, his knowledge goes in that direction. The same method applies to the contemplation of suffering and so on.

Bhojane mattaññūti bhojanamhi pamāṇaññū. Tattha dve pamāṇāni – paṭiggahaṇapamāṇañca paribhogapamāṇañca. Tattha paṭiggahaṇapamāṇe dāyakassa vaso [M 3.0068] veditabbo, deyyadhammassa vaso veditabbo, attano thāmo jānitabbo. Evarūpo hi bhikkhu sace deyyadhammo bahuko hoti, dāyako appaṃ dātukāmo, dāyakassa vasena appaṃ gaṇhāti. Deyyadhammo appo, dāyako bahuṃ dātukāmo, deyyadhammassa vasena appaṃ gaṇhāti. Deyyadhammopi bahu, dāyakopi bahuṃ dātukāmo, attano thāmaṃ ñatvā pamāṇena gaṇhāti. So tāya paṭiggahaṇe mattaññutāya anuppannañca lābhaṃ uppādeti, uppannañca thāvaraṃ karoti dhammikatissamahārājakāle sattavassiko sāmaṇero viya.

‘Knowing moderation in food’ means knowing the proper measure in food. Herein there are two measures: the measure in receiving and the measure in consuming. Regarding the measure in receiving, one should know the donor's wish, one should know the amount of the offering, and one's own capacity should be known. For a bhikkhu of this kind, if the offering is plentiful but the donor wishes to give little, he takes little in accordance with the donor's wish. If the offering is meager but the donor wishes to give much, he takes little in accordance with the amount of the offering. If the offering is plentiful and the donor also wishes to give much, knowing his own capacity, he takes a measured amount. Through such knowledge of moderation in receiving, he generates gains that have not yet arisen and makes stable those that have arisen, like the seven-year-old novice in the time of the righteous Great King Tissa.

Rañño kira pañcahi sakaṭasatehi guḷaṃ āhariṃsu. Rājā “manāpo paṇṇākāro, ayyehi vinā na khādissāmā”ti aḍḍhateyyāni sakaṭasatāni mahāvihāraṃ pesetvā sayampi bhuttapātarāso agamāsi. Bheriyā pahaṭāya dvādasa bhikkhusahassāni sannipatiṃsu. Rājā ekamante ṭhito ārāmikaṃ pakkosāpetvā āha – “rañño nāma dāne pattapūrova pamāṇaṃ, gahitabhājanaṃ [V 3.0068] pūretvāva dehi, sace koci mattapaṭiggahaṇe ṭhito na gaṇhāti, mayhaṃ āroceyyāsī”ti.

It is said that five hundred cartloads of molasses were brought for the king. The king thought, 'This is a pleasing gift; I will not eat it without the venerable ones.' Having sent two hundred and fifty cartloads to the Mahāvihāra, he himself went there after his morning meal. When the drum was struck, twelve thousand bhikkhus assembled. The king, standing to one side, had a monastery attendant summoned and said: 'For a donation from one called a king, a full bowl is the measure. You must fill the bowl that is taken and only then give. If anyone, standing for a measured reception, does not accept a full bowl, you must inform me.'

Atheko mahāthero “mahābodhimahācetiyāni vandissāmī”ti cetiyapabbatā āgantvā, vihāraṃ pavisanto mahāmaṇḍapaṭṭhāne [P 3.0024] bhikkhū guḷaṃ gaṇhante disvā pacchato āgacchantaṃ sāmaṇeraṃ āha, “natthi te guḷena attho”ti. “Āma, bhante, natthī”ti. Sāmaṇera mayaṃ maggakilantā, ekena kapiṭṭhaphalamattena piṇḍakena amhākaṃ atthoti. Sāmaṇero thālakaṃ nīharitvā therassa vassaggapaṭipāṭiyaṃ aṭṭhāsi. Ārāmiko gahaṇamānaṃ pūretvā ukkhipi, sāmaṇero aṅguliṃ cālesi. Tāta sāmaṇera, rājakulānaṃ dāne bhājanapūrameva pamāṇaṃ, thālakapūraṃ gaṇhāhīti. Āma, upāsaka, rājāno nāma mahajjhāsayā honti, amhākaṃ pana upajjhāyassa ettakeneva atthoti.

Then, a certain great elder, thinking, “I will pay homage to the Great Bodhi Tree and the Great Cetiya,” came from Cetiyapabbata. As he was entering the monastery, he saw monks receiving jaggery in the great pavilion. Seeing a novice coming from behind, he said, “Do you have no need for jaggery?” “Yes, venerable sir, I have no need.” The elder then said, “Novice, we are weary from the journey. We have need of a single lump of jaggery the size of a wood-apple fruit.” The novice took out a bowl and stood in the line according to the elder's seniority. The monastery attendant filled the receiving bowl and lifted it, but the novice moved his finger. The attendant said, “Dear novice, in the donations of royal families, a full bowl is the measure. Take a full bowl.” The novice replied, “Yes, lay devotee, kings are indeed of great aspiration, but our preceptor has need of only this much.”

Rājā tassa kathaṃ sutvā, “kiṃ bho sāmaṇero bhaṇatī”ti? Tassa santikaṃ gato. Ārāmiko āha – “sāmi, sāmaṇerassa bhājanaṃ khuddakaṃ, bahuṃ na gaṇhātī”ti. Rājā āha, “ānītabhājanaṃ pūretvā gaṇhatha, bhante”ti. Mahārāja, rājāno nāma mahajjhāsayā honti[M 3.0069], ukkhittabhājanaṃ pūretvāva dātukāmā, amhākaṃ pana upajjhāyassa ettakeneva atthoti. Rājā cintesi – “ayaṃ sattavassikadārako, ajjāpissa mukhato khīragandho na muccati, gahetvā kuṭe vā kuṭumbe vā pūretvā svepi punadivasepi khādissāmāti na vadati, sakkā buddhasāsanaṃ pariggahetu”nti purise āṇāpesi, “bho, pasannomhi sāmaṇerassa, itarānipi aḍḍhateyyāni sakaṭasatāni ānetvā saghaṃssa dethā”ti.

Hearing his words, the king went to him and asked, “Sirs, what is the novice saying?” The monastery attendant said, “Lord, the novice's bowl is small; he does not take much.” The king said, “Venerable sir, please take it, having filled the bowl you brought.” The novice replied, “Great King, kings are indeed of great aspiration and wish to give only by filling the lifted bowl. However, our preceptor has need of only this much.” The king thought: “This is a seven-year-old boy, and even now the scent of milk has not left his mouth. He does not say, ‘I will take this and fill small pots or large bins and eat tomorrow and the day after.’ He is capable of upholding the Buddha's Dispensation.” He then commanded his men, “Sirs, I am pleased with this novice. Bring the other two hundred and fifty cartloads and give them to the Sangha.”

Soyeva pana rājā ekadivasaṃ tittiramaṃsaṃ khāditukāmo cintesi – “sace ahaṃ aṅgārapakkaṃ tittiramaṃsaṃ khāditukāmosmīti aññassa kathessāmi, samantā yojanaṭṭhāne tittirasamugghātaṃ karissantī”ti uppannaṃ pipāsaṃ adhivāsento tīṇi saṃvaccharāni vītināmesi. Athassa kaṇṇesu pubbo saṇṭhāsi, so adhivāsetuṃ asakkonto “atthi nu kho, bho, amhākaṃ koci upaṭṭhākupāsako sīlarakkhako”ti pucchi[P 3.0025]. Āma, deva, atthi, tisso nāma so akhaṇḍasīlaṃ rakkhatīti. Atha naṃ vīmaṃsitukāmo pakkosāpesi. So āgantvā rājānaṃ vanditvā aṭṭhāsi. Tato naṃ āha – “tvaṃ, tāta, tisso nāmā”ti? “Āma [V 3.0069] devā”ti. Tena hi gacchāti. Tasmiṃ gate ekaṃ kukkuṭaṃ āharāpetvā ekaṃ purisaṃ āṇāpesi, “gaccha tissaṃ vadāhi, imaṃ tīhi pākehi pacitvā amhākaṃ upaṭṭhāpehī”ti. So gantvā tathā avoca. So āha – “sace, bho, ayaṃ matako assa, yathā jānāmi, tathā pacitvā upaṭṭhaheyyaṃ. Pāṇātipātaṃ panāhaṃ na karomī”ti. So āgantvā rañño ārocesi.

That very king, one day desiring to eat partridge meat, thought: “If I tell another that I desire to eat partridge meat cooked on embers, they will cause a slaughter of partridges in the area of a yojana all around.” Enduring the craving that had arisen, he let three years pass. Then pus formed in his ears. Unable to endure it, he asked, “Sirs, is there for us any lay attendant who protects his virtue?” They replied, “Yes, Your Majesty, there is. He is named Tissa, and he protects his virtue unbrokenly.” Then, desiring to test him, the king had him summoned. He came, paid homage to the king, and stood there. Then the king asked him, “Dear boy, are you the one named Tissa?” When he replied, “Yes, Your Majesty,” the king said, “In that case, go.” When he had gone, the king had a chicken brought and commanded a man, “Go, tell Tissa, ‘Cook this in three ways and serve it to us.’” He went and said so. Tissa replied, “Sir, if this were dead, I would cook and serve it according to my knowledge. But I do not commit the destruction of life.” The man returned and reported this to the king.

Rājā puna “ekavāraṃ gacchā”ti pesesi. So gantvā, “bho, rājupaṭṭhānaṃ nāma bhāriyaṃ, mā evaṃ kari, punapi sīlaṃ sakkā samādātuṃ, paceta”nti āha. Atha naṃ tisso avoca, “bho, ekasmiṃ nāma attabhāve dhuvaṃ ekaṃ maraṇaṃ, nāhaṃ pāṇātipātaṃ karissāmī”ti. So punapi rañño ārocesi. Rājā tatiyampi pesetvā asampaṭicchantaṃ pakkosāpetvā attanā pucchi. Raññopi tatheva paṭivacanaṃ adāsi. Atha rājā purise āṇāpesi, “ayaṃ rañño āṇaṃ kopeti, gacchathetassa āghātanabhaṇḍikāyaṃ ṭhapetvā, sīsaṃ chindathā”ti. Raho [M 3.0070] ca pana nesaṃ saññamadāsi – “imaṃ santajjayamānā netvā sīsamassa āghātanabhaṇḍikāyaṃ ṭhapetvā āgantvā mayhaṃ ārocethā”ti.

The king sent him again, saying, “Go one more time.” He went and said, “Sir, serving the king is a heavy burden. Do not act thus. It is possible to undertake the precepts again. Cook this.” Then Tissa said to him, “Sir, in one existence, one death is certain. I will not commit the destruction of life.” He again reported this to the king. The king sent for him a third time, and when Tissa did not consent, he had him summoned and questioned him personally. To the king as well, he gave the same reply. Then the king commanded his men, “This one violates the king's command. Go, place him on the execution block and cut off his head.” But in private, he gave them a sign: “Take him away while threatening him, place his head on the execution block, and then come and report to me.”

Te taṃ āghātanabhaṇḍikāyaṃ nipajjāpetvā tamassa kukkuṭaṃ hatthesu ṭhapayiṃsu. So taṃ hadaye ṭhapetvā “ahaṃ, tāta, mama jīvitaṃ tuyhaṃ demi, tava jīvitaṃ ahaṃ gaṇhāmi, tvaṃ nibbhayo gacchā”ti vissajjesi. Kukkuṭo pakkhe papphoṭetvā ākāsena gantvā vaṭarukkhe nilīyi. Tassa kukkuṭassa abhayadinnaṭṭhānaṃ kukkuṭagiri nāma jātaṃ.

They made him lie down on the execution block and placed that chicken in his hands. He placed it on his chest and said, “Dear one, I give my life to you; I take your life. You go without fear,” and released it. The chicken flapped its wings, went through the sky, and perched on a banyan tree. The place where safety was given to that chicken became known as Kukkuṭagiri.

Rājā taṃ pavattiṃ sutvā amaccaputtaṃ pakkosāpetvā sabbābharaṇehi alaṅkaritvā āha – “tāta, mayā tvaṃ etadatthameva vīmaṃsito, mayhaṃ tittiramaṃsaṃ khāditukāmassa tīṇi saṃvaccharāni atikkantāni, sakkhissasi me tikoṭiparisuddhaṃ katvā upaṭṭhāpetu”nti. “Etaṃ nāma, deva, mayhaṃ kamma”nti nikkhamitvā dvārantare ṭhito ekaṃ purisaṃ pātova tayo tittire gahetvā pavisantaṃ [P 3.0026] disvā, dve kahāpaṇe datvā tittire ādāya parisodhetvā, jīrakādīhi vāsetvā, aṅgāresu supakke pacitvā rañño upaṭṭhāpesi. Rājā mahātale sirīpallaṅke nisinnova ekaṃ gahetvā thokaṃ chinditvā mukhe pakkhipi, tāvadevassa sattarasaharaṇīsahassāni pharitvā aṭṭhāsi.

Hearing this account, the king summoned the minister's son, had him adorned with all ornaments, and said: “Dear son, I tested you for this very reason. For me, who has desired to eat partridge meat, three years have passed. Will you be able to prepare it for me, making it pure in three aspects?” “Your Majesty, this is indeed my duty,” he replied. Going out, he stood in a doorway and saw a man entering early in the morning carrying three partridges he had caught. He gave the man two kahāpaṇas, took the partridges, cleaned them, seasoned them with cumin and other spices, cooked them perfectly on embers, and served them to the king. The king was on the grand terrace. While still seated on his royal couch, he took one, cut off a small piece, and placed it in his mouth. At that very moment, the taste spread through and established itself in his seven thousand taste-bearing nerves.

Tasmiṃ [V 3.0070] samaye bhikkhusaṅghaṃ saritvā, “mādiso nāma pathavissaro rājā tittiramaṃsaṃ khāditukāmo tīṇi saṃvaccharāni na labhi, apaccamāno bhikkhusaṅgho kuto labhissatī”ti? Mukhe pakkhittakkhaṇḍaṃ bhūmiyaṃ chaḍḍesi. Amaccaputto jaṇṇukehi patitvā mukhena gaṇhi. Rājā “apehi, tāta, jānāmahaṃ tava niddosabhāvaṃ, iminā nāma kāraṇena mayā etaṃ chaḍḍita”nti kathetvā, “sesakaṃ tatheva saṅgopetvā ṭhapehī”ti āha.

At that moment, remembering the community of monks, he thought, “A king like me, a lord of the earth, being desirous of eating partridge meat, did not obtain it for three years. How then will the community of monks, who do not cook for themselves, obtain it?” He discarded the piece he had placed in his mouth onto the ground. The minister’s son fell on his knees and caught it with his mouth. The king said, “Move away, dear one. I know your blamelessness. It was for this very reason that I discarded it.” Having said this, he added, “Have the remainder well-guarded and kept.”

Punadivase rājakulūpako thero piṇḍāya pāvisi. Amaccaputto taṃ disvā pattaṃ gahetvā rājagehaṃ pavesesi. Aññataro vuḍḍhapabbajitopi therassa pacchāsamaṇo viya hutvā anubandhanto pāvisi. Thero “raññā pakkosāpitabhikkhu bhavissatī”ti pamajji. Amaccaputtopi “therassa upaṭṭhāko bhavissatī”ti pamādaṃ āpajji. Tesaṃ nisīdāpetvā yāguṃ adaṃsu. Yāguyā pītāya rājā tittire upanesi. Thero ekaṃ gaṇhi, itaropi [M 3.0071] ekaṃ gaṇhi. Rājā “anubhāgo atthi, anāpucchitvā khādituṃ na yutta”nti mahātheraṃ āpucchi. Thero hatthaṃ pidahi, mahallakatthero sampaṭicchi. Rājā anattamano hutvā katabhattakiccaṃ theraṃ pattaṃ ādāya anugacchanto āha – “bhante, kulagehaṃ āgacchantehi uggahitavattaṃ bhikkhuṃ gahetvā āgantuṃ vaṭṭatī”ti. Thero tasmiṃ khaṇe aññāsi “na esa raññā pakkosāpito”ti.

On the next day, the elder who was a frequenter of the royal family entered for alms. A certain one who had gone forth in old age also entered, following behind as if he were the elder's attendant monk. The elder became heedless, thinking, “He must be a monk summoned by the king.” Having had them seated, they offered gruel. When the gruel was drunk, the king offered the partridges. The elder took one; the other also took one. The king asked the great elder, “There is an additional portion; it is not proper to eat without asking.” The elder covered his hand; the old monk accepted. The king, being displeased, after the elder had finished his meal, took his bowl and, while following him, said: “Venerable sir, when coming to a family's house, it is proper to bring a monk who has learned the duties.” At that moment, the elder realized, “This one was not summoned by the king.”

Punadivase upaṭṭhākasāmaṇeraṃ gahetvā pāvisi. Rājā tadāpi yāguyā pītāya tittire upanāmesi. Thero ekaṃ aggahesi, sāmaṇero aṅguliṃ cāletvā majjhe chindāpetvā ekakoṭṭhāsameva aggahesi. Rājā taṃ koṭṭhāsaṃ mahātherassa upanāmesi. Mahāthero hatthaṃ pidahi, sāmaṇeropi pidahi. Rājā avidūre nisīditvā khaṇḍākhaṇḍaṃ chinditvā khādanto “uggahitavatte [P 3.0027] nissāya diyaḍḍhatittire khādituṃ labhimhā”ti āha. Tassa maṃse khāditamatteva kaṇṇehi pubbo nikkhami. Tato mukhaṃ vikkhāletvā sāmaṇeraṃ upasaṅkamitvā, “pasannosmi, tāta, aṭṭha te dhuvabhattāni demī”ti āha. Ahaṃ, mahārāja, upajjhāyassa dammīti. Aparāni aṭṭha demīti. Tāni amhākaṃ ācariyassa dammīti. Aparānipi aṭṭha demīti. Tāni samānupajjhāyānaṃ dammīti. Aparānipi aṭṭha demīti. Tāni bhikkhusaṅghassa dammīti. Aparānipi aṭṭha demīti. Sāmaṇero adhivāsesi. Evaṃ paṭiggahaṇamattaṃ jānanto anuppannañceva lābhaṃ uppādeti, uppannañca thāvaraṃ karoti. Idaṃ paṭiggahaṇapamāṇaṃ nāma. Tattha paribhogapamāṇaṃ paccavekkhaṇapayojanaṃ, “idamatthiyaṃ bhojanaṃ bhuñjāmī”ti pana paccavekkhitaparibhogasseva [V 3.0071] payojanattā paribhogapamāṇaṃyeva nāma, taṃ idha adhippetaṃ. Teneva paṭisaṅkhā yonisotiādimāha, itarampi pana vaṭṭatiyeva.

On the next day, he entered, taking an attendant novice. Then also, when the gruel was drunk, the king offered the partridges. The elder accepted one; the novice, moving his finger, had it broken in the middle and accepted only one portion. The king offered that portion to the great elder. The great elder covered his hand; the novice also covered his. The king, sitting nearby, cutting it piece by piece and eating, said, “Relying on those who have learned the duties, we have gotten to eat one and a half partridges.” Just as he finished eating the meat, pus came out from his ears. Then, having rinsed his mouth, he approached the novice and said, “I am pleased, dear one. I give you eight permanent food allowances.” The novice replied, “Great king, I give them to my preceptor.” The king said, “I give another eight.” The novice said, “Those I give to our teacher.” The king said, “I give yet another eight.” The novice said, “Those I give to my fellow preceptees.” The king said, “I give still another eight.” The novice said, “Those I give to the community of monks.” The king said, “I give yet another eight.” The novice consented. Thus, one who knows the measure of acceptance both causes unarisen gain to arise and makes arisen gain stable. This is called the measure of acceptance. Among these, the measure of consumption has its purpose in reflection. Furthermore, because the benefit lies only in consuming after reflection, thus: ‘I eat this food for this purpose,’ this is called the measure of consumption, and that is what is intended here. For that very reason, it is said, ‘Wisely reflecting...’ and so on. However, knowing the other is also proper.

Sīhaseyyanti ettha kāmabhogiseyyā, petaseyyā, sīhaseyyā, tathāgataseyyāti catasso seyyā. Tattha “yebhuyyena, bhikkhave, kāmabhogī vāmena passena sentī”ti (a. ni. 4.246) ayaṃ kāmabhogiseyyā. Tesañhi yebhuyyena dakkhiṇapassena sayāno nāma natthi.

Regarding 'the lion's posture': here there are four postures: the posture of one who enjoys sensual pleasures, the posture of a peta, the lion's posture, and the Tathāgata's posture. Among these, “For the most part, O monks, one who enjoys sensual pleasures lies on the left side”—this is the posture of one who enjoys sensual pleasures. Indeed, among them, there is for the most part no one who lies on the right side.

“Yebhuyyena[M 3.0072], bhikkhave, petā uttānā sentī”ti (a. ni. 4.246) ayaṃ petaseyyā. Petā hi appamaṃsalohitattā aṭṭhisaṅghāṭajaṭitā ekena passena sayituṃ na sakkonti, uttānāva sayanti.

“For the most part, O monks, petas lie on their backs”—this is the posture of a peta. Indeed, because they have little flesh and blood and are entangled in a framework of bones, petas are not able to lie on one side; they lie only on their backs.

“Yebhuyyena, bhikkhave, sīho migarājā naṅguṭṭhaṃ antarasatthimhi anupakkhipitvā dakkhiṇena passena sayatī”ti ayaṃ sīhaseyyā. Tejussadattā hi sīho migarājā dve purimapāde ekasmiṃ, pacchimapāde ekasmiṃ ṭhāne ṭhapetvā naṅguṭṭhaṃ antarasatthimhi pakkhipitvā purimapādapacchimapādanaṅguṭṭhānaṃ ṭhitokāsaṃ sallakkhetvā dvinnaṃ purimapādānaṃ matthake sīsaṃ ṭhapetvā sayati[P 3.0028], divasampi sayitvā pabujjhamāno na utrasanto pabujjhati, sīsaṃ pana ukkhipitvā purimapādādīnaṃ ṭhitokāsaṃ sallakkheti. Sace kiñci ṭhānaṃ vijahitvā ṭhitaṃ hoti, “nayidaṃ tuyhaṃ jātiyā sūrabhāvassa ca anurūpa”nti anattamano hutvā tattheva sayati, na gocarāya pakkamati. Avijahitvā ṭhite pana “tuyhaṃ jātiyā ca sūrabhāvassa ca anurūpamida”nti haṭṭhatuṭṭho uṭṭhāya sīhavijambhitaṃ vijambhitvā kesarabhāraṃ vidhunitvā tikkhattuṃ sīhanādaṃ naditvā gocarāya pakkamati.

“For the most part, O monks, the lion, the king of beasts, lies down on its right side, having tucked its tail between its thighs”—this is the lion's posture. Indeed, because of its majesty, the lion, the king of beasts, places its two front paws in one place and its two hind paws in one place, and having placed its tail between its thighs, and having marked the place where its front paws, hind paws, and tail are situated, it places its head on top of its two front paws and lies down. Even having slept for a whole day, when awakening, it awakens without being startled. Lifting its head, it marks the place where its front paws and so on were situated. If any limb has moved from its place, being displeased, it thinks, “This is not suitable for your birth and your courageous nature,” and lies down right there again; it does not go out for prey. But if they are in place without having moved, being joyful and delighted, it thinks, “This is suitable for your birth and your courageous nature.” Then, rising, it stretches a lion's stretch, shakes its mane, roars a lion's roar three times, and goes out for prey.

Catutthajjhānaseyyā pana tathāgataseyyāti vuccati. Tāsu idha sīhaseyyā āgatā. Ayañhi tejussadairiyāpathattā uttamaseyyā nāma.

Furthermore, the posture of the fourth jhāna is called the Tathāgata's posture. Among these, the lion's posture is mentioned here. Indeed, because it is a posture of majesty, this is called the supreme posture.

Pāde pādanti dakkhiṇapāde vāmapādaṃ. Accādhāyāti atiādhāya, īsakaṃ atikkamma ṭhapetvā. Gopphakena hi gopphake, jāṇunā vā jāṇumhi saṅghaṭṭiyamāne abhiṇhaṃ vedanā uppajjati, cittaṃ ekaggaṃ na hoti, seyyā aphāsukā hoti. Yathā pana na saṅghaṃṭṭeti, evaṃ atikkamma [V 3.0072] ṭhapite vedanā nuppajjati, cittaṃ ekaggaṃ hoti, seyyā phāsukā hoti. Tasmā evaṃ seyyaṃ kappeti.

“Foot upon foot” means placing the left foot on the right foot. “Placing beyond” means placing it slightly beyond, having passed it over a little. For when one ankle strikes the other, or one knee strikes the other, painful sensation frequently arises, the mind is not one-pointed, and the lying down is uncomfortable. But when placed beyond in such a way that they do not strike, painful sensation does not arise, the mind becomes one-pointed, and the lying down is comfortable. Therefore, one arranges the lying down thus.

Sato sampajānoti satiyā ceva sampajaññena ca samannāgato. Kathaṃ niddāyanto sato sampajāno hotīti? Satisampajaññassa appahānena. Ayañhi divasañceva sakalayāmañca āvaraṇīyehi dhammehi cittaṃ parisodhetvā paṭhamayāmāvasāne caṅkamā oruyha pāde dhovantopi mūlakammaṭṭhānaṃ avijahantova dhovati, taṃ avijahantova dvāraṃ [M 3.0073] vivarati, mañce nisīdati, avijahantova niddaṃ okkamati. Pabujjhanto pana mūlakammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvāva pabujjhati. Evaṃ niddaṃ okkamantopi sato sampajāno hoti. Evaṃ pana ñāṇadhātukanti na rocayiṃsu.

“Mindful and clearly comprehending” means endowed with mindfulness and clear comprehension. How is one who is sleeping mindful and clearly comprehending? By not abandoning mindfulness and clear comprehension. Indeed, this monk, having thoroughly cleansed his mind from obstructive states throughout the day and throughout the entire first watch, at the end of the first watch descends from his walking path, and even while washing his feet, he does so without abandoning his primary meditation subject. Without abandoning it, he opens the door. He sits on the bed. Without abandoning it, he falls asleep. Upon waking, however, he awakens only after taking up his primary meditation subject. In this way, even when falling asleep, he is mindful and clearly comprehending. However, the ancient teachers did not approve of this, saying it means 'having that same knowledge-element even when falling asleep'.

Vuttanayena [P 3.0029] panesa cittaṃ parisodhetvā paṭhamayāmāvasāne “upādinnakaṃ sarīraṃ niddāya samassāsessāmī”ti caṅkamā oruyha mūlakammaṭṭhānaṃ avijahantova pāde dhovati, dvāraṃ vivarati, mañce pana nisīditvā mūlakammaṭṭhānaṃ pahāya, “khandhāva khandhesu, dhātuyova dhātūsu paṭihaññantī”ti senāsanaṃ paccavekkhanto kamena niddaṃ okkamati, pabujjhanto pana mūlakammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvāva pabujjhati. Evaṃ niddaṃ okkamantopi sato sampajāno nāma hotīti veditabbo.

But, in the aforesaid manner, this monk, having purified his mind, at the end of the first watch, thinking, 'I will give this appropriated body rest by means of sleep,' descends from the walking path, washes his feet without abandoning his primary meditation subject, and opens the door. But, having sat on the bed and abandoned his primary meditation subject, reflecting on the lodging thus: 'Aggregates are struck by aggregates; elements are struck by elements,' he gradually falls asleep. Upon waking, however, he awakens only after taking up his primary meditation subject. Thus, it should be understood that even when falling asleep, he is called one who is mindful and clearly comprehending.

Iti imasmiṃ sutte tivaṅgikā pubbabhāgavipassanāva kathitā. Ettakeneva pana vosānaṃ anāpajjitvā tāneva indriyabalabojjhaṅgāni samodhānetvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā bhikkhu arahattaṃ pāpuṇātīti. Evaṃ yāva arahattā desanā kathetabbā.

Thus, in this discourse, only the preliminary insight consisting of three factors is taught. But without reaching the conclusion with just this much, the bhikkhu, by integrating those very faculties, powers, and factors of enlightenment and developing insight, attains Arahantship. Thus, the teaching should be expounded up to Arahantship.

3. Kummopamasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on the Simile of the Turtle

240. Tatiye kummoti aṭṭhikummo. Kacchapoti tasseva vevacanaṃ. Anunadītīreti nadiyā anutīre. Gocarapasutoti “sace kiñci phalāphalaṃ labhissāmi, khādissāmī”ti gocaratthāya pasuto ussukko tannibandho. Samodahitvāti samugge viya pakkhipitvā. Saṅkasāyatīti acchati. Samodahanti samodahanto ṭhapento. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yathā kummo aṅgāni sake kapāle samodahanto siṅgālassa otāraṃ na deti, na ca naṃ siṅgālo pasahati, evaṃ [V 3.0073] bhikkhu attano manovitakke sake ārammaṇakapāle samodahanto kilesamārassa otāraṃ na deti, na ca naṃ māro pasahati.

240. In the third discourse, 'turtle' (kummo) means 'bone-turtle' (aṭṭhikummo). 'Kacchapa' is a synonym for it. 'Anunadītīre' means 'along the riverbank.' 'Gocarapasuto' means 'intent on foraging,' that is, eager and committed for the purpose of foraging, thinking: 'If I get any small or large fruit, I will eat it.' 'Samodahitvā' means 'having drawn in,' as if placing into a casket. 'Saṅkasāyati' means 'it stays.' 'Samodahanti' means 'drawing in,' that is, placing well within. This is what is said: Just as a turtle, drawing its limbs into its own shell, does not give an opening to the jackal, and the jackal cannot overpower it, so too, a bhikkhu, drawing in his mental thoughts into his own object-shell, does not give an opening to the Māra of defilements, and Māra cannot overpower him.

Anissitoti taṇhādiṭṭhinissayehi anissito. Aññamaheṭhayānoti aññaṃ kañci puggalaṃ aviheṭhento. Parinibbutoti kilesaparinibbānena parinibbuto. Nūpavadeyya kañcīti aññaṃ kañci puggalaṃ sīlavipattiyā vā ācāravipattiyā vā attānaṃ ukkaṃsetukāmatāya vā [M 3.0074] paraṃ vambhetukāmatāya vā na upavadeyya, aññadatthu pañca dhamme ajjhattaṃ upaṭṭhapetvā, “kālena vakkhāmi, no akālena, bhūtena vakkhāmi[P 3.0030], no abhūtena, saṇhena vakkhāmi, no pharusena, atthasaṃhitena vakkhāmi, no anatthasaṃhitena, mettacitto vakkhāmi, no dosantaro”ti evaṃ ullumpanasabhāvasaṇṭhiteneva cittena viharati.

“Not dependent” (anissito) means not dependent on the supports of craving and views. “Not harming another” (aññamaheṭhayāno) means not harming any other person. “Fully extinguished” (parinibbuto) means fully extinguished by the extinguishing of defilements through the noble path. “One should not reproach anyone” (nūpavadeyya kañci) means one should not reproach any other person because of a corruption of virtue or a corruption of conduct, or out of a desire to exalt oneself or to disparage others. Instead, having established five qualities within himself, he dwells with a mind established in the nature of desiring to uplift, thinking: 'I will speak at the right time, not at the wrong time; I will speak truthfully, not falsely; I will speak gently, not harshly; I will speak with what is beneficial, not with what is unbeneficial; I will speak with a mind of loving-kindness, not with inner animosity.'

4. Paṭhamadārukkhandhopamasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the First Discourse on the Simile of the Log of Wood

241. Catutthe addasāti gaṅgātīre paññattavarabuddhāsane nisinno addasa. Vuyhamānanti caturassaṃ tacchetvā pabbatantare ṭhapitaṃ vātātapena suparisukkhaṃ pāvussake meghe vassante udakena uplavitvā anupubbena gaṅgāya nadiyā sote patitaṃ tena sotena vuyhamānaṃ. Bhikkhū āmantesīti “iminā dārukkhandhena sadisaṃ katvā mama sāsane saddhāpabbajitaṃ kulaputtaṃ dassessāmī”ti dhammaṃ desetukāmatāya āmantesi. Amuṃ mahantaṃ dārukkhandhaṃ gaṅgāya nadiyā sotena vuyhamānanti idaṃ pana aṭṭhadosavimuttattā sotapaṭipannassa dārukkhandhassa apare samuddapattiyā antarāyakare aṭṭha dose dassetuṃ ārabhi.

241. In the fourth discourse, 'he saw' (addasā) means he saw it while seated on the excellent Buddha-seat prepared on the bank of the Ganges. 'Being swept away' (vuyhamānaṃ) means a log that, having been made square and planed, placed in a mountain cleft, and thoroughly dried by wind and sun, when the great clouds of the early monsoon rained, was lifted by the water, gradually fell into the current of the Ganges River, and was being carried along by that current. 'He addressed the bhikkhus' (bhikkhū āmantesi) means he addressed them out of a desire to teach the Dhamma, thinking: 'By making a comparison with this log, I will show a clansman who has gone forth in faith in my dispensation.' Furthermore, regarding 'this large log being carried along by the current of the Ganges River,' he began to show the eight faults that are obstacles for a log that has entered the stream to reach the ocean, this teaching being about being free from eight faults.

Tatrassa evaṃ aṭṭhadosavimuttatā veditabbā – eko hi gaṅgāya nadiyā avidūre pabbatatale jāto nānāvallīhi paliveṭhito paṇḍupalāsataṃ āpajjitvā upacikādīhi khajjamāno tasmiṃyeva ṭhāne apaṇṇattikabhāvaṃ gacchati, ayaṃ dārukkhandho gaṅgaṃ otaritvā vaṅkaṭṭhānesu vilāsamāno sāgaraṃ patvā maṇivaṇṇe ūmipiṭṭhe sobhituṃ na labhati.

In this context, its freedom from eight dangers should be understood thus: For one log, born on a mountain plateau not far from the Ganges River, becomes entangled with various creepers, turns into withered leaves, is devoured by termites and so on, and reaches a state of being unidentifiable in that very place. This log does not get to enter the Ganges, drift through bends, reach the ocean, and be beautiful on the jewel-colored surface of the waves.

Aparo gaṅgātīre bahimūlo antosākho hutvā jāto, ayaṃ kiñcāpi kālena kālaṃ olambinīhi [V 3.0074] sākhāhi udakaṃ phusati, bahimūlattā pana gaṅgaṃ otaritvā vaṅkaṭṭhānesu vilāsamāno sāgaraṃ patvā maṇivaṇṇe ūmipiṭṭhe sobhituṃ na labhati.

Another tree, born on the bank of the Ganges, has its roots outside and its branches leaning inward. Although from time to time its hanging branches touch the water, yet because its roots are outside, it does not get to enter the Ganges, drift through bends, reach the ocean, and be beautiful on the jewel-colored surface of the waves.

Aparo majjhe gaṅgāya jāto, daḷhamūlena pana suppatiṭṭhito, bahi cassa gatā vaṅkasākhā nānāvallīhi ābaddhā, ayampi daḷhamūlattā bahiddhā vallīhi ābaddhattā ca gaṅgaṃ otaritvā…pe… sobhituṃ na labhati.

Another tree, grown in the middle of the Ganges, is well-established with firm roots; and its crooked branches have gone outwards, bound by various creepers. This one too, due to its firm roots and being bound externally by creepers, does not get to enter the Ganges and reach the ocean.

Aparo [M 3.0075] patitaṭṭhāneyeva vālikāya otthaṭo pūtibhāvaṃ āpajjati, ayampi gaṅgaṃ otaritvā…pe… na labhati.

Another log, in the very place where it fell, is covered with sand and rots away. This one too does not get to enter the Ganges and reach the ocean.

Aparo dvinnaṃ pāsāṇānaṃ [P 3.0031] antare jātattā, sunikhāto viya niccalo ṭhito, āgatāgataṃ udakaṃ dvidhā phāleti, ayaṃ pāsāṇantare suṭṭhu patiṭṭhitattā gaṅgaṃ otaritvā…pe… na labhati.

Another tree, because it was born between two rocks, stands motionless like a well-fixed post, splitting the approaching water in two. This one, because of being well-established between the rocks, does not get to enter the Ganges and reach the ocean.

Aparo abbhokāsaṭṭhāne nabhaṃ pūretvā vallīhi ābaddho ṭhito. Ekaṃ dve saṃvacchare atikkamitvā āgate mahoghe sakiṃ vā dvikkhattuṃ vā temeti, ayampi nabhaṃ pūretvā ṭhitatāya ceva ekassa vā dvinnaṃ vā saṃvaccharānaṃ accayena sakiṃ vā dvikkhattuṃ vā temanatāya ca gaṅgaṃ otaritvā…pe… na labhati.

Another tree, standing in an open space and filling the sky, is bound with vines. After one or two years have passed, when a great flood comes, it is wetted once or twice. This one too, because of standing there filling the sky and because it is only wetted once or twice after the passing of one or two years, does not get to enter the Ganges and reach the ocean.

Aparopi majjhe gaṅgāya dīpake jāto mudukkhandhasākho oghe āgate anusotaṃ nipajjitvā, udake gate sīsaṃ ukkhipitvā, naccanto viya tiṭṭhati. Yassatthāya sāgaro gaṅgaṃ evaṃ viya vadati, “bhoti gaṅge tvaṃ mayhaṃ candanasārasalaḷasārādīni nānādārūni āharasi, dārukkhandhaṃ pana nāharasī”ti. Sulabho esa, deva, punavāre jānissāmīti. Punavāre tambavaṇṇena udakena āliṅgamānā viya āgacchati. Sopi tatheva anusotaṃ nipajjitvā, udake gate sīsaṃ ukkhipitvā, naccanto viya tiṭṭhati. Ayaṃ attano mudutāya gaṅgaṃ otaritvā…pe… na labhati.

Another tree, born on a small island in the middle of the Ganges, with a soft trunk and branches, lies down along the current when the flood comes. When the water has gone, it lifts its head and stands as if dancing. It is for this reason that the ocean speaks to the Ganges as if to say: 'O noble Ganges, you bring me various woods such as sandalwood heartwood, pine heartwood, and so on, but you do not bring this log.' The Ganges replies: 'This one is easy to get, lord; next time I will know.' The next time, the Ganges comes as if embracing it with copper-colored water. But that tree too, in the very same way, lies down along the current, and when the water has gone, it lifts its head and stands as if dancing. This one, due to its own softness, does not get to enter the Ganges and reach the ocean.

Aparo gaṅgāya nadiyā tiriyaṃ patito vālikāya ottharito antarasetu viya bahūnaṃ paccayo [V 3.0075] jāto, ubhosu tīresu veḷunaḷakarañjakakudhādayo uplavitvā tattheva lagganti. Tathā nānāvidhā gacchā vuyhamānā bhinnamusalabhinnasuppaahikukkurahatthiassādikuṇapānipi tattheva lagganti. Mahāgaṅgāpi naṃ āsajja bhijjitvā dvidhā gacchati, macchakacchapakumbhīlamakarādayopi tattheva vāsaṃ kappenti. Ayampi tiriyaṃ patitvā mahājanassa paccayattakatabhāvena [P 3.0032] gaṅgaṃ otaritvā vaṅkaṭṭhānesu vilāsamāno sāgaraṃ patvā maṇivaṇṇe ūmipiṭṭhe sobhituṃ na labhati.

Another log, having fallen athwart in the Ganges river and being covered by sand, became like a bridge between islands, a support for many. From both banks, trees such as bamboo, reeds, karañja, and kudha float up and get stuck right there. Likewise, various kinds of bushes being carried along, as well as broken pestles, broken winnowing baskets, and the carcasses of snakes, dogs, elephants, horses, and so on, also get stuck right there. Even the great Ganges, upon reaching it, splits and flows in two. Fish, turtles, crocodiles, makaras, and so on also make their dwelling right there. This log also, having fallen athwart and become a support for many people, does not get to enter the Ganges, drift gracefully through its bends, reach the ocean, and be beautiful upon the jewel-colored surface of the waves.

Iti bhagavā imehi aṭṭhahi dosehi vimuttattā sotapaṭipannassa dārukkhandhassa apare samuddapattiyā antarāyakare aṭṭha dose dassetuṃ amuṃ [M 3.0076] mahantaṃ dārukkhandhaṃ gaṅgāya nadiyā sotena vuyhamānantiādimāha. Tattha na thale ussīdissatīti thalaṃ nābhiruhissati. Na manussaggāho gahessatīti “mahā vatāyaṃ dārukkhandho”ti disvā, uḷumpena taramānā gantvā, gopānasīādīnaṃ atthāya manussā na gaṇhissanti. Na amanussaggāho gahessatīti “mahaggho ayaṃ candanasāro, vimānadvāre naṃ ṭhapessāmā”ti maññamānā na amanussā gaṇhissanti.

Thus, for the log that has entered the stream and is free from these eight faults, the Blessed One, in order to show the other eight faults that are obstacles to its reaching the ocean, spoke the passage beginning, 'That great log, being carried by the current of the Ganges river...' Therein, 'it will not be stranded on high ground' means it will not ascend onto dry land. 'It will not be seized by the grasp of humans' means that humans, seeing it and thinking, 'Oh, what a great log this is!', will go to it, crossing by raft, and will not take it for the purpose of rafters and so on. 'It will not be seized by the grasp of non-humans' means that non-humans, thinking, 'This heartwood of sandalwood is very valuable; we will place it at the door of our celestial mansion,' will not take it.

Evameva khoti ettha saddhiṃ bāhirehi aṭṭhahi dosehi evaṃ opammasaṃsandanaṃ veditabbaṃ – gaṅgāya avidūre pabbatatale jāto tattheva upacikādīhi khajjamāno apaṇṇattikabhāvaṃ gatadārukkhandho viya hi “natthi dinna”ntiādikāya micchādiṭṭhiyā samannāgato puggalo veditabbo. Ayañhi sāsanassa dūrībhūtattā ariyamaggaṃ oruyha samādhikulle nisinno nibbānasāgaraṃ pāpuṇituṃ na sakkoti.

Just so, here the connection of the simile with the eight external faults should be understood in this way: just as a log that grew on a mountain plateau not far from the Ganges and, being eaten by termites and so on right there, reaches an unnameable state, so should a person endowed with wrong view, such as, 'There is no fruit of what is given,' be understood. For such a person, being far from the Dispensation, is unable to descend onto the Noble Path, sit upon the raft of concentration, and reach the ocean of Nibbāna.

Gaṅgātīre bahimūlo antosākho hutvā jāto viya acchinnagihibandhano samaṇakuṭimbikapuggalo daṭṭhabbo. Ayañhi “cittaṃ nāmetaṃ anibaddhaṃ, ‘samaṇomhī’ti vadantova gihī hoti, ‘gihīmhī’ti vadantova samaṇo hoti. Ko jānissati, kiṃ bhavissatī”ti? Mahallakakāle pabbajantopi gihibandhanaṃ na vissajjeti. Mahallakapabbajitānañca sampatti nāma natthi. Tassa sace cīvaraṃ pāpuṇāti, antacchinnakaṃ vā jiṇṇadubbaṇṇaṃ vā pāpuṇāti. Senāsanampi vihārapaccante paṇṇasālā vā maṇḍapo vā pāpuṇāti. Piṇḍāya carantenāpi puttanattakānaṃ dārakānaṃ pacchato caritabbaṃ [P 3.0033] hoti, pariyante nisīditabbaṃ hoti. Tena so dukkhī dummano assūni muñcanto, “atthi me kulasantakaṃ dhanaṃ, kappati nu kho taṃ khādantena [V 3.0076] jīvitu”nti cintetvā ekaṃ vinayadharaṃ pucchati – “kiṃ, bhante ācariya, attano santakaṃ vicāretvā khādituṃ kappati, no kappatī”ti? “Natthettha doso, kappateta”nti. So attano bhajamānake katipaye dubbace durācāre bhikkhū gahetvā, sāyanhasamaye antogāmaṃ gantvā, gāmamajjhe ṭhito gāmike pakkosāpetvā, “amhākaṃ payogato uṭṭhitaṃ āyaṃ kassa dethā”ti āha. Bhante, tumhe pabbajitā, mayaṃ kassa dassāmāti? Kiṃ pabbajitānaṃ attano santakaṃ na vaṭṭatīti? Kuddāla-piṭakaṃ gahetvā, khettamariyādabandhanādīni karonto nānāppakāraṃ pubbaṇṇāparaṇṇañceva [M 3.0077] phalāphale ca saṅgaṇhitvā, hemantagimhavassānesu yaṃ yaṃ icchati, taṃ taṃ pacāpetvā khādanto samaṇakuṭumbiko hutvā jīvati. Kevalamassa pañcacūḷakena dārakena saddhiṃ pādaparicārikāva ekā natthi. Ayaṃ puggalo kiñcāpi olambinīhi sākhāhi udakaṃ phusamāno antosākho rukkho viya cetiyaṅgaṇabodhiyaṅgaṇādīsu bhikkhūnaṃ kāyasāmaggiṃ deti, gihibandhanassa pana acchinnatāya bahimūlattā ariyamaggaṃ otaritvā samādhikulle nisinno nibbānasāgaraṃ pāpuṇituṃ na sakkoti.

A monastic-householder with uncut household bonds should be seen as like a tree that has grown on the bank of the Ganges, with its roots outside and its branches inside. For this person thinks: 'This mind is unstable. While saying, "I am a monk," one becomes a householder. While saying, "I am a householder," one becomes a monk. Who knows what will happen?' Even when ordaining in old age, he does not relinquish his household bonds. And for those ordained in old age, there is no such thing as accomplishment. If he gets a robe, he gets one that is torn at the edge, or old and discolored. If he gets a dwelling, it is a leaf hut or a shed at the edge of the monastery. When going for alms-food, he has to walk behind young monks who are the age of his sons and grandsons, and he has to sit at the end of the line. Because of this, miserable and dejected, shedding tears, he thinks: 'I have family wealth. Is it permissible to live by consuming it?' He then asks a Vinaya expert: 'Venerable teacher, is it permissible to manage and consume one's own property, or is it not?' 'There is no fault in this; it is permissible.' So, taking a few unruly and ill-behaved monks who follow him, he goes into the village in the evening. Standing in the middle of the village, he has the villagers summoned and asks, 'To whom do you give the income that arises from our property?' They reply, 'Venerable sir, you have ordained. To whom should we give it?' He says, 'Is one's own property not allowed for the ordained?' Taking a hoe and a basket, he works on things like marking the field boundaries, gathers various kinds of early and late crops and fruits, and in winter, summer, and the rainy season, has whatever he desires cooked and eats it, living as a monastic-householder. The only things lacking are a wife to serve at his feet and a child with the five-tuft hairstyle. Although this person, like a tree with inward-leaning branches touching the water with its drooping boughs, may give an appearance of bodily unity with the monks in the courtyards of stūpas and Bodhi trees, because his household bonds are uncut and his roots are outside, he is unable to descend onto the Noble Path, sit upon the raft of concentration, and reach the ocean of Nibbāna.

Gaṅgāya majjhe jāto bahiddhā vallīhi ābaddhavaṅkasākhā viya saṅghasantakaṃ nissāya jīvamāno bhinnājīvapuggalo daṭṭhabbo. Ekacco gihibandhanaṃ pahāya pabbajantopi sāruppaṭṭhāne pabbajjaṃ na labhati. Pabbajjā hi nāmesā paṭisandhiggahaṇasadisā. Yathā manussā yattha paṭisandhiṃ gaṇhanti, tesaṃyeva kulānaṃ ācāraṃ sikkhanti, evaṃ bhikkhūpi yesaṃ santike pabbajanti, tesaṃyeva ācāraṃ gaṇhanti. Tasmā ekacco asāruppaṭṭhāne pabbajitvā ovādānusāsanīuddesaparipucchādīhi paribāhiro hutvā pātova muṇḍaghaṭaṃ gahetvā udakatitthaṃ gacchati, ācariyupajjhāyānaṃ [P 3.0034] bhattatthāya khandhe pattaṃ katvā bhattasālaṃ gacchati, dubbacasāmaṇerehi saddhiṃ nānākīḷaṃ kīḷati, ārāmikadārakehi saṃsaṭṭho viharati.

A person of corrupt livelihood, who lives depending on the Sangha's property, should be seen as like a tree that has grown in the middle of the Ganges with crooked branches bound by vines from the outside.
