Some, even when abandoning household bonds and going forth, do not receive ordination in a suitable place. For this ordination is like taking rebirth. Just as humans learn the conduct of the families in which they take rebirth, so too do monks adopt the conduct of those in whose presence they ordain. Therefore, someone, having gone forth in an unsuitable place, becomes excluded from admonition, instruction, recitation, questioning, and so on. Early in the morning, he takes a water pot and goes to the watering place. For the meal of his teachers and preceptors, he places his bowl on his shoulder and goes to the refectory. He plays various games with unruly novices and dwells in association with monastery attendant boys.

So daharabhikkhukāle attano anurūpehi daharabhikkhūhi ceva ārāmikehi ca saddhiṃ saṅghabhogaṃ gantvā, “ayaṃ khīṇāsavehi asukarañño santikā paṭiggahitasaṅghabhogo, tumhe saṅghassa idañcidañca na detha, na hi tumhākaṃ pavattiṃ sutvā rājā vā rājamahāmattā vā attamanā bhavissanti, etha dāni idañcidañca karothā”ti kuddāla-piṭakāni gāhāpetvā heṭṭhā taḷākamātikāsu kattabbakiccāni kārāpetvā bahuṃ pubbaṇṇāparaṇṇaṃ vihāraṃ pavesetvā ārāmikehi attano upakārabhāvaṃ saṅghassa ārocāpeti. Saṅgho “ayaṃ daharo bahūpakāro, imassa [V 3.0077] sataṃ vā dvisataṃ vā dethā”ti dāpeti. Iti so ito cito ca saṅghasantakeneva vaḍḍhanto bahiddhā ekavīsatividhāhi anesanāhi baddho ariyamaggaṃ otaritvā samādhikulle nisinno nibbānasāgaraṃ pāpuṇituṃ na sakkoti.

In his time as a young monk, he, together with other suitable young monks and monastery attendants, would manage the Sangha's property, saying, 'This is the Sangha's property, received from such-and-such a king by the Arahants. You should not give this and that to the Sangha, for if the king or his great ministers hear of your conduct, they will not be pleased. Come now, do this and that.' Having caused them to take hoes and baskets, and having caused the necessary tasks to be performed in the lower channels of the reservoir, he would bring much grain and other produce into the monastery and cause the monastery attendants to announce his helpfulness to the Sangha. The Sangha, saying, 'This young one is very helpful; give him one hundred or two hundred,' would cause it to be given. Thus, prospering here and there by means of the Sangha's property alone, and bound externally by twenty-one kinds of improper seeking, he is unable to descend onto the Noble Path, sit on the raft of concentration, and reach the ocean of Nibbāna.

Patitaṭṭhāneyeva [M 3.0078] vālikāya ottharitvā pūtibhāvaṃ āpāditarukkho viya ālasiyamahagghaso veditabbo. Evarūpañhi puggalaṃ āmisacakkhuṃ paccayalolaṃ vissaṭṭhaācariyupajjhāyavattaṃ uddesaparipucchāyonisomanasikāravajjitaṃ sandhāya pañca nīvaraṇāni atthato evaṃ vadanti – “bho, kassa santikaṃ gacchāmā”ti? Atha thinamiddhaṃ uṭṭhāya evamāha – “kiṃ na passatha? Eso asukavihāravāsī kusītapuggalo asukaṃ nāma gāmaṃ gantvā yāgumatthake yāguṃ, pūvamatthake pūvaṃ, bhattamatthake bhattaṃ ajjhoharitvā vihāraṃ āgamma vissaṭṭhasabbavatto uddesādivirahito mañcaṃ upagacchanto mayhaṃ okāsaṃ karotī”ti.

Like a tree which, in the very place it has fallen, is covered by sand and brought to a state of decay—so should the lazy and gluttonous one be understood. For regarding such a person—whose eye is on material gain, who is greedy for requisites, who has abandoned the duties toward teachers and preceptors, and who is devoid of recitation, questioning, and wise attention—the five hindrances, in effect, speak thus: 'Sirs, to whose presence shall we go?' Then sloth and torpor, having arisen, say: 'Why do you not see? That lazy person dwelling in such-and-such a monastery, having gone to such-and-such a village and having consumed gruel on top of gruel, cakes on top of cakes, and rice on top of rice, returns to the monastery. Having abandoned all duties and being devoid of recitation and so on, as he approaches his bed, he creates an opportunity for me.'

Tato kāmacchandanīvaraṇaṃ uṭṭhāyāha – “bho, tava okāse kate mayhaṃ katova hoti, idāneva so niddāyitvā kilesānurañjitova pabujjhitvā kāmavitakkaṃ vitakkessatī”ti. Tato byāpādanīvaraṇaṃ uṭṭhāyāha – “tumhākaṃ okāse kate mayhaṃ [P 3.0035] katova hoti. Idāneva niddāyitvā vuṭṭhito ‘vattapaṭivattaṃ karohī’ti vuccamāno, ‘bho, ime attano kammaṃ akatvā amhesu byāvaṭā’ti nānappakāraṃ pharusavacanaṃ vadanto akkhīni nīharitvā vicarissatī”ti. Tato uddhaccanīvaraṇaṃ uṭṭhāyāha – “tumhākaṃ okāse kate mayhaṃ katova hoti, kusīto nāma vātāhato aggikkhandho viya uddhato hotī”ti. Atha kukuccanīvaraṇaṃ uṭṭhāyāha – “tumhākaṃ okāse kate mayhaṃ katova hoti, kusīto nāma kukkuccapakatova hoti, akappiye kappiyasaññaṃ kappiye ca akappiyasaññaṃ uppādetī”ti. Atha vicikicchānīvaraṇaṃ uṭṭhāyāha – “tumhākaṃ okāse kate mayhaṃ katova hoti. Evarūpo hi aṭṭhasu ṭhānesu mahāvicikicchaṃ uppādesī”ti. Evaṃ ālasiyamahagghasaṃ pañca nīvaraṇāni caṇḍasunakhādayo viya siṅgacchinnaṃ jaraggavaṃ ajjhottharitvā gaṇhanti. Sopi ariyamaggasotaṃ otaritvā samādhikulle nisinno nibbānasāgaraṃ pāpuṇituṃ na sakkoti.

Then the hindrance of sensual desire, having arisen, says: 'Sir, when an opportunity is made for you, it is made for me as well. Just now, having slept, he will awaken with a mind tainted by defilements and will contemplate thoughts of sensuality.' Then the hindrance of ill-will, having arisen, says: 'When an opportunity is made for you, it is made for me as well. Just now, having awakened from sleep, when told, “Perform your duties,” he will wander about with glaring eyes, uttering various kinds of harsh words, such as, “Sirs, these monks, not doing their own work, are busying themselves with us.”' Then the hindrance of restlessness, having arisen, says: 'When an opportunity is made for you, it is made for me as well; a lazy person is agitated like a mass of fire struck by the wind.' Then the hindrance of remorse, having arisen, says: 'When an opportunity is made for you, it is made for me as well; a lazy person is overcome by remorse, producing the perception of the unallowable as allowable, and of the allowable as unallowable.' Then the hindrance of doubt, having arisen, says: 'When an opportunity is made for you, it is made for me as well. Such a person indeed produces great doubt in eight instances.' Thus, the five hindrances overwhelm and seize the lazy and gluttonous one, just as fierce dogs and other animals overwhelm and seize an old ox with broken horns. He too is unable to descend into the stream of the Noble Path, sit on the raft of concentration, and reach the ocean of Nibbāna.

Dvinnaṃ pāsāṇānaṃ antare nikhātamūlākārena ṭhitarukkho viya diṭṭhiṃ uppādetvā ṭhito diṭṭhigatiko veditabbo. So hi “arūpabhave rūpaṃ atthi, asaññībhave cittaṃ pavattati, bahucittakkhaṇiko [V 3.0078] lokuttaramaggo, anusayo [M 3.0079] cittavippayutto, te ca sattā sandhāvanti saṃsarantī”ti vadanto ariṭṭho viya kaṇṭakasāmaṇero viya ca vicarati. Pisuṇavāco pana hoti, upajjhāyādayo saddhivihārikādīhi bhindanto vicarati. Sopi ariyamaggasotaṃ otaritvā samādhikulle nisinno nibbānasāgaraṃ pāpuṇituṃ na sakkoti.

Like a tree standing as if its root were driven in between two great stones, one who stands fast, having produced a wrong view, should be understood as a person holding wrong views. For he wanders about like the monk Ariṭṭha and the novice Kaṇṭaka, proclaiming: 'In the formless realm, there is form; in the non-percipient realm, mind arises; the supramundane path has many mind-moments; latent tendencies are dissociated from the mind; and those very beings transmigrate and wander on.' Moreover, he is one of slanderous speech; he wanders about, causing division between preceptors and their co-residents. He too is unable to descend into the stream of the Noble Path, sit on the raft of concentration, and reach the ocean of Nibbāna.

Abbhokāse nabhaṃ pūretvā vallīhi ābaddho ṭhito ekaṃ dve saṃvacchare atikkamitvā āgate mahoghe sakiṃ vā dvikkhattuṃ vā temanarukkho viya mahallakakāle pabbajitvā paccante vasamāno dullabhasaṅghadassano ceva dullabhadhammassavano ca puggalo veditabbo. Ekacco hi vuḍḍhakāle pabbajito katipāhena upasampadaṃ labhitvā pañcavassakāle pātimokkhaṃ paguṇaṃ katvā dasavassakāle vinayadharattherassa santike vinayakathākāle maricaṃ vā harītakakhaṇḍaṃ vā mukhe ṭhapetvā bījanena mukhaṃ pidhāya niddāyanto nisīditvā lesakappena katavinayo nāma [P 3.0036] hutvā pattacīvaraṃ ādāya paccantaṃ gacchati. Tatra naṃ manussā sakkaritvā bhikkhudassanassa dullabhatāya “idheva, bhante, vasathā”ti vihāraṃ kāretvā pupphūpagaphalūpagarukkhe ropetvā tattha vāsenti.

Like a tree that stands in the open, filling the sky, bound by creepers, and gets wet once or twice when a great flood comes after one or two years have passed, so should a person be understood who, having gone forth in old age, dwells in the borderlands, with rare sight of the Sangha and rare hearing of the Dhamma. For a certain person, having gone forth in old age, receives higher ordination within a few days, makes the Pātimokkha proficient in five years, and by ten years, during a discourse on the Vinaya in the presence of a Vinaya-holding elder, he sits dozing, having placed a peppercorn or a piece of myrobalan in his mouth and covering his mouth with a fan. Thus, having become one named 'a master of the Vinaya' by way of pretext, he takes his bowl and robes and goes to the borderlands. There, people honor him, and due to the rarity of seeing a monk, they say, 'Venerable sir, please dwell right here.' Having had a monastery built and having planted trees that bear flowers and fruit, they cause him to dwell there.

Atha mahāvihārasadisavihārā bahussutā bhikkhū, “janapade cīvararajanādīni katvā āgamissāmā”ti tattha gacchanti. So te disvā, haṭṭhatuṭṭho vattapaṭivattaṃ katvā, punadivase ādāya bhikkhācāragāmaṃ pavisitvā, “asuko thero suttantiko, asuko abhidhammiko, asuko vinayadharo, asuko tepiṭako, evarūpe there kadā labhissatha, dhammasavanaṃ kārethā”ti vadati. Upāsakā “dhammassavanaṃ kāressāmā”ti vihāramaggaṃ sodhetvā, sappitelādīni ādāya, mahātheraṃ upasaṅkamitvā, “bhante, dhammassavanaṃ kāressāma, dhammakathikānaṃ vicārethā”ti vatvā punadivase āgantvā dhammaṃ suṇanti.

Then, bhikkhus of great learning from a monastery like unto the Mahāvihāra, thinking, 'Having performed the dyeing of robes and other duties in the countryside, we shall return,' went there. Seeing them, he, being joyful and delighted, performed the major and minor duties. On the next day, having taken them with him and entered the alms-round village, he said, 'Such and such a Thera is a master of the Suttanta, such and such is a master of the Abhidhamma, such and such is a master of the Vinaya, and such and such is a master of the Tipiṭaka. When will you ever get such Theras? You should arrange for a hearing of the Dhamma!' The lay devotees, thinking, 'We shall arrange for a hearing of the Dhamma,' cleared the path to the monastery, and taking ghee, oil, and so forth, they approached the Mahāthera and said, 'Venerable sir, we shall arrange for a hearing of the Dhamma. Please arrange for the Dhamma speakers.' Then, on the next day, they came and listened to the Dhamma.

Nevāsikatthero āgantukānaṃ pattacīvarāni paṭisāmento antogabbheyeva divasabhāgaṃ vītināmeti. Divākathiko uṭṭhito sarabhāṇako ghaṭena udakaṃ vamento viya sarabhāṇaṃ bhaṇitvā uṭṭhito, tampi [M 3.0080] so na jānāti. Rattikathiko sāgaraṃ khobhento viya rattiṃ kathetvā uṭṭhito, tampi so na jānāti. Paccūsakathiko kathetvā uṭṭhāsi, tampi so na jānāti. Pātova pana uṭṭhāya mukhaṃ dhovitvā, therānaṃ pattacīvarāni upanāmetvā, bhikkhācāraṃ upagacchanto mahātheraṃ āha [V 3.0079] – “bhante, divākathiko kataraṃ jātakaṃ nāma kathesi, sarabhāṇako kataraṃ suttaṃ nāma bhaṇi, rattikathiko kataraṃ dhammakathaṃ nāma kathesi, paccūsakathiko kataraṃ jātakaṃ nāma kathesi, khandhā nāma kati, dhātuyo nāma kati, āyatanā nāma katī”ti. Evarūpo ekaṃ dve saṃvaccharāni atikkamitvā bhikkhudassanañceva dhammassavanañca labhantopi oghe āgate udakena sakiṃ vā dvikkhattuṃ [P 3.0037] vā temitarukkhasadiso hoti. So evaṃ saṅghadassanato ca dhammassavanato ca paṭikkamma dūre vasanto ariyamaggaṃ otaritvā samādhikulle nisinno nibbānasāgaraṃ pāpuṇituṃ na sakkoti.

The resident Thera, while storing away the bowls and robes of the visiting bhikkhus, passed the daytime only within the inner chamber. The daytime speaker, having finished, arose. The reciter with intonation, having recited with intonation like one pouring out water from a pot, arose; that too he did not know. The nighttime speaker, having spoken throughout the night as if stirring up the ocean, arose; that too he did not know. The dawn-watch speaker, having spoken, arose; that too he did not know. Then, rising early in the morning and washing his face, he brought the bowls and robes to the Theras. As he was approaching the alms-round village, he asked the Mahāthera: 'Venerable sir, what Jātaka did the daytime speaker relate? What Sutta did the reciter with intonation recite? What Dhamma talk did the nighttime speaker give? What Jātaka did the dawn-watch speaker relate? How many are the aggregates? How many are the elements? How many are the sense bases?' Such a person, though he may gain the sight of bhikkhus and the hearing of the Dhamma after one or two years have passed, is like a tree that is soaked by water once or twice when a flood comes. Thus, having withdrawn from the sight of the Saṅgha and the hearing of the Dhamma and dwelling afar, he is not able, having descended to the Noble Path and being seated on the raft of concentration, to reach the ocean of Nibbāna.

Majjhe gaṅgāya dīpake jāto mudurukkho viya madhurassarabhāṇakapuggalo veditabbo. So hi abhiññātāni abhiññātāni vessantarādīni jātakāni uggaṇhitvā, dullabhabhikkhudassanaṃ paccantaṃ gantvā, tattha dhammakathāya pasāditahadayena janena upaṭṭhiyamāno attānaṃ uddissa kate sampannapupphaphalarukkhe nandanavanābhirāme vihāre vasati. Athassa bhārahārabhikkhū taṃ pavattiṃ sutvā, “asuko kira evaṃ upaṭṭhākesu paṭibaddhacitto viharati. Paṇḍito bhikkhu paṭibalo buddhavacanaṃ vā uggaṇhituṃ, kammaṭṭhānaṃ vā manasikātuṃ, ānetvā tena saddhiṃ asukattherassa santike dhammaṃ uggaṇhissāma, asukattherassa santike kammaṭṭhāna”nti tattha gacchanti.

A person who recites with a sweet voice should be understood to be like a tender tree grown on an islet in the middle of the Gaṅgā. For he, having learned the very well-known Jātakas such as the Vessantara, goes to a border region where the sight of a bhikkhu is rare. There, being attended upon by the people whose hearts are gladdened by his Dhamma talk, he dwells in a monastery, delightful like the Nandana Grove and complete with flowering and fruit-bearing trees, which was built for him. Then, the bhikkhus responsible for him, hearing that news, thought: 'It seems that so-and-so dwells with his mind attached to his supporters. He is a wise bhikkhu, capable of learning the word of the Buddha or of attending to a meditation subject. Having brought him, together with him we shall learn the Dhamma in the presence of such-and-such a Thera, and we shall learn the meditation subject in the presence of such-and-such a Thera.' And so they went there.

So tesaṃ vattaṃ katvā sāyanhasamayaṃ vihāracārikaṃ nikkhantehi tehi “imaṃ, āvuso, cetiyaṃ tayā kārita”nti puṭṭho, “āma, bhante”ti vadati. “Ayaṃ bodhi, ayaṃ maṇḍapo, idaṃ uposathāgāraṃ, esā aggisālā, ayaṃ caṅkamo tayā kārito. Ime rukkhe ropāpetvā tayā nandanavanābhirāmo vihāro kārito”ti. “Āma, bhante”ti, vadati.

He, having performed his duties to them, was asked in the evening by those bhikkhus who had gone out for a walk around the monastery, 'Friend, was this cetiya caused to be built by you?' He replied, 'Yes, venerable sirs.' 'This Bodhi tree, this pavilion, this Uposatha hall, this fire-hall, and this walking path—were they caused to be built by you? And having had these trees planted, was this monastery, as delightful as the Nandana Grove, caused to be built by you?' 'Yes, venerable sirs,' he replied.

So sāyaṃ therupaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā vanditvā pucchati – “kasmā, bhante, āgatatthā”ti? “Āvuso, taṃ ādāya gantvā, asukattherassa santike [M 3.0081] dhammaṃ uggaṇhitvā, asukattherassa santike kammaṭṭhānaṃ, asukasmiṃ nāma araññe samaggā samaṇadhammaṃ karissāmāti iminā kāraṇena āgatamhā”ti. Sādhu, bhante, tumhe nāma mayhaṃ atthāya āgatā, ahampi ciranivāsena idha ukkaṇṭhito gacchāmi, pattacīvaraṃ gaṇhāmi, bhanteti. Āvuso, sāmaṇeradaharā maggakilantā, ajja vasitvā sve pacchābhattaṃ gamissāmāti. Sādhu, bhanteti punadivase tehi saddhiṃ piṇḍāya pavisati. Gāmavāsino “amhākaṃ ayyo bahū āgantuke bhikkhū [V 3.0080] gahetvā āgato”ti āsanāni paññāpetvā yāguṃ pāyetvā sukhanisinnakathaṃ [P 3.0038] sutvā bhattaṃ adaṃsu. Therā “tvaṃ, āvuso, anumodanaṃ katvā nikkhama, mayaṃ udakaphāsukaṭṭhāne bhattakiccaṃ karissāmā”ti nikkhantā.

In the evening, he went to attend upon the Theras, and having paid homage, he asked, 'Venerable sirs, for what reason have you come?' 'Friend, we have come for this reason: to take you with us, and having gone, to learn the Dhamma in the presence of such-and-such a Thera, to learn the meditation subject in the presence of such-and-such a Thera, and then, in unity, to practice the ascetic's duties in such-and-such a forest.' 'Excellent, venerable sirs! You have come for my benefit. I too, having lived here for a long time, have become discontented. I shall go; I shall get my bowl and robe, venerable sirs.' 'Friend, the young novices are weary from the journey. Let us stay today and go tomorrow after the meal.' 'Very well, venerable sirs.' The next day, he entered for alms together with them. The villagers, thinking, 'Our master has come, bringing many visiting bhikkhus,' prepared seats, served them rice-gruel, and after listening to a pleasant talk, they gave the meal. The Theras, having departed first, said, 'Friend, you give the anumodanā and then come out. We shall do our meal duties at a place with convenient water.'

Gāmavāsino anumodanaṃ sutvā pucchiṃsu, “kuto, bhante, therā āgatā”ti? Ete amhākaṃ ācariyupajjhāyā samānupajjhāyā sandiṭṭhā sambhattāti. Kasmā āgatāti? Maṃ gahetvā gantukāmatāyāti. Tumhe pana gantukāmāti? Āmāvusoti. Kiṃ vadetha, bhante, amhehi kassa uposathāgāraṃ kāritaṃ, kassa bhojanasālā, kassa aggisālādayo kāritā, mayaṃ maṅgalāmaṅgalesu kassa santikaṃ gamissāmāti? Mahāupāsikāyopi tattheva nisīditvā assūni pavattayiṃsu. Daharo “tumhesu evaṃ dukkhitesu ahaṃ gantvā kiṃ karissāmi? There uyyojessāmī”ti vihāraṃ gato.

The villagers, having heard the anumodanā, asked, 'Venerable sir, from where have the Theras come?' 'They are our teachers and preceptors, those with the same preceptor, our acquaintances and companions.' 'For what reason have they come?' 'Because of their desire to take me and go.' 'But do you wish to go?' 'Yes, friends.' 'What are you saying, venerable sir? For whom did we have the Uposatha hall built? For whom the dining hall? For whom were the fire-hall and so forth built? To whom shall we go on auspicious and inauspicious occasions?' The great female lay devotees also, sitting right there, shed tears. The young monk thought, 'When you are so distressed, what shall I accomplish by going? I will send the Theras away,' and he went to the monastery.

Therāpi katabhattakiccā pattacīvarāni gahetvā nisinnā taṃ disvāva, “kiṃ, āvuso, cirāyasi, divā hoti, gacchāmā”ti āhaṃsu. Āma, bhante, tumhe sukhitā, asukagehassa iṭṭhakāmūlaṃ ṭhitasaṇṭhāneneva ṭhitaṃ, asukagehādīnaṃ cittakammamūlādīni atthi, gatassāpi me cittavikkhepo bhavissati, tumhe purato gantvā asukavihāre cīvaradhovanarajanādīni karotha, ahaṃ tattha sampāpuṇissāmīti. Te tassa osakkitukāmataṃ ñatvā tvaṃ pacchā āgaccheyyāsīti pakkamiṃsu.

The elders, having also completed their meal duties, took their bowls and robes and were seated. Upon seeing him, they said, 'Friend, why do you delay? The day is advanced; let us go.' 'Yes, venerable sirs. You are at ease. The brick foundation of such-and-such a building remains just as it was left, and for other buildings, the painting work on the foundations and so forth is still there. Even if I go, I will have a distraction of mind. You go ahead and perform the tasks of washing robes, dyeing, and so on at such-and-such a monastery. I will arrive there.' They, knowing his desire to shrink back, departed, thinking, 'You may come later.'

So there anugantvā nivatto vihārameva āgantvā bhojanasālādīni olokento vihāraṃ rāmaṇeyyakaṃ disvā cintesi – “sādhu vatamhi na gato. Sace agamissaṃ, koci, deva, dhammakathiko āgantvā[M 3.0082], sabbesaṃ manaṃ bhinditvā, vihāraṃ attano nikāyasantakaṃ kareyya, atha mayā pacchā āgantvā etassa pacchato laddhapiṇḍaṃ bhuñjantena caritabbaṃ bhavissatī”ti.

He, having followed the elders and turned back, came to the very monastery. Looking at the dining hall and other places, and seeing the delightful monastery, he thought: 'It is indeed good that I did not go. If I had gone, some Dhamma preacher might come, and having broken the minds of all, might make the monastery the property of his own fraternity. Then I, having come later, would have to live by eating the alms-food received after him.'

So aparena samayena suṇāti, “te kira bhikkhū gataṭṭhāne ekanikāyadvenikāyaekapiṭakadvepiṭakādivasena buddhavacanaṃ [P 3.0039] uggaṇhitvā aṭṭhakathācariyā jātā vinayadharā jātā sataparivārāpi sahassaparivārāpi caranti. Ye panettha samaṇadhammaṃ kātuṃ gatā, te ghaṭentā vāyamantā sotāpannā jātā, sakadāgāmino anāgāmino arahanto jātā, mahāsakkārena parinibbutā”ti[V 3.0081]. So cintesi – “sace ahampi agamissaṃ, mayhampesā sampatti abhavissa, imaṃ pana ṭhānaṃ muñcituṃ asakkonto ativiya parihīnamhī”ti. Ayaṃ puggalo attano mudutāya taṃ ṭhānaṃ amuñcanto ariyamaggaṃ otaritvā samādhikulle nisinno nibbānasāgaraṃ pāpuṇituṃ na sakkoti.

He, at a later time, hears: 'Those monks, it is said, in the place where they went, having learned the Buddha's word by way of one Nikāya, two Nikāyas, one Piṭaka, two Piṭakas, and so on, have become teachers of the commentaries and have become upholders of the Vinaya. They wander with retinues of a hundred and even a thousand. Those among them who went to practice the recluse's duties, striving and exerting themselves, have become stream-enterers, once-returners, non-returners, and arahants; they have attained final Nibbāna with great honor.' He thought: 'If I too had gone, this accomplishment would have been mine. But being unable to relinquish this place, I have greatly declined.' This person, due to his own softness, not relinquishing that place, is unable, having descended upon the noble path and being seated on the bank of concentration, to reach the ocean of Nibbāna.

Gaṅgāya nadiyā tiriyaṃ patitvā, vālikāya otthaṭabhāvena antarasetu viya hutvā, bahūnaṃ paccayo jātarukkho viya rathavinītamahāariyavaṃsacandopamādipaṭipadāsu aññataraṃ paṭipadaṃ uggahetvā ṭhito olīnavuttiko puggalo veditabbo. So hi taṃ paṭipattinissitaṃ dhammaṃ uggahetvā pakatiyā mañjussaro cittalapabbatādisadisaṃ mahantaṃ ṭhānaṃ gantvā cetiyaṅgaṇavattādīni karoti. Atha naṃ dhammassavanaggaṃ pattaṃ āgantukā daharā “dhammaṃ kathehī”ti vadanti. So sammā uggahitaṃ dhammaṃ paṭipadaṃ dīpetvā katheti. Athassa paṃsukūlikapiṇḍapātikādayo sabbe theranavamajjhimā bhikkhū “aho sappuriso”ti attamanā bhavanti.

A person of withdrawn conduct should be known, who, like a tree that has grown, having fallen across the river Ganges and become like a bridge between islands due to being covered by sand, is a support for many; and who has learned one or another of the practices from the Rathavinīta Sutta, the Mahā-ariyavaṃsa, the Candopama-paṭipadā, and so on. For he, having learned that teaching which is based on practice, being naturally of sweet voice, goes to a great place like Mount Cittala and performs the duties of the stupa courtyard and so on. Then, young newcomers who have come to a Dhamma hearing say to him, 'Preach the Dhamma.' He preaches the well-learned Dhamma, illuminating the practice. Then all the elder, new, and middle monks—such as wearers of rag-robes and alms-food eaters—become pleased, thinking, 'Oh, what a good man!'

So kassaci nidānamattaṃ, kassaci upaḍḍhagāthaṃ, kassaci gāthaṃ upaṭṭhāpento ayapaṭṭakena ābandhanto viya daharasāmaṇere saṅgaṇhitvā mahāthere upasaṅkamitvā, “bhante, ayaṃ porāṇakavihāro atthi, ettha koci tatruppādo”ti?, Pucchati. Therā – “kiṃ vadesi, āvuso, catuvīsati karīsasahassāni tatruppādo”ti. Bhante, tumhe evaṃ vadetha, uddhane pana aggipi na jalatīti. Āvuso, mahāvihāravāsīhi laddhā [M 3.0083] nāma evameva nassanti, na koci saṇṭhapetīti. Bhante, porāṇakarājūhi dinnaṃ khīṇāsavehi paṭiggahitaṃ kasmā ete nāsentīti? Āvuso, tādisena dhammakathikena sakkā bhaveyya laddhunti. Bhante, mā evaṃ vadetha, amhe paṭipattidīpakadhammakathikā nāma, tumhe maṃ “saṅghakuṭumbiko vihārupaṭṭhāko”ti [P 3.0040] maññamānā kātukāmāti. Kiṃ nu kho, āvuso, akappiyametaṃ, tumhādisehi pana kathite amhākaṃ uppajjeyyāti? Tena hi, bhante, ārāmikesu āgatesu amhākaṃ bhāraṃ karotha, ekaṃ kappiyadvāraṃ kathessāmāti.

He, prompting for some just the introduction, for some half a verse, for some a whole verse, as if binding them with an iron band, gathered the young monks and novices. Having approached the great elders, he asks, 'Venerable sirs, this is an ancient monastery. Is there any revenue belonging to it here?' The elders replied, 'What are you saying, friend? Twenty-four thousand karīsas are its revenue.' 'Venerable sirs, you say so, but in the hearth not even a fire burns.' 'Friend, things obtained by the residents of the Mahāvihāra are indeed lost just like this; no one restores them.' 'Venerable sirs, what was given by ancient kings and accepted by those whose cankers are destroyed—why do they let it be destroyed?' 'Friend, by such a Dhamma preacher as you, it might be possible to obtain it.' 'Venerable sirs, do not speak thus. We are called Dhamma preachers who illuminate the practice. You, thinking of me as a steward of the Saṅgha and a monastery attendant, seem to want to make me so.' 'What now, friend, is this improper? But if preached by one like you, gain would arise for us.' 'In that case, venerable sirs, when the monastery workers come, make it our responsibility; we will speak of a suitable method.'

So pātova gantvā, sannipātasālāyaṃ ṭhatvā, ārāmikesu āgatesu “upāsakā asukakhette bhāgo kuhiṃ, asukakhette kahāpaṇaṃ kuhi”ntiādīni vatvā, aññassa khettaṃ gahetvā, aññassa deti. Evaṃ anukkamena taṃ taṃ paṭisedhento tassa tassa dento tathā akāsi[V 3.0082], yathā yāguhatthā pūvahatthā bhattahatthā telamadhuphāṇitaghatādihatthā ca attanova santikaṃ āgacchanti. Sakalavihāro ekakolāhalo hoti, pesalā bhikkhū nibbijja apakkamiṃsu.

He, having gone early in the morning and stood in the assembly hall, when the monastery workers had come, would say such things as, 'Lay devotees, where is the share from such-and-such a field? Where is the coin from such-and-such a field?' Taking the field of one, he gives it to another. Thus, in sequence, refusing this one and that one, and giving to this one and that one, he acted in such a way that those with porridge in hand, cakes in hand, meals in hand, and oil, honey, molasses, ghee, and so on in hand, would come to his own presence. The entire monastery became one great uproar; the virtuous monks, becoming disgusted, departed.

Sopi ācariyupajjhāyehi vissaṭṭhakānaṃ bahūnaṃ dubbacapuggalānaṃ upajjhaṃ dento vihāraṃ pūreti. Āgantukā bhikkhū vihāradvāre ṭhatvāva “vihāre ke vasantī”ti, pucchitvā, “evarūpā nāma bhikkhū”ti sutvā bāhireneva pakkamanti. Ayaṃ puggalo sāsane tiriyaṃ nipannatāya mahājanassa paccayabhāvaṃ upagato ariyamaggaṃ otaritvā samādhikulle nisinno nibbānasāgaraṃ pāpuṇituṃ na sakkoti.

He too fills the monastery by giving the preceptorship to many intractable persons who had been abandoned by their teachers and preceptors. Visiting monks, standing right at the monastery gate, ask, 'Who lives in the monastery?' Upon hearing, 'Monks of such-and-such a kind,' they depart from the outside. This person, due to lying crosswise in the Dispensation, having become a means of support for many people, is unable, having descended upon the noble path and being seated on the bank of concentration, to reach the ocean of Nibbāna.

Bhagavantaṃ etadavocāti “nibbānapabbhārā”ti padena osāpitaṃ dhammadesanaṃ ñatvā anusandhikusalatāya etaṃ “kiṃ nu kho, bhante”tiādivacanaṃ avoca. Tathāgatopi hi imissaṃ parisati nisinno “anusandhikusalo bhikkhu atthi, so maṃ pañhaṃ pucchissatī”ti tasseva okāsakaraṇatthāya imasmiṃ ṭhāne desanaṃ niṭṭhāpesi.

'He spoke thus to the Blessed One' means: having known that the Dhamma discourse was concluded with the phrase 'inclining towards Nibbāna,' due to his skill in making connections, he spoke the words beginning with, 'What indeed, venerable sir?' For the Tathāgata too, seated in this assembly, concluded the discourse at this point for the very purpose of giving him the opportunity, thinking, 'There is a monk skilled in making connections; he will ask me a question.'

Idāni orimaṃ tīrantiādinā nayena vuttesu ajjhattikāyatanādīsu evaṃ upagamanānupagamanādīni veditabbāni. “Mayhaṃ cakkhu-pasannaṃ, ahaṃ appamattakampi rūpārammaṇaṃ [M 3.0084] paṭivijjhituṃ sakkomī”ti etaṃ nissāya cakkhuṃ assādentopi [P 3.0041] timirakavātādīhi upahatapasādo “amanāpaṃ mayhaṃ cakkhu, mahantampi rūpārammaṇaṃ vibhāvetuṃ na sakkomī”ti domanassaṃ āpajjantopi cakkhāyatanaṃ upagacchati nāma. Aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ anattāti tiṇṇaṃ lakkhaṇānaṃ vasena vipassanto pana na upagacchati nāma. Sotādīsupi eseva nayo.

Now, concerning the internal sense-bases and so forth that have been mentioned, the acts of approaching, not approaching, and so on, should be understood in this way, by the method beginning with 'the near shore.' One who delights in the eye, relying on it and thinking, 'My eye is clear; I am able to discern even a slight form-object,' or one who, with the eye-faculty impaired by such things as cataracts or wind disorders, becomes distressed, thinking, 'My eye is unpleasant; I am not able to make out even a large form-object,' is said to approach the eye-base. However, one who contemplates by means of the three characteristics—impermanence, suffering, and non-self—is said not to approach it. This same method applies to the ear and the other bases.

Manāyatane pana “manāpaṃ vata me mano, kiñci vāmato aggahetvā sabbaṃ dakkhiṇatova gaṇhātī”ti vā “manena me cintitacintitassa alābho nāma natthī”ti vā evaṃ assādentopi, “ducintitacintitassa me mano appadakkhiṇaggāhī”ti evaṃ domanassaṃ uppādentopi manāyatanaṃ upagacchati nāma. Iṭṭhe pana rūpe rāgaṃ, aniṭṭhe paṭighaṃ uppādento rūpāyatanaṃ upagacchati nāma. Saddāyatanādīsupi eseva nayo.

Regarding the mind-base, one who delights in it, thinking, 'Indeed, my mind is agreeable; it grasps everything correctly, not incorrectly,' or, 'There is no failure to obtain whatever I think of with my mind'; or one who generates displeasure, thinking, 'My mind, which thinks ill-conceived thoughts, is accustomed to grasping wrongly,' is said to approach the mind-base. Furthermore, one who generates lust for a desirable form and aversion for an undesirable one is said to approach the form-base. This same method applies to the sound-base and the others.

Nandīrāgassetaṃ adhivacananti yathā hi majjhe saṃsīditvā thalaṃ pattaṃ dārukkhandhaṃ saṇhathūlavālikā [V 3.0083] pidahati, so puna sīsaṃ ukkhipituṃ na sakkoti, evaṃ nandīrāgena ābaddho puggalo catūsu mahāapāyesu patito mahādukkhena pidhīyati, so anekehipi vassasahassehi puna sīsaṃ ukkhipituṃ na sakkoti. Tena vuttaṃ “nandīrāgassetaṃ adhivacana”nti.

'This is a designation for delight and lust'—just as a log, having sunk in the middle of a river and reached a sandbank, is covered by fine and coarse sand, and cannot lift its head again, so too a person bound by delight and lust, having fallen into the four great woeful states, is covered by great suffering. For even many thousands of years, they cannot lift their head again. Therefore, it is said, 'This is a designation for delight and lust.'

Asmimānassetaṃ adhivacananti yathā hi thale āruḷho dārukkhandho heṭṭhā gaṅgodakena ceva upari vassena ca temento anukkamena sevālapariyonaddho “pāsāṇo nu kho esa khāṇuko”ti vattabbataṃ āpajjati, evameva asmimānena unnato puggalo paṃsukūlikaṭṭhāne paṃsukūliko hoti, dhammakathikaṭṭhāne dhammakathiko, bhaṇḍanakārakaṭṭhāne bhaṇḍanakārako, vejjaṭṭhāne vejjo, pisuṇaṭṭhāne pisuṇo. So nānappakāraṃ anesanaṃ āpajjanto tāhi tāhi āpattīhi paliveṭhito “atthi nu kho assa abbhantare kiñci sīlaṃ, udāhu natthī”ti vattabbataṃ āpajjati. Tena vuttaṃ “asmimānassetaṃ adhivacana”nti.

“This is a designation for the conceit ‘I am.’” Just as a log cast up on the bank, being soaked by the water of the Ganges from below and by rain from above, gradually becomes covered with moss and becomes liable to be spoken of as “Is this a rock, or is it a stump?”; even so, a person puffed up with the conceit ‘I am’ becomes a rag-robe wearer in the place of rag-robe wearers, a Dhamma speaker in the place of Dhamma speakers, a quarrel-maker in the place of quarrel-makers, a physician in the place of physicians, and a slanderer in the place of slanderers. Falling into various kinds of wrong livelihood and being enveloped by those various offenses, they become liable to be spoken of thus: “Is there any virtue within them, or is there not?” Therefore, it is said, “This is a designation for the conceit ‘I am.’”

Pañcannetaṃ [P 3.0042] kāmaguṇānaṃ adhivacananti yathā hi āvaṭṭe patitadārukhandho antoyeva pāsāṇādīsu āhatasamabbhāhato bhijjitvā cuṇṇavicuṇṇaṃ hoti, evaṃ pañcakāmaguṇāvaṭṭe patitapuggalo catūsu [M 3.0085] apāyesu kammakāraṇakhuppipāsādidukkhehi āhatasamabbhāhato dīgharattaṃ cuṇṇavicuṇṇataṃ āpajjati. Tena vuttaṃ “pañcannetaṃ kāmaguṇānaṃ adhivacana”nti.

“This is a designation for the five strands of sensual pleasure.” Just as a log that has fallen into a whirlpool is struck and battered against rocks and so on within it, breaking apart and being reduced to fragments and powder; even so, a person who has fallen into the whirlpool of the five strands of sensual pleasure is struck and battered for a long time in the four woeful states by sufferings such as punishments, hunger, and thirst, and is reduced to fragments and powder. Therefore, it is said, “This is a designation for the five strands of sensual pleasure.”

Dussīloti nissīlo. Pāpadhammoti lāmakadhammo. Asucīti na suci. Saṅkassarasamācāroti “imassa maññe imassa maññe idaṃ kamma”nti evaṃ parehi saṅkāya saritabbasamācāro. Saṅkāya vā paresaṃ samācāraṃ saratītipi saṅkassarasamācāro. Tassa hi dve tayo jane kathente disvā, “mama dosaṃ maññe kathentī”ti tesaṃ samācāraṃ saṅkassarati dhāvatīti saṅkassarasamācāro.

“Unvirtuous” (dussīla) means devoid of virtue. “Of bad qualities” (pāpadhamma) means of base qualities. “Impure” (asuci) means not pure. “Of suspicious conduct” (saṅkassarasamācāra) means having conduct that must be recalled with suspicion by others, who think: “I suppose this is his deed, I suppose this is his deed.” Or, “of suspicious conduct” means that he recalls the conduct of others with suspicion. For when he sees two or three people talking, he thinks: “They must be talking about my faults,” and his suspicion runs to their conduct—thus, he is of suspicious conduct.

Samaṇapaṭiññoti salākaggahaṇādīsu “kittakā vihāre samaṇā”ti gaṇanāya āraddhāya “ahampi samaṇo, ahampi samaṇo”ti paṭiññaṃ deti, salākaggahaṇādīni karoti. Brahmacāripaṭiññoti uposathapavāraṇādīsu “ahampi brahmacārī”ti paṭiññāya tāni kammāni pavisati[V 3.0084]. Antopūtīti vakkahadayādīsu apūtikassapi guṇānaṃ pūtibhāvena, antopūti. Avassutoti rāgena tinto. Kasambujātoti rāgādīhi kilesehi kacavarajāto.

“One who claims to be an ascetic” (samaṇapaṭiñña) means that when a count is undertaken in activities such as the taking of voting-tickets, with the question: “How many ascetics are in the monastery?”, he makes the claim: “I too am an ascetic, I too am an ascetic,” and engages in activities such as taking the tickets. “One who claims to be a celibate” (brahmacāripaṭiñña) means that during the Uposatha and Pavāraṇā ceremonies, with the claim: “I too am a celibate,” he participates in those formal acts. “Rotten inside” (antopūti) means that even if a person's kidneys, heart, and so on are not physically rotten, they are rotten inside because of the rottenness of their qualities. “Leaky” (avassuta) means soaked with lust. “Become like rubbish” (kasambujāta) means having become like filth and rubbish due to defilements such as lust.

Etadavocāti gogaṇaṃ gaṅgātīrābhimukhaṃ katvā parisapariyante ṭhito ādito paṭṭhāya yāva pariyosānā satthu dhammadesanaṃ sutvā, “satthā orimatīrādīnaṃ anupagacchantādivasena sakkā paṭipattiṃ pūretunti vadati. Yadi evaṃ pūretuṃ sakkā, ahaṃ pabbajitvā pūressāmī”ti cintetvā etaṃ “ahaṃ kho, bhante”tiādivacanaṃ avoca.

“He spoke thus” (etadavoca) means that, standing at the edge of the assembly after having made the herd of cattle face the bank of the Ganges, and having heard the Teacher's Dhamma discourse from beginning to end, he thought: “The Teacher teaches that it is possible to fulfill the practice by means of not approaching the near shore and so on. If it is possible to fulfill it in this way, I will go forth and fulfill it.” Having thought thus, he spoke those words beginning, “Venerable sir, I...”

Vacchagiddhiniyoti vacchesu sasnehā thanehi khīraṃ paggharantehi vacchakasnehena sayameva gamissantīti. Niyyātehevāti niyyātehiyeva. Gāvīsu hi aniyyātitāsu gosāmikā āgantvā, “ekā gāvī na dissati, eko goṇo, eko vacchako na dissatī”ti tuyhaṃ piṭṭhito piṭṭhito vicarissanti, iti te aphāsukaṃ [P 3.0043] bhavissati. Pabbajjā ca nāmesā saiṇassa na ruhati, aṇaṇā pabbajjā ca buddhādīhi saṃvaṇṇitāti dassanatthaṃ evamāha. Niyyātāti niyyātitā. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ.

“Cows longing for their calves” (vacchagiddhiniyo) means that, with affection for their calves and with milk dripping from their udders, they will go by themselves out of love for their calves. “You must hand them over” (niyyāteheva) means you must indeed hand them over. For if the cows are not handed over, their owners will come and follow right behind you, saying: “One cow is missing, one bull is missing, one calf is missing.” Thus, it will be uncomfortable for you. Furthermore, this going forth does not prosper for one who is in debt; only the going forth of one free from debt is praised by the Buddhas and others—he spoke thus to show this meaning. “They have been handed over” (niyyātā) means they have been returned. In this discourse, the round of existence and the rolling back of the round are taught.

5. Dutiyadārukkhandhopamasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Second Discourse on the Simile of the Log

242. Pañcame [M 3.0086] kimilāyanti kimilānāmake nagare. Saṃkiliṭṭhanti paṭicchannakālato paṭṭhāya asaṃkiliṭṭhā nāma āpatti natthi, evarūpaṃ saṃkiliṭṭhaṃ āpattiṃ. Na vuṭṭhānaṃ paññāyatīti parivāsamānattaabbhānehi vuṭṭhānaṃ na dissati.

5. In the fifth discourse, kimilāyaṃ means in the city named Kimilā. Saṃkiliṭṭhaṃ (defiled) means that from the time of concealment onward, there is no offense called “undefiled”; such a defiled offense is meant. Na vuṭṭhānaṃ paññāyati (no emergence is apparent) means emergence by means of probation, penance, or rehabilitation is not seen.

6. Avassutapariyāyasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Avassutapariyāya Sutta

243. Chaṭṭhe navaṃ santhāgāranti adhunā kāritaṃ santhāgāraṃ, ekā mahāsālāti attho. Uyyogakālādīsu hi rājāno tattha ṭhatvā, “ettakā purato gacchantu, ettakā pacchā, ettakā ubhohi passehi, ettakā hatthī abhiruhantu, ettakā asse, ettakā rathesu tiṭṭhantū”ti evaṃ santhaṃ karonti, mariyādaṃ bandhanti, tasmā taṃ ṭhānaṃ santhāgāranti vuccati. Uyyogaṭṭhānato ca āgantvā yāva gehesu allagomayaparibhaṇḍādīni kārenti, tāva dve tīṇi divasāni te rājāno [V 3.0085] tattha santharantītipi santhāgāraṃ. Tesaṃ rājūnaṃ saha atthānusāsanaṃ agārantipi santhāgāraṃ. Gaṇarājāno hi te, tasmā uppannaṃ kiccaṃ ekassa vasena na chijjati, sabbesaṃ chandopi laddhuṃ vaṭṭati, tasmā sabbe tattha sannipatitvā anusāsanti. Tena vuttaṃ “saha atthānusāsanaṃ agārantipi santhāgāra”nti. Yasmā pana te tattha [P 3.0044] sannipatitvā, “imasmiṃ kāle kasituṃ vaṭṭati, imasmiṃ kāle vapitu”nti evamādinā nayena gharāvāsakiccāni sammantayanti, tasmā chiddāvachiddaṃ gharāvāsaṃ tattha santharantītipi, santhāgāraṃ. Acirakāritaṃ hotīti iṭṭhakakammasudhākammacittakammādivasena susajjitaṃ devavimānaṃ viya adhunā niṭṭhāpitaṃ. Samaṇena vāti ettha yasmā gharavatthupariggahaṇakāleyeva devatā attano vasanaṭṭhānaṃ gaṇhanti, tasmā “devena vā”ti avatvā, “samaṇena vā brāhmaṇena vā kenaci vā manussabhūtenā”ti vuttaṃ.

242. In the sixth discourse, navaṃ santhāgāraṃ (a new assembly hall) means a recently constructed assembly hall; the meaning is a great hall. For during times of military campaigns and so on, kings stand there and make arrangements, saying: “Let so many go forward, so many behind, so many on both flanks; let so many mount elephants, so many horses, so many stand on chariots.” In this way they make agreements and establish boundaries; therefore, that place is called an assembly hall (santhāgāra). Also, after returning from the battlefield, for two or three days, until they have the houses prepared with fresh cow dung plaster and so on, those kings rest there and recover from their fatigue; for this reason also it is an assembly hall. It is also an assembly hall because it is a house for the joint administration of affairs for those kings. For they are oligarchic kings (gaṇarājāno); therefore, a matter that has arisen is not decided by one alone, but it is proper to obtain the consent of all. Therefore, all gather there and deliberate. For that reason it is said: “it is also an assembly hall because it is a house for the joint administration of affairs.” Furthermore, because they gather there and consult on household matters in this way, “At this time it is suitable to plow, at this time to sow,” and so on, they thereby mend the flawed household life; for this reason also it is an assembly hall. Acirakāritaṃ (recently constructed) means it was well-adorned by means of brickwork, plasterwork, painting, and so on, like a divine mansion, and has now been completed. Regarding samaṇena vā (by an ascetic or…): here, because deities take possession of their dwelling places at the very time of taking possession of the house-site, instead of saying “by a deity,” it is said: “by an ascetic, or a brahmin, or any human being.”

Yena [M 3.0087] bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṃsūti santhāgāraṃ niṭṭhitanti sutvā “gacchāma naṃ passissāmā”ti gantvā dvārakoṭṭhakato paṭṭhāya sabbaṃ oloketvā “idaṃ santhāgāraṃ ativiya manoramaṃ sassirikaṃ. Kena paṭhamaṃ paribhuttaṃ amhākaṃ dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāya assā”ti cintetvā – “amhākaṃ ñātiseṭṭhassa paṭhamaṃ diyyamānepi satthunova anucchavikaṃ, dakkhiṇeyyavasena diyyamānepi satthunova anucchavikaṃ, tasmā satthāraṃ paṭhamaṃ paribhuñjāpessāma, bhikkhusaṅghassa ca āgamanaṃ karissāma, bhikkhusaṅghe āgate tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ āgatameva bhavissati, satthāraṃ tiyāmarattiṃ amhākaṃ dhammakathaṃ kathāpessāma, iti tīhi ratanehi paribhuttaṃ pacchā mayaṃ paribhuñjissāma, evaṃ no dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāya bhavissatī”ti sanniṭṭhānaṃ katvā upasaṅkamiṃsu.

The phrase ‘yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṃsu’ (they approached where the Blessed One was) means that having heard that the assembly hall was completed, they thought, ‘Let us go and see it.’ Having gone and observed everything, starting from the gatehouse, they thought: ‘This assembly hall is exceedingly delightful and splendid. If used first by someone, for whose long-term welfare and happiness would it be?’ Then they considered: ‘Even if it were given first to the chief of our kinsmen, it is only fitting for the Teacher. Even if it were given on the basis of worthiness for offerings, it is only fitting for the Teacher. Therefore, we will have the Teacher use it first, and we will arrange for the arrival of the Saṅgha of bhikkhus. When the Saṅgha of bhikkhus arrives, the Tipiṭaka, the word of the Buddha, will have arrived as well. We will have the Teacher preach the Dhamma to us throughout the three watches of the night. Thus, after it has been used by the Three Jewels, we will use it. In this way, it will be for our long-term welfare and happiness.’ Having made this resolution, they approached.

Yena navaṃ santhāgāraṃ tenupasaṅkamiṃsūti taṃdivasaṃ kira santhāgāraṃ kiñcāpi rājakulānaṃ dassanatthāya devavimānaṃ viya susajjitaṃ hoti supaṭijaggitaṃ, buddhārahaṃ pana katvā apaññattaṃ. Buddhā hi nāma araññajjhāsayā araññārāmā antogāme vaseyyuṃ vā no vā, tasmā “bhagavato manaṃ jānitvāva, paññāpessāmā”ti cintetvā, te bhagavantaṃ upasaṅkamiṃsu, idāni pana manaṃ labhitvā paññāpetukāmā yena santhāgāraṃ tenupasaṅkamiṃsu.

The phrase ‘yena navaṃ santhāgāraṃ tenupasaṅkamiṃsu’ (they approached the new assembly hall) means: it is said that on that day, although the assembly hall was beautifully adorned and well-prepared like a divine mansion for the royal families to see, and though it had been made suitable for the Buddha, the furnishings had not yet been laid out. For Buddhas, indeed, have an inclination for the forest and delight in the forest; it was not known whether they would dwell inside a village or not. Therefore, thinking, ‘Only after knowing the Blessed One’s mind shall we lay out the furnishings,’ they had approached the Blessed One. Now, having received his consent and wishing to lay out the furnishings, they approached the assembly hall.

Sabbasanthariṃ santhāgāraṃ santharitvāti yathā sabbameva santhataṃ hoti, evaṃ taṃ santharāpetvā. Sabbapaṭhamaṃ [V 3.0086] tāva “gomayaṃ nāma sabbamaṅgalesu vaṭṭatī”ti sudhāparikammakatampi bhūmiṃ allagomayena opuñjāpetvā, parisukkhabhāvaṃ ñatvā, yathā akkantaṭṭhāne [P 3.0045] padaṃ paññāyati, evaṃ catujjātiyagandhehi limpāpetvā upari nānāvaṇṇakaṭasārake santharitvā tesaṃ upari mahāpiṭṭhikakojave ādiṃ katvā hatthattharaassattharasīhattharabyagghattharacandattharakasūriyattharakacittattharakādīhi nānāvaṇṇehi attharakehi santharitabbayuttakaṃ sabbokāsaṃ santharāpesuṃ. Tena vuttaṃ “sabbasanthariṃ santhāgāraṃ santharitvā”ti.

The phrase ‘sabbasanthariṃ santhāgāraṃ santharitvā’ (having spread coverings throughout the assembly hall) means having had it spread in such a way that the entire assembly hall was covered. First, thinking, ‘Cow dung is suitable in all auspicious ceremonies,’ they had the floor, even though it was already finished with plaster, smeared with fresh cow dung. Having ascertained that it was thoroughly dry, they had it anointed with four kinds of perfumes in such a way that footprints would not be visible where it was trodden. Over that, they spread mats of various colors. Over those mats, beginning with large carpets big enough for sixteen dancers to dance upon, they had the entire suitable space covered with various colored spreads, such as elephant-saddle cloths, horse-saddle cloths, lion-skin spreads, tiger-skin spreads, spreads with moon-shapes, spreads with sun-shapes, and figured spreads. For that reason, it is said: ‘sabbasanthariṃ santhāgāraṃ santharitvā’.

Āsanāni paññāpetvāti majjhaṭṭhāne tāva maṅgalathambhaṃ nissāya mahārahaṃ buddhāsanaṃ paññāpetvā, tattha tattha yaṃ yaṃ mudukañca manoramañca paccattharaṇaṃ[M 3.0088], taṃ taṃ paccattharitvā ubhatolohitakaṃ manuññadassanaṃ upadhānaṃ upadahitvā upari suvaṇṇarajatatārakavicittavitānaṃ bandhitvā gandhadāmapupphadāmapattadāmādīhi alaṅkaritvā samantā dvādasahatthe ṭhāne pupphajālaṃ kāretvā, tiṃsahatthamattaṃ ṭhānaṃ paṭasāṇiyā parikkhipāpetvā pacchimabhittiṃ nissāya bhikkhusaṅghassa pallaṅkapīṭhaapassayapīṭhamuṇḍapīṭhāni paññāpetvā upari setapaccattharaṇehi paccattharāpetvā pācīnabhittiṃ nissāya attano attano mahāpiṭṭhikakojave paññāpetvā manoramāni haṃsalomādipūritāni upadhānāni ṭhapāpesuṃ “evaṃ akilamamānā sabbarattiṃ dhammaṃ suṇissāmā”ti. Idaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “āsanāni paññāpetvā”ti.

‘Āsanāni paññāpetvāti’ (Having prepared the seats): First, in the central place, near the auspicious pillar, they prepared the Buddha's seat, worthy of great ones. There, they spread whatever soft and pleasing covering was available. They placed a large supporting cushion of pleasing appearance, red on both sides, and above, they tied a canopy made wonderful with gold and silver stars. They decorated it with garlands of perfume, garlands of flowers, garlands of foliage, and so on, and had a flower-net made in a place of twelve cubits all around. Having had a space of about thirty cubits enclosed with a cloth curtain, they had couches, chairs with backs, and stools prepared for the Saṅgha of bhikkhus near the western wall, and had them covered with white cloths. Near the eastern wall, for themselves, they had large carpets spread and had pleasing, stuffed, large cushions placed, thinking, ‘Thus, without becoming weary, we shall listen to the Dhamma all night long.’ With reference to this, it was said, ‘āsanāni paññāpetvā’.

Udakamaṇikaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvāti mahākucchikaṃ udakacāṭiṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā “evaṃ bhagavā ca bhikkhusaṅgho ca yathāruciyā hatthe vā dhovissanti pāde vā, mukhaṃ vā vikkhālessantī”ti tesu tesu ṭhānesu maṇivaṇṇassa udakassa pūrāpetvā vāsatthāya nānāpupphāni ceva udakavāsacuṇṇāni ca pakkhipitvā kadalipaṇṇehi pidahitvā patiṭṭhāpesuṃ. Idaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “udakamaṇikaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā”ti.

‘Udakamaṇikaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvāti’ (Having set up the water vessel): They had large-bellied water pots set up, thinking, ‘Thus the Blessed One and the Saṅgha of bhikkhus will wash their hands or feet, or rinse their mouths, according to their wish.’ In those various places, having filled them with water the color of gems, and for the purpose of perfuming, having put in various flowers and perfumed powders for water, they covered them with banana leaves and had them set up. With reference to this, it was said, ‘udakamaṇikaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā’.

Telappadīpaṃ āropetvāti rajatasuvaṇṇādimayadaṇḍadīpikāsu yonakarūpakirātarūpakādīnaṃ hatthe ṭhapitasuvaṇṇarajatādimayakapallikāsu ca telappadīpaṃ jālāpetvāti attho. Yena [P 3.0046] bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṃsūti ettha pana te sakyarājāno na kevalaṃ santhāgārameva, atha kho yojanāvaṭṭe kapilavatthusmiṃ nagaravīthiyopi sammajjāpetvā dhaje ussāpetvā gehadvāresu puṇṇaghaṭe ca kadaliyo ca ṭhapāpetevā sakalanagaraṃ dīpamālādīhi vippakiṇṇatārakaṃ viya katvā “khīrūpage dārake [V 3.0087] khīraṃ pāyetha, dahare kumāre lahuṃ lahuṃ bhojetvā sayāpetha, uccāsaddaṃ mā karittha, ajja ekarattiṃ satthā antogāme vasissati, buddhā nāma appasaddakāmā hontī”ti bheriṃ carāpetvā sayaṃ daṇḍadīpikā ādāya yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṃsu.

‘Telappadīpaṃ āropetvāti’ (Having lit the oil lamp): This means having had oil lamps lit on lamp-stands made of silver, gold, and so on, and in small bowls made of gold, silver, and so on, placed in the hands of figures of Yavanas, Kirātas, and others. As for ‘Yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṃsu’ (They approached where the Blessed One was): Here, those Sakyan kings did not only decorate the assembly hall, but indeed, in Kapilavatthu, which was a yojana in circumference, they also had the city streets swept, had flags raised, and had full water pots and banana trees placed at the doors of houses. Having made the entire city like a sky scattered with stars with rows of lamps, they had a drum beaten, announcing: ‘Let the nursing infants be given milk to drink. Having fed the young children quickly, put them to sleep. Do not make a loud noise. Today, for one night, the Teacher will dwell inside the village. Buddhas, indeed, are fond of little sound.’ Then, taking lamp-stands themselves, they approached where the Blessed One was.

Atha [M 3.0089] kho bhagavā nivāsetvā pattacīvaramādāya saddhiṃ bhikkhusaṅghena yena navaṃ santhāgāraṃ tenupasaṅkamīti “yassa dāni, bhante, bhagavā kālaṃ maññatī”ti evaṃ kira kāle ārocite bhagavā lākhārasatintarattakoviḷārapupphavaṇṇaṃ rattadupaṭṭaṃ kattariyā padumaṃ kantento viya, saṃvidhāya timaṇḍalaṃ paṭicchādento nivāsetvā suvaṇṇapāmaṅgena padumakalāpaṃ parikkhipanto viya, vijjulatāsassirikaṃ kāyabandhanaṃ bandhitvā rattakambalena gajakumbhaṃ pariyonandhanto viya, ratanasatubbedhe suvaṇṇagghike pavāḷajālaṃ khipamāno viya suvaṇṇacetiye rattakambalakañcukaṃ paṭimuñcanto viya, gacchantaṃ puṇṇacandaṃ rattavaṇṇavalāhakena paṭicchādayamāno viya, kañcanapabbatamatthake supakkalākhārasaṃ parisiñcanto viya, cittakūṭapabbatamatthakaṃ vijjulatāya parikkhipanto viya ca sacakkavāḷasineruyugandharaṃ mahāpathaviṃ sañcāletvā gahitaṃ nigrodhapallavasamānavaṇṇaṃ rattavarapaṃsukūlaṃ pārupitvā, gandhakuṭidvārato nikkhami kañcanaguhato sīho viya udayapabbatakūṭato puṇṇacando viya ca. Nikkhamitvā pana gandhakuṭipamukhe aṭṭhāsi.

Then the Blessed One, having dressed and taken his bowl and robe, together with the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, approached the new assembly hall. When the time was announced, 'Now, venerable sir, the Blessed One knows the time,' the Blessed One dressed, covering the three circles of his body. He wore a very red double-layered under-robe, the color of the koviḷāra flower, red as if soaked in lac-juice, arranging it well like one cutting a lotus with scissors. He tied his waistband, which was resplendent like a flash of lightning, like one encircling a cluster of lotuses with a golden tool. He donned the excellent red rag-robe, the color of a banyan sprout, taken after having caused the great earth, along with the Cakkavāḷa, Sineru, and Yugandhara mountains, to tremble—like covering an elephant's frontal globes with a red blanket; like casting a coral net over a golden stupa one hundred ratanas high; like putting a red woolen mantle on a golden cetiya; like a red-colored cloud covering the moving full moon; like pouring well-matured lac-juice on the summit of a golden mountain; and like encircling the summit of Mount Cittakūṭa with a flash of lightning. He emerged from the door of the perfumed chamber like a lion from a golden cave, and like the full moon and the morning sun rising from the peak of the Udaya mountain. Having emerged, He stood at the front of the perfumed chamber.

Athassa kāyato meghamukhehi vijjukalāpā viya rasmiyo nikkhamitvā suvaṇṇarasadhārāparisekapiñjarapattapupphaphalaviṭape viya ārāmarukkhe kariṃsu. Tāvadeva ca attano attano pattacīvaramādāya mahābhikkhusaṅgho bhagavantaṃ parivāresi. Te pana parivāretvā ṭhitā bhikkhū evarūpā [P 3.0047] ahesuṃ – appicchā santuṭṭhā pavivittā asaṃsaṭṭhā āraddhavīriyā vattāro vacanakkhamā codakā pāpagarahino sīlasampannā samādhisampannā paññāvimuttivimuttiñāṇadassanasampannā. Tehi parivārito bhagavā rattakambalaparikkhitto viya suvaṇṇakkhandho, rattapadumasaṇḍamajjhagatā viya suvaṇṇanāvā, pavāḷavedikāparikkhitto viya suvaṇṇapāsādo virocittha. Sāriputtamoggallānādayo mahātherāpi naṃ meghavaṇṇaṃ paṃsukūlaṃ pārupitvā maṇivammavammikā viya mahānāgā parivārayiṃsu vantarāgā bhinnakilesā vijaṭitajaṭā chinnabandhanā kule vā gaṇe vā alaggā.

Then, from His body, rays emerged like flashes of lightning from the mouths of dark clouds. Like streams of liquid gold, they made the monastery trees all around appear as if their leaves, flowers, fruits, and boughs were tawny from being sprinkled. At that very moment, the great Saṅgha of bhikkhus, each taking his own bowl and robe, surrounded the Blessed One. Those bhikkhus who stood surrounding him were of such a nature: of few wishes, content, secluded, un-gregarious, of aroused energy, able to speak, patient when spoken to, admonishers, reprovers of evil, accomplished in virtue, accomplished in concentration, and accomplished in wisdom, liberation, and the knowledge and vision of liberation. Surrounded by those bhikkhus, the Blessed One shone like a mass of gold wrapped in a red blanket, like a golden boat in the midst of a thicket of red lotuses, and like a golden palace surrounded by a coral railing. The great elders, such as Sāriputta and Moggallāna, having donned rag-robes the color of a dark cloud, surrounded him like great elephants adorned with jeweled breastplates. They had vomited out passion, their defilements were destroyed, their tangle of craving was disentangled, their bonds were cut, and they were unattached to family or to a group.

Iti bhagavā sayaṃ vītarāgo vītarāgehi, vītadoso vītadosehi, vītamoho vītamohehi[V 3.0088], nittaṇho nittaṇhehi, nikkileso nikkilesehi, sayaṃ buddho bahussutabuddhehi parivārito pattaparivāritaṃ viya [M 3.0090] kesaraṃ, kesaraparivāritā viya kaṇṇikā, aṭṭhanāgasahassaparivārito viya chaddanto nāgarājā, navutihaṃsasahassaparivārito viya dhataraṭṭho haṃsarājā, senaṅgaparivārito viya cakkavattirājā, marugaṇaparivārito viya sakko devarājā, brahmagaṇaparivārito viya hāritamahābrahmā, tārāgaṇaparivārito viya puṇṇacando asamena buddhavesena aparimāṇena buddhavilāsena kapilavatthugāmimaggaṃ paṭipajji.

Thus the Blessed One, himself free from passion, accompanied by those free from passion; himself free from aversion, accompanied by those free from aversion; himself free from delusion, accompanied by those free from delusion; himself free from craving, accompanied by those free from craving; himself free from defilements, accompanied by those free from defilements; himself awakened, surrounded by the lesser Buddhas endowed with the learning of the scriptures and the learning of penetration—like a stamen surrounded by petals, like a pericarp surrounded by filaments, like the elephant king Chaddanta surrounded by eight thousand elephants, like the goose king Dhataraṭṭha surrounded by ninety thousand geese, like a universal monarch surrounded by his army, like Sakka, the king of gods, surrounded by the hosts of gods, like the great Brahmā Hārita surrounded by the hosts of Brahmās, like the full moon surrounded by the hosts of stars—with an incomparable Buddha-guise and immeasurable Buddha-grace, set out on the road to the city of Kapilavatthu.

Athassa puratthimakāyato suvaṇṇavaṇṇā rasmi uṭṭhahitvā asītihatthaṭṭhānaṃ aggahesi pacchima-kāyato, dakkhiṇahatthato, vāmahatthato suvaṇṇavaṇṇā rasmi uṭṭhahitvā asītihatthaṭṭhānaṃ aggahesi. Upari kesantato paṭṭhāya sabbakesāvaṭṭehi moragīvavaṇṇā rasmi uṭṭhahitvā gaganatale asītihatthaṭṭhānaṃ aggahesi. Heṭṭhā pādatalehi pavāḷavaṇṇā rasmi uṭṭhahitvā ghanapathaviṃ asītihatthaṭṭhānaṃ aggahesi. Evaṃ samantā asītihatthaṭṭhānaṃ chabbaṇṇā buddharasmiyo vijjotamānā vipphandamānā kañcanadaṇḍadīpikāhi niccharitvā ākāsaṃ pakkhandajālā viya cātuddīpikamahāmeghato nikkhantavijjulatā viya vidhāviṃsu. Sabbadisābhāgā suvaṇṇacampakapupphehi vikiriyamānā viya, suvaṇṇaghaṭato nikkhantasuvaṇṇarasadhārāhi siñcamānā viya, pasāritasuvaṇṇapaṭaparikkhittā viya, verambhavātasamuṭṭhitakiṃsukakaṇikārapupphacuṇṇasamokiṇṇā [P 3.0048] viya vippabhāsiṃsu.

Then, from the front of his body, golden-colored rays arose and took up a space of eighty cubits. From the back of his body, from his right hand, and from his left hand, golden-colored rays arose and took up a space of eighty cubits. Above, starting from the tips of his hair, from all the hair-whorls, peacock-neck-colored rays arose and took up a space of eighty cubits in the expanse of the sky. Below, from the soles of his feet, coral-colored rays arose, and splitting the solid earth, took up a space of eighty cubits. Thus, all around, for a space of eighty cubits, the six-colored Buddha-rays, shining and shimmering, ran about like a net of leaping flames issuing from golden torches on stands, and like streaks of lightning issuing from a great cloud over the four continents. All regions of the directions shone forth, as if scattered with golden campaka flowers, as if sprinkled with streams of golden liquid issuing from a golden pot, as if screened off by outstretched golden cloths, and as if strewn all over with the powder of kiṃsuka and kaṇikāra flowers raised up by the Veramba wind.

Bhagavatopi asītianubyañjanabyāmappabhādvattiṃsavaralakkhaṇasamujjalasarīraṃ samuggatatārakaṃ viya gaganatalaṃ, vikasitamiva padumavanaṃ, sabbapāliphullo viya yojanasatiko pāricchattako, paṭipāṭiyā ṭhapitānaṃ dvattiṃsacandānaṃ dvattiṃsasūriyānaṃ dvattiṃsacakkavattīnaṃ dvattiṃsadevarājānaṃ dvattiṃsamahābrahmānaṃ siriyā siriṃ abhibhavamānaṃ viya virocittha, yathā taṃ dasahi pāramīhi dasahi upapāramīhi dasahi paramatthapāramīhi sammadeva pūritāhi samatiṃsapāramitāhi alaṅkataṃ. Kappasatasahassādhikāni cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni dinnadānaṃ rakkhitasīlaṃ katakalyāṇakammaṃ ekasmiṃ attabhāve otaritvā vipākaṃ dātuṃ ṭhānaṃ alabhamānaṃ sambādhapattaṃ viya ahosi. Nāvāsahassabhaṇḍaṃ ekanāvaṃ āropanakālo viya, sakaṭasahassabhaṇḍaṃ ekasakaṭaṃ āropanakālo viya, pañcavīsatiyā gaṅgānaṃ [M 3.0091] oghassa sambhijja mukhadvāre ekato rāsibhūtakālo viya ahosi.

The Blessed One's body—resplendent with the eighty minor characteristics, the fathom-long aura, and the thirty-two excellent marks of a great man—shone like the expanse of the sky with its stars well-risen, like a blossomed lotus grove, like the Pāricchattaka tree, a hundred yojanas in size, in full bloom. It shone as if overwhelming the splendor of thirty-two moons, thirty-two suns, thirty-two universal monarchs, thirty-two kings of gods, and thirty-two great Brahmās, all placed in succession. For it was adorned by the thirty perfections, which had been completely fulfilled as the ten perfections, the ten secondary perfections, and the ten supreme perfections. The giving that was given, the virtue that was protected, and the good kamma that was done over four incalculable aeons and more than one hundred thousand kappas, having descended into a single existence, became as if constrained, unable to find a place to give its result. It was like the time of loading the cargo of a thousand ships onto a single ship, like the time of loading the cargo of a thousand carts onto a single cart, or like the time when the floodwaters of twenty-five rivers, having merged, become a single mass at the mouth of the ocean.

Imāya [V 3.0089] buddhasiriyā obhāsamānassāpi ca bhagavato purato anekāni daṇḍadīpikāsahassāni ukkhipiṃsu, tathā pacchato, vāmapasse, dakkhiṇapasse. Jātisumanacampakavanamallikārattuppala-nīluppala-bakulasinduvārapupphāni ceva nīlapītādivaṇṇasugandhagandhacuṇṇāni ca cātuddīpikameghavissaṭṭhā udakavuṭṭhiyo viya vippakiriyiṃsu. Pañcaṅgikatūriyanigghosā ceva buddhadhammasaṅghaguṇapaṭisaṃyuttā thutighosā ca sabbā disā pūrayiṃsu. Devamanussanāgasupaṇṇagandhabbayakkhādīnaṃ akkhīni amatapānaṃ viya labhiṃsu. Imasmiṃ pana ṭhāne ṭhatvā padasahassena gamanavaṇṇaṃ vattuṃ vaṭṭati. Tatridaṃ mukhamattaṃ –

And even while the Blessed One was shining with this Buddha-splendor, in front of him many thousands of torches on stands were held up; likewise behind, on his left side, and on his right side. Jāti, sumana, campaka, vanamallikā, red lotus, blue lotus, bakula, and sinduvāra flowers, as well as fragrant powders of blue, yellow, and other colors with pleasant scents, were scattered like showers of water released by a great cloud covering the four continents. The sounds of the fivefold musical instruments and the sounds of praise connected with the virtues of the Buddha, Dhamma, and Saṅgha filled all directions. The eyes of devas, humans, nāgas, supaṇṇas, gandhabbas, yakkhas, and others were as if they had obtained a drink of the deathless. However, standing in this place, it is fitting to speak a description of his gait with a thousand words. Herein, this is but a brief utterance:

“Evaṃ sabbaṅgasampanno, kampayanto vasundharaṃ;

Aheṭhayanto pāṇāni, yāti lokavināyako.

“Thus, perfect in every way, causing the earth to tremble, not harming living beings, the Leader of the World goes.

“Dakkhiṇaṃ [P 3.0049] paṭhamaṃ pādaṃ, uddharanto narāsabho

Gacchanto sirisampanno, sobhate dvipaduttamo.

“Lifting his right foot first, the Bull among Men, walking endowed with splendor, the supreme among bipeds, shines.

“Gacchato buddhaseṭṭhassa, heṭṭhāpādatalaṃ mudu;

Samaṃ samphusate bhūmiṃ, rajasā nupalippati.

“As the foremost Buddha walks, the soft sole of his foot below evenly touches the ground; it is not smeared by dust.

“Ninnaṭṭhānaṃ unnamati, gacchante lokanāyake;

Unnatañca samaṃ hoti, pathavī ca acetanā.

“When the Leader of the World walks, a low place rises up, and a high place becomes level, although the earth is insentient.

“Pāsāṇā sakkharā ceva, kathalā khāṇukaṇṭakā;

Sabbe maggā vivajjanti, gacchante lokanāyake.

“When the Leader of the World walks, stones and gravel, potsherds, stumps, and thorns—all avoid the path.

“Nātidūre uddharati, naccāsanne ca nikkhipaṃ;

Aghaṭṭayanto niyyāti, ubho jāṇū ca gopphake.

“He does not lift his foot too far, nor place it down too near; he proceeds without his two knees and two ankles striking against each other.

“Nātisīghaṃ pakkamati, sampannacaraṇo muni;

Na cāpi saṇikaṃ yāti, gacchamāno samāhito.

“The sage endowed with perfect conduct does not proceed too quickly, nor does he go too slowly, walking while composed.

“Uddhaṃ [V 3.0090] adho ca tiriyañca, disañca vidisaṃ tathā;

Na pekkhamāno so yāti, yugamattañhi pekkhati.

“He does not go looking upwards, downwards, or across, nor to the cardinal and intermediate directions; indeed, he looks only a yoke's length before him.

“Nāgavikkantacāro [M 3.0092] so, gamane sobhate jino;

Cāruṃ gacchati lokaggo, hāsayanto sadevake.

“That Victor, whose gait is like the stride of a noble elephant, is beautiful in his going. The foremost in the world walks gracefully, causing the world with its devas to rejoice.

“Uḷurājāva sobhanto, catucārīva kesarī;

Tosayanto bahū satte, puraṃ seṭṭhaṃ upāgamī”ti.

“Shining like the king of stars, like a lion that roams on four feet; delighting many beings, he approached the excellent city.”

Vaṇṇakālo nāma kiresa, evaṃvidhesu kālesu buddhassa sarīravaṇṇe vā guṇavaṇṇe vā dhammakathikassa thāmoyeva pamāṇaṃ. Cuṇṇiyapadehi vā gāthābandhena vā yattakaṃ sakkoti, tattakaṃ vattabbaṃ. Dukkathitanti na vattabbaṃ. Appamāṇavaṇṇā hi buddhā. Tesaṃ buddhāpi anavasesato vaṇṇaṃ vattuṃ asamatthā, pageva itarā pajāti. Iminā sirivilāsena alaṅkatapaṭiyattaṃ sakyarājakulaṃ pavisitvā bhagavā pasannacittena janena gandhadhūmavāsacuṇṇādīhi pūjiyamāno santhāgāraṃ pāvisi. Tena vuttaṃ “atha kho bhagavā nivāsetvā pattacīvaramādāya saddhiṃ [P 3.0050] bhikkhusaṅghena yena navaṃ santhāgāraṃ tenupasaṅkamī”ti.

This, it is said, is the time for praise. At such times, when speaking of the Buddha's physical qualities or his virtues, the strength of the Dhamma speaker is the only measure. Whether with prose passages or with verse composition, as much as one is able, that much should be spoken. It should not be said, “It is poorly spoken.” For Buddhas are of immeasurable qualities. Even other Buddhas are incapable of speaking of their qualities without remainder, how much more so other beings. With this splendorous grace, the Blessed One, having entered the royal city of the Sakyans, which was adorned and prepared, was honored by the people with delighted minds with perfumes, incense, fragrant powders, and so on, as he entered the assembly hall. Therefore it was said: “Then the Blessed One, having dressed, taking his bowl and outer robe, together with the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, approached the new assembly hall.”

Bhagavantaṃyeva purakkhatvāti bhagavantaṃ purato katvā. Tattha bhagavā bhikkhūnañceva upāsakānañca majjhe nisinno gandhodakena nhāpetvā dukūlacumbaṭakena vodakaṃ katvā jātihiṅgulakena majjitvā rattakambalapaliveṭhite pīṭhe ṭhapitarattasuvaṇṇaghanapaṭimā viya ativirocittha. Ayaṃ panettha porāṇānaṃ vaṇṇabhaṇanamaggo –

The phrase “having placed only the Blessed One at their head” means having placed the Blessed One in front. There, the Blessed One, seated in the midst of the bhikkhus and the lay devotees, having been bathed with fragrant water, dried with a fine linen cloth, and polished with pure vermilion, shone exceedingly, like a solid statue of red gold placed on a seat wrapped in a red blanket. This, in this matter, is the ancient masters' path of descriptive praise:

“Gantvāna maṇḍalamāḷaṃ, nāgavikkantacāraṇo;

Obhāsayanto lokaggo, nisīdi varamāsane.

“Having gone to the assembly hall, he whose stride is like a noble elephant's, the foremost in the world, sat upon the excellent seat, illuminating it.

“Tahiṃ nisinno naradammasārathi,

Devātidevo satapuññalakkhaṇo;

Buddhāsane [V 3.0091] majjhagato virocati,

Suvaṇṇanekkhaṃ viya paṇḍukambale.

“Seated there on the Buddha-seat, the Tamer of persons to be tamed, the God beyond gods, endowed with the marks of a hundred merits, positioned in the middle, he shines like a piece of gold on a Paṇḍukambala stone slab.

“Nekkhaṃ jambonadasseva, nikkhittaṃ paṇḍukambale;

Virocati vītamalo, maṇiverocano yathā.

“The Stainless One shines, like a piece of Jambu-river gold placed upon the Paṇḍukambala stone slab, and like the Verocana jewel.

“Mahāsālova [M 3.0093] samphullo, nerurājāva’laṅkato;

Suvaṇṇayūpasaṅkāso, padumo kokanado yathā.

“Like a great Sāla tree in full bloom, like Meru, king of mountains, when adorned; resembling a golden pillar, and like a red Kokanada lotus.

“Jalanto dīparukkhova, pabbatagge yathā sikhī;

Devānaṃ pāricchattova, sabbaphullo virocatī”ti.

“Like a blazing lamp-tree, like a fire upon a mountain peak, like the Pāricchattaka tree of the devas in full bloom, he shines.” This is the descriptive praise.

Kāpilavatthave sakye bahudeva rattiṃ dhammiyā kathāyāti ettha dhammakathā nāma santhāgārānumodanāpaṭisaṃyuttā pakiṇṇakakathā veditabbā. Tadā hi bhagavā ākāsagaṅgaṃ otārento viya pathavojaṃ ākaḍḍhanto viya mahājambuṃ matthake gahetvā cālento viya yojanikaṃ madhubhaṇḍaṃ cakkayantena pīḷetvā madhupānaṃ pāyamāno viya kapilavatthuvāsīnaṃ sakyānaṃ hitasukhāvahaṃ pakiṇṇakakathaṃ kathesi. “Āvāsadānaṃ nāmetaṃ, mahārāja, mahantaṃ, tumhākaṃ āvāso mayā paribhutto, bhikkhusaṅghena ca paribhutto[P 3.0051], mayā ca bhikkhusaṅghena ca paribhutto pana dhammaratanena paribhuttoyevāti tīhi ratanehi paribhutto nāma hoti. Āvāsadānasmiñhi dinne sabbadānaṃ dinnameva hoti. Bhummaṭṭhakapaṇṇasālāya vā sākhāmaṇḍapassa vāpi ānisaṃso nāma paricchindituṃ na sakkā. Āvāsadānānubhāvena hi bhave bhave nibbattassāpi sambādhitagabbhavāso na hoti, dvādasahattho ovarako viya mātukucchi asambādhova hotī”ti. Evaṃ nānānayavicittaṃ bahuṃ dhammiṃ kathaṃ kathetvā –

Regarding the phrase "instructed the Sakyans of Kapilavatthu for much of the night with a talk on the Dhamma": here, "Dhamma talk" should be understood as a miscellaneous discourse connected with the expression of appreciation for the assembly hall. For at that time, the Blessed One, as if causing the celestial Ganges to descend, as if drawing up the essence of the earth, as if seizing a great Jambu tree by its top and shaking it, as if pressing a league-sized container of honey with a machine and causing them to drink the honey-drink, delivered a miscellaneous discourse bringing welfare and happiness to the Sakyans dwelling in Kapilavatthu. "This gift of a dwelling, O great kings, is a great thing. Your dwelling has been used by me, and it has been used by the Saṅgha of bhikkhus. Since it has been used by me and the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, it has indeed been used by the Jewel of the Dhamma. Thus, it is said to be used by the Three Jewels. For when the gift of a dwelling is given, all gifts are thereby given. The benefit of even a leaf-hut on the ground or a pavilion of branches cannot be measured. For by the power of the gift of a dwelling, in life after life, even when one is conceived, one does not have a confined stay in the womb; the mother's womb is unconfined, like a chamber twelve cubits wide." Thus, having delivered a lengthy Dhamma talk, variegated with many methods—

“Sītaṃ uṇhaṃ paṭihanti, tato vāḷamigāni ca;

Sirīsape ca makase, sisire cāpi vuṭṭhiyo.

“A monastery wards off cold and heat, and furthermore, beasts of prey, crawling things and mosquitoes, and also the chill and the rains.

“Tato [V 3.0092] vātātapo ghoro, sañjāto paṭihaññati;

Leṇatthañca sukhatthañca, jhāyituñca vipassituṃ.

“Furthermore, the fierce wind and sun that have arisen are dispelled. It is for the purpose of shelter and for happiness, for meditating and for practicing insight.

“Vihāradānaṃ saṅghassa, aggaṃ buddhena vaṇṇitaṃ;

Tasmā hi paṇḍito poso, sampassaṃ atthamattano.

“The gift of a monastery to the Saṅgha is praised by the Buddha as supreme. Therefore, a wise person, seeing their own welfare,

“Vihāre kāraye ramme, vāsayettha bahussute;

Tesaṃ annañca pānañca, vatthasenāsanāni ca.

“should have delightful monasteries built, and have the learned dwell therein. To them, food and drink, robes and lodgings,

“Dadeyya [M 3.0094] ujubhūtesu, vippasannena cetasā;

Te tassa dhammaṃ desenti, sabbadukkhāpanūdanaṃ;

Yaṃ so dhammaṃ idhaññāya, parinibbāti anāsavo”ti. (cūḷava. 295) –

“one should give to the upright with a confident mind. They teach him the Dhamma that dispels all suffering. Understanding which Dhamma here, he, free from taints, attains Parinibbāna.”

Evaṃ “ayampi āvāse ānisaṃso, ayampi āvāse ānisaṃso”ti bahudeva rattiṃ atirekataraṃ diyaḍḍhayāmaṃ āvāsānisaṃsakathaṃ kathesi. Tattha imā tāva gāthāva saṅgahaṃ āruḷhā, pakiṇṇakadhammadesanā pana saṅgahaṃ nārohati. Sandassetvātiādīni vuttatthāneva.

Thus, saying, "This is a benefit of a dwelling, and this is a benefit of a dwelling," for much of the night, for one and a half watches, he spoke exceedingly on the benefits of a dwelling. Among these, these verses were included in the canonical collection, but the miscellaneous Dhamma teaching was not included in the collection. The meaning of "having instructed," and so on, is as already stated.

Abhikkantāti atikkantā dve yāmā gatā. Yassa dāni kālaṃ maññathāti yassa tumhe gamanassa kālaṃ maññatha, gamanakālo tumhākaṃ, gacchathāti vuttaṃ hoti. Kasmā pana bhagavā te uyyojesīti? Anukampāya. Sukhumālā hi te, tiyāmarattiṃ nisīditvā vītināmentānaṃ sarīre ābādho uppajjeyya. Bhikkhusaṅghopi mahā, tassa ṭhānanisajjānaṃ okāso laddhuṃ vaṭṭatīti ubhayānukampāya uyyojesi.

"The night is far advanced" means the night has passed; two watches are gone. "It is now time for what you are thinking" means: "The time for departure which you know; it is time for you to depart; go." Why did the Blessed One dismiss them? Out of compassion. For they were delicate; if they were to spend the three watches of the night sitting, illness might arise in their bodies. Also, the Saṅgha of bhikkhus was large, and it was fitting for them to get an opportunity for standing and sitting. Therefore, out of compassion for both parties, he dismissed them.

Vigatathinamiddhoti tatra kira bhikkhū yāmadvayaṃ ṭhitāpi [P 3.0052] nisinnāpi acālayiṃsu, pacchimayāme pana āhāro pariṇamati, tassa pariṇatattā bhikkhusaṅgho vigatathinamiddho jātoti akāraṇametaṃ. Buddhānañhi kathaṃ suṇantassa kāyikacetasikadarathā na honti, kāyacittalahutādayo uppajjanti, tena tesaṃ dve yāme ṭhitānampi nisinnānampi dhammaṃ suṇantānaṃ thinamiddhaṃ vigataṃ, pacchimayāmepi sampatte tathā vigatameva jātaṃ. Tenāha “vigatathinamiddho”ti.

Regarding "free from sloth and torpor": It is said that there the bhikkhus, for two watches, whether standing or sitting, did not stir. Some say that in the last watch the food digests, and due to its digestion the Saṅgha of bhikkhus became free from sloth and torpor. This is not the reason. For when one listens to the discourse of the Buddhas, bodily and mental distress do not arise; lightness of body and mind, and so on, arise. Therefore, for them, listening to the Dhamma for two watches, whether standing or sitting, sloth and torpor were absent. Even when the last watch arrived, it remained absent just so. Therefore, he said, "free from sloth and torpor."

Piṭṭhi [V 3.0093] me āgilāyatīti kasmā āgilāyati? Bhagavato hi chabbassāni mahāpadhānaṃ padahantassa mahantaṃ kāyadukkhaṃ ahosi, athassa aparabhāge mahallakakāle piṭṭhivāto uppajjīti. Akāraṇaṃ vā etaṃ. Pahoti hi bhagavā uppannaṃ vedanaṃ vikkhambhetvā ekampi dvepi sattāhāni ekapallaṅkena nisīdituṃ. Santhāgārasālaṃ pana catūhi iriyāpathehi paribhuñjitukāmo ahosi. Tattha pādadhovanaṭṭhānato yāva dhammāsanā agamāsi, ettake ṭhāne gamanaṃ nipphannaṃ. Dhammāsanaṃ pattaṃ thokaṃ ṭhatvā nisīdi, ettake ṭhāne ṭhānaṃ nipphannaṃ. Dveyāmaṃ dhammāsane nisīdi, ettake ṭhāne [M 3.0095] nisajjā nipphannā. Idāni dakkhiṇena passena thokaṃ nipanne sayanaṃ nipphajjissatīti evaṃ catūhi iriyāpathehi paribhuñjitukāmo ahosi. Upādinnakasarīrañca nāma “no āgilāyatī”ti na vattabbaṃ, tasmā ciranisajjāya sañjātaṃ appakampi āgilāyanaṃ gahetvā evamāha.

My back is aching—why does it ache? For the Blessed One, during his six years of striving with great exertion, there was great bodily pain. Then, in a later part of his life, in his old age, a back ailment arose. Or, this is not the reason. For the Blessed One is capable of suppressing an arisen feeling by means of meditative attainment and sitting in a single cross-legged posture for one week or even two weeks. However, he wished to use the assembly hall in all four postures. From the place for washing feet up to the Dhamma seat, he went; in that distance, the posture of walking was accomplished. Having reached the Dhamma seat, he stood for a short while and then sat down; in that space, the posture of standing was accomplished. He sat on the Dhamma seat for two watches; in that space, the posture of sitting was accomplished. Now, by lying down for a short while on his right side, the posture of lying down will be accomplished. Thus, he wished to use it in all four postures. Moreover, regarding a body that is clung to, it cannot be said, 'It does not ache.' Therefore, taking even the slight aching that arose from prolonged sitting, he spoke thus.

Saṅghāṭiṃ paññāpetvāti santhāgārassa kira ekapasse te rājāno paṭasāṇiṃ parikkhipāpetvā kappiyamañcakaṃ paññāpetvā kappiyapaccattharaṇena attharitvā upari suvaṇṇatārakagandhamālādidāmapaṭimaṇḍitaṃ vitānaṃ bandhitvā gandhatelappadīpaṃ āropayiṃsu, “appeva nāma satthā dhammāsanato vuṭṭhāya thokaṃ vissamanto idha nipajjeyya, evaṃ no imaṃ santhāgāraṃ bhagavatā catūhi iriyāpathehi paribhuttaṃ dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāya bhavissatī”ti. Satthāpi [P 3.0053] tadeva sandhāya tattha saṅghāṭiṃ paññāpetvā nipajji. Uṭṭhānasaññaṃ manasi karitvāti “ettakaṃ kālaṃ atikkamitvā vuṭṭhahissāmī”ti vuṭṭhānasaññaṃ citte ṭhapetvā, tañca kho aniddāyantova therassa dhammakathaṃ suṇamāno.

"Having had the saṅghāṭi robe spread out": it is said that on one side of the assembly hall, those kings, having had a cloth curtain set up all around, prepared a suitable couch, spread it with a suitable covering, tied a canopy above adorned with golden stars, fragrant garlands, and other such festoons, and lit a fragrant oil lamp, thinking, "Perhaps the Teacher, rising from the Dhamma seat and resting for a short while, might lie down here. Thus, this assembly hall having been used by the Blessed One in all four postures will be for our welfare and happiness for a long time." The Teacher too, with reference to that, had his saṅghāṭi robe spread out there and lay down. "Having kept the perception of rising in mind": having placed in his mind the perception of rising, thinking, "After this much time has passed, I will rise." And he did this without falling asleep, listening to the elder's Dhamma talk out of respect for the Dhamma.

Avassutapariyāyanti avassutassa pariyāyaṃ, avassutassa kāraṇanti attho. Adhimuccatīti kilesādhimuccanena adhimuccati, giddho hoti. Byāpajjatīti byāpādavasena pūticitto hoti. Cakkhutoti cakkhubhāvena. Māroti kilesamāropi devaputtamāropi. Otāranti vivaraṃ. Ārammaṇanti paccayaṃ. Naḷāgāratiṇāgāraṃ viya hi savisevanāni āyatanāni, tiṇukkā viya kilesuppattirahaṃ ārammaṇaṃ, tiṇukkāya ṭhapitaṭhapitaṭṭhāne aṅgārassujjalanaṃ viya ārammaṇe āpāthamāgate kilesānaṃ uppatti. Tena vuttaṃ labhetha māro otāranti.

'Avassutapariyāya' means the discourse on one who is defiled, that is, the reason for being defiled. 'He is attached' means he is attached by way of the attachment of defilements; he becomes greedy.
