'He is hostile' means he has a corrupted mind by way of ill will. 'Cakkhuto' means by way of the eye. 'Māra' means both the Māra of defilements and Māra the devaputta. 'Otāraṃ' means an opening. 'Ārammaṇaṃ' means a condition. For the sense bases that are accompanied by involvement are to be seen as like a reed hut or a grass hut. The object, which is a suitable ground for the arising of defilements, is to be seen as like a grass torch. Just as a house blazes up wherever a grass torch is placed, so the arising of defilements is to be seen when an object comes into range. Therefore it is said: 'Māra would find an opening.'

Sukkapakkhe [V 3.0094] bahalamattikapiṇḍāvalepanaṃ kūṭāgāraṃ viya nibbisevanāni āyatanāni, tiṇukkā viya vuttapakārārammaṇaṃ, tiṇukkāya ṭhapitaṭhapitaṭṭhāne nibbāpanaṃ viya nibbisevanānaṃ āyatanānaṃ ārammaṇe āpāthamāgate kilesapariḷāhassa anuppatti. Tena vuttaṃ neva labhetha māro otāranti.

The sense bases that are without involvement are to be seen as like a gabled house in the bright fortnight, freshly plastered with a thick layer of clay and wet cow-dung. The object of the type already mentioned is to be seen as like a grass torch. Just as a grass torch is extinguished wherever it is placed, so the non-arising of the fever of defilements is to be seen when an object comes into the range of the sense bases that are without involvement. Therefore it is said: 'Māra would not find an opening.'

7. Dukkhadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

7. The Explanation of the Dukkhadhamma Sutta

244. Sattame [M 3.0096] dukkhadhammānanti dukkhasambhavadhammānaṃ. Pañcasu hi khandhesu sati chedanavadhabandhanādibhedaṃ dukkhaṃ sambhavati, tasmā te dukkhasambhavadhammattā dukkhadhammāti vuccanti. Tathā kho panassāti tenākārenassa. Yathāssa kāme passatoti yenākārenassa kāme passantassa. Yathā carantanti yenākārena cārañca vihārañca anubandhitvā carantaṃ. Aṅgārakāsūpamā [P 3.0054] kāmā diṭṭhā hontīti pariyeṭṭhimūlakassa ceva paṭisandhimūlakassa ca dukkhassa vasena aṅgārakāsu viya mahāpariḷāhāti diṭṭhā honti. Kāme pariyesantānañhi nāvāya mahāsamuddogāhanaajapathasaṅkupathapaṭipajjanaubhatobyūḷhasaṅgāmapakkhandanādivasena pariyeṭṭhimūlakampi, kāme paribhuñjantānaṃ kāmaparibhogacetanāya catūsu apāyesu dinnapaṭisandhimūlakampi mahāpariḷāhadukkhaṃ uppajjati. Evametassa duvidhassāpi dukkhassa vasena aṅgārakāsu viya mahāpariḷāhāti diṭṭhā honti.

244. In the seventh sutta, 'dukkhadhammānaṃ' means 'phenomena that are the cause of suffering.' For, when the five aggregates are present, suffering with its various forms such as cutting, killing, and binding arises. Therefore, because they are phenomena that are the cause of suffering, they are called 'dukkhadhammā.' 'Tathā kho panassa' means: 'in that manner, for him.' 'Yathāssa kāme passato' means: 'in whatever manner, for him who sees sensual pleasures.' 'Yathā carantaṃ' means: 'in whatever manner, pursuing the mind's grazing and its abiding.' 'Aṅgārakāsūpamā kāmā diṭṭhā honti' means: 'sensual pleasures are seen as being like a pit of burning embers.' They are seen as a great torment, like a pit of burning embers, by way of the suffering rooted in seeking and the suffering rooted in rebirth. For those who seek sensual pleasures, suffering rooted in seeking arises through such things as plunging into the great ocean by boat, undertaking journeys on goat-paths and thorny paths, and charging into battles where both sides are arrayed. For those who enjoy sensual pleasures, the great torment of suffering rooted in the rebirth given in the four woeful states arises from the volition of enjoying sensual pleasures. Thus, by way of these two kinds of suffering, they are seen as a great torment, like a pit of burning embers.

Dāyanti aṭaviṃ. Puratopi kaṇṭakoti purimapasse vijjhitukāmo viya āsannaṭṭhāneyeva ṭhitakaṇṭako. Pacchatotiādīsupi eseva nayo. Heṭṭhā pana pādehi akkantaṭṭhānassa santike, na akkantaṭṭhāneyeva. Evaṃ so kaṇṭakagabbhaṃ paviṭṭho viya bhaveyya. Mā maṃ kaṇṭakoti mā maṃ kaṇṭako vijjhīti kaṇṭakavedhaṃ rakkhamāno.

'Dāyaṃ' means 'a forest' ('aṭaviṃ'). 'Purato pi kaṇṭako' means: a thorn stands in front, as if wishing to pierce from the front, standing right near one's foot. This same method should be applied to the phrases beginning with 'pacchatoti' ('behind'). However, 'below' means near the place trodden by the feet, not on the very place that was trodden. Thus, that person would be as if he had entered a thicket of thorns. 'Mā maṃ kaṇṭako' means: 'May the thorn not pierce me,' while guarding against being pierced by a thorn.

Dandho, bhikkhave, satuppādoti satiyā uppādoyeva dandho, uppannamattāya pana tāya kāci kilesā niggahitāva honti, na saṇṭhātuṃ sakkonti. Cakkhudvārasmiñhi rāgādīsu uppannesu dutiyajavanavārena “kilesā me uppannā”ti ñatvā tatiye javanavāre saṃvarajavanaṃyeva javati. Anacchariyañcetaṃ, yaṃ vipassako tatiyajavanavāre kilese niggaṇheyya. Cakkhudvāre pana iṭṭhārammaṇe āpāthagate bhavaṅgaṃ āvaṭṭetvā āvajjanādīsu uppannesu voṭṭhabbanānantaraṃ sampattakilesajavanavāraṃ [V 3.0095] nivattetvā kusalameva uppādeti. Āraddhavipassakānañhi ayamānisaṃso bhāvanāpaṭisaṅkhāne patiṭṭhitabhāvassa.

'Dandho, bhikkhave, satuppādo' means that the arising of mindfulness itself is slow. But, just as it has arisen, by it some defilements are suppressed; they are unable to persist. For, at the eye-door, when lust and so forth have arisen, having known in the second javana-moment, 'Defilements have arisen in me,' in the third javana-moment, only the javana of restraint runs. And this is not wonderful, that an insight meditator would suppress the defilements in the third javana-moment. However, at the eye-door, when a desirable object has come into range, having averted the life-continuum (bhavaṅga), and after adverting and so forth have arisen, immediately after the determining-moment (voṭṭhabbana), he turns back the javana-moment of defilement that should have occurred and produces only a wholesome one. For this is the benefit for those who have undertaken insight meditation, of being established in the reflection on the development of meditation.

Abhihaṭṭhuṃ [M 3.0097] pavāreyyunti sudinnattherassa viya raṭṭhapālakulaputtassa viya ca kāyena vā satta ratanāni abhiharitvā vācāya vā “amhākaṃ dhanato yattakaṃ icchasi, tattakaṃ [P 3.0055] gaṇhā”ti vadantā pavāreyyuṃ. Anudahantīti sarīre paliveṭhitattā uṇhapariḷāhaṃ janetvā anudahanti. Sañjātasede vā sarīre laggantā anusentītipi attho. Yañhi taṃ, bhikkhave, cittanti idaṃ yasmā citte anāvaṭṭante puggalassa āvaṭṭanaṃ nāma natthi. Evarūpañhi cittaṃ anāvaṭṭanti, tasmā vuttaṃ. Iti imasmiṃ sutte vipassanābalameva dīpitaṃ.

'Abhihaṭṭhuṃ pavāreyyuṃ' means they would invite, as they did for the Elder Sudinna and for the clansman Raṭṭhapāla, either by bringing forth the seven treasures with their body, or by inviting with their speech, saying, 'Take as much as you wish from our wealth.' 'Anudahanti' means: because they are wrapped around the body, they generate warmth and heat, and thus they burn. Alternatively, the meaning can be understood as: clinging to a body that has produced sweat, they lie upon it. 'Yañhi taṃ, bhikkhave, cittaṃ' was said because, when the mind does not turn back, there is no such thing as the turning back of the person. And such a mind does not turn back; therefore, this was said. Thus, in this sutta, only the power of insight meditation is shown.

8. Kiṃsukopamasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Kiṃsukopama Sutta

245. Aṭṭhame dassananti paṭhamamaggassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Paṭhamamaggo hi kilesapahānakiccaṃ sādhento paṭhamaṃ nibbānaṃ passati, tasmā dassananti vuccati. Gotrabhuñāṇaṃ pana kiñcāpi maggato paṭhamataraṃ passati, passitvā pana kattabbakiccassa kilesapahānassa abhāvena na dassananti vuccati. Apica cattāropi maggā dassanameva. Kasmā? Sotāpattimaggakkhaṇe dassanaṃ visujjhati, phalakkhaṇe visuddhaṃ. Sakadāgāmianāgāmiarahattamaggakkhaṇe visujjhati, phalakkhaṇe visuddhanti evaṃ kathentānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ sutvā so bhikkhu “ahampi dassanaṃ visodhetvā arahattaphale patiṭṭhito dassanavisuddhikaṃ nibbānaṃ sacchikatvā viharissāmī”ti taṃ bhikkhuṃ upasaṅkamitvā evaṃ pucchi. So phassāyatanakammaṭṭhāniko channaṃ phassāyatanānaṃ vasena rūpārūpadhamme pariggahetvā arahattaṃ patto. Ettha hi purimāni pañca āyatanāni rūpaṃ, manāyatanaṃ arūpaṃ. Iti so attanā adhigatamaggameva kathesi.

245. In the eighth sutta, 'seeing' (dassana) is a designation for the first path. For the first path, by accomplishing the task of abandoning defilements, first sees Nibbāna; therefore, it is called 'seeing'. Although the change-of-lineage knowledge (gotrabhūñāṇa) sees earlier than the path, after seeing, because there is no task of abandoning defilements to be done, it is not called 'seeing'. Moreover, all four paths are also 'seeing'. Why? At the moment of the stream-entry path, seeing is being purified; at the moment of fruition, it is purified. At the moments of the once-returner, non-returner, and Arahant paths, seeing is being purified; at the moments of their fruitions, it is purified. Having heard the monks speaking thus, that monk thought, 'I too, having purified my seeing and become established in the fruition of Arahantship, will dwell having realized Nibbāna, which is the purification of seeing.' Approaching that monk, he asked thus. That monk, whose meditation subject was the sense bases of contact, had attained Arahantship by comprehending material and immaterial phenomena by way of the six sense bases of contact. Here, the first five sense bases are material, and the mind base is immaterial. Thus, he spoke only of the path he himself had attained.

Asantuṭṭhoti padesasaṅkhāresu ṭhatvā kathitattā asantuṭṭho. Evaṃ kirassa ahosi – “ayaṃ padesasaṅkhāresu ṭhatvā kathesi. Sakkā nu kho padesasaṅkhāresu ṭhatvā dassanavisuddhikaṃ nibbānaṃ pāpuṇitu”nti? Tato naṃ pucchi – “āvuso, tvaṃyeva nu kho idaṃ dassanavisuddhikaṃ nibbānaṃ jānāsi, udāhu aññepi jānantā atthī”ti. Atthāvuso, asukavihāre asukatthero nāmāti. So tampi upasaṅkamitvā pucchi. Etenupāyena aññampi aññampīti.

He was dissatisfied because the other spoke while established in formations with a remainder. It seems he thought: 'This one spoke while established in formations with a remainder. Is it truly possible to attain Nibbāna, the purification of vision, while established in formations with a remainder?' Then he asked him: 'Friend, do you alone know this Nibbāna, the purification of vision, or are there others who also know it?' 'Yes, friend, in such-and-such a monastery there is an elder by such-and-such a name.' So he approached him as well and asked. In this way, he went on to ask another, and then yet another.

Ettha [V 3.0096][M 3.0098] ca dutiyo pañcakkhandhakammaṭṭhāniko rūpakkhandhavasena [P 3.0056] rūpaṃ, sesakkhandhavasena nāmanti nāmarūpaṃ vavatthapetvā anukkamena arahattaṃ patto. Tasmā sopi attanā adhigatamaggameva kathesi. Ayaṃ pana “imesaṃ aññamaññaṃ na sameti, paṭhamena sappadesasaṅkhāresu ṭhatvāva kathitaṃ, iminā nippadesesū”ti asantuṭṭho hutvā tatheva taṃ pucchitvā pakkāmi.

Now, the second monk, whose meditation subject was the five aggregates, had gradually attained Arahantship by defining name-and-form—form by way of the form aggregate, and name by way of the remaining aggregates. Therefore, he too spoke only of the path he himself had attained. But the inquiring monk, being dissatisfied, thought, 'Their accounts do not agree with each other. The first spoke while established in formations with a remainder, whereas this one spoke of being established in formations without a remainder.' And so, having asked him in the same way, he departed.

Tatiyo mahābhūtakammaṭṭhāniko cattāri mahābhūtāni saṅkhepato ca vitthārato ca pariggahetvā arahattaṃ patto, tasmā ayampi attanā adhigatamaggameva kathesi. Ayaṃ pana “imesaṃ aññamaññaṃ na sameti, paṭhamena sappadesasaṅkhāresu ṭhatvā kathitaṃ, dutiyena nippadesesu, tatiyena atisappadesesū”ti asantuṭṭho hutvā tatheva taṃ pucchitvā pakkāmi.

The third monk, whose meditation subject was the great elements, had attained Arahantship by comprehending the four great elements both in brief and in detail. Therefore, he too spoke only of the path he himself had attained. But the inquiring monk, being dissatisfied, thought, 'Their accounts do not agree with each other. The first spoke while established in formations with a remainder, the second in formations without a remainder, and the third in formations with an excessive remainder.' And so, having asked him in the same way, he departed.

Catuttho tebhūmakakammaṭṭhāniko. Tassa kira samappavattā dhātuyo ahesuṃ, kallasarīraṃ balapattaṃ, kammaṭṭhānānipissa sabbāneva sappāyāni, atītā vā saṅkhārā hontu anāgatā vā paccuppannā vā kāmāvacarā vā rūpāvacarā vā arūpāvacarā vā, sabbepi sappāyāva. Asappāyakammaṭṭhānaṃ nāma natthi. Kālesupi purebhattaṃ vā hotu pacchābhattaṃ vā paṭhamayāmādayo vā, asappāyo kālo nāma natthi. Yathā nāma cāribhūmiṃ otiṇṇo mahāhatthī hatthena gahetabbaṃ hattheneva luñcitvā gaṇhāti, pādehi paharitvā gahetabbaṃ pādehi paharitvā gaṇhāti, evameva sakale tebhūmakadhamme kalāpaggāhena gahetvā sammasanto arahattaṃ patto, tasmā esopi attanā adhigatamaggameva kathesi. Ayaṃ pana “imesaṃ aññamaññaṃ na sameti. Paṭhamena sappadesasaṅkhāresu ṭhatvā kathitaṃ, dutiyena nippadesesu, puna tatiyena sappadesesu, catutthena nippadesesuyevā”ti asantuṭṭho hutvā taṃ pucchi – “kiṃ nu kho, āvuso, idaṃ dassanavisuddhikaṃ [P 3.0057] nibbānaṃ tumhehi attanova dhammatāya ñātaṃ, udāhu kenaci vo akkhāta”nti? Āvuso, mayaṃ kiṃ jānāma? Atthi pana sadevake loke sammāsambuddho, taṃ nissāyetaṃ amhehi ñātanti. So cintesi – “ime bhikkhū mayhaṃ ajjhāsayaṃ gahetvā kathetuṃ na sakkonti, ahaṃ sabbaññubuddhameva pucchitvā nikkaṅkho bhavissāmī”ti yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami.

The fourth monk's meditation subject was the three realms. It is said that his elements were in equilibrium, his body was fit and strong, and all meditation subjects were suitable for him. Whether formations were past, future, or present, whether they pertained to the sense-sphere realm, the fine-material realm, or the immaterial realm—all were suitable. There was no such thing as an unsuitable meditation subject. Regarding time, whether it was before the meal or after the meal, or during the first watch of the night and so on, there was no such thing as an unsuitable time. Just as a great elephant that has entered a foraging ground plucks with its trunk what is to be taken with the trunk, and kicks with its feet what is to be taken by kicking with the feet, in the same way, this monk attained Arahantship by grasping all the phenomena of the three realms in groups and contemplating them. Therefore, he too spoke only of the path he himself had attained. But the inquiring monk, being dissatisfied, thought, 'Their accounts do not agree with each other. The first spoke while established in formations with a remainder, the second in formations without a remainder, then the third again in formations with a remainder, and the fourth only in formations without a remainder.' So he asked him: 'Friend, was this Nibbāna, the purification of vision, known by you through your own nature, or was it declared to you by someone?' 'Friend, what do we know on our own? There is a Perfectly Self-Enlightened One in the world with its gods. It is by relying on him that we have known this.' That monk reflected: 'These monks are unable to grasp my disposition and speak accordingly. I will ask the Omniscient Buddha himself and become free from doubt.' And he went to where the Blessed One was.

Bhagavā [M 3.0099] tassa vacanaṃ sutvā “yehi te pañho kathito, te cattāropi khīṇāsavā, sukathitaṃ tehi, tvaṃ pana attano andhabālatāya taṃ na sallakkhesī”ti na evaṃ vihesesi. Kārakabhāvaṃ [V 3.0097] panassa ñatvā “atthagavesako esa, dhammadesanāya eva naṃ bujjhāpessāmī”ti kiṃsukopamaṃ āhari. Tattha bhūtaṃ vatthuṃ katvā evamattho vibhāvetabbo – ekasmiṃ kira mahānagare eko sabbaganthadharo brāhmaṇavejjo paṇḍito paṭivasati. Atheko nagarassa pācīnadvāragāmavāsī paṇḍurogapuriso tassa santikaṃ āgantvā taṃ vanditvā aṭṭhāsi. Vejjapaṇḍito tena saddhiṃ sammoditvā “kenatthena āgatosi bhadramukhā”ti, pucchi. Rogenamhi, ayya, upadduto, bhesajjaṃ me kathehīti. Tena hi, bho, gaccha, kiṃsukarukkhaṃ chinditvā, sosetvā jhāpetvā, tassa khārodakaṃ gahetvā iminā ciminā ca bhesajjena yojetvā, ariṭṭhaṃ katvā piva, tena te phāsukaṃ bhavissatīti. So tathā katvā nirogo balavā pāsādiko jāto.

The Blessed One, having heard his words, did not torment him by saying: 'Those by whom your question was answered, all four are ones whose cankers are destroyed. It was well-spoken by them, but you, due to your own blind foolishness, did not comprehend it.' Instead, knowing his state as a practitioner, He thought, 'This one is a seeker of the meaning; I shall make him understand through a Dhamma discourse,' and so He brought forth the simile of the Kiṃsuka tree. Therein, taking a real story as the basis, the meaning should be made clear thus: It is said that in a certain great city, there lived a wise brahmin physician who was a master of all medical texts. Then a man with the disease of paleness, a resident of a village near the eastern gate of the city, came into his presence, paid homage to him, and stood there. The wise physician, after exchanging pleasantries with him, asked, 'Good sir, for what reason have you come?' 'Venerable sir, I am afflicted by disease. Please tell me a medicine.' 'Well then, good sir, go, cut down a Kiṃsuka tree, dry it, and burn it. Then, taking its alkaline water and combining it with this and that medicine, prepare a medicinal tonic and drink it. Through that, you will become well.' He, having done so, became free from disease, strong, and of pleasing appearance.

Athañño dakkhiṇadvāragāmavāsī puriso teneva rogena āturo “asuko kira bhesajjaṃ katvā arogo jāto”ti sutvā taṃ upasaṅkamitvā pucchi – “kena te, samma, phāsukaṃ jāta”nti. Kiṃsukāriṭṭhena nāma, gaccha tvampi karohīti. Sopi tathā katvā tādisova jāto.

Then another man, a resident of a village near the southern gate, afflicted with that very same disease, heard, 'It is said that so-and-so, having prepared a medicine, became free from disease.' He approached that man and asked: 'Friend, by what did you become well?' 'By the renowned Kiṃsuka tonic. Go, you too prepare it.' He too, having done so, became just like that.

Athañño pacchimadvāragāmavāsī…pe… uttaradvāragāmavāsī puriso teneva rogena āturo “asuko kira bhesajjaṃ katvā arogo jāto”ti taṃ upasaṅkamitvā pucchi “kena te, samma, phāsukaṃ jāta”nti? Kiṃsukāriṭṭhena nāma, gaccha tvampi karohīti. Sopi tathā katvā tādisova jāto.

Then another man, a resident of a village near the western gate... and a man, a resident of a village near the northern gate, afflicted with that very same disease, heard, 'It is said that so-and-so, having prepared a medicine, became free from disease.' He approached that person and asked: 'Friend, by what did you become well?' 'By the renowned Kiṃsuka tonic. Go, you too prepare it.' He too, having done so, became just like that.

Athañño paccantavāsī adiṭṭhapubbakiṃsuko eko puriso teneva rogena āturo ciraṃ tāni tāni bhesajjāni katvā roge avūpasamamāne “asuko kira nagarassa pācīnadvāragāmavāsī [P 3.0058] puriso bhesajjaṃ katvā arogo jāto”ti sutvā “gacchāmahampi, tena katabhesajjaṃ karissāmī”ti daṇḍamolubbha anupubbena tassa santikaṃ gantvā, “kena te, samma, phāsukaṃ jāta”nti pucchi. Kiṃsukāriṭṭhena sammāti. Kīdiso [M 3.0100] pana so kiṃsukoti. Jhāpitagāme ṭhitajhāmathūṇo viyāti. Iti so puriso attanā diṭṭhākārenava kiṃsukaṃ ācikkhi. Tena hi diṭṭhakāle kiṃsuko patitapatto khāṇukakāle diṭṭhattā tādisova hoti.

Then another man, a border-dweller who had never before seen a Kiṃsuka tree, was afflicted with that very same disease. When the disease did not subside after he had prepared various medicines for a long time, he heard, 'It is said that a certain man, a resident of a village near the eastern gate of the city, prepared a medicine and became free from disease.' Thinking, 'I too shall go; I will prepare the medicine that he prepared,' he leaned on a staff and gradually went into that man's presence. He asked, 'Friend, by what did you become well?' 'By the Kiṃsuka tonic, friend.' He then asked, 'But what is that Kiṃsuka like?' The man replied, 'It is like a burnt post standing in a burnt village.' Thus that man described the Kiṃsuka tree according to the appearance he himself had seen. Indeed, at the time he saw it, the Kiṃsuka had shed its leaves, and having been seen in its stump-like state, it was just like that.

So pana puriso sutamaṅgalikattā “ayaṃ ‘jhāpitagāme jhāmathūṇo viyā’ti āha, amaṅgalametaṃ[V 3.0098]. Etasmiñhi me bhesajje katepi rogo na vūpasamissatī”ti tassa veyyākaraṇena asantuṭṭho taṃ pucchi – “kiṃ nu kho, bho, tvaññeva kiṃsukaṃ jānāsi, udāhu aññopi atthī”ti. Atthi, bho, dakkhiṇadvāragāme asuko nāmāti. So taṃ upasaṅkamitvā pucchi, svāssa pupphitakāle diṭṭhattā attano dassanānurūpena “lohitako kiṃsuko”ti āha. So “ayaṃ purimena viruddhaṃ āha, kāḷako lohitakato suvidūradūre”ti tassapi veyyākaraṇena asantuṭṭho “atthi pana, bho, aññopi koci kiṃsukadassāvī, yena kiṃsuko diṭṭhapubbo”ti? Pucchitvā, “atthi pacchimadvāragāme asuko nāmā”ti vutte tampi upasaṅkamitvā pucchi. Svāssa phalitakāle diṭṭhattā attano dassanānurūpena “ocirakajāto ādinnasipāṭiko”ti āha. Phalitakālasmiñhi kiṃsuko olambamānacīrako viya adhomukhaṃ katvā gahitaasikoso viya ca sirīsarukkho viya ca lambamānaphalo hoti. So “ayaṃ purimehi viruddhaṃ āha, na sakkā imassa vacanaṃ gahetu”nti tassapi veyyākaraṇena asantuṭṭho “atthi pana, bho, aññopi koci kiṃsukadassāvī, yena kiṃsuko diṭṭhapubbo”ti? Pucchitvā, “atthi uttaradvāragāme asuko nāmā”ti vutte tampi upasaṅkamitvā pucchi. So assa sañchannapattakāle diṭṭhattā attano dassanānurūpena “bahalapattapalāso sandacchāyo”ti āha. Sandacchāyo nāma saṃsanditvā ṭhitacchāyo.

But that man, being one who believed in auspicious hearings, thought, 'This one says, "like a burnt post in a burnt village"; this is inauspicious. Indeed, even if I prepare this medicine, my disease will not subside.' Dissatisfied with his explanation, he asked him, 'Good sir, do you alone know the Kiṃsuka tree, or is there another?' 'There is, good sir, one named so-and-so in the village at the southern gate.' So he approached that man and asked. That one, having seen it at the time of flowering, said in accordance with his own sight, 'The Kiṃsuka is red.' The man thought, 'This one says something contrary to the first one; a black thing is very, very far from a red thing.' Dissatisfied with his explanation as well, he asked, 'Good sir, is there anyone else who has seen the Kiṃsuka, by whom the Kiṃsuka has been seen before?' Upon being told, 'There is one named so-and-so in the village at the western gate,' he approached him too and asked. That one, having seen it at the time of fruiting, said in accordance with his own sight, 'It has hanging clusters like hair-braids and has tender pods.' Indeed, at the time of fruiting, the Kiṃsuka has hanging fruit, like a hanging hair-braid, like a small sword-sheath held pointing downwards, and like a Sirīsa tree. The man thought, 'This one says something contrary to the previous ones; it is not possible to accept this one's word.' Dissatisfied with his explanation as well, he asked, 'Good sir, is there anyone else who has seen the Kiṃsuka, by whom the Kiṃsuka has been seen before?' Upon being told, 'There is one named so-and-so in the village at the northern gate,' he approached him too and asked. That one, having seen it at the time of its dense foliage, said in accordance with his own sight, 'It has thick mature leaves and tender shoots, and provides dense shade.' 'Dense shade' means shade that stands densely interwoven.

So “ayampi purimehi viruddhaṃ āha, na sakkā imassa vacanaṃ gahetu”nti tassapi veyyākaraṇena asantuṭṭho [P 3.0059] taṃ āha, “kiṃ nu kho, bho, tumhe attanova dhammatāya kiṃsukaṃ jānātha, udāhu kenaci vo akkhāto”ti? Kiṃ, bho, mayaṃ jānāma? Atthi pana mahānagarassa majjhe [M 3.0101] amhākaṃ ācariyo vejjapaṇḍito, taṃ nissāya amhehi ñātanti. “Tena hi ahampi ācariyameva upasaṅkamitvā nikkaṅkho bhavissāmī”ti tassa santikaṃ upasaṅkamitvā taṃ vanditvā aṭṭhāsi. Vejjapaṇḍito tena saddhiṃ sammoditvā, “kenatthena āgatosi bhadramukhā”ti pucchi. Rogenamhi, ayya, upadduto, bhesajjaṃ me kathethāti. Tena hi, bho, gaccha, kiṃsukarukkhaṃ chinditvā sosetvā jhāpetvā tassa khārodakaṃ gahetvā iminā ciminā ca bhesajjena yojetvā ariṭṭhaṃ katvā piva, etena te phāsukaṃ bhavissatīti. So tathā katvā nirogo balavā pāsādiko jāto.

He thought, 'This one also speaks in contradiction to the previous ones; his words cannot be accepted.' Dissatisfied with this explanation as well, he said to him, 'Now, good sirs, do you know the kiṃsuka tree by your own nature, or has someone told you about it?' 'What do we know, good sir? But in the middle of the great city there is our teacher, a wise physician; relying on him, we know it.' 'Then I too will approach the teacher and be free from doubt.' He approached him, paid homage, and stood there. The wise physician exchanged greetings with him and asked, 'For what reason have you come, good sir?' 'I am afflicted by illness, venerable sir; please prescribe medicine for me.' 'Well then, good sir, go, cut down a kiṃsuka tree, dry it, burn it, take its ash-lye water, mix it with this and that medicine, prepare a medicinal drink, and drink it. This will bring you relief.' He did so and became free from illness, strong, and pleasing in appearance.

Tattha mahānagaraṃ viya nibbānanagaraṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Vejjapaṇḍito viya sammāsambuddho. Vuttampi ce [V 3.0099] taṃ “bhisakko sallakattoti kho, sunakkhatta, tathāgatassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ arahato sammāsambuddhassā”ti (ma. ni. 3.65) catūsu dvāragāmesu cattāro vejjantevāsikā viya cattāro dassanavisuddhipattā khīṇāsavā. Paccantavāsī paṭhamapuriso viya pañhapucchako bhikkhu. Paccantavāsino catunnaṃ vejjantevāsikānaṃ kathāya asantuṭṭhassa ācariyameva upasaṅkamitvā pucchanakālo viya imassa bhikkhuno catunnaṃ dassanavisuddhipattānaṃ khīṇāsavānaṃ kathāya asantuṭṭhassa satthāraṃ upasaṅkamitvā pucchanakālo.

Therein, the great city should be seen as the city of Nibbāna. The wise physician is like the Perfectly Enlightened One. And this was stated: '“Physician and surgeon,” Sunakkhatta, is a designation for the Tathāgata, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One.' The four physician's apprentices in the villages at the four gates are like the four destroyers of defilements who have attained purity of vision. The border-dwelling man is like the monk who asks the question. The time when the border-dweller, dissatisfied with the talk of the four physician's apprentices, approached the teacher to ask is like the time when this monk, dissatisfied with the talk of the four destroyers of defilements who have attained purity of vision, approaches the Teacher to ask.

Yathā yathā adhimuttānanti yena yenākārena adhimuttānaṃ. Dassanaṃ suvisuddhanti nibbānadassanaṃ suṭṭhu visuddhaṃ. Tathā tathā kho tehi sappurisehi byākatanti tena tenevākārena tuyhaṃ tehi sappurisehi kathitaṃ. Yathā hi “kāḷako kiṃsuko”ti kathento na aññaṃ kathesi, attanā diṭṭhanayena kiṃsukameva kathesi, evameva chaphassāyatanānaṃ vasena dassanavisuddhipattakhīṇāsavopi imaṃ pañhaṃ kathento na aññaṃ kathesi, attanā adhigatamaggena dassanavisuddhikaṃ nibbānameva kathesi.

'As they are resolved in whatever way' means in whatever manner they are resolved. 'The vision is perfectly pure' means the vision of Nibbāna is thoroughly pure. 'Thus indeed, by those good people it has been explained' means in just that way, it has been spoken to you by those good people. For just as one speaking of 'the black kiṃsuka tree' did not speak of anything else, but spoke only of the kiṃsuka tree according to the way they saw it, in the same way, the destroyer of defilements who has attained purity of vision, when speaking by way of the six sense bases, did not speak of anything else, but spoke only of Nibbāna, which is the purity of vision, by means of the path they had attained.

Yathā ca “lohitako ocirakajāto bahalapattapalāso kiṃsuko”ti kathentopi na aññaṃ kathesi, attanā diṭṭhanayena kiṃsukameva kathesi, evameva pañcupādānakkhandhavasena catumahābhūtavasena tebhūmakadhammavasena dassanavisuddhipattakhīṇāsavopi imaṃ pañhaṃ kathento [M 3.0102] na aññaṃ kathesi, attanā adhigatamaggena dassanavisuddhikaṃ nibbānameva kathesi.

And just as one who spoke of 'the red kiṃsuka, with hanging pods and thick leaves' did not speak of anything else, but spoke only of the kiṃsuka tree according to the way they saw it, in the same way, the destroyer of defilements who has attained purity of vision, when answering this question, did not speak of anything else, but spoke only of Nibbāna, which is the purity of vision, by means of the path they had attained.

Tattha yathā kāḷakakāle kiṃsukadassāvinopi taṃ dassanaṃ bhūtaṃ [P 3.0060] tacchaṃ na tena aññaṃ diṭṭhaṃ, kiṃsukova diṭṭho, evameva chaphassāyatanavasena dassanavisuddhipattassāpi khīṇāsavassa dassanaṃ bhūtaṃ tacchaṃ, na tena aññaṃ kathitaṃ, attanā adhigatamaggena dassanavisuddhikaṃ nibbānameva kathitaṃ. Yathā ca lohitakāle ocirakajātakāle bahalapattapalāsakāle kiṃsukadassāvinopi taṃ dassanaṃ bhūtaṃ tacchaṃ, na tena aññaṃ diṭṭhaṃ, kiṃsukova diṭṭho, evameva pañcupādānakkhandhavasena catumahābhūtavasena tebhūmakadhammavasena dassanavisuddhipattassāpi khīṇāsavassa dassanaṃ bhūtaṃ tacchaṃ, na tena aññaṃ kathitaṃ, attanā adhigatamaggena dassanavisuddhikaṃ nibbānameva kathitaṃ.

Therein, just as at the time of being black, the seeing of one who saw the kiṃsuka tree was real and true—nothing else was seen by them, only the kiṃsuka was seen—even so, for the destroyer of defilements who has attained purity of vision by way of the six sense bases, that vision is real and true. Nothing else was spoken of by them; only Nibbāna, which is the purity of vision, was spoken of by means of the path they had attained. And just as at the time of being red, at the time of having hanging pods, and at the time of having thick leaves, the seeing of one who saw the kiṃsuka tree was real and true—nothing else was seen by them, only the kiṃsuka was seen—even so, for the destroyer of defilements who has attained purity of vision by way of the five aggregates of clinging, by way of the four great elements, or by way of the phenomena of the three planes, that vision is real and true. Nothing else was spoken of by them; only Nibbāna, which is the purity of vision, was spoken of by means of the path they had attained.

Seyyathāpi[V 3.0100], bhikkhu rañño paccantimaṃ nagaranti idaṃ kasmā āraddhaṃ? Sace tena bhikkhunā taṃ sallakkhitaṃ, athassa dhammadesanatthaṃ āraddhaṃ. Sace na sallakkhitaṃ, athassa iminā nagaropamena tassevatthassa dīpanatthāya āvibhāvanatthāya āraddhaṃ. Tattha yasmā majjhimapadese nagarassa pākārādīni thirāni vā hontu dubbalāni vā, sabbaso vā mā hontu, corāsaṅkā na honti, tasmā taṃ aggahetvā “paccantimaṃ nagara”nti āha. Daḷhuddhāpanti thirapākāraṃ. Daḷhapākāratoraṇanti thirapākārañceva thiratoraṇañca. Toraṇāni nāma hi purisubbedhāni nagarassa alaṅkāratthaṃ karīyanti, coranivāraṇatthānipi hontiyeva. Atha vā toraṇanti piṭṭhasaṅghāṭassetaṃ nāmaṃ, thirapiṭṭhasaṅghāṭantipi attho. Chadvāranti nagaradvāraṃ nāma ekampi hoti dvepi satampi sahassampi, idha pana satthā chadvārikanagaraṃ dassento evamāha. Paṇḍitoti paṇḍiccena samannāgato. Byattoti veyyattiyena samannāgato visadañāṇo. Medhāvīti ṭhānuppattikapaññāsaṅkhātāya medhāya samannāgato.

‘Just as, monk, a king's frontier city’—why was this introduced? If that monk had understood it, then it was introduced for the purpose of teaching the Dhamma to him. If he had not understood it, then it was introduced with this simile of the city for the purpose of illustrating and clarifying that very matter for him. Therein, because in the central regions a city's walls and so on may be strong or weak, or may not exist at all, and there is no suspicion of thieves, therefore, not taking that as an example, he said ‘a frontier city.’ ‘With a strong rampart’ means with a strong wall. ‘With strong walls and gateways’ means both strong walls and strong gateways. For gateways are made three persons high for the adornment of the city, and they also serve to prevent thieves. Alternatively, ‘gateway’ is a name for a door-post; the meaning is also ‘with strong door-posts.’ ‘With six gates’—a city gate may be one, two, a hundred, or a thousand, but here the Teacher, wishing to show a city with six gates, spoke thus. ‘Wise’ means endowed with wisdom. ‘Competent’ means endowed with skill, with clear knowledge. ‘Intelligent’ means endowed with the intelligence called wisdom that arises according to the situation.

Puratthimāya disāyātiādimhi bhūtamatthaṃ katvā evamattho veditabbo – samiddhe kira mahānagare sattaratanasampanno rājā cakkavatti rajjaṃ anusāsati, tassetaṃ paccantanagaraṃ rājāyuttavirahitaṃ[P 3.0061], atha purisā āgantvā [M 3.0103] “amhākaṃ, deva, nagare āyuttako natthi, dehi no kiñci āyuttaka”nti āhaṃsu. Rājā ekaṃ puttaṃ datvā “gacchatha, etaṃ ādāya tattha abhisiñcitvā vinicchayaṭṭhānādīni katvā vasathā”ti. Te tathā akaṃsu. Rājaputto pāpamittasaṃsaggena katipāheyeva surāsoṇḍo hutvā, sabbāni vinicchayaṭṭhānādīni hāretvā, nagaramajjhe dhuttehi parivārito suraṃ pivanto naccagītādiratiyā vītināmeti. Atha rañño āgantvā ārocayiṃsu.

In the text beginning with ‘From the eastern direction,’ taking the actual meaning, it should be understood as follows: It is said that in a prosperous great city, a wheel-turning monarch endowed with the seven treasures rules the kingdom righteously. A frontier city of his was without a royally appointed governor. Then, men came and said, ‘Your Majesty, in our city there is no governor; give us some governor.’ The king gave one of his sons, saying, ‘Go. Taking him, consecrate him there, and having established places of judgment and so on, dwell there.’ They did so. The prince, through association with bad friends, became a drunkard in just a few days. Having abolished all the places of judgment and so on, surrounded by drunkards in the middle of the city, he passed the time drinking liquor, delighting in dancing, singing, and other such pleasures. Then, they came and reported to the king.

Rājā ekaṃ paṇḍitaṃ amaccaṃ āṇāpesi – “gaccha kumāraṃ ovaditvā, vinicchayaṭṭhānādīni kāretvā, puna abhisekaṃ katvā, ehī”ti. Na sakkā, deva, kumāraṃ ovadituṃ, caṇḍo kumāro ghāteyyāpi manti. Athekaṃ balasampannaṃ yodhaṃ āṇāpesi – “tvaṃ iminā saddhiṃ gantvā sace so ovāde na tiṭṭhati, sīsamassa chindāhī”ti. Iti so amacco yodho cāti idaṃ sīghaṃ dūtayugaṃ tattha gantvā dovārikaṃ pucchi – “kahaṃ, bho, nagarassa sāmi kumāro”ti. Esa majjhesiṅghāṭake suraṃ pivanto dhuttaparivārito gītādiratiṃ anubhonto nisinnoti. Atha taṃ dūtayugaṃ gantvā amacco tāvettha, “sāmi, vinicchayaṭṭhānādīni [V 3.0101] kira kāretvā sādhukaṃ rajjaṃ anusāsā”ti āha. Kumāro asuṇanto viya nisīdi. Atha naṃ yodho sīse gahetvā, “sace rañño āṇaṃ karosi, kara, no ce, ettheva te sīsaṃ pātessāmī”ti khaggaṃ abbāhi. Paricārakā dhuttā tāvadeva disāsu palāyiṃsu. Kumāro bhīto sāsanaṃ sampaṭicchi. Athassa te tattheva abhisekaṃ katvā setacchattaṃ ussāpetvā “sammā rajjaṃ anusāsāhī”ti raññā vuttaṃ yathābhūtavacanaṃ niyyātetvā yathāgatamaggameva paṭipajjiṃsu. Imamatthaṃ āvikaronto bhagavā “puratthimāya disāyā”ti āha.

The king ordered a wise minister: ‘Go, admonish the prince, have the places of judgment and so on established, perform the consecration again, and come back.’ The minister replied, ‘It is not possible, Your Majesty, to admonish the prince. The prince is fierce; he might even kill me.’ Then the king ordered a strong warrior: ‘You go with this man, and if the prince does not abide by the admonition, cut off his head.’ Thus, that minister and the warrior, this swift pair of messengers, went there and asked the gatekeeper: ‘Where, sir, is the prince, the lord of the city?’ He informed them: ‘He is seated at the crossroads in the middle of the city, drinking liquor, surrounded by drunkards, experiencing delight in singing and other such pleasures.’ Then that pair of messengers went there, and the minister first said: ‘Lord, it is said you should have the places of judgment and so on established and rule the kingdom well.’ The prince sat as if not hearing. Then the warrior, seizing him by the head, said: ‘If you will carry out the king's command, do so. If not, I will make your head fall right here,’ and unsheathed his sword. The attendant drunkards immediately fled in all directions. The prince, being frightened, accepted the instruction. Then they, right there, having performed his consecration and raised the white parasol, delivered the king's message, the words as they truly were—‘Rule the kingdom righteously’—and returned by the very path they had come. Making this meaning clear, the Blessed One said, ‘From the eastern direction...’

Tatridaṃ [P 3.0062] opammasaṃsandanaṃ – samiddhamahānagaraṃ viya hi nibbānanagaraṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ, sattaratanasamannāgato rājā cakkavatti viya sattabojjhaṅgaratanasamannāgato dhammarājā sammāsambuddho, paccantimanagaraṃ viya sakkāyanagaraṃ, tasmiṃ nagare kūṭarājaputto viya imassa bhikkhuno kūṭacittuppādo, kūṭarājaputtassa dhuttehi parivāritakālo viya imassa bhikkhuno pañcahi nīvaraṇehi samaṅgikālo, dve sīghadūtā viya samathakammaṭṭhānañca vipassanākammaṭṭhānañca, mahāyodhena sīse gahitakālo viya uppannapaṭhamajjhānasamādhinā [M 3.0104] niccalaṃ katvā cittaggahitakālo, yodhena sīse gahitamatte dhuttānaṃ disāsu palāyitvā dūrībhāvo viya paṭhamajjhānamhi uppannamatte nīvaraṇānaṃ dūrībhāvo, “karissāmi rañño sāsana”nti sampaṭicchitamatte vissaṭṭhakālo viya jhānato vuṭṭhitakālo, amaccena rañño sāsanaṃ ārocitakālo viya samādhinā cittaṃ kammaniyaṃ katvā vipassanākammaṭṭhānassa vaḍḍhitakālo, tatthevassa tehi dvīhi dūtehi katābhisekassa setacchattaussāpanaṃ viya samathavipassanākammaṭṭhānaṃ nissāya arahattappattassa vimuttisetacchattussāpanaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Herein is the connection of the simile: The city of Nibbāna should be seen as the prosperous great city. The Dhamma King, the Perfectly Enlightened One, endowed with the seven treasures of the enlightenment factors, should be seen as the wheel-turning monarch endowed with the seven treasures. The city of individuality should be seen as the frontier city. The arising of a deceitful mind in this monk should be seen as the deceitful prince in that city. The time when this monk is endowed with the five hindrances should be seen as the time the deceitful prince was surrounded by drunkards. The serenity meditation subject and the insight meditation subject should be seen as the two swift messengers. The time of grasping the mind, having made it unshakable with the concentration of the first jhāna that has arisen, should be seen as the time the great warrior seized the head. The state of the hindrances being far away just as the first jhāna has arisen should be seen as the state of the drunkards being far away, having fled in all directions, just as the warrior seized the head. The time of emerging from jhāna should be seen as the time of being released just upon accepting, 'I will carry out the king's instruction.' The time of developing the insight meditation subject, having made the mind workable with concentration, should be seen as the time the minister announced the king's instruction. The raising of the white parasol of liberation for one who has attained Arahantship by relying on the serenity and insight meditation subjects should be understood as the raising of the white parasol for that prince who was consecrated right there by those two messengers.

Nagaranti kho bhikkhu imassetaṃ cātumahābhūtikassa kāyassa adhivacanantiādīsu pana cātumahābhūtikassātiādīnaṃ padānaṃ attho heṭṭhā vitthāritova. Kevalaṃ pana viññāṇarājaputtassa nivāsaṭṭhānattā ettha kāyo “nagara”nti vutto, tasseva dvārabhūtattā cha āyatanāni “dvārānī”ti, tesu dvāresu niccaṃ suppatiṭṭhitattā sati “dovāriko”ti, kammaṭṭhānaṃ ācikkhantena dhammarājena pesitattā samathavipassanā “sīghaṃ dūtayuga”nti. Ettha mahāyodho viya samatho, paṇḍitāmacco viya vipassanā veditabbā.

'City,' O bhikkhu, is a designation for this body made of the four great elements. However, as for the meaning of the words beginning with 'of the one made of the four great elements,' it has already been explained in detail below. But merely because it is the dwelling place for the prince that is consciousness, the body is here called a 'city.' Because they are the doors to that very body, the six sense bases are called 'doors.' Because it is always well-established at those doors, mindfulness is called the 'gatekeeper.' Because they are sent by the Dhamma King who points out the meditation subject, serenity and insight are called the 'swift pair of messengers.' Herein, serenity should be known as the great warrior, and insight should be known as the wise minister.

Majjhe siṅghāṭakoti nagaramajjhe siṅghāṭako. Mahābhūtānanti hadayavatthussa nissayabhūtānaṃ mahābhūtānaṃ[V 3.0102]. Vatthurūpassa hi paccayadassanatthamevetaṃ catumahābhūtaggahaṇaṃ kataṃ. Nagaramajjhe pana so rājakumāro viya sarīramajjhe hadayarūpasiṅghāṭake nisinno samathavipassanādūtehi arahattābhisekena abhisiñcitabbo vipassanāviññāṇarājaputto daṭṭhabbo. Nibbānaṃ pana yathābhūtasabhāvaṃ akuppaṃ [P 3.0063] adhikārīti katvā yathābhūtaṃ vacananti vuttaṃ. Ariyamaggo pana yādisova pubbabhāgavipassanāmaggo, ayampi aṭṭhaṅgasamannāgatattā tādisoyevāti katvā yathāgatamaggoti vutto. Idaṃ tāvettha dhammadesanatthaṃ ābhatāya upamāya saṃsandanaṃ.

'At the crossroads in the middle' means a crossroads in the middle of the city. 'Of the great elements' refers to the great elements that are the support for the heart-base. Indeed, this taking of the four great elements is done only for the purpose of showing the condition for the physical base. However, like that prince in the middle of the city, the prince that is insight-consciousness—seated at the crossroads of the heart-material in the middle of the body, to be consecrated with the consecration of Arahantship by the messengers of serenity and insight—should be seen. Furthermore, Nibbāna is called 'the word as it truly is' because it establishes its unshakeable, true nature. The noble path, however, is called 'the path as it has come' because, just as the preliminary insight path is, this noble path too, being endowed with the eight factors, is just like that. This, firstly, is the connection with the simile brought for the purpose of teaching the Dhamma.

Tassevatthassa pākaṭīkaraṇatthaṃ ābhatapakkhe pana idaṃ saṃsandanaṃ – ettha hi chadvārūpamā chaphassāyatanavasena dassanavisuddhipattaṃ khīṇāsavaṃ dassetuṃ [M 3.0105] ābhatā, nagarasāmiupamā pañcakkhandhavasena, siṅghāṭakūpamā catumahābhūtavasena, nagarūpamā tebhūmakadhammavasena dassanavisuddhipattaṃ khīṇāsavaṃ dassetuṃ ābhatā. Saṅkhepato panimasmiṃ sutte catusaccameva kathitaṃ. Sakalenapi hi nagarasambhārena dukkhasaccameva kathitaṃ, yathābhūtavacanena nirodhasaccaṃ, yathāgatamaggena maggasaccaṃ, dukkhassa pana pabhāvikā taṇhā samudayasaccaṃ. Desanāpariyosāne pañhapucchako bhikkhu sotāpattiphale patiṭṭhitoti.

Furthermore, in the section brought forward to make that very meaning clear, this is the connection: Here, the simile of the six doors is brought to show the arahant who has attained purity of vision by way of the six sense bases; the simile of the lord of the city, by way of the five aggregates; the simile of the crossroads, by way of the four great elements; and the simile of the city, by way of the phenomena of the three planes, to show the arahant who has attained purity of vision. In brief, however, in this sutta only the four truths are taught. For by the entire city-equipment, only the truth of suffering is stated; by the phrase 'as it truly is,' the truth of cessation; by 'the path as it has come,' the truth of the path; and craving, the producer of suffering, is the truth of the origin. At the end of the teaching, the monk who asked the question was established in the fruit of stream-entry.

9. Vīṇopamasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Explanation of the Vīṇopama Sutta

246. Navame yassa kassaci, bhikkhave, bhikkhussa vā bhikkhuniyā vāti idaṃ satthā yathā nāma mahākuṭumbiko mahantaṃ kasikammaṃ katvā, nipphannasasso gharadvāre maṇḍapaṃ katvā, ubhatosaṅghassa dānaṃ pavatteyya. Kiñcāpi tena ubhatosaṅghassa dānaṃ paṭṭhapitaṃ, dvīsu pana parisāsu santappitāsu sesajanampi santappetiyeva, evameva bhagavā samadhikāni cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni pāramiyo pūretvā bodhimaṇḍe sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ adhigantvā pavattitavaradhammacakko jetavanamahāvihāre nisinno bhikkhuparisāya ceva bhikkhuniparisāya ca mahādhammayāgaṃ yajanto vīṇopamasuttaṃ ārabhi. Taṃ penetaṃ kiñcāpi dve parisā sandhāya āraddhaṃ, catunnampi pana parisānaṃ avāritaṃ. Tasmā sabbehipi sotabbañceva saddhātabbañca, pariyogāhitvā cassa attharaso vinditabboti.

246. In the ninth sutta, regarding the phrase 'Whatever monk or nun, bhikkhus,' the Teacher spoke just as a great householder, having cultivated a vast field and the harvest having ripened, would set up a pavilion at his house door and offer alms to both Sanghas. Though he had established the alms-giving for both Sanghas, when the two assemblies were satisfied, he certainly satisfied the remaining people as well. In the same way, the Blessed One, having fulfilled the perfections for four incalculable aeons and more than one hundred thousand, attained omniscience at the seat of enlightenment, and having set rolling the supreme Wheel of Dhamma, while seated in the Jetavana Great Monastery, performing the great sacrifice of Dhamma for the assembly of monks and the assembly of nuns, began the Vīṇopama Sutta. And this, indeed, though commenced with the two assemblies in mind, is not restricted to them but open to all four assemblies. Therefore, all should listen to it, place faith in it, and having thoroughly penetrated it, experience the flavour of its meaning.

Tattha [P 3.0064] chandotiādīsu chando nāma pubbuppattikā dubbalataṇhā, so rañjetuṃ na sakkoti[V 3.0103]. Aparāparaṃ uppajjamānā pana balavataṇhā rāgo nāma, so rañjetuṃ sakkoti. Daṇḍādānādīni kātuṃ asamattho pubbuppattiko dubbalakodho doso nāma. Tāni kātuṃ samattho aparāparuppattiko balavakodho paṭighaṃ nāma. Moho pana mohanasammohanavasena uppannaṃ aññāṇaṃ. Evamettha pañcahipi padehi tīṇi akusalamūlāni gahitāni. Tesu gahitesu sabbepi tammūlakā kilesā gahitāva honti. “Chando rāgo”ti vā padadvayena aṭṭhalobhasahagatacittuppādā, “doso [M 3.0106] paṭigha”nti padadvayena dve domanassasahagatacittuppādā, mohapadena lobhadosarahitā dve uddhaccavicikicchāsahagatacittuppādā gahitāti. Evaṃ sabbepi dvādasa cittuppādā dassitāva honti.

Herein, among terms such as ‘chanda,’ ‘chanda’ is weak craving that arises first; it is not able to cause attachment. However, the strong craving arising successively is called ‘rāga’; it is able to cause attachment. The weak anger that arises first, unable to perform actions such as inflicting punishment, is called ‘dosa.’ The strong anger, arising successively and capable of performing such actions, is called ‘paṭigha.’ ‘Moha,’ however, is the ignorance that arises by way of deluding and bewildering. Thus, herein, by these five terms, the three unwholesome roots are taken. When these are taken, all defilements rooted in them are also taken. Alternatively, by the two terms ‘chanda’ and ‘rāga,’ the eight types of consciousness accompanied by greed are taken; by the two terms ‘dosa’ and ‘paṭigha,’ the two types of consciousness accompanied by displeasure are taken; by the term ‘moha,’ the two types of consciousness accompanied by restlessness and doubt, which are devoid of greed and hatred, are taken. In this way, all twelve types of unwholesome consciousness are shown.

Sabhayoti kilesacorānaṃ nivāsaṭṭhānattā sabhayo. Sappaṭibhayoti vadhabandhanādīnaṃ kāraṇattā sappaṭibhayo. Sakaṇṭakoti rāgādīhi kaṇṭakehi sakaṇṭako. Sagahanoti rāgagahanādīhi sagahano. Ummaggoti devalokaṃ vā manussalokaṃ vā nibbānaṃ vā gacchantassa amaggo. Kummaggoti kucchitajegucchibhūtaṭṭhānagamanaekapadikamaggo viya apāyasampāpakattā kummaggo. Duhitikoti ettha ihitīti iriyanā, dukkhā ihiti etthāti, duhitiko. Yasmiñhi magge mūlaphalādikhādanīyaṃ vā sāyanīyaṃ vā natthi, tasmiṃ iriyanā dukkhā hoti, na sakkā taṃ paṭipajjitvā icchitaṭṭhānaṃ gantuṃ. Kilesamaggampi paṭipajjitvā na sakkā sampattibhavaṃ gantunti kilesamaggo duhitikoti vutto. Dvīhitikotipi pāṭho, esevattho. Asappurisasevitoti kokālikādīhi asappurisehi sevito.

‘With fear’ (sabhayo) means it is with fear because it is the dwelling place of the thieves that are the defilements. ‘With peril’ (sappaṭibhayo) means it is with peril because it is the cause of execution, imprisonment, and so forth. ‘Thorny’ (sakaṇṭako) means it is thorny with the thorns of lust and so forth. ‘Dense’ (sagahano) means it is dense with the thickets of lust and so forth. ‘The wrong way’ (ummaggo) means it is not the path for one going to the heavenly world, the human world, or Nibbāna. ‘A bad path’ (kummaggo) means it is a bad path because, like a single-footpath going to a disgusting and repulsive place, it leads to the plane of misery. ‘Of difficult going’ (duhitiko) means: herein, ‘ihiti’ is movement (iriyanā); ‘movement is difficult (dukkhā) here (ettha)’; therefore, it is ‘duhitiko.’ For on a path where there is no edible food such as roots and fruits, movement is difficult; it is not possible to follow it and go to the desired place. Having followed the path of defilements, it is not possible to go to a fortunate existence; therefore the path of defilements is called ‘of difficult going.’ There is also the reading ‘dvīhitiko,’ which has the same meaning. ‘Frequented by ignoble persons’ (asappurisasevito) means it is frequented by ignoble persons such as Kokālika and others.

Tato cittaṃ nivārayeti tehi cakkhuviññeyyehi rūpehi taṃ chandādivasena pavattacittaṃ asubhāvajjanādīhi upāyehi nivāraye. Cakkhudvārasmiñhi iṭṭhārammaṇe rāge uppanne asubhato āvajjantassa cittaṃ nivattati, aniṭṭhārammaṇe dose uppanne mettato āvajjantassa cittaṃ nivattati, majjhattārammaṇe [P 3.0065] mohe uppanne uddesaparipucchaṃ garuvāsaṃ āvajjantassa cittaṃ nivattati. Evaṃ asakkontena pana satthumahattataṃ dhammassa svākkhātatā saṅghassa suppaṭipatti ca āvajjitabbā. Satthumahattataṃ paccavekkhatopi hi dhammassa svākkhātataṃ saṅghassa suppaṭipattiṃ paccavekkhatopi cittaṃ nivattati. Tena vuttaṃ “asubhāvajjanādīhi upāyehi nivāraye”ti.

‘Then, one should restrain the mind’: from those forms cognizable by the eye, one should restrain by means such as reflection on the unlovely that mind which proceeds by way of desire and so forth. For at the eye-door, when lust has arisen toward a pleasing object, the mind turns away for one who reflects on it as unlovely. When aversion has arisen toward an unpleasing object, the mind turns away for one who reflects with loving-kindness. When delusion has arisen toward a neutral object, the mind turns away for one who reflects on learning through instruction and questioning, and dwelling with a teacher. But if one is unable to do so, then one should reflect on the greatness of the Teacher, the well-proclaimed nature of the Dhamma, and the good practice of the Sangha. Indeed, even by reflecting on the greatness of the Teacher, or by reflecting on the well-proclaimed nature of the Dhamma and the good practice of the Sangha, the mind turns away. Therefore, it was said: ‘one should restrain by means such as reflection on the unlovely.’

Kiṭṭhanti kiṭṭhaṭṭhāne uppannasassaṃ. Sampannanti paripuṇṇaṃ sunipphannaṃ. Kiṭṭhādoti sassakhādako[V 3.0104]. Evameva khoti ettha sampannakiṭṭhaṃ viya pañca kāmaguṇā daṭṭhabbā, kiṭṭhādo goṇo viya kūṭacittaṃ, kiṭṭhārakkhassa pamādakālo viya bhikkhuno chasu dvāresu satiṃ pahāya vicaraṇakālo, kiṭṭhārakkhassa pamādamāgamma goṇena gahitagabbhassa kiṭṭhassa khāditattā sassasāmino [M 3.0107] sassaphalānadhigamo viya chadvārarakkhikāya satiyā vippavāsamāgamma pañcakāmaguṇaṃ assādentena cittena kusalapakkhassa nāsitattā bhikkhuno sāmaññaphalādhigamābhāvo veditabbo.

‘Crop’ (kiṭṭha) means the grain grown in a cultivated place. ‘Abundant’ (sampanna) means complete, well-produced. ‘Crop-eater’ (kiṭṭhādo) means a grain-eater. Here, just so, the five strands of sensual pleasure should be seen as an abundant crop; the deceitful mind, as the crop-eating ox; the time when a monk wanders after abandoning mindfulness at the six doors, as the time of negligence for the crop-guard. Just as the crop owner fails to obtain the fruits of the crop because the ox, taking advantage of the crop-guard’s negligence, has eaten the crop with its full ear, so too, it should be understood that a monk fails to attain the fruits of the ascetic life because the wholesome side has been destroyed by the mind which, due to the absence of mindfulness that guards the six doors, savors the five strands of sensual pleasure.

Uparighaṭāyanti dvinnaṃ siṅgānaṃ antare. Suniggahitaṃ niggaṇheyyāti ghaṭāyaṃ patiṭṭhite nāsārajjuke suṭṭhu niggahitaṃ katvā niggaṇheyya. Daṇḍenāti muggarasadisena thūladaṇḍakena. Evañhi so bhikkhave goṇoti evaṃ so kiṭṭhārakkhassa pamādamanvāya yasmiṃ yasmiṃ khaṇe kiṭṭhaṃ otaritukāmo hoti, tasmiṃ tasmiṃ khaṇe evaṃ niggaṇhitvā tāḷetvā osajjanena nibbisevanabhāvaṃ upanīto goṇo.

‘Uparighaṭāyaṃ’ means in the space between the two horns. ‘He should restrain it well’ means, having firmly pressed down on the nose-rope established on the yoke, he should restrain it. ‘Daṇḍena’ means with a thick stick like a mallet. ‘Thus, monks, that ox’ means: thus that ox, on account of the crop-guard's negligence, whenever it desires to enter the crop, at that very moment, having been restrained in this way, struck, and then released with threats, is brought to a state of non-transgression.

Evameva khoti idhāpi sampannakiṭṭhamiva pañca kāmaguṇā daṭṭhabbā, kiṭṭhādo viya kūṭacittaṃ, kiṭṭhārakkhassa appamādo viya imassa bhikkhuno chasu dvāresu satiyā avissajjanaṃ, daṇḍo viya suttanto, goṇassa kiṭṭhābhimukhakāle daṇḍena tāḷanaṃ viya cittassa bahiddhā puthuttārammaṇābhimukhakāle anamataggiyadevadūtaādittaāsīvisūpamaanāgatabhayādīsu [P 3.0066] taṃ taṃ suttaṃ āvajjetvā cittuppādassa puthuttārammaṇato nivāretvā mūlakammaṭṭhāne otāraṇaṃ veditabbaṃ. Tenāhu porāṇā –

‘Just so’ means: here too, the five strands of sensual pleasure should be seen like an abundant crop; the deceitful mind like the crop-eating ox; this monk’s non-abandonment of mindfulness at the six sense doors like the heedfulness of the crop-guard; the suttas like the stick. Just as the ox is struck with a stick when it turns toward the crop, so too, when the mind turns outward toward diverse objects, by reflecting on those various suttas—such as the Anamatagga, the Devadūta, the Āditta, the Āsīvisūpama, the Anāgatabhaya, and so forth—one should know to prevent the arising of mind from going to those diverse objects and bring it down to the primary meditation subject. Therefore, the ancients said:

“Subhāsitaṃ sutvā mano pasīdati,

Dameti naṃ pītisukhañca vindati;

Tadassa ārammaṇe tiṭṭhate mano,

Goṇova kiṭṭhādako daṇḍatajjito”ti.

“Having heard what is well-spoken, the mind becomes clear; one tames it and finds joy and happiness. Then that mind of his stands firm in its object, like a crop-eating ox, threatened by a stick.”

Udujitanti tajjitaṃ. Sudujitanti sutajjitaṃ, sujitantipi attho. Udu, sudūti idaṃ pana nipātamattameva. Ajjhattanti gocarajjhattaṃ. Santiṭṭhatītiādīsu paṭhamajjhānavasena santiṭṭhati, dutiyajjhānavasena sannisīdati, tatiyajjhānavasena ekodi hoti, catutthajjhānavasena [V 3.0105] samādhiyati. Sabbampi vā etaṃ paṭhamajjhānavasena veditabbaṃ. Ettāvatā hi sammāsambuddhena samathānurakkhaṇaindriyasaṃvarasīlaṃ nāma kathitaṃ.

‘Udujitaṃ’ means threatened. ‘Sudujitaṃ’ means well-threatened; the meaning can also be ‘well-conquered.’ ‘Udu’ and ‘sudu,’ however, are merely particles. ‘Internally’ (ajjhattaṃ) means internally within one's meditation resort. In ‘santiṭṭhati,’ etc.: by means of the first jhāna, it stands firm; by means of the second jhāna, it settles down; by means of the third jhāna, it becomes unified; by means of the fourth jhāna, it becomes concentrated. Or all of this can be understood by means of the first jhāna. For by this much, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One has spoken of what is called the virtue of sense restraint which protects tranquility.

Rañño vāti kassacideva paccantarañño vā. Saddaṃ suṇeyyāti paccūsakāle pabuddho kusalena vīṇāvādakena vādiyamānāya madhurasaddaṃ suṇeyya. Rajanīyotiādīsu cittaṃ rañjetīti rajanīyo. Kāmetabbatāya [M 3.0108] kamanīyo. Cittaṃ madayatīti madanīyo. Cittaṃ mucchitaṃ viya karaṇato mucchiyatīti mucchanīyo. Ābandhitvā viya gahaṇato bandhatīti bandhanīyo. Alaṃ me, bhoti vīṇāya saṇṭhānaṃ disvā taṃ anicchanto evamāha. Upadhāraṇeti veṭṭhake. Koṇanti caturassaṃ sāradaṇḍakaṃ.

'Or of a king' means of some border-region king. 'He might hear a sound' means: awakening at dawn, he might hear the sweet sound of a lute being played by a skilled lute-player. In 'rajanīya,' etc.: it causes attachment in the mind, thus it is 'rajanīya' (causing attachment). Because it is desirable, it is 'kamanīya' (desirable). It intoxicates the mind, thus it is 'madanīya' (intoxicating). Because it makes the mind as if to swoon, it is 'mucchanīya' (stupefying). Because it grasps as if binding, it is 'bandhanīya' (binding). 'Enough for me, sir': seeing the form of the lute and not desiring it, he spoke thus. 'Upadhāraṇe' means the tuning pegs. 'Koṇaṃ' means the four-sided plectrum made of heartwood.

So taṃ vīṇanti so rājā “āharatha naṃ vīṇaṃ, ahamassā saddaṃ pasissāmī”ti taṃ vīṇaṃ gahetvā. Dasadhā vātiādīsu paṭhamaṃ tāva dasadhā phāleyya, athassā saddaṃ apassanto satadhā phāleyya, tathāpi apassanto sakalikaṃ sakalikaṃ kareyya, tathāpi apassanto “sakalikā jhāyissanti, saddo pana nikkhamitvā palāyissati, tadā naṃ passissāmī”ti agginā ḍaheyya. Tathāpi apassanto “sallahukāni [P 3.0067] masicuṇṇāni vātena bhassissanti, saddo sāradhaññaṃ viya pādamūle patissati, tadā naṃ passissāmī”ti mahāvāte vā ophuneyya. Tathāpi apassanto “masicuṇṇāni yathodakaṃ gamissanti, saddo pana pāraṃ gacchanto puriso viya nikkhamitvā tarissati, tadā naṃ passissāmī”ti nadiyā vā sīghasotāya pavāheyya.

'So taṃ vīṇaṃ' means: that king, saying, 'Bring that lute, I will see its sound,' took that lute. In 'dasadhā vā,' etc.: first, he would split it into ten parts. Then, not seeing its sound, he would split it into a hundred parts. Still not seeing its sound, he would break it into tiny pieces. Still not seeing it, thinking, 'The pieces will burn, but the sound will come out and flee; then I will see it,' he would burn it with fire. Still not seeing it, thinking, 'The very light ash powders will be scattered by the wind, but the sound will fall at my feet like solid grain; then I will see it,' he would winnow it in a great wind. Still not seeing it, thinking, 'The ash powders will go with the water, but the sound will come out and cross over like a man going to the far shore; then I will see it,' he would set it adrift in a swift-flowing river.

Evaṃ vadeyyāti sabbehipimehi upāyehi apassanto te manusse evaṃ vadeyya. Asatī kirāyanti asatī kira ayaṃ vīṇā, lāmikāti attho. Asatīti lāmakādhivacanametaṃ. Yathāha –

'Thus he would say' means: not seeing the sound by all these means, he would say thus to those people. 'Asatī kirāyaṃ' means: 'Surely, this lute is insubstantial,' the meaning is that it is inferior. This term 'asatī' is a designation for what is inferior. As it is said:

“Asā lokitthiyo nāma, velā tāsaṃ na vijjati;

Sārattā ca pagabbhā ca, sikhī sabbaghaso yathā”ti. (jā. 1.1.61);

“In this world, women are called 'asā' (worthless); for them, no boundary exists. They are passionate and bold, like an all-consuming fire.”

Yathevaṃ yaṃkiñci vīṇā nāmāti na kevalañca vīṇāyeva lāmikā, yatheva pana ayaṃ vīṇā [V 3.0106] nāma, evaṃ yaṃkiñci aññampi tantibaddhaṃ, sabbaṃ taṃ lāmakamevāti attho. Evameva khoti ettha vīṇā viya pañcakkhandhā daṭṭhabbā, rājā viya yogāvacaro. Yathā so rājā taṃ vīṇaṃ dasadhā phālanato paṭṭhāya vicinanto saddaṃ adisvā vīṇāya anatthiko hoti, evaṃ yogāvacaro pañcakkhandhe sammasanto ahanti vā mamanti vā gahetabbaṃ apassanto khandhehi anatthiko hoti. Tenassa taṃ khandhasammasanaṃ [M 3.0109] dassento rūpaṃ samanvesati yāvatā rūpassa gatītiādimāha.

Just as whatever is called a lute: the meaning is that it is not merely the lute alone that is inferior, but just as this thing called a lute is inferior, so too is any other stringed instrument; all of it is simply inferior. In the same way, here the five aggregates should be seen as like the lute, and the yogi practitioner as like the king. Just as that king, searching from the splitting of the lute into ten parts onwards, does not see any sound and becomes disinterested in the lute, so too the yogi practitioner, scrutinizing the five aggregates, not seeing anything to be grasped as 'I' or 'mine,' becomes disinterested in the aggregates. Therefore, showing that scrutinizing of the aggregates, the Blessed One said, 'He investigates form as far as the range of form,' and so on.

Tattha samanvesatīti pariyesati. Yāvatā rūpassa gatīti yattakā rūpassa gati. Tattha gatīti gatigati, sañjātigati, salakkhaṇagati, vibhavagati, bhedagatīti pañcavidhā honti. Tattha idaṃ rūpaṃ nāma heṭṭhā avīcipariyantaṃ katvā upari akaniṭṭhabrahmalokaṃ anto katvā etthantare saṃsarati vattati, ayamassa gatigati nāma.

Therein, 'samanvesati' means 'pariyesati' (he seeks/investigates). 'Yāvatā rūpassa gati' (as far as the range of form) means 'yattakā rūpassa gati' (whatever is the range of form). Therein, 'gati' (range) is of five kinds: gatigati (range of destination), sañjātigati (range of origin), salakkhaṇagati (range of characteristics), vibhavagati (range of dissolution), and bhedagati (range of breaking apart). Among these, this thing called form, having the Avīci hell as its lower limit and the Akaniṭṭha brahma world as its upper limit, wanders and occurs within this space. This is called its 'gatigati' (range of destination).

Ayaṃ pana kāyo neva padumagabbhe, na puṇḍarīkanīluppalādīsu sañjāyati, āmāsayapakkāsayānaṃ pana antare bahalandhakāre duggandhapavanavicarite paramajegucche okāse pūtimacchādīsu kimi viya sañjāyati[P 3.0068], ayaṃ rūpassa sañjātigati nāma.

This body, however, does not arise in the heart of a lotus, nor among white lotuses, blue lotuses, and so on. Rather, it arises in the space between the stomach and the intestines, in a place of thick darkness, pervaded by foul-smelling winds, a most disgusting location, like a worm in rotting fish and the like. This is called the 'sañjātigati' (range of origin) of form.

Duvidhaṃ pana rūpassa lakkhaṇaṃ, “ruppatīti kho, bhikkhave, tasmā rūpa”nti (saṃ. ni. 3.79) evaṃ vutta ruppanasaṅkhātaṃ paccattalakkhaṇañca aniccādibhedaṃ sāmaññalakkhaṇañca, ayamassa salakkhaṇagati nāma.

Furthermore, the characteristic of form is twofold: the individual characteristic, designated as 'affliction' as stated in the passage, 'It is afflicted, O monks, therefore it is called form'; and the general characteristic, which has divisions such as impermanence. This is called its 'salakkhaṇagati' (range of characteristics).

“Gati migānaṃ pavanaṃ, ākāso pakkhinaṃ gati;

Vibhavo gati dhammānaṃ, nibbānaṃ arahato gatī”ti. (pari. 339) –

“The forest is the destination of beasts, the sky is the destination of birds; dissolution is the destination of phenomena, Nibbāna is the destination of the Arahant.”

Evaṃ vutto rūpassa abhāvo vibhavagati nāma. Yo panassa bhedo, ayaṃ bhedagati nāma. Vedanādīsupi eseva nayo. Kevalañhettha upari yāva bhavaggā tesaṃ sañjātigati, salakkhaṇagatiyañca vedayitasañjānanaabhisaṅkharaṇavijānanavasena paccattalakkhaṇaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Thus spoken, the absence of form is called the 'vibhavagati' (range of dissolution). Furthermore, its breaking apart is called the 'bhedagati' (range of breaking apart). In regard to feeling and so on, this same method applies. However, herein, their 'sañjātigati' (range of origin) should be understood as extending up to the peak of existence. And in the 'salakkhaṇagati' (range of characteristics), the individual characteristic should be understood by way of feeling, perceiving, forming, and cognizing.

Tampi tassa na hotīti yadetaṃ rūpādīsu ahanti vā mamanti vā asmīti vā evaṃ niddiṭṭhaṃ [V 3.0107] diṭṭhitaṇhāmānaggāhattayaṃ, tampi tassa khīṇāsavassa na hotīti yathānusandhināva suttāgataṃ. Tena vuttaṃ mahāaṭṭhakathāyaṃ –

As for the phrase 'That too does not occur for him': whatever triad of grasping—by view, craving, and conceit—is indicated in regard to forms and so on as 'I,' 'mine,' or 'I am,' that triad of grasping does not occur for him, the one whose cankers are destroyed. Thus it comes in the sutta in its proper sequence. Therefore, it is said in the Great Commentary:

“Ādimhi sīlaṃ kathitaṃ, majjhe samādhibhāvanā;

Pariyosāne ca nibbānaṃ, esā vīṇopamā kathā”ti.

“In the beginning, morality is taught; in the middle, the development of concentration; and at the end, Nibbāna. This is the discourse with the simile of the lute.”

10. Chappāṇakopamasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse on the Simile of the Six Animals

247. Dasame [M 3.0110] arugattoti vaṇasarīro. Tesaṃyeva arūnaṃ pakkattā pakkagatto. Saravananti kaṇḍavanaṃ. Evameva khoti arugatto puriso viya dussīlapuggalo veditabbo. Tassa kusakaṇṭakehi viddhassa sarapattehi ca asidhārūpamehi vilikhitagattassa bhiyyosomattāya dukkhadomanassaṃ viya tattha tattha sabrahmacārīhi “ayaṃ so imesañca imesañca kammānaṃ kārako”ti vuccamānassa uppajjanadukkhaṃ veditabbaṃ.

10. In the tenth sutta, 'arugatto' means one with a body of sores. Because those very sores have festered, he is 'pakkagatto' (one with a festering body). 'Saravanaṃ' means a forest of arrow-reeds. Just so, an immoral person is to be understood as being like a man with a body of sores. Just as there is pain and displeasure beyond measure for one whose body is pierced by reed-thorns and scraped by reed-leaves that are like sword-blades, so too the suffering that arises for an immoral person when censured by his companions in the holy life here and there, saying, 'This is the one who does these and those deeds,' should be understood.

Labhati vattāranti labhati codakaṃ. Evaṃkārīti evarūpānaṃ vejjakammadūtakammādīnaṃ kārako. Evaṃsamācāroti vidhavā gocarādivasena evarūpagocaro. Asucigāmakaṇṭakoti [P 3.0069] asuddhaṭṭhena asuci, gāmavāsīnaṃ vijjhanaṭṭhena kaṇṭakoti gāmakaṇṭako.

247. 'Labhati vattāraṃ' means he gets a reprover. 'Evaṃkārī' means he is a doer of such things as the work of a physician, the work of a messenger, and so on. 'Evaṃsamācāro' means his resort is of such a nature, by way of resorting to widows and the like. 'Asucigāmakaṇṭako' means he is impure (asuci) in the sense of being unclean, and a village-thorn (gāmakaṇṭako) in the sense of piercing the village-dwellers.

Pakkhinti hatthisoṇḍasakuṇaṃ. Ossajjeyyāti vissajjeyya. Āviñcheyyunti ākaḍḍheyyuṃ. Pavekkhāmīti pavisissāmi. Ākāsaṃ ḍessāmīti ākāsaṃ uppatissāmi.

Pakkhin means the hatthiliṅga bird. Ossajjeyya means one should release. Āviñcheyyuṃ means they would pull. Pavekkhāmi means I will enter. Ākāsaṃ ḍessāmi means I will fly into the sky.

Etesu pana ahi “bhogehi maṇḍalaṃ bandhitvā supissāmī”ti vammikaṃ pavisitukāmo hoti. Susumāro “dūre bilaṃ pavisitvā nipajjissāmī”ti udakaṃ pavisitukāmo hoti. Pakkhī “ajaṭākāse sukhaṃ vicarissāmī”ti ākāsaṃ ḍetukāmo hoti. Kukkuro “uddhanaṭṭhāne chārikaṃ byūhitvā usumaṃ gaṇhanto nipajjissāmī”ti gāmaṃ pavisitukāmo hoti. Siṅgālo “manussamaṃsaṃ khāditvā piṭṭhiṃ pasāretvā sayissāmī”ti āmakasusānaṃ pavisitukāmo hoti. Makkaṭo “ucce rukkhe abhiruhitvā disādisaṃ pakkhandissāmī”ti vanaṃ pavisitukāmo hoti.

Furthermore, among these, the snake, thinking, 'I will sleep, having formed a coil with my body,' desires to enter an anthill. The crocodile, thinking, 'I will lie down, having entered a hole in the deep,' desires to enter the water. The bird, thinking, 'I will roam happily in the open sky,' desires to fly into the sky. The dog, thinking, 'I will lie down, taking warmth after scattering the ashes in the hearth,' desires to enter the village. The jackal, thinking, 'I will sleep, having stretched out my back after eating human flesh,' desires to enter a charnel ground for uncremated bodies. The monkey, thinking, 'I will leap from one direction to another, having climbed a tall tree,' desires to enter the forest.

Anuvidhāyeyyunti [V 3.0108] anugaccheyyuṃ, anuvidhiyeyyuntipi pāṭho, anuvidhānaṃ āpajjeyyunti attho. Yattha so yāti, tattheva gaccheyyunti vuttaṃ hoti. Evamevāti ettha cha pāṇakā viya chāyatanāni daṭṭhabbāni, daḷharajju viya taṇhā, majjhe gaṇṭhi viya avijjā. Yasmiṃ yasmiṃ dvāre ārammaṇaṃ balavaṃ hoti, taṃ taṃ āyatanaṃ tasmiṃ tasmiṃ ārammaṇe āviñchati.

Anuvidhāyeyyuṃ means they would follow after. There is also the reading anuvidhiyeyyuṃ. The meaning is that they would fall into line with the stronger one. It means that wherever that one goes, to that very place they would go. Just so, herein the six animals are to be seen as the six sense bases, craving as the strong rope, and ignorance as the knot in the middle. At whichever sense-door an object is powerful, that sense base pulls one towards that object.

Imaṃ [M 3.0111] pana upamaṃ bhagavā sarikkhakena vā āhareyya āyatanānaṃ vā nānattadassanavasena. Tattha sarikkhakena tāva visuṃ appanākiccaṃ natthi, pāḷiyaṃyeva appitā. Āyatanānaṃ nānattadassanena pana ayaṃ appanā – ahi nāmesa bahi sittasammaṭṭhe ṭhāne nābhiramati, saṅkāraṭṭhānatiṇapaṇṇagahanavammikāniyeva pana pavisitvā nipannakāle abhiramati, ekaggataṃ āpajjati. Evameva cakkhupetaṃ visamajjhāsayaṃ, maṭṭhāsu suvaṇṇabhittiādīsu nābhiramati, oloketumpi na icchati, rūpacittapupphalatādivicittesuyeva [P 3.0070] pana abhiramati. Tādisesu hi ṭhānesu cakkhumhi appahonte mukhampi vivaritvā oloketukāmo hoti.

Now, the Blessed One might have brought this simile either by way of similarity or by way of showing the diversity of the sense bases. Therein, in the case of similarity, there is no separate task of application, as it is already applied in the Pāḷi text itself. However, in showing the diversity of the sense bases, this is the application: A snake, for instance, does not delight in a place outside that is sprinkled and swept smooth. Rather, it is only after entering a rubbish heap, a thicket of grass and leaves, or an anthill that it delights when lying down and attains one-pointedness. Similarly, this eye also has an inclination for the uneven. It does not delight in smooth things like golden walls and so on; it does not even wish to look. Rather, it delights only in variegated things such as painted figures, flowers, fruits, and vine patterns. Indeed, in such places, when the eye is not sufficient, one desires to look even by opening the mouth.

Susumāropi bahi nikkhanto gahetabbaṃ na passati, akkhiṃ nimīletvā carati. Yadā pana byāmasatamattaṃ udakaṃ ogāhitvā bilaṃ pavisitvā nipanno hoti, tadā tassa cittaṃ ekaggaṃ hoti, sukhaṃ supati. Evameva sotampetaṃ bilajjhāsayaṃ ākāsasannissitaṃ, kaṇṇacchiddakūpakeyeva ajjhāsayaṃ karoti, kaṇṇacchiddākāsoyeva tassa saddasavane paccayo hoti. Ajaṭākāsopi vaṭṭatiyeva. Antoleṇasmiñhi sajjhāye kayiramāne na leṇacchadanaṃ bhinditvā saddo bahi nikkhamati, dvāravātapānachiddehi pana nikkhamitvā dhātuparamparā ghaṭṭento āgantvā sotapasādaṃ ghaṭṭeti. Atha tasmiṃ kāle “asukaṃ nāma sajjhāyatī”ti leṇapiṭṭhe nisinnā jānanti.

The crocodile, too, when it comes out, does not see what is to be grasped; it moves with its eyes closed. But when it has dived into water about a hundred fathoms deep, entered a hole, and is lying down, then its mind becomes one-pointed, and it sleeps happily. Similarly, this ear also has an inclination for a cavity, being dependent on space. It has its inclination only in the well that is the ear-hole, and the space of the ear-hole itself is the condition for its hearing of sound. Even open space is suitable. For when a recitation is being done inside a cave, the sound does not go outside by breaking through the cave's roof. Rather, having gone out through the openings of doors and windows, it comes striking the elements in succession and strikes the sensitive organ of the ear. Then, at that time, those sitting outside the cave know, 'Such-and-such is being recited.'

Evaṃ sante sampattagocaratā hoti, kiṃ panetaṃ sampattagocaranti? Āma sampattagocaraṃ. Yadi evaṃ dūre bheriādīsu vajjamānesu “dūre saddo”ti jānanaṃ na bhaveyyāti. No na bhavati. Sotapasādasmiñhi ghaṭṭite “dūre saddo, āsanne saddo, paratīre orimatīre”ti tathā tathā jānanākāro hoti, dhammatā esāti. Kiṃ etāya dhammatāya? Yato yato chiddaṃ, tato tato savanaṃ hoti candimasūriyādīnaṃ dassanaṃ viyāti asampattagocaramevetaṃ.

If this is so, it has the nature of taking an object that has arrived. But then, does this ear-faculty take an object that has arrived? Yes, it takes an object that has arrived. If so, then when drums and so forth are being sounded at a distance, the knowing, 'the sound is distant,' should not occur. No, it is not that it does not occur. For when the ear-sensitivity is struck, the mode of knowing arises in just such a way: 'the sound is distant,' 'the sound is near,' 'on the far shore,' 'on the near shore.' This is its nature. So it is said in the Great Commentary. What of this nature? From wherever there is an opening, from there hearing occurs, just like the seeing of the moon, sun, and so on. Therefore, this ear-faculty indeed takes an object that has not arrived.

Pakkhīpi [V 3.0109] rukkhe vā bhūmiyaṃ vā na ramati. Yadā pana ekaṃ vā dve vā leḍḍupāte atikkamma ajaṭākāsaṃ pakkhando hoti, tadā ekaggacittataṃ āpajjati. Evameva ghānampi ākāsajjhāsayaṃ vātūpanissayagandhagocaraṃ. Tathā [M 3.0112] hi gāvo navavuṭṭhe deve bhūmiṃ ghāyitvā ghāyitvā ākāsābhimukho hutvā vātaṃ ākaḍḍhanti. Aṅgulīhi gandhapiṇḍaṃ gahetvāpi ca upasiṅghanakāle vātaṃ anākaḍḍhanto neva tassa gandhaṃ jānāti.

The bird, too, does not delight in a tree or on the ground. But when, having gone beyond the distance of one or two clod-throws, it soars into the open sky, then it attains one-pointedness of mind. In the same way, the nose-faculty also has an inclination for space and has odor, which is dependent on wind, as its object. Indeed, when it has just rained, cows, having sniffed the ground again and again, face the sky and draw in the wind. And even when one has taken a lump of perfume with the fingers, if at the time of smelling one does not draw in the wind, one does not know its scent at all.

Kukkuropi bahi caranto khemaṭṭhānaṃ na passati, leḍḍudaṇḍādīhi upadduto hoti. Antogāmaṃ pavisitvā uddhanaṭṭhāne [P 3.0071] chārikaṃ byūhitvā nipannassa panassa phāsu hoti. Evameva jivhāpi gāmajjhāsayā āposannissitarasārammaṇā. Tathā hi tiyāmarattiṃ samaṇadhammaṃ katvāpi pātova pattacīvaramādāya gāmaṃ pavisitabbaṃ hoti. Sukkhakhādanīyassa ca na sakkā kheḷena atemitassa rasaṃ jānituṃ.

A dog, too, wandering outside, does not see a safe place and is tormented by clods, sticks, and so on. But for it, having entered a village and scraped aside the ashes in the hearth-place, lying down is pleasant. In the same way, the tongue-faculty also has an inclination for the village and has taste, which is dependent on the water element, as its object. Indeed, even after having practiced the duties of a recluse for the three watches of the night, one should enter the village early in the morning, having taken bowl and robe. Furthermore, it is not possible to know the taste of dry food that has not been moistened by saliva.

Siṅgālopi bahi caranto ratiṃ na vindati, āmakasusāne manussamaṃsaṃ khāditvā nipannasseva panassa phāsu hoti. Evameva kāyopi upādiṇṇakajjhāsayo pathavīsannissitaphoṭṭhabbārammaṇo. Tathā hi aññaṃ upādiṇṇakaṃ alabhamānā sattā attanova hatthatale sīsaṃ katvā nipajjanti. Ajjhattikabāhirā cassa pathavī ārammaṇaggahaṇe paccayo hoti. Susanthatassāpi hi sayanassa heṭṭhāṭhitānampi vā phalakānaṃ na sakkā anisīdantena vā anuppīḷantena vā thaddhamudubhāvo jānitunti ajjhattikabāhirā pathavī etassa phoṭṭhabbajānane paccayo hoti.

A jackal, too, wandering outside, does not find delight; only for one that has eaten human flesh in a charnel ground for uncremated bodies and is lying down is it pleasant. In the same way, the body-faculty also has an inclination for what is clung to and has the tangible, which is dependent on the earth element, as its object. Indeed, beings, not getting another clung-to body, lie down placing their head on their own palm. Furthermore, the internal and external earth element is a condition for its grasping of an object. For, of a well-spread bed, or of planks situated underneath, the state of hardness or softness cannot be known by one who is not sitting down or not pressing upon them. Thus, the internal and external earth element is a condition for this body-faculty in knowing the tangible.

Makkaṭopi bhūmiyaṃ vicaranto nābhiramati, hatthasatubbedhaṃ panassa rukkhaṃ āruyha viṭapapiṭṭhe nisīditvā disāvidisā olokentasseva phāsuko hoti. Evaṃ manopi nānajjhāsayo bhavaṅgapaccayo, diṭṭhapubbepi nānārammaṇajjhāsayaṃ karotiyeva mūlabhavaṅgaṃ panassa paccayo hotīti ayamettha saṅkhepo, vitthārena pana āyatanānaṃ nānattaṃ visuddhimagge āyatananiddese vuttameva.

A monkey, too, roaming on the ground, does not delight; but for it, having climbed a tree a hundred cubits high and, having sat on a branch, looking out at the cardinal and intermediate directions is pleasant. Similarly, the mind also has various inclinations and has the life-continuum as its condition. Even regarding things previously seen, it indeed creates various inclinations towards objects, but the root life-continuum is its condition. This is the summary here. But in detail, the diversity of the sense-bases has been stated in the Visuddhimagga, in the Exposition of the Sense-Bases.

Taṃ cakkhu nāviñchatīti taṇhārajjukānaṃ āyatanapāṇakānaṃ kāyagatāsatithambhe baddhānaṃ nibbisevanabhāvaṃ āpannattā nākaḍḍhatīti imasmiṃ sutte pubbabhāgavipassanāva kathitā.

“The eye does not pull it” means: because the sense-base creatures, which have craving as their rope and are bound to the post of mindfulness directed to the body, have reached a state of non-indulgence, it does not drag them. Thus, in this sutta, only insight belonging to the preliminary part is taught.

11. Yavakalāpisuttavaṇṇanā

11. Exposition of the Yavakalāpi Sutta

248. Ekādasame [V 3.0110][M 3.0113][P 3.0072] yavakalāpīti lāyitvā ṭhapitayavapuñjo. Byābhaṅgihatthāti kājahatthā. Chahi byābhaṅgīhi haneyyunti chahi puthulakājadaṇḍakehi potheyyuṃ. Sattamoti tesu chasu janesu yave pothetvā pasibbake pūretvā ādāya gatesu añño sattamo āgaccheyya. Suhatatarā assāti yaṃ tattha avasiṭṭhaṃ atthi bhusapalāpamattampi, tassa gahaṇatthaṃ suṭṭhutaraṃ hatā.

248. In the eleventh sutta, “heap of barley” means a pile of barley that has been reaped and laid out. “With carrying poles in hand” means with well-made carrying poles in hand. “They would strike with six carrying poles” means they would beat with six thick, well-made carrying poles. “The seventh”: when those six people had threshed the barley, filled their sacks, and gone away, another, a seventh, would come. “It would be more thoroughly struck”: whatever remained there, even just chaff and straw, would be struck even more thoroughly in order to be taken.

Evameva khoti ettha catumahāpatho viya cha āyatanāni daṭṭhabbāni, catumahāpathe nikkhittayavakalāpī viya satto, cha byābhaṅgiyo viya iṭṭhāniṭṭhamajjhattavasena aṭṭhārasa ārammaṇāni, sattamā byābhaṅgī viya bhavapatthanā kilesā. Yathā catumahāpathe ṭhapitā yavakalāpī chahi byābhaṅgīhi haññati, evamime sattā aṭṭhārasahi ārammaṇadaṇḍakehi chasu āyatanesu haññanti. Yathā sattamena suhatatarā honti, evaṃ sattā bhavapatthanakilesehi suhatatarā honti bhavemūlakaṃ dukkhaṃ anubhavamānā.
