Similarly, here, the six sense bases should be seen as like the four-way crossroads. A being is like the heap of barley placed at the four-way crossroads. The eighteen sense-objects—by way of the pleasant, unpleasant, and neutral—are like the six carrying poles. The defilements of craving for existence are like the seventh carrying pole. Just as the heap of barley placed at the four-way crossroads is struck by six carrying poles, so too are these beings struck in the six sense bases by the eighteen object-sticks. And just as it is more thoroughly struck by the seventh, so too are beings more thoroughly struck by the defilements of craving for existence, experiencing suffering rooted in existence.

Idāni nesaṃ taṃ bhavapatthanakilesaṃ dassetuṃ bhūtapubbaṃ, bhikkhavetiādimāha. Tatrāti sudhammāyaṃ bhummaṃ, sudhammāya devasabhāya dvāreti attho. Dhammikā kho devāti dhammikā ete devā nāma, yehi mādisaṃ asurādhipatiṃ gahetvā mayhaṃ bhedanamattampi na katanti sandhāya vadati. Adhammikā devāti adhammikā ete devā nāma, ye mādisaṃ asurādhipatiṃ navagūthasūkaraṃ viya kaṇṭhapañcamehi bandhanehi bandhitvā nisīdāpenti. Evaṃ sukhumaṃ kho, bhikkhave, vepacittibandhananti taṃ kira padumanāḷasuttaṃ viya makkaṭajālasuttaṃ viya ca sukhumaṃ hoti, chettuṃ pana neva vāsiyā na pharasunā sakkā. Yasmā pana citteneva bajjhati, cittena muccati, tasmā [P 3.0073] “vepacittibandhana”nti vuttaṃ.

Now, to show them that defilement of craving for existence, he spoke the words beginning with “Bhikkhus, in the past...” Here, “in that” (tatra) is a locative in the sense of proximity, meaning “at the door of the Sudhammā celestial assembly hall.” “Righteous are the gods” means: “These gods are indeed righteous, who, having captured a lord of asuras like me, did not inflict suffering on me even to the extent of tearing me apart”—this he says with reference to this meaning. “Unrighteous are the gods” means: “These gods are indeed unrighteous, who, having captured a lord of asuras like me, bind him with five bonds, the neck being the fifth, and make him sit like a pig that eats fresh dung.” “Thus subtle, O bhikkhus, is the binding of Vepacitti”—it is said that this binding is as fine as the fiber within a lotus stalk or a spider's web, yet it cannot be cut by an adze or an axe. But since one is bound by the mind alone and freed by the mind alone, therefore it is called “the binding of Vepacitti.”

Tato sukhumataraṃ mārabandhananti kilesabandhanaṃ panesaṃ tatopi sukhumataraṃ, neva cakkhussa āpāthaṃ gacchati, na iriyāpathaṃ nivāreti. Tena hi baddhā sattā pathavitalepi ākāsepi yojanasatampi yojanasahassampi [M 3.0114] gacchantipi āgacchantipi. Chijjamānaṃ panetaṃ ñāṇeneva chijjati, na aññenāti “ñāṇamokkhaṃ bandhana”ntipi vuccati.

As to “The bond of Māra is even more subtle than that”: this bond of the defilements is indeed even more subtle than that. It does not come into the range of the eye, nor does it obstruct the bodily postures. Indeed, beings bound by it travel and return a hundred yojanas or even a thousand yojanas on the earth's surface and in the sky. But when it is to be cut, this bond is cut by knowledge alone, not by anything else. Therefore, it is also called “the bond from which one is freed by knowledge.”

Maññamānoti [V 3.0111] taṇhādiṭṭhimānānaṃ vasena khandhe maññanto. Baddho mārassāti mārabandhanena baddho. Karaṇatthe vā etaṃ sāmivacanaṃ, kilesamārena baddhoti attho. Mutto pāpimatoti mārassa bandhanena mutto. Karaṇattheyeva vā idaṃ sāmivacanaṃ, pāpimatā kilesabandhanena muttoti attho.

“Conceiving” means one who conceives the aggregates by way of craving, views, and conceit. “Bound by Māra” means bound by the bond of Māra. Or, this genitive is used in the sense of the instrumental; the meaning is: bound by the defilement-Māra. “Freed from the Evil One” means freed from the bond of Māra. Or, this genitive should be understood in the sense of the instrumental; the meaning is: freed from the evil bond of the defilements.

Asmīti padena taṇhāmaññitaṃ vuttaṃ. Ayamahasmīti diṭṭhimaññitaṃ. Bhavissanti sassatavasena diṭṭhimaññitameva. Na bhavissanti ucchedavasena. Rūpītiādīni sassatasseva pabhedadīpanāni. Tasmāti yasmā maññitaṃ ābādhaṃ antodosanikantanavasena rogo ceva gaṇḍo ca sallañca, tasmā. Iñjitantiādīni yasmā imehi kilesehi sattā iñjanti ceva phandanti ca papañcitā ca honti pamattākārapattā, tasmā tesaṃ ākāradassanatthaṃ vuttāni.

By the word “I am” is stated conceiving through craving. By “I am this” is stated conceiving through views. “Will exist” is indeed conceiving through views by way of eternalism. “Will not exist” is by way of annihilationism. Terms like “possessed of form” and so on are expositions of the varieties of eternalism itself. Therefore: because this conceiving—in the sense of being an affliction, an internal corruption, and a cutting—is a disease, a boil, and a dart, therefore... The terms “agitated” and so on are stated to show the nature of these defilements, because by these defilements beings are agitated, they tremble, they are subject to proliferation, and they fall into a state of heedlessness.

Mānagatavāre pana mānassa gataṃ mānagataṃ, mānapavattīti attho. Mānoyeva mānagataṃ gūthagataṃ muttagataṃ viya. Tattha asmīti idaṃ taṇhāya sampayuttamānavasena vuttaṃ. Ayamahamasmīti diṭṭhivasena. Nanu ca diṭṭhisampayutto nāma māno natthīti? Āma natthi, mānassa pana appahīnattā diṭṭhi nāma hoti. Mānamūlakaṃ diṭṭhiṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

Now, in the section on the course of conceit, “the course of conceit” (mānagataṃ) means “the occurrence of conceit” (mānapavattī). Conceit itself is “the course of conceit,” like “gone to excrement” (gūthagataṃ). Therein, “I am” is spoken in terms of conceit associated with craving. “This I am” is by way of views. But is there not such a thing as conceit associated with views? Indeed, there is not. However, because conceit is unabandoned, a view comes to be. This was said with reference to a view that is rooted in conceit. The rest is clear everywhere.

Āsīvisavaggo.

The Chapter on the Venomous Snakes

Catuttho paṇṇāsako.

The Fourth Set of Fifty

Saḷāyatanasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on the Six Sense Bases is concluded.

2. Vedanāsaṃyuttaṃ

2. The Connected Discourses on Feeling

1. Sagāthāvaggo

1. The Chapter with Verses

1. Samādhisuttavaṇṇanā

1. Exposition of the Samādhi Sutta

249. Vedanāsaṃyutte [V 3.0112][P 3.0074][M 3.0115] sagāthāvaggassa paṭhame samāhitoti upacārena vā appanāya vā samāhito. Vedanā ca pajānātīti vedanā dukkhasaccavasena pajānāti. Vedanānañca sambhavanti tāsaṃyeva sambhavaṃ samudayasaccavasena pajānāti. Yattha cetāti yatthetā vedanā nirujjhanti, taṃ nibbānaṃ nirodhasaccavasena pajānāti. Khayagāminanti tāsaṃyeva vedanānaṃ khayagāminaṃ maggaṃ maggasaccavasena pajānāti. Nicchāto parinibbutoti nittaṇho hutvā kilesaparinibbānena parinibbuto. Evamettha sutte sammasanacāravedanā kathitā. Gāthāsu dvīhi padehi samathavipassanā kathitā, sesehi catusaccaṃ kathitaṃ. Evamettha sabbasaṅgāhiko catubhūmakadhammaparicchedo vutto.

249. In the first [discourse] of the Sagāthāvagga in the Connected Discourses on Feeling, 'one who is composed' (samāhito) means one who is composed either through access concentration or absorption concentration. 'And he understands feelings' (vedanā ca pajānāti) means he understands feelings in terms of the truth of suffering. 'And the origin of feelings' (vedanānañca sambhavaṃ) means he understands their very origin in terms of the truth of origin. 'Where they cease' (yattha cetā) means he understands that Nibbāna, where these feelings cease, in terms of the truth of cessation. 'Leading to destruction' (khayagāminaṃ) means he understands the path leading to the destruction of those very feelings in terms of the truth of the path. 'Desireless, fully extinguished' (nicchāto parinibbuto) means, having become without craving, he is fully extinguished through the extinction of defilements. Thus, in this sutta, feelings as the domain for insight contemplation are explained. In the verses, serenity and insight are explained by two terms, while the remaining terms explain the Four Truths. In this way, a comprehensive classification of phenomena pertaining to the four planes is presented here.

2. Sukhasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Exposition of the Sukha Sutta

250. Dutiye adukkhamasukhaṃ sahāti adukkhamasukhañca sukhadukkhehi saha. Ajjhattañca bahiddhā cāti attano ca parassa ca. Mosadhammanti nassanasabhāvaṃ. Palokinanti palujjanakaṃ bhijjanasabhāvaṃ. Phussa phussa vayaṃ passanti ñāṇena phusitvā phusitvā vayaṃ passanto. Evaṃ tattha virajjatīti evaṃ tāsu vedanāsu virajjati. Idhāpi sutte sammasanacāravedanā kathitā, gāthāsu ñāṇaphusanaṃ.

250. In the second [discourse], 'accompanied by neither-pain-nor-pleasure' (adukkhamasukhaṃ saha) means neither-pain-nor-pleasure together with pleasure and pain. 'Internally and externally' (ajjhattañca bahiddhā ca) means of oneself and of others. 'Of a decaying nature' (mosadhammaṃ) means having the characteristic of perishing. 'Crumbling' (palokinaṃ) means having the nature of breaking and crumbling. 'Touching and touching, seeing its passing away' (phussa phussa vayaṃ passan) means seeing its passing away by repeatedly touching with knowledge. 'Thus, one becomes dispassionate toward it' (evaṃ tattha virajjati) means thus one becomes dispassionate toward those feelings. Here too, in the discourse, feelings as the domain for insight contemplation are explained; in the verses, touching with knowledge is explained.

3. Pahānasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Exposition of the Pahāna Sutta

251. Tatiye [V 3.0113][P 3.0075] acchecchi taṇhanti sabbampi taṇhaṃ chindi samucchindi. Vivattayi saṃyojananti dasavidhampi saṃyojanaṃ parivattayi nimmūlakamakāsi. Sammāti hetunā kāraṇena. Mānābhisamayāti mānassa dassanābhisamayā, pahānābhisamayā ca. Arahattamaggo hi kiccavasena mānaṃ sampassati, ayamassa [M 3.0116] dassanābhisamayo. Tena diṭṭho pana so tāvadeva pahīyati, diṭṭhavisena diṭṭhasattānaṃ jīvitaṃ viya. Ayamassa pahānābhisamayo.

251. In the third [discourse], 'he cut off craving' (acchecchi taṇhaṃ) means he severed and completely cut off all craving. 'He rolled back the fetters' (vivattayi saṃyojanaṃ) means he overturned the tenfold fetters, making them rootless. 'Rightly' (sammā) means by reason and cause. 'Through the overcoming of conceit' (mānābhisamayā) means through the realization of conceit by way of seeing and by way of abandoning. For the path of arahantship, by way of its function, sees conceit—this is its realization by way of seeing. But once it is seen by that path, it is immediately abandoned, like the life of a being looked upon by a snake with venom in its gaze. This is its realization by way of abandoning.

Antamakāsi dukkhassāti evaṃ arahattamaggena mānassa diṭṭhattā ca pahīnattā ca ye ime “kāyabandhanassa anto jīrati (cūḷava. 278) haritantaṃ vā”ti (ma. ni. 1.304) evaṃ vuttaantimamariyādanto ca, “antamidaṃ, bhikkhave, jīvikāna”nti (itivu. 91; saṃ. ni. 3.80) evaṃ vuttalāmakanto ca, “sakkāyo eko anto”ti (a. ni. 6.61; cūḷani. tissametteyyamāṇavapucchāniddesa 11) evaṃ vuttakoṭṭhāsanto ca, “esevanto dukkhassa sabbapaccayasaṅkhayā”ti (saṃ ni. 2.51; 2.4.71; udā. 71) evaṃ vuttakoṭanto cāti cattāro antā, tesu sabbasseva vaṭṭadukkhassa aduṃ catutthakoṭisaṅkhātaṃ antamakāsi, paricchedaṃ parivaṭumaṃ akāsi, antimasamussayamattāvasesaṃ dukkhamakāsīti vuttaṃ hoti.

'He made an end of suffering' (antamakāsi dukkhassa) means that by the path of arahantship, due to the seeing and abandoning of conceit, he made an end of all the suffering of the round, which is reckoned as the fourth of these four 'ends' (antā) that are spoken of: the end as a final boundary, as in 'the end of the bodily bond decays' and 'a green end'; the lowest end, as in 'this, monks, is the lowest end of livelihoods'; the end as a portion, as in 'the personal identity is one end'; and the end as an extremity, as in 'this is the end of suffering, the cessation of all conditions'. It is said that he made suffering remain only as the final embodiment.

Sampajaññaṃ na riñcatīti sampajaññaṃ na jahati. Saṅkhyaṃ nopetīti ratto duṭṭho mūḷhoti paññattiṃ na upeti, taṃ paññattiṃ pahāya khīṇāsavo nāma hotīti attho. Imasmiṃ sutte ārammaṇānusayo kathito.

'He does not relinquish clear comprehension' (sampajaññaṃ na riñcati) means he does not abandon clear comprehension. 'He does not come to reckoning' (saṅkhyaṃ na upeti) means he does not come to the designation of being passionate, hateful, or deluded; abandoning that designation, he is called one whose taints are destroyed. This is the meaning. In this sutta, the latent tendency related to the object is explained.

4. Pātālasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Exposition of the Pātāla Sutta

252. Catutthe pātāloti pātassa alaṃ pariyatto, natthi ettha patiṭṭhāti pātālo. Asantaṃ avijjamānanti asambhūtattaṃ apaññāyamānattaṃ. Evaṃ vācaṃ bhāsatīti atthi mahāsamudde pātāloti evaṃ vācaṃ. So hi yaṃ taṃ balavāmukhaṃ mahāsamuddassa udakaṃ vegena pakkhanditvā [P 3.0076] cakkavāḷaṃ vā sineruṃ vā āhacca yojanadviyojanadasayojanappamāṇampi uggantvā puna mahāsamudde [V 3.0114] patati, yassa patitaṭṭhāne mahānarakapapāto viya hoti, yaṃ loke balavāmukhanti vuccati. Taṃ sandhāya evaṃ vadati.

252. In the fourth [sutta], pātāla means: it is suitable for, and capable of, falling (`pātassa alaṃ pariyatto`); herein there is no standing place (`natthi ettha patiṭṭhā`); therefore, it is a `pātāla`. Non-existent, not found (`asantaṃ avijjamānaṃ`) means having an untrue meaning (`asambhūtattaṃ`), having a meaning that is not clearly known (`apaññāyamānattaṃ`). Thus he speaks (`evaṃ vācaṃ bhāsati`) refers to this speech: “There is a `pātāla` in the great ocean.” For that water of the great ocean at the `balavāmukha` (powerful mouth), rushing with great force, striking either the Cakkavāḷa mountain range or Mount Sineru, and rising up to a measure of one, two, or even ten yojanas, falls again into the great ocean. The place where it falls is like a great precipice of hell, which is called a `balavāmukha` in the world. Referring to that, he speaks thus.

Yasmā pana tattha tathārūpānaṃ macchakacchapadevadānavānaṃ patiṭṭhāpi hoti sukhanivāsopi, tasmā asantaṃ asaṃvijjamānaṃ taṃ taṃ vācaṃ bhāsati nāma. Yasmā pana sabbaputhujjanā sārīrikāya dukkhavedanāya patiṭṭhātuṃ na sakkonti, tasmā pātassa alanti atthena ayameva pātāloti dassento sārīrikānaṃ kho etaṃ bhikkhavetiādimāha.

However, since in that place there is a footing and a pleasant dwelling for suitable fish, turtles, devas, and asuras, therefore, it is called speaking words that are untrue and non-existent. But because all worldlings are unable to stand firm in the face of bodily painful feeling, therefore, to show that this very [feeling] is the `pātāla` in the sense of 'it is capable of falling,' He said the words beginning with: 'Monks, this is an epithet for bodily [painful feelings]...'

Pātāle [M 3.0117] na paccuṭṭhāsīti pātālasmiṃ na patiṭṭhāsi. Gādhanti patiṭṭhaṃ. Akkandatīti anibaddhaṃ vippalāpaṃ vilapanto kandati. Dubbaloti dubbalañāṇo. Appathāmakoti ñāṇathāmassa parittatāya parittathāmako. Imasmiṃ sutte ariyasāvakoti sotāpanno. Sotāpanno hi ettha dhuraṃ, balavavipassako na tikkhabuddhi uppannaṃ vedanaṃ ananuvattitvā patiṭṭhātuṃ samattho yogāvacaropi vaṭṭati.

He did not stand firm in the pātāla (`pātāle na paccuṭṭhāsi`) means he did not find a footing in the `pātāla`. A firm footing (`gādhaṃ`) means a standing place (`patiṭṭhaṃ`). He cries out (`akkandati`) means, lamenting with incoherent and perverse speech, he wails. Weak (`dubbalo`) means weak in wisdom. Without strength (`appathāmako`) means having little strength due to the slightness of the strength of his wisdom. In this sutta, noble disciple (`ariyasāvako`) means a stream-enterer. Indeed, the stream-enterer is the principal one here. However, a practitioner of meditation (`yogāvacara`) who has strong insight and sharp wisdom, and is able to stand firm without pursuing an arisen feeling, is also suitable.

5. Daṭṭhabbasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Exposition of the Daṭṭhabba Sutta

253. Pañcame dukkhato daṭṭhabbāti vipariṇāmanavasena dukkhato daṭṭhabbā. Sallatoti dukkhāpanavinivijjhanaṭṭhena sallāti daṭṭhabbā. Aniccatoti adukkhamasukhā hutvā abhāvākārena aniccato daṭṭhabbā. Addāti addasa. Santanti santasabhāvaṃ.

253. In the fifth [sutta], to be seen as suffering (`dukkhato daṭṭhabbā`) means it is to be seen as suffering by way of its changing nature. As a dart (`sallato`) means it is to be seen as a dart because of its nature of inflicting pain and piercing. As impermanent (`aniccato`) means that neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling, having come to be, is to be seen as impermanent through its characteristic of ceasing to exist. He saw (`addā`) means he perceived (`addasa`). The peaceful (`santaṃ`) means the peaceful state (`santasabhāvaṃ`).

6. Sallasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Exposition of the Salla Sutta

254. Chaṭṭhe tatrāti tesu dvīsu janesu. Anuvedhaṃ vijjheyyāti tasseva vaṇamukhassa aṅgulantare vā dvaṅgulantare vā āsannapadese anugatavedhaṃ. Evaṃ viddhassa hi sā anuvedhā vedanā [P 3.0077] paṭhamavedanāya balavatarā hoti, pacchā uppajjamānā domanassavedanāpi evameva purimavedanāya balavatarā hoti. Dukkhāya vedanāya nissaraṇanti dukkhāya vedanāya hi samādhimaggaphalāni nissaraṇaṃ, taṃ so na jānāti, kāmasukhameva nissaraṇanti jānāti. Tāsaṃ vedanānanti tāsaṃ sukhadukkhavedanānaṃ. Saññutto naṃ vedayatīti kilesehi sampayuttova hutvā taṃ vedanaṃ [V 3.0115] vedayati, na vippayutto. Saññutto dukkhasmāti karaṇatthe nissakkaṃ, dukkhena sampayuttoti attho. Saṅkhātadhammassāti viditadhammassa tulitadhammassa. Bahussutassāti pariyattibahussutassa paṭivedhabahussutassa ca. Sammā pajānāti bhavassa pāragūti bhavassa pāraṃ nibbānaṃ gato, tadeva nibbānaṃ sammā pajānāti. Imasmimpi sutte ārammaṇānusayova kathito. Ariyasāvakesu ca khīṇāsavo ettha dhuraṃ, anāgāmīpi vaṭṭatīti vadanti.

254. In the sixth [sutta], there (`tatrā`) refers to those two persons. He would be pierced by a subsequent dart (`anuvedhaṃ vijjheyya`) means a subsequent piercing in a nearby place, one or two finger-breadths away from that very wound-opening. Indeed, for one thus pierced, that feeling from the subsequent piercing is stronger than the first feeling. Likewise, the feeling of displeasure that arises later is also stronger than the previous feeling. As for the escape from painful feeling (`dukkhāya vedanāya nissaraṇaṃ`): indeed, the escape from painful feeling is absorption, the path, and fruition. He does not know that; he knows only sensual pleasure as the escape. Of those feelings (`tāsaṃ vedanānaṃ`) means of those pleasant and painful feelings. Conjoined, he feels it (`saññutto naṃ vedayati`) means that, being conjoined with defilements, he experiences that feeling, not when disjoined from them. As for conjoined with suffering (`saññutto dukkhasmā`): the ablative case should be understood in the instrumental sense; the meaning is 'conjoined with suffering' (`dukkhena sampayutto`). Of one who has comprehended the Dhamma (`saṅkhātadhammassa`) means of one who has known the Dhamma, who has weighed the Dhamma. Of one who is very learned (`bahussutassa`) means of one who is very learned in the scriptures and in penetration. One who has gone beyond existence rightly understands (`sammā pajānāti bhavassa pāragū`) means: having gone to the far shore of existence, Nibbāna, he rightly understands that very Nibbāna. In this sutta, too, the latent tendency regarding the object is spoken of. And among the noble disciples, the one whose cankers are destroyed is the principal one here; they say that a non-returner is also suitable.

7. Paṭhamagelaññasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the First Discourse on Sickness.

255. Sattame [M 3.0118] yena gilānasālā tenupasaṅkamīti “sadevake loke aggapuggalo tathāgatopi gilānupaṭṭhānaṃ gacchati, upaṭṭhātabbayuttakā nāma gilānāti bhikkhū saddahitvā okappetvā gilāne upaṭṭhātabbe maññissantī”ti ca “ye tattha kammaṭṭhānasappāyā, tesaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathessāmī”ti ca cintetvā upasaṅkami. Kāye kāyānupassītiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ parato vakkhāma. Aniccānupassīti aniccataṃ anupassanto. Vayānupassīti vayaṃ anupassanto. Virāgānupassīti virāgaṃ anupassanto. Nirodhānupassīti nirodhaṃ anupassanto. Paṭinissaggānupassīti paṭinissaggaṃ anupassanto.

255. In the seventh [sutta], regarding the passage 'he approached the sick hall,' he approached thinking: "Even the Tathāgata, the foremost person in the world with its devas, goes to attend to the sick. The monks, having believed and accepted this, will consider that the sick are to be attended to." And also: "For those there for whom a meditation subject is suitable, I will teach a meditation subject." What should be said regarding 'contemplating the body in the body,' etc., that we will speak of later. 'Contemplating impermanence' means contemplating the state of impermanence. 'Contemplating vanishing' means contemplating vanishing. 'Contemplating dispassion' means contemplating dispassion. 'Contemplating cessation' means contemplating cessation. 'Contemplating relinquishment' means contemplating relinquishment.

Ettāvatā kiṃ dassitaṃ hoti? Imassa bhikkhuno āgamanīyapaṭipadā, satipaṭṭhānāpi hi pubbabhāgāyeva, sampajaññepi aniccānupassanā vayānupassanā virāgānupassanāti ca imāpi tisso anupassanā pubbabhāgāyeva, nirodhānupassanāpi paṭinissaggānupassanāpi imā dve missakā. Ettāvatā [P 3.0078] imassa bhikkhuno bhāvanākālo dassitoti. Sesaṃ vuttanayameva.

By this much, what is shown? The preliminary path of practice for this monk. For indeed, the establishments of mindfulness are only the preliminary part. And in clear comprehension, these three contemplations—contemplation of impermanence, contemplation of vanishing, and contemplation of dispassion—are also only the preliminary part. The contemplation of cessation and the contemplation of relinquishment, these two are mixed. By this much, the time for this monk's cultivation is shown. The rest is as has been explained.

8-9. Dutiyagelaññasuttādivaṇṇanā

8-9. Commentary on the Second Discourse on Sickness, etc.

256-257. Aṭṭhame imameva phassaṃ paṭiccāti vutte bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ, atthato panetaṃ ninnānākaraṇaṃ. Kāyova hi ettha phassoti vutto. Navamaṃ uttānameva.

In the eighth [sutta], when it is said 'dependent on contact,' this was spoken according to the disposition of those who were to understand. But in terms of meaning, this does not constitute a difference. For indeed, it is the body itself that is spoken of here as 'contact.' The ninth [sutta] is self-evident.

10. Phassamūlakasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse Rooted in Contact.

258. Dasame [V 3.0116] sukhavedaniyanti sukhavedanāya paccayabhūtaṃ. Sesesupi eseva nayo. Anupadavaṇṇanā panettha heṭṭhā vitthāritāva. Imasmiṃ suttadvaye sammasanacāravedanā kathitā.

258. In the tenth [sutta], 'conducive to pleasant feeling' means that which has become a condition for pleasant feeling. In the remaining cases, this is the same method. The word-by-word commentary on this has already been elaborated below. In these two suttas, feeling as the resort for comprehension is taught.

Sagāthāvaggo paṭhamo.

The first is the Chapter with Verses.

2. Rahogatavaggo

2. The Chapter on Seclusion.

1. Rahogatasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on Seclusion.

259. Rahogatavaggassa [M 3.0119] paṭhame yaṃ kiñci vedayitaṃ, taṃ dukkhasminti yaṃ kiñci vedayitaṃ, taṃ sabbaṃ dukkhanti attho. Saṅkhārānaṃyeva aniccatantiādīsu yā esā saṅkhārānaṃ aniccatā khayadhammatā vayadhammatā vipariṇāmadhammatā, etaṃ sandhāya yaṃ kiñci vedayitaṃ, taṃ dukkhanti mayā bhāsitanti dīpeti. Yā hi saṅkhārānaṃ aniccatā, vedanānampi sā aniccatā eva. Aniccatā ca nāmesā maraṇaṃ, maraṇato uttari dukkhaṃ nāma natthīti iminā adhippāyena sabbā vedanā dukkhāti vuttā. Atha kho pana bhikkhu mayāti idaṃ na kevalaṃ ahaṃ vedanānaṃyeva nirodhaṃ paññāpemi, imesampi dhammānaṃ nirodhaṃ paññāpemīti dassanatthaṃ āraddhaṃ. Vūpasamo ca passaddhiyo ca evarūpāya desanāya bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena [P 3.0079] vuttā. Saññāvedayitanirodhaggahaṇena cettha cattāro āruppā gahitāva hontīti veditabbā.

259. In the first [discourse] of the Chapter on Seclusion, the phrase 'whatever is felt, that is in suffering' means: whatever is felt, all that is suffering. Regarding the passage beginning 'the impermanence of formations,' it shows that with reference to this impermanence, this nature of decay, this nature of vanishing, and this nature of change of formations, it was spoken by me: 'Whatever is felt, that is suffering.' For whatever is the impermanence of formations, that is also the impermanence of feelings. And this impermanence is death; there is no suffering beyond death. With this intention, all feelings are said to be suffering. The phrase 'But, bhikkhus, by me' is begun to show: 'I do not make known the cessation of feelings alone; I also make known the cessation of these phenomena.' 'Calming' and 'tranquilities' are spoken of according to the disposition of those who are to understand by such a teaching. And here, it should be known that by the taking up of 'the cessation of perception and feeling,' the four formless attainments are included.

2-3. Paṭhamaākāsasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-3. Commentary on the First Ākāsa Sutta, and so forth.

260-261. Dutiye puthū vāyanti mālutāti bahū vātā vāyanti. Sesaṃ uttānatthamevāti. Tatiyaṃ vinā gāthāhi bujjhantānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ.

In the second sutta, ‘Puthū vāyanti mālutā’ means ‘many winds blow.’ The rest has an obvious meaning. The third sutta was spoken with three verses, according to the inclination of those who could understand.

4. Agārasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Agāra Sutta.

262. Catutthe [V 3.0117] puratthimāti puratthimāya. Evaṃ sabbattha. Sāmisāpi sukhā vedanātiādīsu sāmisā sukhā nāma kāmāmisapaṭisaṃyuttā vedanā. Nirāmisā sukhā nāma paṭhamajjhānādivasena vipassanāvasena anussativasena ca uppannā vedanā. Sāmisā dukkhā nāma kāmāmiseneva sāmisā vedanā, nirāmisā dukkhā nāma anuttaresu vimokkhesu pihaṃ upaṭṭhāpayato pihapaccayā uppannadomanassavedanā. Sāmisā adukkhamasukhā nāma kāmāmiseneva sāmisā vedanā. Nirāmisā adukkhamasukhā nāma catutthajjhānavasena uppannā adukkhamasukhā vedanā.

262. In the fourth sutta, ‘puratthimā’ means ‘from the eastern direction.’ Thus it should be understood for all terms. In the texts beginning with ‘sāmisā sukhā vedanā,’ ‘sāmisā sukhā’ refers to a feeling connected with the gratification of the senses. ‘Nirāmisā sukhā’ refers to a feeling that has arisen by way of the first jhāna and so forth, by way of insight, and by way of recollection. ‘Sāmisā dukkhā’ refers to a feeling connected with the gratification of the senses. ‘Nirāmisā dukkhā’ refers to the feeling of displeasure that has arisen on account of longing for one who establishes longing for the supreme liberations. ‘Sāmisā adukkhamasukhā’ refers to a feeling connected with the gratification of the senses. ‘Nirāmisā adukkhamasukhā’ refers to a neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling that has arisen by way of the fourth jhāna.

5-8. Paṭhamaānandasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-8. Commentary on the First Ānanda Sutta, and so forth.

263-266. Pañcamādīni [M 3.0120] cattāri heṭṭhā kathitanayāneva. Purimāni panettha dve paripuṇṇapassaddhikāni, pacchimāni upaḍḍhapassaddhikāni. Desanāya bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttāni.

The four suttas beginning with the fifth are of the same method as explained above. However, among these, the first two have complete tranquility; the latter two were spoken with a teaching of partial tranquility, according to the inclination of those who could understand.

9-10. Pañcakaṅgasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Pañcakaṅga Sutta, and so forth.

267-268. Navame pañcakaṅgo thapatīti pañcakaṅgoti tassa nāmaṃ, vāsipharasunikhādanadaṇḍamuggarakāḷasuttanāḷisaṅkhātehi vā pañcahi aṅgehi samannāgatattā so pañcakaṅgoti paññāto. Thapatīti vaḍḍhakījeṭṭhako. Udāyīti paṇḍitaudāyitthero. Pariyāyanti kāraṇaṃ. Dvepānandāti dvepi, ānanda, pariyāyenāti [P 3.0080] kāraṇena. Ettha ca kāyikacetasikavasena dve veditabbā, sukhādivasena tissopi, indriyavasena sukhindriyādikā pañca, dvāravasena cakkhusamphassajādikā cha, upavicāravasena “cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā somanassaṭhāniyaṃ rūpaṃ upavicaratī”tiādikā aṭṭhārasa, cha gehasitāni somanassāni, cha nekkhammasitāni, cha gehasitāni domanassāni, cha nekkhammasitāni, cha gehasitā upekkhā, cha nekkhammasitāti evaṃ chattiṃsa. Tā atīte chattiṃsa, anāgate chattiṃsa, paccuppanne chattiṃsāti evaṃ aṭṭhasataṃ vedanā veditabbā.

In the ninth sutta, regarding ‘Pañcakaṅgo thapati’: ‘Pañcakaṅga’ was his name; or, he was known as ‘Pañcakaṅga’ because he was endowed with five tools, namely, an adze, an axe, a chisel, a long-handled mallet, and a measuring line. ‘Thapati’ means the head carpenter. ‘Udāyī’ is the Elder Udāyī the Wise. ‘Pariyāya’ means reason. ‘Dvepānanda’ means ‘two, Ānanda,’ and ‘pariyāyena’ means ‘by reason.’ And here, feelings should be understood as two by way of the bodily and the mental; and also three by way of the pleasant and so forth; five by way of the faculties, such as the pleasure faculty; six by way of the doors, such as that born of eye-contact; eighteen by way of reflection, such as, “having seen a form with the eye, one reflects on a form that is a basis for joy,” and so forth. There are six joys based on the household life, six joys based on renunciation; six sorrows based on the household life, six sorrows based on renunciation; six equanimities based on the household life, and six equanimities based on renunciation—thus thirty-six. These should be understood as thirty-six in the past, thirty-six in the future, and thirty-six in the present, thus one hundred and eight feelings.

Pañcime [V 3.0118] ānanda kāmaguṇāti ayaṃ pāṭiyekko anusandhi. Na kevalañhi dve ādiṃ katvā vedanā bhagavatā paññattā, pariyāyena ekāpi vedanā kathitā, taṃ dassento pañcakaṅgassa thapatino vādaṃ upatthambhetuṃ imaṃ desanaṃ ārabhi. Abhikkantataranti sundarataraṃ. Paṇītataranti atappakataraṃ. Ettha ca catutthajjhānato paṭṭhāya adukkhamasukhā vedanā, sāpi santaṭṭhena paṇītaṭṭhena ca sukhanti vuttā. Nirodho avedayitasukhavasena sukhaṃ nāma jāto. Pañcakāmaguṇavasena hi aṭṭhasamāpattivasena ca uppannaṃ vedayitaṃ sukhaṃ nāma, nirodho avedayitasukhaṃ nāma. Iti vedayitasukhaṃ vā hotu avedayitasukhaṃ vā, niddukkhabhāvasaṅkhātena sukhaṭṭhena ekantasukhameva jātaṃ.

“Pañcime, Ānanda, kāmaguṇā” (These five strands of sensual pleasure, Ānanda)—this is a separate connection. For not only were feelings, beginning with two, laid down by the Blessed One, but a single feeling was also taught by way of another reason. Wishing to show this and to support the statement of Pañcakaṅga the carpenter, He began this discourse. “Abhikkantataraṃ” means ‘more beautiful.’ “Paṇītataraṃ” means ‘more sublime.’ Here, the neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling from the fourth jhāna onwards is also called “pleasure” due to its peaceful and sublime nature. Cessation becomes named “pleasure” by way of being a pleasure that is not felt. The pleasure that has arisen by way of the five strands of sensual pleasure and by way of the eight attainments is called “felt pleasure,” while cessation is called “unfelt pleasure.” Thus, whether it is felt pleasure or unfelt pleasure, it becomes exclusively pleasure because of the meaning of ‘pleasure,’ which is described as the state of being without suffering.

Yattha yatthāti yasmiṃ yasmiṃ ṭhāne. Sukhaṃ upalabbhatīti vedayitaṃ sukhaṃ vā avedayitaṃ sukhaṃ vā upalabbhati. Taṃ taṃ tathāgato sukhasmiṃ paññapeti, taṃ sabbaṃ tathāgato niddukkhabhāvaṃ sukhasmiṃyeva paññapetīti idha [M 3.0121] bhagavā nirodhasamāpattiṃ sīsaṃ katvā neyyapuggalassa vasena arahattanikūṭeneva desanaṃ niṭṭhāpesi. Dasamaṃ uttānatthamevāti.

‘Yattha yattha’ means ‘in whatever place.’ ‘Sukhaṃ upalabbhati’ means ‘whether felt pleasure or unfelt pleasure is found.’ ‘Taṃ taṃ Tathāgato sukhasmiṃ paññapeti’ means ‘All of that the Tathāgata declares simply as pleasure, because of its state of being without suffering.’ Here, the Blessed One, making the attainment of cessation the pinnacle, concluded the discourse for the sake of a person to be guided, with the very peak of the fruit of Arahantship. The tenth sutta has an obvious meaning.

Rahogatavaggo dutiyo.

The Second Chapter on Solitude.

3. Aṭṭhasatapariyāyavaggo

3. The Chapter on the One Hundred and Eight Modes

1. Sīvakasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Sīvaka Sutta

269. Tatiyavaggassa [P 3.0081] paṭhame moḷiyasīvakoti sīvakoti tassa nāmaṃ. Cūḷā panassa atthi, tasmā moḷiyasīvakoti vuccati. Paribbājakoti channaparibbājako. Pittasamuṭṭhānānīti pittapaccayāni. Vedayitānīti vedanā. Tattha pittapaccayā tisso vedanā uppajjanti. Kathaṃ? Ekacco hi “pittaṃ me kupitaṃ dujjānaṃ kho pana jīvita”nti dānaṃ deti, sīlaṃ samādiyati uposathakammaṃ karoti, evamassa kusalavedanā uppajjati. Ekacco “pittabhesajjaṃ karissāmī”ti pāṇaṃ hanati, adinnaṃ ādiyati, musā bhaṇati, dasa dussīlyakammāni karoti, evamassa akusalavedanā uppajjati. Ekacco “ettakenapi me bhesajjakaraṇena pittaṃ na [V 3.0119] vūpasammati, alaṃ yaṃ hoti. taṃ hotū”ti majjhatto kāyikavedanaṃ adhivāsento nipajjati, evaṃ assa abyākatavedanā uppajjati.

1. In the first sutta of the third chapter, regarding 'Moḷiyasīvaka': 'Sīvaka' is his name. Furthermore, since he has a large topknot, he is called 'Moḷiyasīvaka.' 'Wanderer' means a 'covered' wanderer. 'Originated from bile' means caused by bile. 'Things felt' means feelings. Therein, three feelings arise with bile as their cause. How? For instance, one person, thinking, 'My bile is agitated, and life is indeed uncertain,' gives alms, undertakes the precepts, and performs the Uposatha observance; thus, a wholesome feeling arises in him. Another, thinking, 'I will prepare a medicine for bile,' kills a living being, takes what is not given, speaks falsely, and commits the ten unwholesome deeds; thus, an unwholesome feeling arises in him. Yet another, thinking, 'Even with this much preparation of medicine, my bile does not subside. Enough! Let what will be, be,' becomes indifferent and lies down, enduring the bodily feeling; thus, an indeterminate feeling arises in him.

Sāmampi kho etanti taṃ taṃ pittavikāraṃ disvā attanāpi etaṃ veditabbaṃ. Saccasammatanti bhūtasammataṃ. Lokopi hissa sarīre sabalavaṇṇatādipittavikāraṃ disvā “pittamassa kupita”nti jānāti. Tasmāti yasmā sāmañca viditaṃ lokassa ca saccasammataṃ atidhāvanti, tasmā. Semhasamuṭṭhānādīsupi eseva nayo. Ettha pana sannipātikānīti tiṇṇampi pittādīnaṃ kopena samuṭṭhitāni. Utupariṇāmajānīti visabhāgaututo jātāni. Jaṅgaladesavāsīnañhi anupadese vasantānaṃ visabhāgo utu uppajjati, anupadesavāsīnañca jaṅgaladeseti evaṃ malayasamuddatīrādivasenāpi utuvisabhāgatā uppajjatiyeva. Tato jātāti utupariṇāmajātāni nāma.

269. 'This is also to be known by oneself' means that seeing that particular disorder of bile, this should be known by oneself. 'Regarded as true' means regarded as real. The world, too, seeing in one's body the disorder of bile, such as the state of having spots and blotches in complexion, knows, 'This person's bile is agitated.' 'Therefore' is said because they transgress what is known by oneself and is regarded as true by the world; therefore... In cases of feelings arising from phlegm and so on, this is the same method. Here, 'arising from a combination' means those arising from the disturbance of all three—bile, phlegm, and wind. 'Born from a change of season' means born from an unsuitable season. For those dwelling in an arid region, an unsuitable season arises when they are dwelling in a marshy region, and for those dwelling in a marshy region, when in an arid region. In this way, an unsuitability of season also arises by means of arid regions, the seashore, and so on. Those born from that are called 'born from a change of season.'

Visamaparihārajānīti [M 3.0122] mahābhāravahanasudhākoṭṭanādito vā [P 3.0082] avelāya carantassa sappaḍaṃsakūpapātādito vā visamaparihārato jātāni. Opakkamikānīti “ayaṃ coro vā pāradāriko vā”ti gahetvā jaṇṇukakapparamuggarādīhi nippothanaupakkamaṃ paccayaṃ katvā uppannāni. Etaṃ bahi upakkamaṃ labhitvā koci vuttanayeneva kusalaṃ karoti, koci akusalaṃ, koci adhivāsento nipajjati. Kammavipākajānīti kevalaṃ kammavipākato, jātāni. Tesupi hi uppannesu vuttanayeneva koci kusalaṃ karoti, koci akusalaṃ, koci adhivāsento nipajjati. Evaṃ sabbavāresu tividhāva vedanā honti.

'Born from improper care' means those arising from improper care, such as from carrying heavy loads, pounding plaster, or traveling at improper times, or from causes such as snakebites or falling into wells. 'Born from assault' means those that arise when, having been seized as 'This is a thief' or 'This is an adulterer,' one is beaten with knees, elbows, clubs, and so on, making that assault a condition. Having received such an external assault, a certain person performs wholesome actions in the manner already stated; a certain person, unwholesome actions; and a certain person endures it and lies down. 'Born from the result of kamma' means those born solely from the result of kamma. When these have arisen, in the very manner already stated, a certain person performs wholesome actions; a certain person, unwholesome actions; and a certain person endures it and lies down. Thus, in all instances, feelings are of three kinds.

Tattha purimehi sattahi kāraṇehi uppannā sārīrikā vedanā sakkā paṭibāhituṃ, kammavipākajānaṃ pana sabbabhesajjānipi sabbaparittānipi nālaṃ paṭighātāya. Imasmiṃ sutte lokavohāro nāma kathitoti.

Therein, physical feeling arising from the first seven causes can be warded off, but those born of the result of kamma cannot be counteracted by any protective charms. In this sutta, what is called 'worldly convention' is taught.

2-10. Aṭṭhasatasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-10. Commentary on the Sutta on the One Hundred and Eight Modes, and Others.

270-278. Dutiye aṭṭhasatapariyāyanti aṭṭhasatassa kāraṇabhūtaṃ. Dhammapariyāyanti dhammakāraṇaṃ. Kāyikā ca cetasikā cāti ettha kāyikā kāmāvacareyeva labbhanti, cetasikā catubhūmikāpi[V 3.0120]. Sukhātiādīsu sukhā vedanā arūpāvacare natthi, sesāsu tīsu bhūmīsu labbhanti, dukkhā kāmāvacarāva, itarā catubhūmikā. Pañcake sukhindriyadukkhindriyadomanassindriyāni kāmāvacarāneva, somanassindriyaṃ tebhūmakaṃ, upekkhindriyaṃ catubhūmakaṃ. Chakke pañcasu dvāresu vedanā kāmāvacarāva, manodvāre catubhūmikā, aṭṭhārasake chasu iṭṭhārammaṇesu somanassena saha upavicarantīti somanassūpavicārā. Sesadvayepi eseva nayo. Iti ayaṃ desanā vicāravasena āgatā, taṃsampayuttānaṃ pana somanassādīnaṃ vasena idha aṭṭhārasa vedanā veditabbā.

In the second sutta, 'the method of one hundred and eight' means that which is the cause for knowing the one hundred and eight. 'The exposition of the Dhamma' means the cause which is the teaching. Regarding 'bodily and mental,' here, bodily feelings are found only in the sense-sphere; mental feelings are of the four planes. Regarding 'pleasant' and so on, pleasant feeling does not exist in the formless sphere; it is found in the remaining three planes. Painful feeling is only of the sense-sphere. The other, equanimous feeling, is of the four planes. In the pentad, the faculties of pleasure, pain, and displeasure are only of the sense-sphere; the faculty of joy is of three planes, and the faculty of equanimity is of four planes. In the hexad, feelings at the five doors are only of the sense-sphere; at the mind-door, they are of the four planes. In the set of eighteen, because they investigate together with joy regarding the six desirable objects, they are called 'investigations accompanied by joy.' This same method applies to the remaining two. Thus, this teaching comes by way of investigation; however, here the eighteen feelings should be understood by way of the feelings of joy and so on, which are associated with that investigation.

Cha gehasitāni somanassānītiādīsu “cakkhuviññeyyānaṃ rūpānaṃ iṭṭhānaṃ kantānaṃ manāpānaṃ manoramānaṃ lokāmisapaṭisaṃyuttānaṃ paṭilābhaṃ vā paṭilābhato samanupassato pubbe vā paṭiladdhapubbaṃ atītaṃ niruddhaṃ vipariṇataṃ samanussarato uppajjati somanassaṃ. Yaṃ evarūpaṃ [P 3.0083] somanassaṃ, idaṃ [M 3.0123] vuccati gehasitaṃ somanassa”nti (ma. ni. 3.306). Evaṃ chasu dvāresu vuttakāmaguṇanissitāni somanassāni cha gehasitasomanassāni nāma.

In the passage beginning, 'Six kinds of joy based on the household life,' it is said: 'When joy arises in one who contemplates the acquisition of forms cognizable by the eye that are wished for, dear, agreeable, delightful, and connected with worldly acquisitions, or in one who recollects forms previously acquired that are past, ceased, and changed—whatever joy is of such a kind, this is called joy based on the household life' (MN 3.306). Thus, the kinds of joy dependent on the qualities of sensual pleasure, spoken of in connection with the six sense doors, are called the six kinds of joy based on the household life.

“Rūpānaṃ tveva aniccataṃ viditvā vipariṇāmavirāganirodhaṃ ‘pubbe ceva rūpā etarahi ca, sabbe te rūpā aniccā dukkhā vipariṇāmadhammā’ti evametaṃ yathābhūtaṃ sammappaññāya passato uppajjati somanassaṃ. Yaṃ evarūpaṃ somanassaṃ, idaṃ vuccati nekkhammasitaṃ somanassa”nti (ma. ni. 3.306) evaṃ chasu dvāresu iṭṭhārammaṇe āpāthagate aniccatādivasena vipassanaṃ paṭṭhapetvā ussukkāpetuṃ sakkontassa “ussukkitā me vipassanā”ti somanassajātassa uppannasomanassāni cha nekkhammasitasomanassāni nāma.

“Having understood the impermanence of forms themselves, their changing, fading away, and cessation, and seeing with right wisdom as it really is that ‘Forms in the past and forms at present—all those forms are impermanent, suffering, and subject to change,’ joy arises. Whatever joy is of such a kind, this is called joy based on renunciation” (MN 3.306). Thus, when a desirable object comes into the range of the six doors, for one who is able to arouse insight—having established it by way of impermanence and so on—the joys that arise for one in whom joy is born, thinking, “My insight has been aroused,” are called the six kinds of joy based on renunciation.

“Cakkhuviññeyyānaṃ rūpānaṃ iṭṭhānaṃ kantānaṃ manāpānaṃ manoramānaṃ lokāmisapaṭisaṃyuttānaṃ appaṭilābhaṃ vā appaṭilābhato samanupassato pubbe vā paṭiladdhapubbaṃ atītaṃ niruddhaṃ vipariṇataṃ samanussarato uppajjati domanassaṃ. Yaṃ evarūpaṃ domanassaṃ, idaṃ vuccati gehasitaṃ domanassa”nti. Evaṃ chasu dvāresu “iṭṭhārammaṇaṃ nānubhavissāmi nānubhavāmī”ti vitakkayato uppannāni kāmaguṇanissitadomanassāni cha gehasitadomanassāni nāma.

“When displeasure arises in one who contemplates the non-acquisition of forms cognizable by the eye that are wished for, dear, agreeable, delightful, and connected with worldly acquisitions, or in one who recollects that forms previously acquired are past, ceased, and changed—whatever displeasure is of such a kind, this is called displeasure based on the household life.” Thus, the kinds of displeasure dependent on the qualities of sensual pleasure that arise at the six doors for one who thinks, “I will not experience the desirable object,” or “I am not experiencing it,” are called the six kinds of displeasure based on the household life.

“Rūpānaṃ tveva aniccataṃ viditvā vipariṇāmavirāganirodhaṃ ‘pubbe ceva rūpā etarahi ca[V 3.0121], sabbe te rūpā aniccā dukkhā vipariṇāmadhammā’ti evametaṃ yathābhūtaṃ sammappaññāya disvā anuttaresu vimokkhesu pihaṃ upaṭṭhāpeti ‘kudāssu nāmāhaṃ tadāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharissāmi, yadariyā etarahi āyatanaṃ upasampajja viharantī”ti. Iti anuttaresu vimokkhesu pihaṃ upaṭṭhāpayato uppajjati pihapaccayā domanassaṃ. Yaṃ evarūpaṃ domanassaṃ, idaṃ vuccati nekkhammasitaṃ domanassanti; evaṃ chasu dvāresu iṭṭhārammaṇe āpāthagate anuttaravimokkhasaṅkhātaariyaphaladhammesu pihaṃ upaṭṭhāpetvā tadadhigamāya aniccatādivasena vipassanaṃ paṭṭhapetvā ussukkāpetuṃ asakkontassa “imampi pakkhaṃ imampi māsaṃ imampi saṃvaccharaṃ vipassanaṃ ussukkāpetvā ariyabhūmiṃ [P 3.0084] pāpuṇituṃ nāsakkhi”nti anusocato uppannāni domanassāni cha nekkhammasitadomanassāni nāma.

“Having understood the impermanence of forms themselves, their changing, fading away, and cessation, and seeing with right wisdom as it really is that ‘Forms in the past and forms at present—all those forms are impermanent, suffering, and subject to change,’ one establishes longing for the unsurpassed liberations: ‘When, indeed, will I attain and dwell in that sphere which the noble ones now attain and dwell in?’ Thus, for one who establishes longing for the unsurpassed liberations, displeasure arises on account of that longing. Whatever displeasure is of such a kind, this is called displeasure based on renunciation.” Thus, when a desirable object comes into the range of the six doors, for one who is unable to arouse insight for its attainment—having established longing for the noble fruition-states called the unsurpassed liberations and having established insight by way of impermanence and so on—the kinds of displeasure that arise for one who grieves, thinking, “For this fortnight, this month, this year, though I have aroused insight, I have not been able to reach the noble state,” are called the six kinds of displeasure based on renunciation.

“Cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā uppajjati upekkhā bālassa mūḷhassa puthujjanassa anodhijinassa avipākajinassa anādīnavadassāvino assutavato puthujjanassa[M 3.0124]. Yā evarūpā upekkhā, rūpaṃ sā nātivattati, tasmā sā upekkhā gehasitāti vuccatī”ti; evaṃ chasu dvāresu iṭṭhārammaṇe āpāthagate guḷapiṇḍake nilīnamakkhikā viya rūpādīni anativattamānā tattheva laggā laggitā hutvā uppannakāmaguṇanissitā upekkhā cha gehasitaupekkhā nāma.

“Having seen a form with the eye, equanimity arises in a foolish, deluded ordinary person, one who has not conquered the boundaries, who has not conquered the results, who does not see the danger, an uninstructed ordinary person. Such equanimity does not transcend form; therefore, that equanimity is called ‘based on the household life.’” Thus, when a desirable object comes into the range of the six doors, the kinds of equanimity dependent on the qualities of sensual pleasure that arise—not transcending forms and so on, but, like a fly settled on a ball of molasses, having become stuck and attached right there—are called the six kinds of equanimity based on the household life.

“Rūpānaṃ tveva aniccataṃ viditvā vipariṇāmavirāganirodhaṃ pubbe ceva rūpā etarahi ca, ‘sabbe te rūpā aniccā dukkhā vipariṇāmadhammā’ti evametaṃ yathābhūtaṃ sammappaññāya passato uppajjati upekkhā. Yā evarūpā upekkhā, rūpaṃ sā ativattati, tasmā sā upekkhā nekkhammasitāti vuccatī”ti; evaṃ chasu dvāresu iṭṭhādiārammaṇe āpāthagate iṭṭhe arajjantassa aniṭṭhe adussantassa asamapekkhane amuyhantassa uppannā vipassanāñāṇasampayuttā upekkhā nekkhammasitaupekkhā nāma. Imasmiṃ sutte sabbasaṅgāhako catubhūmakadhammaparicchedo kathito. Tatiyādīni uttānatthāneva.

“Having understood the impermanence of forms themselves, their changing, fading away, and cessation, and seeing with right wisdom as it really is that ‘Forms in the past and forms at present—all those forms are impermanent, suffering, and subject to change,’ equanimity arises. Such equanimity transcends form; therefore, that equanimity is called ‘based on renunciation.’” Thus, when a desirable or other object comes into the range of the six doors, the equanimity associated with insight-knowledge that arises in one who is not attached to the desirable, not repulsed by the undesirable, and not deluded in what is to be regarded with equanimity, is called equanimity based on renunciation. In this sutta, a comprehensive classification of phenomena of the four planes is taught. The third and following suttas are of clear meaning.

11. Nirāmisasuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Spiritual Discourse

279. Ekādasame sāmisāti kilesāmisena sāmisā. Nirāmisatarāti nirāmisāyapi jhānapītiyā nirāmisatarāva. Nanu ca dvīsu jhānesu pīti mahaggatāpi hoti lokuttarāpi, paccavekkhaṇapīti [V 3.0122] lokiyāva, sā kasmā nirāmisatarā jātāti? Santapaṇītadhammapaccavekkhaṇavasena uppannattā. Yathā hi rājavallabho cūḷupaṭṭhāko appaṭihārikaṃ yathāsukhaṃ rājakulaṃ pavisanto seṭṭhisenāpatiādayo pādena paharantopi na gaṇeti. Kasmā? Rañño āsannaparicārakattā. Iti so tehi uttaritaro hoti, evamayampi santapaṇītadhammapaccavekkhaṇavasena [P 3.0085] uppannattā lokuttarapītitopi uttaritarāti veditabbā. Sesavāresupi eseva nayo.

279. In the eleventh discourse, 'with allure' (sāmisa) means with the allure of the defilements. 'More without allure' (nirāmisatara) means even more without allure than the joy of jhāna, which is without allure. But is not the joy in the two jhānas both sublime (mahaggata) and supramundane (lokuttara)? Reflective joy, however, is mundane (lokiya). So why has it become 'more without allure'? Because it arises by means of reflecting on the peaceful and sublime Dhamma. Just as a royal favorite, a young attendant, entering the royal palace at will without hindrance, pays no mind even if he strikes great treasurers, generals, and so on with his foot. Why? Because of being a close attendant to the king. Thus he is superior to them. Similarly, this joy, because it arises by means of reflecting on the peaceful and sublime Dhamma, should be understood as superior even to supramundane joy. This same method should be understood in the remaining sections as well.

Vimokkhavāre pana rūpapaṭisaṃyutto vimokkho attano ārammaṇabhūtena rūpāmisavaseneva sāmiso nāma, arūpapaṭisaṃyutto rūpāmisābhāvena nirāmiso nāmāti.

In the section on liberations, a liberation connected with form is called 'with allure' by means of the allure of form which is its own object. A liberation not connected with form is called 'without allure' because of the absence of the allure of form.

Vedanāsaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on Feeling is concluded.

3. Mātugāmasaṃyuttaṃ

3. The Connected Discourses on Women

1. Paṭhamapeyyālavaggo

1. The First Abbreviation Chapter

1-2. Mātugāmasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-2. Commentary on the Discourses Beginning with the Discourse on Women

280-281. Mātugāmasaṃyuttassa [V 3.0123][M 3.0125][P 3.0086] paṭhame aṅgehīti aguṇaṅgehi. Na ca rūpavāti na rūpasampanno virūpo duddasiko. Na ca bhogavāti na bhogasampanno niddhano. Na ca sīlavāti na sīlasampanno dussīlo. Alaso cāti kantanapacanādīni kammāni kātuṃ na sakkoti, kusīto ālasiyo nisinnaṭṭhāne nisinnova, ṭhitaṭhāne ṭhitova niddāyati eva. Pajañcassa na labhatīti assa purisassa kulavaṃsapatiṭṭhāpakaṃ puttaṃ na labhati, vañjhitthī nāma hoti. Sukkapakkho vuttavipariyāyena veditabbo. Dutiyaṃ paṭhame vuttanayeneva parivattetabbaṃ.

In the first sutta of the Mātugāma Saṃyutta, 'with qualities' means with bad qualities. 'Not beautiful' means not endowed with beauty, ugly, unpleasant to look at. 'Not wealthy' means not endowed with wealth, poor. 'Not virtuous' means not endowed with virtue, immoral. 'And lazy' means she is unable to do tasks such as spinning and cooking; she is indolent, slothful; she just sits where she sits, and just stands where she stands; she just sleeps. 'And she does not obtain offspring for him' means she does not obtain for that man a son who would establish the family lineage; she is called a barren woman. The bright side should be understood in the opposite way to what has been said. The second sutta should be adapted by applying the qualities to the man in the same way as stated in the first.

3. Āveṇikadukkhasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on Exclusive Suffering

282. Tatiye āveṇikānīti pāṭipuggalikāni purisehi asādhāraṇāni. Pāricariyanti paricārikabhāvaṃ.

282. In the third discourse, 'unique' means individual, not shared with men. 'Service' means the state of being an attendant.

4. Tīhidhammehisuttādivaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Discourses Beginning with the Discourse on Three Qualities

283-303. Catutthe maccheramalapariyuṭṭhitenāti pubbaṇhasamayasmiñhi mātugāmo khīradadhisaṅgopanarandhanapacanādīni kātuṃ āraddho, puttakehipi yāciyamāno kiñci dātuṃ na icchati. Tenetaṃ vuttaṃ “pubbaṇhasamayaṃ maccheramalapariyuṭṭhitena cetasā”ti. Majjhanhikasamaye pana mātugāmo kodhābhibhūtova hoti, antoghare kalahaṃ alabhanto [P 3.0087] paṭivissakagharampi gantvā kalahaṃ [V 3.0124] karoti, sāmikassa ca ṭhitanisinnaṭṭhānāni vilokento vicarati. Tena vuttaṃ “majjhanhikasamayaṃ issāpariyuṭṭhitena cetasā”ti. Sāyanhe panassā asaddhammapaṭisevanāya cittaṃ namati. Tena vuttaṃ “sāyanhasamayaṃ kāmarāgapariyuṭṭhitena cetasā”ti. Pañcamādīni uttānatthāneva.

In the fourth discourse, 'overrun by the stain of stinginess' is said because in the morning a woman, having started to do tasks like storing milk and curd and cooking, does not wish to give anything even when asked by her young children. Therefore it was said: 'in the morning, with a mind overrun by the stain of stinginess.' At midday, a woman is overcome by anger; not finding a quarrel in the house, she goes to a neighbor’s house and makes a quarrel, and she wanders about, eyeing the places where her husband stands and sits. Therefore it was said: 'at midday, with a mind overrun by jealousy.' In the evening, her mind inclines towards engaging in wrong practice. Therefore it was said: 'in the evening, with a mind overrun by sensual lust.' The fifth and following discourses have an obvious meaning.

3. Balavaggo

3. The Chapter on Strength

1. Visāradasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on Assurance

304. Dasame [M 3.0126] rūpabalantiādīsu rūpasampatti rūpabalaṃ, bhogasampatti bhogabalaṃ, ñātisampatti ñātibalaṃ, puttasampatti puttabalaṃ, sīlasampatti sīlabalaṃ. Pañcasīladasasīlāni akhaṇḍāni katvā rakkhantassa hi sīlasampattiyeva sīlabalaṃ nāma hoti. Imāni kho bhikkhave pañca balānīti imāni pañca upatthambhanaṭṭhena balāni nāma vuccanti.

304. In the tenth discourse, regarding 'the strength of form' and so on: the excellence of form is the strength of form; the excellence of wealth is the strength of wealth; the excellence of relatives is the strength of relatives; the excellence of sons is the strength of sons; the excellence of virtue is the strength of virtue. Indeed, for one who keeps the five precepts or the ten precepts unbroken, the excellence of virtue itself is called the strength of virtue. 'These, bhikkhus, are the five strengths'—these five are called strengths in the sense of support.

2-10. Pasayhasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-10. Commentary on the Discourses Beginning with 'Overpowering'

305-313. Pasayhāti abhibhavitvā. Abhibhuyya vattatīti abhibhavati ajjhottharati. Neva rūpabalaṃ tāyatīti neva rūpabalaṃ tāyituṃ rakkhituṃ sakkoti. Nāsenteva naṃ, kule na vāsentīti “dussīlā saṃbhinnācārā atikkantamariyādā”ti gīvāyaṃ gahetvā nīharanti, na tasmiṃ kule vāsenti. Vāsenteva naṃ kule, na nāsentīti “kiṃ rūpena bhogādīhi vā, parisuddhasīlā esā ācārasampannā”ti ñatvā ñātakā tasmiṃ kule vāsentiyeva, na nāsenti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

'Overpowering' means having overcome. 'He lives overpowering' means he overcomes, he overwhelms. 'The power of form does not protect' means the power of form is not able to protect or guard. 'They destroy her, they do not keep her in the family' means, saying, 'She is immoral, of corrupt conduct, transgressing boundaries,' they seize her by the neck and expel her; they do not keep her in that family. 'They keep her in the family, they do not destroy her' means, knowing, 'What use is form or wealth and so on? She is of purified virtue, accomplished in conduct,' the relatives keep her in that family; they do not destroy her. The rest is clear in meaning throughout.

Mātugāmasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses Concerning Women is concluded.

4. Jambukhādakasaṃyuttaṃ

4. The Connected Discourses on Jambukhādaka

1. Nibbānapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on Questions about Nibbāna

314. Jambukhādakasaṃyutte [V 3.0125][P 3.0088][M 3.0127] jambukhādako paribbājakoti evaṃnāmo therassa bhāgineyyo channaparibbājako. Yo kho āvuso rāgakkhayoti nibbānaṃ āgamma rāgo khīyati, tasmā nibbānaṃ rāgakkhayoti vuccati. Dosamohakkhayesupi eseva nayo.

314. In the Jambukhādaka Saṃyutta, Jambukhādaka the wanderer was the nephew of the Elder Sāriputta. As for 'Friend, the destruction of lust': because lust is destroyed on account of Nibbāna, therefore Nibbāna is called the destruction of lust. This same principle applies to the destruction of hatred and delusion.

Yo pana imināva suttena kilesakkhayamattaṃ nibbānanti vadeyya, so vattabbo “kassa kilesānaṃ khayo, kiṃ attano, udāhu paresa”nti? Addhā “attano”ti vakkhati. Tato pucchitabbo “gotrabhuñāṇassa kiṃ ārammaṇa”nti? Jānamāno “nibbāna”nti vakkhati. Kiṃ pana gotrabhuñāṇakkhaṇe kilesā khīṇā khīyanti khīyissantīti? “Khīṇā”ti vā “khīyantī”ti vā na vattabbā, “khīyissantī”ti pana vattabbāti. Kiṃ pana tesu akhīṇesuyeva kilesesu gotrabhuñāṇaṃ kilesakkhayaṃ ārammaṇaṃ karotīti? Addhā evaṃ vutte niruttaro bhavissati.

But if someone were to say that Nibbāna is merely the destruction of defilements based on this very discourse, they should be asked: 'Whose defilements are destroyed? One's own or another's?' Surely, they would say, 'One's own.' Then they should be asked: 'What is the object of gotrabhū-knowledge?' Knowing, they would say, 'Nibbāna.' But at the moment of gotrabhū-knowledge, are the defilements destroyed, being destroyed, or to be destroyed? It should not be said that they are 'destroyed' or 'being destroyed,' but rather that they 'will be destroyed.' But with those defilements not yet destroyed, does gotrabhū-knowledge take the destruction of defilements as its object? Surely, when this is said, they will be without an answer.

Maggañāṇenāpi cetaṃ yojetabbaṃ. Maggakkhaṇepi hi kilesā “khīṇā”ti vā “khīyissantī”ti vā na vattabbā, “khīyantī”ti pana vattabbā, na ca akhīṇesuyeva kilesesu kilesakkhayo ārammaṇaṃ hoti, tasmā sampaṭicchitabbametaṃ. Yaṃ āgamma rāgādayo khīyanti, taṃ nibbānaṃ. Taṃ panetaṃ “rūpino dhammā arūpino dhammā”tiādīsu (dha. sa. dukamātikā 11) dukesu arūpino dhammāti saṅgahitattā na kilesakkhayamattamevāti.

This should also be applied with path-knowledge. Indeed, even at the moment of the path, it should not be said that the defilements are 'destroyed' or 'will be destroyed,' but rather that they are 'being destroyed.' And the destruction of defilements is not the object while the defilements themselves are not yet destroyed. Therefore, this is to be accepted. Nibbāna is that on account of which lust and other defilements are destroyed. Furthermore, this Nibbāna is not merely the destruction of defilements, for it is included under 'formless phenomena' in the dyads such as 'phenomena with form, formless phenomena' (Dhs. Dukamātikā 11).

2. Arahattapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Arahattapañhā Sutta

315. Arahattapañhabyākaraṇe [V 3.0126] yasmā arahattaṃ rāgadosamohānaṃ khīṇante uppajjati, tasmā “rāgakkhayo dosakkhayo mohakkhayo”ti vuttaṃ.

315. In the explanation of the questions concerning Arahantship, since Arahantship arises at the end of the destruction of lust, hatred, and delusion, it is therefore said: 'the destruction of lust, the destruction of hatred, the destruction of delusion.'

3-15. Dhammavādīpañhāsuttādivaṇṇanā

3–15. Commentary on the Dhammavādīpañhā Sutta and so forth

316-328. Te [M 3.0128][P 3.0089] loke sugatāti te rāgādayo pahāya gatattā suṭṭhu gatāti sugatā. Dukkhassa kho āvuso pariññatthanti vaṭṭadukkhassa parijānanatthaṃ. Dukkhatāti dukkhasabhāvo. Dukkhadukkhatātiādīsu dukkhasaṅkhāto dukkhasabhāvo dukkhadukkhatā. Sesapadadvayepi eseva nayo.

316–328. As for 'Te loke sugatā': they are called 'Sugata' because, having abandoned lust and so forth, they have gone well; thus they are 'well-gone.' As for 'Dukkhassa kho āvuso pariññatthaṃ': it means for the sake of fully understanding the suffering of the round of existence. As for 'Dukkhatā': it means the nature of suffering. In passages such as 'dukkha-dukkhatā', the state with the nature of suffering, designated as 'suffering', is 'dukkha-dukkhatā'. This same method should be understood for the remaining two terms as well.

16. Dukkarapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

16. Commentary on the Dukkarapañhā Sutta

329. Abhiratīti pabbajjāya anukkaṇṭhanatā. Naciraṃ āvusoti āvuso dhammānudhammappaṭipanno bhikkhu “pāto anusiṭṭho sāyaṃ visesamadhigamissati, sāyaṃ anusiṭṭho pāto visesamadhigamissatī”ti (ma. ni. 2.345) vuttattā ghaṭento vāyamanto nacirassaṃ lahuyeva arahaṃ assa, arahatte patiṭṭhaheyyāti dasseti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

329. As for 'Abhirati': it is the state of not being discontented with the monastic life. As for 'Naciraṃ āvuso': because it was said, 'If instructed in the morning, he will attain a special distinction in the evening; if instructed in the evening, he will attain a special distinction in the morning' (MN 2.345), it shows this meaning: 'Friend, a monk practicing in accordance with the Dhamma, by striving and making an effort, would before long, quickly indeed, become an Arahant and be established in Arahantship.' The rest has an obvious meaning everywhere.

Jambukhādakasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Jambukhādaka Saṃyutta is concluded.

5. Sāmaṇḍakasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Sāmaṇḍaka Saṃyutta

330-331. Sāmaṇḍakasaṃyuttepi [V 3.0127] imināva nayena attho veditabbo.

330–331. In the Sāmaṇḍaka Saṃyutta also, the meaning should be understood by this very method.

Sāmaṇḍakasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Sāmaṇḍaka Saṃyutta is concluded.

6. Moggallānasaṃyuttaṃ

6. The Moggallāna Saṃyutta

1-8. Paṭhamajhānapañhāsuttādivaṇṇanā

1–8. Commentary on the Paṭhamajhānapañhā Sutta and so forth

332-339. Moggallānasaṃyutte [V 3.0128][M 3.0129] kāmasahagatāti pañcanīvaraṇasahagatā. Tassa hi paṭhamajjhānavuṭṭhitassa pañca nīvaraṇāni santato upaṭṭhahiṃsu. Tenassa taṃ paṭhamajjhānaṃ hānabhāgiyaṃ nāma ahosi. Taṃ pamādaṃ ñatvā [P 3.0090] satthā “mā pamādo”ti ovādaṃ adāsi. Dutiyajjhānādīsupi imināva nayena attho veditabbo. Ārammaṇasahagatameva hettha “sahagata”nti vuttaṃ.

332–339. In the Moggallāna Saṃyutta, as for 'kāmasahagatā': it means accompanied by the five hindrances. For when he had arisen from the first jhāna, the five hindrances appeared to him in a tranquil manner. Because of that, that first jhāna became for him one that partakes in decline. Knowing that negligence, the Teacher gave the instruction, 'Do not be negligent.' In the case of the second jhāna and so on, the meaning should be understood in this same way. Here, it is only accompaniment by way of object that is called 'sahagata'.

9. Animittapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Animittapañhā Sutta

340. Animittaṃ cetosamādhinti niccanimittādīni pahāya pavattaṃ vipassanāsamādhiṃyeva sandhāyetaṃ vuttanti. Nimittānusāri viññāṇaṃ hotīti evaṃ iminā vipassanāsamādhivihārena viharato vipassanāñāṇe tikkhe sūre vahamāne. Yathā nāma purisassa tikhiṇena pharasunā rukkhaṃ chindantassa “suṭṭhu vata me pharasu vahatī”ti khaṇe khaṇe pharasudhāraṃ olokentassa chejjakiccaṃ na nipphajjati, evaṃ therassāpi “sūraṃ vata me hutvā ñāṇaṃ vahatī”ti vipassanaṃ ārabbha nikanti uppajjati. Atha vipassanākiccaṃ sādhetuṃ nāsakkhi. Taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “nimittānusāri viññāṇaṃ hotī”ti. Sabbanimittānaṃ amanasikārā animittaṃ cetosamādhiṃ upasampajja vihāsinti sabbesaṃ niccasukhaattanimittānaṃ amanasikārena animittaṃ vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanāsampayuttaṃ cetosamādhiṃ nibbānārammaṇaṃ uparimaggaphalasamādhiṃ upasampajja vihāsiṃ.

340. As for 'Animittaṃ cetosamādhiṃ': this is said referring only to the insight-concentration that occurs by abandoning the signs of permanence and so forth. As for 'Nimittānusāri viññāṇaṃ hoti': for one dwelling by means of this abiding in insight-concentration, when the insight-knowledge is proceeding, being sharp and valiant. Just as for a man cutting a tree with a sharp axe, if he were to look at the axe-blade moment by moment thinking, 'Indeed, my axe serves its purpose very well!', the task of cutting would not be accomplished; similarly, for the Elder, a craving for delight arises with respect to his insight, thinking, 'Indeed, my knowledge proceeds, being valiant!' Referring to that, it is said, 'nimittānusāri viññāṇaṃ hoti'. As for 'Sabbanimittānaṃ amanasikārā animittaṃ cetosamādhiṃ upasampajja vihāsiṃ': 'I dwelt having attained the signless concentration of mind—which is associated with insight leading to emergence—by not attending to all the signs of permanence, pleasure, and self; and I dwelt having attained the concentration of the higher path and fruition, which has Nibbāna as its object.'

10-11. Sakkasuttādivaṇṇanā

10–11. Commentary on the Sakka Sutta and So Forth

341-342. Aveccappasādenāti [V 3.0129] acalappasādena. Dasahi ṭhānehīti dasahi kāraṇehi. Adhigaṇhantīti abhibhavanti, atikkamitvā tiṭṭhanti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

341–342. Aveccappasādena means with unshakeable confidence. Dasahi ṭhānehi means by ten reasons. Adhigaṇhanti means they conquer, they surpass, they stand beyond. The rest has an obvious meaning everywhere.

Moggallānasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Moggallāna Saṃyutta is concluded.

7. Cittasaṃyuttaṃ

7. The Citta Saṃyutta

1. Saṃyojanasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Saṃyojana Sutta

343. Cittasaṃyuttassa [V 3.0130][P 3.0091][M 3.0130] paṭhame macchikāsaṇḍeti evaṃnāmake vanasaṇḍe. Ayamantarākathā udapādīti porāṇakattherā atiracchānakathā honti, nisinnanisinnaṭṭhāne pañhaṃ samuṭṭhāpetvā ajānantā pucchanti, jānantā vissajjenti, tena nesaṃ ayaṃ kathā udapādi. Migapathakanti evaṃnāmakaṃ attano bhogagāmaṃ. So kira ambāṭakārāmassa piṭṭhibhāge hoti. Tenupasaṅkamīti “therānaṃ pañhaṃ vissajjetvā phāsuvihāraṃ katvā dassāmī”ti cintetvā upasaṅkami. Gambhīre buddhavacaneti atthagambhīre ceva dhammagambhīre ca buddhavacane. Paññācakkhu kamatīti ñāṇacakkhu vahati pavattati.

343. In the first sutta of the Citta Saṃyutta, macchikāsaṇḍa means a forest grove of that name. Ayamantarākathā udapādi means this: the ancient elders are not given to worldly talk; therefore, wherever they sit, they raise a question. Those who do not know ask, and those who know answer. For that reason, this talk arose for them. Migapathaka means his own village for enjoyment, named thus. It is said to be located behind the mango grove. Tenupasaṅkami means he thought, “Having answered the elders’ question, I will bring them to a comfortable abiding,” and so he approached. Gambhīre buddhavacane means in the Buddha’s word, which is deep in meaning and deep in the Dhamma. Paññācakkhu kamati means the eye of knowledge proceeds and functions.

2. Paṭhamaisidattasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the First Isidatta Sutta

344. Dutiye āyasmantaṃ theranti tesu theresu jeṭṭhakaṃ mahātheraṃ. Tuṇhī ahosīti jānantopi avisāradattā na kiñci byāhari. Byākaromahaṃ bhanteti “ayaṃ thero neva attanā byākaroti, na aññe ajjhesati, upāsakopi bhikkhusaṅghaṃ viheseti, ahametaṃ byākaritvā phāsuvihāraṃ katvā dassāmī”ti cintetvā āsanato vuṭṭhāya therassa santikaṃ gantvā evaṃ okāsamakāsi, katāvakāso pana attano āsane nisīditvā byākāsi.

344. In the second, āyasmantaṃ theraṃ refers to the most senior elder among those elders. Tuṇhī ahosi means even though he knew, due to a lack of confidence, he did not say anything. Byākaromahaṃ bhante means thinking, “This elder neither answers himself nor requests others, and this lay follower is vexing the community of monks; I will answer this and bring them to a comfortable abiding,” he rose from his seat, approached the elder, and asked for permission. Having obtained permission, he sat down in his own seat and answered.

Sahatthāti sahatthena. Santappesīti yāvadicchakaṃ dento suṭṭhu tappesi. Sampavāresīti “alaṃ ala”nti hatthasaññāya ceva vācāya ca paṭikkhipāpesi. Onītapattapāṇinoti [P 3.0092] pāṇito apanītapattā dhovitvā thavikāya osāpetvā aṃse laggitapattāti attho.

Sahatthā means with his own hand. Santappesi means giving as much as desired, he thoroughly satisfied them. Sampavāresi means he had them indicate they had had enough both by gesture and by word, saying, “Enough, enough.” Onītapattapāṇino means having bowls removed from the hand, washed, placed in a bag, and slung on the shoulder—this is the meaning.

3. Dutiyaisidattasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Second Isidatta Sutta

345. Tatiye [V 3.0131] avantiyāti dakkhiṇāpathe avantiraṭṭhe. Kalyāṇaṃ vuccatīti “catūhi paccayehi paṭijaggissāmī”ti vacanaṃ niddosaṃ anavajjaṃ vuccati tayā upāsakāti adhippāyena vadati.

345. In the third, avantiyā means in the Avanti country in the Southern Path. Kalyāṇaṃ vuccati means he speaks with the intention: 'Upāsaka, by you a faultless, blameless statement is spoken, namely, “I will attend with the four requisites.”'

4. Mahakapāṭihāriyasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Mahakapāṭihāriya Sutta.

346. Catutthe [M 3.0131] sesakaṃ vissajjethāti tassa kira therehi saddhiṃyeva kaṃsathālaṃ pamajjitvā pāyāsaṃ vaḍḍhetvā adaṃsu. So bhuttapāyāso therehiyeva saddhiṃ gantukāmo cintesi “ghare tāva upāsikā sesakaṃ vicāreti, idha panime dāsakammakārā mayā avuttā na vicāressanti, evāyaṃ paṇītapāyāso nassissatī”ti tesaṃ anujānanto evamāha. Kuthitanti kudhitaṃ, heṭṭhā santattāya vālikāya upari ātapena ca atitikhiṇanti attho. Idaṃ pana tepiṭake buddhavacane asambhinnapadaṃ. Paveliyamānenāti paṭiliyamānena sādhu khvassa bhanteti “phāsuvihāraṃ karissāmi nesa”nti cintetvā evamāha.

346. In the fourth, regarding sesakaṃ vissajjetha ('dispose of the remainder'): It is said that for that householder Citta, while he was together with the elders, the servants wiped a golden bowl, served milk-rice, and gave it. Having eaten the milk-rice, he, wishing to go together with the elders, considered: 'At home, the female lay follower will arrange for the remainder, but here these servants, if not permitted by me, will not arrange for it. In that case, this excellent milk-rice will be wasted.' Thus, wishing to grant them permission, he spoke thus. Kuthitaṃ means it is seething; the meaning is that it is extremely hot, from the heated sand below and the sun's heat above. This, however, is an uncompounded term in the word of the Buddha, the Tipiṭaka. Paveliyamānena means melting away. Regarding sādhu khvassa bhante ('It would be good, venerable sir'): The venerable Mahaka spoke thus, thinking, 'I will create a comfortable abiding for them.'

Iddhābhisaṅkhāraṃ abhisaṅkharīti adhiṭṭhāniddhiṃ akāsi. Ettha ca “mandamando sītakavāto vāyatu, abbhamaṇḍapaṃ katvā devo ekamekaṃ phusāyatū”ti evaṃ nānāparikammaṃ – “savāto devo vassatū”ti evaṃ adhiṭṭhānaṃ ekatopi hoti. “Savāto devo vassatūti ekatoparikammaṃ, mandamando sītakavāto vāyatu, abbhamaṇḍapaṃ katvā devo [P 3.0093] ekamekaṃ phusāyatū”ti evaṃ nānāadhiṭṭhānaṃ hoti. Vuttanayeneva nānāparikammaṃ nānādhiṭṭhānaṃ, ekato parikammaṃ ekato adhiṭṭhānampi hotiyeva. Yathā tathā karontassa pana pādakajjhānato vuṭṭhāya kataparikammassa parikammānantarena mahaggataadhiṭṭhānacitteneva taṃ ijjhati. Okāsesīti vippakiri.

Iddhābhisaṅkhāraṃ abhisaṅkharī (‘He performed a feat of psychic power’) means he created a psychic power of determination. And here, it can occur that there are various preliminary acts—such as, ‘Let a gentle, cool wind blow; having made a cloud-pavilion, let the rain-god drizzle drop by drop’—and a single determination: ‘Let the rain-god rain with wind.’ It can also occur that there is a single preliminary act—‘Let the rain-god rain with wind’—and various determinations: ‘Let a gentle, cool wind blow; having made a cloud-pavilion, let the rain-god drizzle drop by drop.’ In the way just stated, there can be various preliminary acts and various determinations, and also a single preliminary act and a single determination. However, for one who acts in any of these ways, having arisen from the foundational jhāna and having performed the preliminary act, that feat succeeds by means of the sublime mind of determination immediately following the preliminary act. Okāsesīti vippakiri means he scattered.

5. Paṭhamakāmabhūsuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the First Kāmabhū Sutta.

347. Pañcame nelaṅgoti niddoso. Setapacchādoti setapaṭicchādano. Anīghanti niddukkhaṃ. Muhuttaṃ tuṇhī hutvāti tassa atthapekkhanatthaṃ tīṇi piṭakāni kaṇṇe kuṇḍalaṃ viya sañcālento [V 3.0132] “ayaṃ imassa attho, ayaṃ imassa attho”ti upaparikkhaṇatthaṃ muhuttaṃ tuṇhī hutvā. Vimuttiyāti arahattaphalavimuttiyā. Imaṃ pana pañhaṃ kathento upāsako dukkaraṃ akāsi. Sammāsambuddho hi “passatha no tumhe, bhikkhave, etaṃ bhikkhuṃ [M 3.0132] āgacchantaṃ odātakaṃ tanukaṃ tuṅganāsika”nti (saṃ. ni. 2.245) attano diṭṭhena kathesi. Ayaṃ pana nayaggāhena “arahato etaṃ adhivacana”nti āha.

347. In the fifth, nelaṅgo means faultless. Setapacchādo means having a white covering, that is, the fruit of arahantship. Anīghaṃ means free from suffering. Muhuttaṃ tuṇhī hutvā means: having been silent for a moment in order to examine, seeking the meaning of that verse, shaking the three Piṭakas as if they were an earring in the ear, thinking, 'This is the meaning of this word; this is the meaning of this word.' Vimuttiyā means by the liberation that is the fruit of arahantship. But this lay follower, in answering this question, performed a difficult feat. For the Perfectly Enlightened One spoke based on what he had seen with his own omniscient knowledge, saying: 'Bhikkhus, do you see that bhikkhu coming—fair, slim, with a prominent nose?' But this one, by grasping the method, said, 'This is a designation for an arahant.'

6. Dutiyakāmabhūsuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Second Kāmabhū Sutta

348. Chaṭṭhe kati nu kho bhante saṅkhārāti ayaṃ kira, gahapati, nirodhaṃ valañjeti, tasmā “nirodhapādake saṅkhāre pucchissāmī”ti cintetvā evamāha. Theropissa adhippāyaṃ ñatvā puññābhisaṅkhārādīsu anekesu saṅkhāresu vijjamānesupi kāyasaṅkhārādayova ācikkhanto tayo kho gahapatītiādimāha. Tattha kāyappaṭibaddhattā kāyena saṅkharīyati nibbattīyatīti kāyasaṅkhāro. Vācāya saṅkharoti nibbattetīti vacīsaṅkhāro. Cittappaṭibaddhattā cittena saṅkharīyati nibbattīyatīti cittasaṅkhāro.

348. In the sixth, regarding 'How many formations are there, venerable sir?': It is said that this householder practices the attainment of cessation. Therefore, thinking, 'I will ask about the formations that are the basis for cessation,' he spoke thus. The Elder, having understood his intention, although there are many formations such as meritorious formations and so on, explained only the bodily formations and the like, saying, 'There are three, householder,' and so on. Therein, because it is connected with the body, it is formed by the body, it is produced by the body; therefore, it is a bodily formation. It forms speech, it produces speech; therefore, it is a verbal formation. Because it is connected with the mind, it is formed by the mind, it is produced by the mind; therefore, it is a mental formation.

Katamo [P 3.0094] pana bhanteti idha kiṃ pucchati? “Ime saṅkhārā aññamaññaṃ missā āluḷitā avibhūtā duddīpanā. Tathā hi kāyadvāre ādānaggahaṇamuñcanacopanāni pāpetvā uppannā aṭṭha kāmāvacarakusalacetanā dvādasa akusalacetanāti evaṃ kusalākusalā vīsati cetanāpi, assāsapassāsāpi kāyasaṅkhārotveva vuccanti. Vacīdvāre hanusañcopanaṃ vacībhedaṃ pāpetvā uppannā vuttappakārāva vīsati cetanāpi vitakkavicārāpi vacīsaṅkhārotveva vuccanti. Kāyavacīdvāresu copanaṃ apatvā raho nisinnassa cintayato uppannā kusalākusalā ekūnatiṃsacetanāpi, saññā ca vedanā cāti ime dve dhammāpi cittasaṅkhārotveva vuccanti. Evaṃ ime saṅkhārā aññamaññaṃ missā āluḷitā avibhūtā duddīpanā, te pākaṭe vibhūte katvā kathāpessāmī”ti pucchi.

As for 'But which, venerable sir?,' what does he ask here? He asks with this intention: 'These formations are mutually mixed, jumbled, indistinct, and difficult to demonstrate. For indeed, at the body-door, the eight sense-sphere wholesome volitions and twelve unwholesome volitions that have arisen having brought about taking, grasping, releasing, and moving—thus these twenty wholesome and unwholesome volitions—and also the in-breaths and out-breaths, are called “bodily formation.” At the speech-door, the twenty volitions of the aforementioned kind that have arisen having brought about the movement of the jaw and the breaking into speech, and also applied and sustained thought, are called “verbal formation.” For one sitting in seclusion and thinking, without having arrived at movement at the body- and speech-doors, the twenty-nine wholesome and unwholesome volitions that have arisen, and these two states—perception and feeling—are called “mental formation.” Thus, these formations are mutually mixed, jumbled, indistinct, and difficult to demonstrate. I will have them explained after they have been made apparent and distinct.'

Kasmā pana bhanteti idha kāyasaṅkhārādināmassa padatthaṃ pucchati. Tassa vissajjane kāyappaṭibaddhāti kāyanissitā. Kāye sati honti, asati na honti. Cittappaṭibaddhāti cittanissitā. Citte sati honti, asati na honti.

As for 'But why, venerable sir?,' here he asks for the meaning of the term for the name 'bodily formation' and so on. In the answer to that, 'connected with the body' means dependent on the body. When the body exists, they exist; when it does not exist, they do not exist. 'Connected with the mind' means dependent on the mind. When the mind exists, they exist; when it does not exist, they do not exist.

Idāni [V 3.0133][M 3.0133] “kiṃ nu kho esa saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ valañjeti, no valañjeti, ciṇṇavasī vā tattha no ciṇṇavasī”ti jānanatthaṃ pucchanto kathaṃ pana bhante saññāvedayitanirodhasamāpatti hotīti āha. Tassa vissajjane samāpajjissanti vā samāpajjāmīti vā padadvayena nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasamāpattikālo kathito. Samāpannoti padena antonirodho. Tathā purimehi dvīhi padehi sacittakakālo kathito, pacchimena acittakakālo.

Now, asking in order to know, 'Does he perhaps practice the cessation of perception and feeling, or does he not? Is he one with mastery through practice in it, or not?' he said, 'But how, venerable sir, does the attainment of the cessation of perception and feeling occur?' In the answer to that, with the two words 'I will attain' or 'I am attaining,' the time of the attainment of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception is stated. By the word 'attained,' it is the inner cessation. Likewise, by the first two words, the period with consciousness is stated; by the last, the period without consciousness.

Pubbeva tathā cittaṃ bhāvitaṃ hotīti nirodhasamāpattito pubbe addhānaparicchedakāleyeva “ettakaṃ kālaṃ acittako bhavissāmī”ti addhānaparicchedaṃ cittaṃ bhāvitaṃ hoti. Yaṃ taṃ tathattāya upanetīti yaṃ pana evaṃ bhāvitaṃ cittaṃ, taṃ puggalaṃ tathattāya acittakabhāvāya upaneti. Vacīsaṅkhāro paṭhamaṃ nirujjhatīti sesasaṅkhārehi paṭhamaṃ dutiyajjhāneyeva nirujjhati. Tato kāyasaṅkhāroti [P 3.0095] tato paraṃ kāyasaṅkhāro catutthajjhāne nirujjhati. Tato cittasaṅkhāroti tato paraṃ cittasaṅkhāro antonirodhe nirujjhati. Āyūti rūpajīvitindriyaṃ. Viparibhinnānīti upahatāni vinaṭṭhāni.

As for 'Previously, the mind has been so developed': before the attainment of cessation, at the very time of determining the duration, the mind that determines the duration has been developed with the thought, 'For such a time, I will be without mind.' As for 'That which leads one to that state': that mind, having been so developed, leads that person to that state, to the state of being without mind. As for 'The verbal formation ceases first': it ceases first, before the other formations, in the second jhāna itself. As for 'Then the bodily formation': after that, the bodily formation ceases in the fourth jhāna. As for 'Then the mental formation': after that, the mental formation ceases within the cessation. As for 'Life-force': the material life-faculty. As for 'Destroyed': damaged, ruined.

Tattha keci “nirodhasamāpannassa ‘cittasaṅkhāro ca niruddho’ti vacanato cittaṃ aniruddhaṃ hoti, tasmā sacittakāpi ayaṃ samāpattī”ti vadanti. Te vattabbā – “vacīsaṅkhāropissa niruddho”ti vacanato vācā aniruddhā hoti, tasmā nirodhasamāpannena dhammampi kathentena sajjhāyampi karontena nisīditabbaṃ siyā.
