Yo cāyaṃ mato kālaṅkato, tassāpi cittasaṅkhāro niruddhoti vacanato cittaṃ aniruddhaṃ bhaveyya, tasmā kālaṅkate mātāpitaro vā arahante vā jhāpentena ānantariyakammaṃ kataṃ bhaveyya. Iti byañjane abhinivesaṃ akatvā ācariyānaṃ naye ṭhatvā attho upaparikkhitabbo. Attho hi paṭisaraṇaṃ, na byañjanaṃ.

Herein, some say: 'For one who has attained cessation, because of the saying, “the mental formation has ceased,” the mind is not ceased. Therefore, this attainment is also one with consciousness.' They should be told: 'Because of the saying, “for him the verbal formation has also ceased,” speech would not be ceased. Therefore, it would be fitting for one who has attained cessation to sit while teaching the Dhamma and making recitation. And for that one who is dead, who has passed away, because of the saying, “for him too the mental formation has ceased,” the mind would not be ceased. Therefore, by one who cremates parents or arahants who have passed away, an act with immediate result would be done.' Thus, without adhering to the mere letter, standing in the tradition of the teachers, the meaning should be investigated. For the meaning is the refuge, not the letter.

Indriyāni vippasannānīti kiriyamayapavattasmiñhi vattamāne bahiddhārammaṇesu pasāde ghaṭṭentesu indriyāni kilamanti, upahatāni makkhittāni viya honti vātādīhi uṭṭhitarajena catumahāpathe ṭhapitaādāso viya. Yathā pana thavikāya pakkhipitvā mañjūsādīsu ṭhapito ādāso antoyeva [M 3.0134] virocati, evaṃ nirodhasamāpannassa bhikkhuno antonirodhe pañca pasādā ativiya virocanti. Tena vuttaṃ “indriyāni vippasannānī”ti.

As for 'The faculties are exceedingly clear': For when the process of bodily and verbal action is occurring, as external objects strike the sensitive faculties, the faculties become weary; they become as if damaged and smeared, like a mirror placed at a crossroads and smeared by dust raised by wind and so on. But just as a mirror, having been put in a bag and placed in a chest or the like, shines only within, so too for the bhikkhu who has attained cessation, the five sensitive faculties shine exceedingly within the cessation. For that reason, it was said, 'The faculties are exceedingly clear.'

Vuṭṭhahissanti [V 3.0134] vā vuṭṭhahāmīti vā padadvayena antonirodhakālo kathito, vuṭṭhitoti padena phalasamāpattikālo. Tathā purimehi dvīhi padehi acittakakālo kathito, pacchimena sacittakakālo. Pubbeva tathā cittaṃ bhāvitaṃ hotīti nirodhasamāpattito pubbe addhānaparicchedakāleyeva “ettakaṃ kālaṃ acittako hutvā tato paraṃ sacittako bhavissāmī”ti addhānaparicchedaṃ cittaṃ bhāvitaṃ hoti. Yaṃ taṃ tathattāya upanetīti yaṃ evaṃ bhāvitaṃ cittaṃ, taṃ puggalaṃ tathattāya sacittakabhāvāya upaneti. Iti heṭṭhā nirodhasamāpajjannakālo [P 3.0096] gahito, idha nirodhato vuṭṭhānakālo.

By the two words 'I will arise' or 'I am arising,' the period within cessation is stated. By the word 'arisen,' the period of the attainment of fruition is stated. Likewise, by the first two words, the period without consciousness is stated; by the last, the period with consciousness. As for 'Previously, the mind has been so developed': before the attainment of cessation, at the very time of determining the duration, the mind that determines the duration has been developed with the thought, 'Having been without consciousness for this long, after that I will be with consciousness.' As for 'That which leads one to that state': that mind, having been so developed, leads that person to that state, to the state of being with consciousness. Thus, in the previous section, the time of entering cessation was taken; here, the time of arising from cessation is taken.

Idāni nirodhakathaṃ kathetuṃ kāloti nirodhakathā kathetabbā siyā. Sā panesā “dvīhi balehi samannāgatattā tayo ca saṅkhārānaṃ paṭipassaddhiyā soḷasahi ñāṇacariyāhi navahi samādhicariyāhi vasībhāvatāpaññā nirodhasamāpattiyaṃ ñāṇa”nti mātikaṃ ṭhapetvā sabbākārena visuddhimagge kathitā, tasmā tattha kathitanayeneva gahetabbā. Ko panāyaṃ nirodho nāma? Catunnaṃ khandhānaṃ paṭisaṅkhā appavatti. Atha kimatthametaṃ samāpajjantīti? Saṅkhārānaṃ pavatte ukkaṇṭhitā sattāhaṃ acittakā hutvā sukhaṃ viharissāma, diṭṭhadhammanibbānaṃ nāmetaṃ yadidaṃ nirodhoti etadatthaṃ samāpajjanti.

Now is the time to discuss cessation; the discussion on cessation should be given. This discussion, however, is fully explained in the Visuddhimagga, having established the outline: 'Through being endowed with two powers, the calming of the three formations, sixteen modes of conduct of knowledge, nine modes of conduct of concentration, the wisdom that is mastery, and the knowledge in the attainment of cessation.' Therefore, it should be understood according to the method explained there. What, then, is this so-called cessation? It is the non-occurrence of the four aggregates due to reflective knowledge. Then, for what purpose do they attain this? Being weary of the occurrence of formations, they think, 'We shall dwell happily for seven days, having become without mind; this which is cessation is called Nibbāna in the present life.' For this purpose, they attain it.

Cittasaṅkhāro paṭhamaṃ uppajjatīti nirodhā vuṭṭhahantassa hi phalasamāpatticittaṃ paṭhamaṃ uppajjati. Taṃsampayuttaṃ saññañca vedanañca sandhāya “cittasaṅkhāro paṭhamaṃ uppajjatī”ti āha. Tato kāyasaṅkhāroti tato paraṃ bhavaṅgasamaye kāyasaṅkhāro uppajjati.

'The mental formation arises first': Indeed, for one emerging from cessation, the fruition-attainment consciousness arises first. Referring to the perception and feeling associated with it, it was said, 'The mental formation arises first.' 'Then the bodily formation': After that, at the moment of the life-continuum, the bodily formation arises.

Kiṃ pana phalasamāpatti assāsapassāse na samuṭṭhāpetīti? Samuṭṭhāpeti. Imassa pana catutthajjhānikā phalasamāpatti, sā na samuṭṭhāpeti. Kiṃ vā etena? Phalasamāpatti paṭhamajjhānikā vā hotu dutiyatatiyacatutthajjhānikā vā, santasamāpattito vuṭṭhitassa bhikkhuno assāsapassāsā [M 3.0135] abbohārikā honti, tesaṃ abbohārikabhāvo sañjīvattheravatthunā veditabbo. Sañjīvattherassa hi samāpattito vuṭṭhāya kiṃsukapupphasadise vītaccitaṅgāre maddamānassa gacchato cīvare aṃsumattampi na jhāyi, usmākāramattampi nāhosi. Samāpattibalaṃ [P 3.0097] nāmetanti vadanti. Evameva santāya phalasamāpattiyā vuṭṭhitassa bhikkhuno assāsapassāsā abbohārikā hontīti bhavaṅgasamayenevetaṃ kathitanti veditabbaṃ.

But does the fruition attainment not produce in-and-out breathing? It does produce it. However, for this monk, the fruition attainment is of the fourth jhāna, and that does not produce it. Or what of this? Whether the fruition attainment is of the first, second, third, or fourth jhāna, the in-and-out breaths of a monk who has arisen from a peaceful attainment are not discernible. Their being not discernible should be understood through the story of the Elder Sañjīva. Indeed, for the Elder Sañjīva, who, having arisen from attainment, was walking while treading on flameless embers like kiṃsuka flowers, not even a thread of his robe was burned, nor was there even a trace of heat. They say, 'This is called the power of attainment.' Just so, it should be understood that for a monk who has arisen from the peaceful fruition attainment, the in-and-out breaths are not discernible, and this was said with reference only to the moment of the life-continuum.

Tato [V 3.0135] vacīsaṅkhāroti tato paraṃ kiriyamayapavattavalañjanakāle vacīsaṅkhāro uppajjati. Kiṃ bhavaṅgaṃ vitakkavicāre na samuṭṭhāpetīti? Samuṭṭhāpeti. Taṃsamuṭṭhānā pana vitakkavicārā vācaṃ abhisaṅkhātuṃ na sakkontīti kiriyamayapavattavalañjanakālenevetaṃ kathitaṃ.

'Then the verbal formation': After that, at the time of engaging in the process of action, the verbal formation arises. Does the life-continuum not produce initial and sustained thought? It does produce them. However, the initial and sustained thoughts that arise from it are unable to form speech. Therefore, this was said with reference only to the time of engaging in the process of action.

Suññato phassotiādayo saguṇenāpi ārammaṇenāpi kathetabbā. Saguṇena tāva suññatā nāma phalasamāpatti, tāya sahajātaphassaṃ sandhāya “suññato phasso”ti vuttaṃ. Animittappaṇihitesupi eseva nayo. Ārammaṇena pana nibbānaṃ rāgādīhi suññattā suññatā nāma, rāganimittādīnaṃ abhāvā animittaṃ, rāgadosamohappaṇidhīnaṃ abhāvā appaṇihitaṃ, suññataṃ nibbānaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā uppannaphalasamāpattisamphasso suññato nāma. Animittappaṇihitesupi eseva nayo.

'Contact from emptiness,' etc., should be explained both by its own quality and by its object. First, by its own quality: fruition attainment is called 'emptiness.' Referring to the contact co-arisen with that, it is said, 'contact from emptiness.' This same method applies to the signless and the undirected. By its object, however: Nibbāna is called 'emptiness' because it is empty of lust, etc.; 'signless' because of the absence of the sign of lust, etc.; 'undirected' because of the absence of the aspirations of lust, hatred, and delusion. The contact associated with the fruition attainment that has arisen having taken empty Nibbāna as its object is called 'from emptiness.' This same method applies to the signless and the undirected.

Aparā āgamaniyakathā nāma hoti. Suññataanimittaappaṇihitāti hi vipassanāpi vuccati. Tattha yo bhikkhu saṅkhāre aniccato pariggahetvā aniccato disvā aniccato vuṭṭhāti, tassa vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanā animittā nāma hoti. Yo dukkhato pariggahetvā dukkhato disvā dukkhato vuṭṭhāti, tassa appaṇihitā nāma. Yo anattato pariggahetvā anattato disvā anattato vuṭṭhāti, tassa suññatā nāma. Tattha animittavipassanāya maggo animitto nāma, animittamaggassa phalaṃ animittaṃ nāma, animittaphalasamāpattisahajāte phasse phusante “animitto phasso phusatī”ti vuccati. Appaṇihitasuññatesupi eseva nayo. Āgamaniyena kathite pana suññato vā phasso animitto vā phasso appaṇihito vā phassoti vikappo āpajjeyya, tasmā saguṇena [M 3.0136] ceva ārammaṇena ca kathetabbaṃ. Evañhi tayo phassā phusantīti sameti.

There is another explanation, called the one based on the scriptures. For insight is also called 'emptiness, signless, and undirected.' Therein, for a monk who, having fully grasped formations as impermanent, having seen them as impermanent, emerges by way of impermanence, his insight leading to emergence is called 'signless.' For one who, having fully grasped them as suffering, having seen them as suffering, emerges by way of suffering, his is called 'undirected.' For one who, having fully grasped them as non-self, having seen them as non-self, emerges by way of non-self, his is called 'emptiness.' Therein, through signless insight, the path is called 'signless'; the fruit of the signless path is called 'signless.' When experiencing the contact co-arisen with the attainment of the signless fruit, it is said, 'one experiences signless contact.' This same method should be known for the undirected and emptiness. However, if explained according to the scriptural method, the dilemma would arise: 'Is it contact from emptiness, or contact from signlessness, or contact from desirelessness?' Therefore, it should be explained by its own quality and by its object. For in this way, the statement 'the three contacts are experienced' is consistent.

Vivekaninnantiādīsu nibbānaṃ viveko nāma. Tasmiṃ viveke ninnaṃ onatanti vivekaninnaṃ. Vivekapoṇanti aññato [P 3.0098] agantvā yena viveko, tena vaṅkaṃ viya hutvā ṭhitanti vivekapoṇaṃ. Yena viveko, tena patamānaṃ viya ṭhitanti vivekapabbhāraṃ.

In 'inclined to seclusion,' etc., Nibbāna is called 'seclusion.' Being inclined and bent toward that seclusion is 'inclined to seclusion.' 'Leaning toward seclusion' means not going elsewhere, it stands as if bent toward where seclusion is. 'Sloping toward seclusion' means it stands as if falling toward where seclusion is.

7. Godattasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Godatta Sutta

349. Sattame [V 3.0136] nānatthā ceva nānābyañjanā cāti byañjanampi nesaṃ nānaṃ, atthopi. Tattha byañjanassa nānatā pākaṭā. Attho pana appamāṇā cetovimutti bhūmantarato mahaggatā hoti rūpāvacarā, ārammaṇato sattapaṇṇattiārammaṇā. Ākiñcaññā bhūmantarato mahaggatā arūpāvacarā, ārammaṇato navattabbārammaṇā. Suññatā bhūmantarato kāmāvacarā, ārammaṇato saṅkhārārammaṇā. Vipassanā hi ettha suññatāti adhippetā. Animittā bhūmantarato lokuttarā, ārammaṇato nibbānārammaṇā.

349. In the seventh sutta, they are 'different in meaning and different in expression'; thus their expression is different, and so is their meaning. Therein, the difference in expression is clear. The meaning, however, is not. The immeasurable liberation of mind, in terms of plane, is an exalted state belonging to the form sphere, with its object being the concept of beings. The liberation of mind of nothingness, in terms of plane, is an exalted state belonging to the formless sphere, with its object being that which cannot be specified. The liberation of mind of emptiness, in terms of plane, belongs to the sense sphere, with its object being formations. For here, by 'emptiness', insight is intended. The signless liberation of mind, in terms of plane, is supramundane, with its object being Nibbāna.

Rāgo kho bhante pamāṇakaraṇotiādīsu yathā pabbatapāde pūtipaṇṇakasaṭaudakaṃ nāma hoti kāḷavaṇṇaṃ, olokentānaṃ byāmasatagambhīraṃ viya khāyati, yaṭṭhiṃ vā rajjuṃ vā gahetvā minantassa piṭṭhipādottharaṇamattampi na hoti; evameva yāva rāgādayo nuppajjanti, tāva puggalaṃ sañjānituṃ na sakkā hoti, sotāpanno viya sakadāgāmī viya anāgāmī viya ca khāyati. Yadā panassa rāgādayo uppajjanti, tadā ratto duṭṭho mūḷhoti paññāyati. Iti te “ettako aya”nti puggalassa pamāṇaṃ dassentāva uppajjantīti pamāṇakaraṇā nāma vuttā.

Regarding 'Venerable sir, lust is a measure-maker,' and so forth: Just as at the foot of a mountain there is a pool of water with decaying leaves and rubbish, dark in color, which appears a hundred fathoms deep to those who look at it, but when one takes a stick or a rope and measures it, it is not even enough to cover the instep of one's foot; so too, as long as lust and other defilements do not arise, one cannot discern a person. They may appear like a stream-enterer, a once-returner, or a non-returner. But when lust and other defilements arise in them, then they are recognized as lustful, hateful, and deluded. Thus, since they arise as if showing the measure of a person—'This one is of such a measure'—they are called 'measure-makers.'

Yāvatā kho bhante appamāṇā cetovimuttiyoti yattakā appamāṇā cetovimuttiyo. Kittakā pana tā? Cattāro brahmavihārā, cattāro maggā, cattāri phalānīti dvādasa. Tatra brahmavihārā pharaṇaappamāṇatāya appamāṇā, sesā pamāṇakārakānaṃ kilesānaṃ abhāvena nibbānampi appamāṇameva, cetovimutti pana na hoti, tasmā na gahitaṃ. Akuppāti arahattaphalacetovimutti. Sā hi tāsaṃ sabbajeṭṭhikā, tasmā [M 3.0137] “aggamakkhāyatī”ti vuttā. Rāgo [P 3.0099] kho bhante kiñcananti rāgo uppajjitvā puggalaṃ kiñcati maddati palibundhati, tasmā kiñcananti vutto. Manussā kira goṇehi khalaṃ maddāpentā “kiñcehi kapila kiñcehi kāḷakā”ti vadanti. Evaṃ maddanaṭṭho kiñcanaṭṭhoti veditabbo. Dosamohesupieseva nayo.

Regarding 'Venerable sir, as far as the immeasurable liberations of mind,' this means as many as there are immeasurable liberations of mind. But how many are they? Twelve: the four divine abodes, the four paths, and the four fruits. Among these, the divine abodes are immeasurable due to the immeasurability of their pervasion. The rest are immeasurable due to the absence of defilements that act as measures. Nibbāna is also immeasurable, but it is not a liberation of mind; therefore, it is not included. 'Unshakable' refers to the liberation of mind that is the fruit of arahantship. For it is the chief of them all; therefore, it is said, 'It is called the highest.' Regarding 'Venerable sir, lust is an impediment (kiñcana)': Lust arises and afflicts, crushes, and oppresses a person; therefore, it is called 'kiñcana.' It is said that when people have oxen trample the threshing floor, they say, 'Trample (kiñcehi), tawny one! Trample (kiñcehi), black one!' Thus, the meaning of 'kiñcana' should be understood as 'trampling' or 'oppressing.' The same principle applies to hatred and delusion.

Ākiñcaññā cetovimuttiyo nāma nava dhammā ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ maggaphalāni ca. Tattha ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ kiñcanaṃ ārammaṇaṃ assa natthīti ākiñcaññaṃ. Maggaphalāni kiñcanānaṃ maddanapalibundhanakilesānaṃ [V 3.0137] natthitāya ākiñcaññāni, nibbānampi ākiñcaññaṃ, cetovimutti pana na hoti, tasmā na gahitaṃ.

The liberations of mind of nothingness are nine states: the base of nothingness, and the paths and fruits. Therein, the base of nothingness is called 'nothingness' (ākiñcañña) because for it there is nothing whatsoever (kiñcana) as an object. The paths and fruits are called 'nothingness' (ākiñcaññāni) because of the absence of the defilements that crush and oppress, which are impediments (kiñcana). Nibbāna is also 'nothingness,' but it is not a liberation of mind; therefore, it is not included.

Rāgo kho bhante nimittakaraṇotiādīsu yathā nāma dvinnaṃ kulānaṃ sadisā dve vacchakā honti. Yāva tesaṃ lakkhaṇaṃ na kataṃ hoti, tāva “ayaṃ asukakulassa vacchako, ayaṃ asukakulassā”ti na sakkā hoti jānituṃ. Yadā pana tesaṃ tisūlādīsu aññataraṃ lakkhaṇaṃ kataṃ hoti, tadā sakkā hoti jānituṃ. Evameva yāva puggalassa rāgo nuppajjati, tāva na sakkā hoti jānituṃ “ariyo vā puthujjano vā”ti. Rāgo panassa uppajjamānova “sarāgo nāma ayaṃ puggalo”ti sañjānananimittaṃ karonto viya uppajjati, tasmā nimittakaraṇoti vutto. Dosamohesupi eseva nayo.

Regarding 'Venerable sir, lust is a maker of signs,' and so forth: Just as when there are two similar calves from two families, as long as a mark has not been made on them, it is not possible to know, 'This calf belongs to such-and-such a family, and that calf belongs to such-and-such a family.' But when a mark, such as a trident or the like, has been made on them, then it becomes possible to know. In the same way, as long as lust has not arisen in a person, it is not possible to know whether they are a noble one or a worldling. But lust, when arising in them, arises as if making a sign for recognition, showing, 'This person is indeed lustful.' Therefore, it is called a 'maker of signs.' The same principle applies to hatred and delusion.

Animittā cetovimuttiyo nāma terasa dhammā vipassanā, cattāro āruppā, cattāro maggā, cattāri phalāni. Tattha vipassanā niccanimittaṃ sukhanimittaṃ attanimittaṃ ugghāṭetīti animittā nāma. Cattāro āruppā rūpanimittassa abhāvā animittā nāma. Maggaphalāni nimittakarānaṃ kilesānaṃ abhāvena animittāni, nibbānampi animittameva, taṃ pana cetovimutti na hoti, tasmā na gahitaṃ. Atha kasmā suññatā cetovimutti na gahitāti? Sā “suññā rāgenā”tiādivacanato sabbattha anupaviṭṭhāva, tasmā visuṃ na gahitāti.

The signless liberations of mind are thirteen states: insight, the four formless attainments, the four paths, and the four fruits. Herein, insight is called signless because it eradicates the sign of permanence, the sign of pleasure, and the sign of self. The four formless attainments are called signless due to the absence of the sign of form. The paths and fruits are signless because of the absence of defilements that create signs. Nibbāna is also signless, but it is not a liberation of mind; therefore, it is not included. Why then is the emptiness liberation of mind not included? Because it is already included everywhere, as stated in the phrase 'empty of lust,' and so forth; therefore, it is not included separately.

Ekatthāti [M 3.0138] ārammaṇavasena ekatthā “appamāṇaṃ ākiñcaññaṃ suññataṃ animitta”nti hi sabbānetāni nibbānasseva nāmāni. Iti iminā pariyāyena ekatthā. Aññasmiṃ pana [P 3.0100] ṭhāne appamāṇāpi hoti, aññasmiṃ ākiñcaññā, aññasmiṃ suññatā, aññasmiṃ animittāti iminā pariyāyena nānābyañjanāti.

Regarding 'of one meaning,' they are of one meaning by way of their object; for all these—the immeasurable, nothingness, emptiness, and the signless—are indeed names for Nibbāna alone. Thus, for this reason, they are of one meaning. But in another context, there is also the immeasurable; in another, nothingness; in another, emptiness; and in another, the signless. Thus, for this reason, they are of different expressions.

8. Nigaṇṭhanāṭaputtasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Nigaṇṭha Nāṭaputta Sutta

350. Aṭṭhame tenupasaṅkamīti sayaṃ āgatāgamo viññātasāsano anāgāmī ariyasāvako samāno kasmā naggabhoggaṃ nissirikaṃ nigaṇṭhaṃ upasaṅkamīti? Upavādamocanatthañceva vādāropanatthañca. Nigaṇṭhā kira “samaṇassa gotamassa sāvakā thaddhakhadirakhāṇukasadisā, kenaci [V 3.0138] saddhiṃ paṭisanthārampi na karontī”ti upavadanti, tassa upavādassa mocanatthañca, “vādañcassa āropessāmī”ti upasaṅkami. Na khvāhaṃ ettha bhante bhagavato saddhāya gacchāmīti yassa ñāṇena asacchikataṃ hoti. So “evaṃ kireta”nti aññassa saddhāya gaccheyya, mayā pana ñāṇenetaṃ sacchikataṃ, tasmā nāhaṃ ettha bhagavato saddhāya gacchāmīti dīpento evamāha.

350. In the eighth discourse, regarding 'he approached him' (tenupasaṅkami), a question might arise: 'Why did he, being a noble disciple, a non-returner, one who has mastered the scriptural teachings and has penetrated the teaching of realization, approach the naked, crooked, and graceless Nigaṇṭha?' For the purpose of freeing himself from blame and for the purpose of establishing a doctrine. The Nigaṇṭhas, it is said, reproach thus: 'The disciples of the ascetic Gotama are like hard, solid acacia stakes; they do not even engage in friendly conversation with anyone.' He approached for the purpose of freeing himself from that reproach, and thinking, 'I will establish a doctrine with him.' Regarding 'Indeed, venerable sir, I do not go here out of faith in the Blessed One': one for whom this has not been realized by knowledge would go by faith in another, thinking, 'So it is, it seems.' But by me, this has been realized by knowledge. Therefore, explaining, 'I do not go here out of faith in the Blessed One,' he spoke thus.

Ulloketvāti kāyaṃ unnāmetvā kucchiṃ nīharitvā gīvaṃ paggayha sabbaṃ disaṃ pekkhamāno ulloketvā. Bādhetabbaṃ maññeyyāti yathā vinivijjhitvā na nikkhamati, evaṃ paṭibāhitabbaṃ maññeyya bandhitabbaṃ vā. Sahadhammikāti sakāraṇā. Atha maṃ paṭihareyyāsi saddhiṃ nigaṇṭhaparisāyāti etesaṃ atthe ñāte atha me nigaṇṭhaparisāya saddhiṃ abhigaccheyyāsi, patīhārassa me santikaṃ āgantvā attano āgatabhāvaṃ jānāpeyyāsīti attho. Eko pañhoti eko pañhamaggo, ekaṃ pañhagavesananti attho. Eko uddesoti ekaṃ nāma kinti? Ayaṃ eko uddeso. Ekaṃ veyyākaraṇanti “sabbe sattā āhāraṭṭhitikā”ti (khu. pā. 4.1; a. ni. 10.27) idaṃ ekaṃ veyyākaraṇaṃ. Evaṃ sabbattha attho veditabbo.

Regarding 'looking up' (ulloketvā): having raised his body, pushed out his belly, stretched his neck, and looking towards all directions, he looked up. Regarding 'he might think to obstruct' (bādhetabbaṃ maññeyya): he might think it can be obstructed in such a way that one cannot penetrate and exit, or that it can be bound. Regarding 'with valid reason' (sahadhammikā): with cause. Regarding 'Then you should bring it to me together with the Nigaṇṭha assembly' (atha maṃ paṭihareyyāsi saddhiṃ nigaṇṭhaparisāya): the meaning is, 'When the meaning of these things is known, then you should approach me together with the Nigaṇṭha assembly; having come to my presence, you should make known the fact of your arrival.' Regarding 'one question' (eko pañho): one path of questioning, meaning the search for one question. Regarding 'one formulation' (eko uddeso): 'What is one?' This is one formulation. Regarding 'one explanation' (ekaṃ veyyākaraṇaṃ): 'All beings are sustained by nutriment' (Khp 4; AN 10.27)—this is one explanation. In this way, the meaning should be understood in all instances.

9. Acelakassapasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Acelakassapa Sutta

351. Navame [M 3.0139][P 3.0101] kīvaciraṃ pabbajitassāti kīvaciro kālo pabbajitassāti attho. Uttari manussadhammāti manussadhammo nāma dasakusalakammapathā, tato manussadhammato uttari. Alamariyañāṇadassanavisesoti ariyabhāvaṃ kātuṃ samatthatāya alamariyoti saṅkhāto ñāṇadassanaviseso. Naggeyyāti naggabhāvato. Muṇḍeyyāti muṇḍabhāvato. Pavāḷanipphoṭanāyāti pāvaḷanipphoṭanato, bhūmiyaṃ nisīdantassa ānisadaṭṭhāne laggānaṃ paṃsurajavālikānaṃ phoṭanatthaṃ gahitamorapiñchamattatoti attho.

351. In the ninth discourse, regarding 'How long has one been ordained?' (kīvaciraṃ pabbajitassa), the meaning is 'how long is the time for one who has been ordained?' Regarding 'beyond human qualities' (uttari manussadhamma): the human dhamma is the ten wholesome courses of action; this is what is beyond those human dhammas. Regarding 'a special knowledge and vision sufficient for nobility' (alamariyañāṇadassanaviseso): a special knowledge and vision designated as 'alamariya' (sufficient for nobility) because of its ability to bring about the state of a noble one. Regarding 'by nakedness' (naggeyya): from the state of being unclothed. Regarding 'by being shaven-headed' (muṇḍeyya): from the state of being shaven-headed. Regarding 'by shaking off a cloak' (pāvaḷanipphoṭanāya): from shaking off a cloak; the meaning is: merely from the peacock-feather whisk taken for the purpose of striking off the soil, dust, and sand adhering to the buttocks of one sitting on the ground.

10. Gilānadassanasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse on Visiting the Sick

352. Dasame ārāmadevatāti pupphārāmaphalārāmesu adhivatthā devatā. Vanadevatāti vanasaṇḍesu adhivatthā devatā. Rukkhadevatāti mattarājakāle vessavaṇo ca devatāti evaṃ tesu tesu rukkhesu adhivatthā devatā. Osadhitiṇavanappatīsūti harītakāmalakīādīsu muñjapabbajādīsu [V 3.0139] vanajeṭṭharukkhesu ca adhivatthā devatā. Saṃgammāti sannipatitvā. Samāgammāti tato tato samāgantvā. Paṇidhehīti patthanāvasena ṭhapehi. Ijjhissati sīlavato cetopaṇidhīti samijjhissati sīlavantassa cittapatthanā. Dhammikoti dasakusaladhammasamannāgato agatigamanarahito. Dhammarājāti tasseva vevacanaṃ, dhammena vā laddharajjattā dhammarājā. Tasmāti “yasmā tena hi, ayyaputta, amhepi ovadāhī”ti vadatha, tasmā. Appaṭivibhattanti “idaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ dassāma, idaṃ attanā bhuñjissāmā”ti evaṃ avibhattaṃ bhikkhūhi saddhiṃ sādhāraṇameva bhavissatīti.

352. In the tenth discourse, regarding `ārāmadevatā` (deities of the park): deities who preside over flower parks and fruit parks. Regarding `vanadevatā` (deities of the forest): deities who preside over forest thickets. Regarding `rukkhadevatā` (tree deities): deities who preside over various trees, such as the spirit-king Matta, the palm-tree spirit, and the deity Vessavaṇa. Regarding `osadhitiṇavanappatīsu` (in medicinal herbs, grass, and forest trees): deities who preside over medicinal plants like myrobalan and emblic myrobalan, in grasses like muñja and pabbaja, and in great forest trees. Regarding `saṃgamma` (having come together): having assembled. Regarding `samāgamma` (having gathered): having come together from various places. Regarding `paṇidehi` (make a resolution): establish the mind by way of aspiration. Regarding `ijjhissati sīlavato cetopaṇidhi` (the mental resolution of a virtuous one will succeed): the mental aspiration of a virtuous person will be fulfilled. Regarding `dhammika` (righteous): endowed with the ten wholesome dhammas and free from following the four wrong courses. Regarding `dhammarājā` (righteous king): it is a synonym for that very word; or, because of having obtained kingship righteously, he is a righteous king. Regarding `tasmā` (therefore): it is because you say, 'Therefore, venerable sir, advise us too.' Regarding `appaṭivibhatta` (undivided): it means it will be undivided, shared in common with the monks, not divided thus: 'This we will give to the monks, this we will consume ourselves.'

Cittasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses with Citta is concluded.

8. Gāmaṇisaṃyuttaṃ

8. The Connected Discourses with Headmen

1. Caṇḍasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Caṇḍa Discourse

353. Gāmaṇisaṃyuttassa [V 3.0140][P 3.0102][M 3.0140] paṭhame caṇḍo gāmaṇīti dhammasaṅgāhakattherehi caṇḍoti gahitanāmo eko gāmaṇi. Pātukarotīti bhaṇḍantaṃ paṭibhaṇḍanto akkosantaṃ paccakkosanto paharantaṃ paṭipaharanto pākaṭaṃ karotīti dasseti. Na pātukarotīti akkuṭṭhopi pahaṭopi kiñci paccanīkaṃ akarontoti dasseti.

353. In the first discourse of the Connected Discourses with Headmen, regarding `Caṇḍa the headman` (caṇḍo gāmaṇi): a certain headman who was given the name 'Caṇḍa' by the elder monks, the compilers of the Dhamma. Regarding `He makes manifest` (pātukaroti): it shows that he makes it evident by quarreling back at one who quarrels, reviling in return one who reviles, and striking back at one who strikes. Regarding `He does not make manifest` (na pātukaroti): it shows that even when reviled or struck, he does not do anything in opposition.

2. Tālapuṭasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Tālapuṭa Discourse

354. Dutiye tālapuṭoti evaṃnāmako. Tassa kira bandhanā pamuttatālapakkavaṇṇo viya mukhavaṇṇo vippasanno ahosi, tenassa tālapuṭoti nāmaṃ akaṃsu. Svāyaṃ abhinīhārasampanno pacchimabhavikapuggalo. Yasmā pana paṭisandhi nāma aniyatā ākāse khittadaṇḍasadisā, tasmā esa naṭakule nibbatti. Vuḍḍhippatto pana naṭasippe aggo hutvā sakalajambudīpe pākaṭo jāto. Tassa pañca sakaṭasatāni pañca mātugāmasatāni parivāro, bhariyāyapissa tāvatakāvāti mātugāmasahassena ceva sakaṭasahassena ca saddhiṃ yaṃ yaṃ nagaraṃ vā nigamaṃ vā pavisati, tatthassa puretarameva satasahassaṃ denti. Samajjavesaṃ gaṇhitvā pana mātugāmasahassena saddhiṃ kīḷaṃ karontassa yaṃ hatthūpagapādūpagādiābharaṇajātaṃ khipanti, tassa pariyanto natthi. So taṃdivasaṃ mātugāmasahassaparivārito rājagahe kīḷaṃ katvā paripakkañāṇattā saparivārova yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami.

354. In the second discourse, regarding `Tālapuṭa`: one so named. It is said that his facial complexion was exceptionally clear, like the color of a ripe palm fruit freed from its stalk; therefore, they gave him the name Tālapuṭa. He was a person whose aspiration was fulfilled, one in his final existence. However, because rebirth-linking is not fixed, like a stick thrown into the air, he was reborn into a family of dancers. Having reached maturity, he became foremost in the art of dancing and became famous throughout all of Jambudīpa. His retinue consisted of five hundred carts and five hundred women, and his wife also had a retinue of that same size. Thus, together with a thousand women and a thousand carts, whatever city or town he entered, they gave him a hundred thousand in advance. Furthermore, when he took on the guise of a festival performer and performed with the thousand women, there was no limit to the kinds of ornaments, such as bracelets and anklets, that were thrown to him. On that day, having performed in Rājagaha surrounded by a retinue of a thousand women, because his wisdom was mature, he approached the Blessed One together with his retinue.

Saccālikenāti [P 3.0103] saccena ca alikena ca. Tiṭṭhatetanti tiṭṭhatu etaṃ. Rajanīyāti rāgappaccayā mukhato pañcavaṇṇasuttanīharaṇavātavuṭṭhidassanādayo aññe ca kāmassādasaṃyuttākāradassanakā [V 3.0141] abhinayā. Bhiyyosomattāyāti adhikappamāṇattāya. Dosanīyāti dosappaccayā hatthapādacchedādidassanākārā. Mohanīyāti mohappaccayā udakaṃ gahetvā telakaraṇaṃ, telaṃ gahetvā udakakaraṇanti evamādayo māyāpabhedā.

Regarding `saccālikena` (by truth and falsehood): by truth and by falsehood. Regarding `tiṭṭhatetaṃ` (let that stand): let this stand. Regarding `rajanīya` (arousing lust): these are performances that are a condition for lust, such as displaying the pulling of five-colored thread from the mouth, showing a storm and rain arising, and other acts that show things connected with the enjoyment of sensual pleasure. Regarding `bhiyyosomattāya` (for the sake of exceeding measure): for the sake of an excessive amount. Regarding `dosanīya` (arousing hatred): these are acts that are a condition for hatred, such as showing the cutting off of hands and feet. Regarding `mohanīya` (arousing delusion): these are various kinds of illusions that are a condition for delusion, such as taking water and making it into oil, or taking oil and making it into water, and so on.

Pahāso [M 3.0141] nāma nirayoti visuṃ pahāsanāmako nirayo nāma natthi, avīcisseva pana ekasmiṃ koṭṭhāse naccantā viya gāyantā viya ca naṭavesaṃ gahetvāva paccanti, taṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Nāhaṃ, bhante, etaṃ rodāmīti ahaṃ, bhante, etaṃ bhagavato byākaraṇaṃ na rodāmīti evaṃ sakammakavasenettha attho veditabbo, na assuvimocanamattena. “Mataṃ vā ammarodantī”tiādayo cettha aññepi vohārā veditabbā.

Regarding `Pahāso nāma nirayo` (the hell named Laughter): there is no separate hell named Pahāsa. Rather, in one section of the great Avīci hell itself, they are tormented as if dancing and as if singing, having taken on the guise of a dancer. This was said in reference to that. Regarding `Nāhaṃ, bhante, etaṃ rodāmi` (Venerable sir, I do not weep over this): the meaning should be understood as, 'Venerable sir, I do not bewail this declaration of the Blessed One.' Thus, the meaning here should be understood in the transitive sense, not merely in the sense of shedding tears. And here, other usages such as `Mataṃ vā ammarodantī` (They weep for the dead, mother) should also be understood.

3-5. Yodhājīvasuttādivaṇṇanā

3-5. Commentary on the Yodhājīva Sutta and others

355-357. Tatiye yodhājīvoti yuddhena jīvikaṃ kappanako dhammasaṅgāhakattherehi evaṃ gahitanāmo. Ussahati vāyamatīti ussāhaṃ vāyāmaṃ karoti. Pariyāpādentīti maraṇaṃ paṭipajjāpenti. Dukkaṭanti duṭṭhu kataṃ. Duppaṇihitanti duṭṭhu ṭhapitaṃ. Parajito nāma nirayoti ayampi na visuṃ eko nirayo, avīcisseva pana ekasmiṃ koṭṭhāse pañcāvudhasannaddhā [P 3.0104] phalakahatthā hatthiassarathe āruyha saṅgāme yujjhantā viya paccanti, taṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Catutthapañcamesupi eseva nayo.

In the third discourse, regarding `yodhājīvo` (one who lives by fighting): one who earns a living by warfare, so named by the elders who compiled the Dhamma. Regarding 'He strives, he exerts' (ussahati vāyamati): he makes an effort and an exertion. Regarding 'They cause to perish' (pariyāpāpenti): they cause to undergo death. Regarding 'Wrongly done' (dukkaṭa): badly done. Regarding 'Wrongly directed' (duppaṇihita): badly established. Regarding 'the hell named 'Defeated'' (parajito nāma nirayo): this also is not a separate hell, but in one compartment of Avīci hell, they are tormented as if armed with five weapons, holding shields, mounted on elephants, horses, or chariots, and fighting in battle. It was said in reference to this. In the fourth and fifth discourses also, this is the same method.

6. Asibandhakaputtasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Asibandhakaputta Sutta

358. Chaṭṭhe pacchābhūmakāti pacchābhūmivāsino. Kāmaṇḍalukāti sakamaṇḍaluno. Sevālamālikāti pātova udakato sevālañceva uppalādīni ca gahetvā udakasuddhikabhāvajānanatthāya mālaṃ katvā piḷandhanakā. Udakorohakāti sāyaṃpātaṃ udakaṃ orohanakā. Uyyāpentīti upari yāpenti. Saññāpentīti sammā ñāpenti. Saggaṃ nāma okkāmentīti parivāretvā ṭhitā “gaccha, bho, brahmalokaṃ, gaccha, bho, brahmaloka”nti vadantā saggaṃ pavesenti. Anuparisakkeyyāti anuparigaccheyya. Ummujjāti ummujja uṭṭhaha. Thalamuplavāti thalamabhiruha. Tatra yāssāti tatra yā bhaveyya. Sakkharā vā kaṭhalā vāti sakkharā ca kaṭhalā ca[V 3.0142]. Sā adhogāmī assāti sā adho gaccheyya, heṭṭhāgāmī bhaveyya. Adhogacchāti heṭṭhā gaccha.

358. In the sixth sutta, 'those of the western land': inhabitants of the western region. 'Water-pot carriers': those with their own water pots. 'Wearers of seaweed garlands': those who, in the morning, gather seaweed, lotuses, and so on from the water, make them into garlands, and wear them for the purpose of knowing the state of their purity through water. 'Those who descend into the water': those who descend into the water in the evening and morning. 'They send up': they cause them to go upwards. 'They make known': they make known correctly. 'They cause them to enter heaven': surrounding them, they say, 'Go, good sir, to the Brahma-world! Go, good sir, to the Brahma-world!' and thus cause them to enter heaven. 'Should go around': should go around repeatedly. 'Emerge': emerge and rise up. 'Float to the shore': ascend to dry land. 'Whatever might be there': whatever would be there. 'Gravel or a potsherd': gravel and a potsherd. 'It would go downward': it would go down, it would be going below. 'Go downward': go below.

7. Khettūpamasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Khettūpama Sutta

359. Sattame [M 3.0142] jaṅgalanti thaddhaṃ na mudu. Ūsaranti sañjātaloṇaṃ. Pāpabhūmīti lāmakabhūmibhāgaṃ. Maṃdīpātiādīsu ahaṃ dīpo patiṭṭhā etesanti maṃdīpā. Ahaṃ leṇo allīyanaṭṭhānaṃ etesanti maṃleṇā. Ahaṃ tāṇaṃ rakkhā etesanti maṃtāṇā. Ahaṃ saraṇaṃ bhayanāsanaṃ etesanti maṃsaraṇā. Viharantīti maṃ evaṃ katvā viharanti.

359. In the seventh sutta, 'a wilderness' (jaṅgalaṃ) means hard, not soft. 'Saline' (ūsaraṃ) means where salt has formed. 'Bad land' (pāpabhūmi) means an inferior portion of land. In phrases such as maṃdīpā ('those for whom I am an island'), the meaning is: 'I am an island (dīpo), a support (patiṭṭhā), for them (etesaṃ)', thus they are maṃdīpā. 'I am a cave (leṇo), a place of shelter (allīyanaṭṭhānaṃ), for them', thus they are maṃleṇā. 'I am a protection (tāṇaṃ), a guard (rakkhā), for them', thus they are maṃtāṇā. 'I am a refuge (saraṇaṃ), a destroyer of fear (bhayanāsanaṃ), for them', thus they are maṃsaraṇā. 'They dwell' (viharanti) means having made me thus, they dwell.

Gobhattampīti dhaññaphalassa abhāvena lāyitvā kalāpakalāpaṃ bandhitvā ṭhapitaṃ gimhakāle gunnampi khādanaṃ bhavissatīti attho. Udakamaṇikoti kucchiyaṃ maṇikamekhalāya evaṃ laddhanāmo bhājanaviseso. Ahārī aparihārīti udakaṃ na harati na pariharati, na pariyādiyatīti attho. Iti [P 3.0105] imasmiṃ sutte sakkaccadhammadesanāva kathitā. Buddhānañhi asakkaccadhammadesanā nāma natthi. Sīhasamānavuttino hi buddhā, yathā sīho pabhinnavaravāraṇassapi sasabiḷārādīnampi gahaṇatthāya ekasadisameva vegaṃ karoti, evaṃ buddhāpi ekassa desentāpi dvinnaṃ bahūnaṃ bhikkhuparisāya bhikkhuniupāsakaupāsikāparisāyapi titthiyānampi desentā sakkaccameva desenti. Catasso pana parisā saddahitvā okappetvā suṇantīti tāsaṃ desanā sakkaccadesanā nāma jātā.

'Cattle fodder' (gobhattaṃ) means that, due to the absence of grain, having been reaped and tied up in bundles, it is stored with the thought that it will be food even for cattle in the hot season. 'A water pot' (udakamaṇiko) is a particular kind of vessel so named because of a jeweled girdle on its belly. 'Not taking away, not being taken from' (ahārī aparihārī) means it does not carry water away, is not diminished, and is not exhausted. Thus, in this sutta, the respectful teaching of the Dhamma is spoken of. For the Buddhas, there is no such thing as a disrespectful teaching of the Dhamma. The Buddhas have conduct like that of a lion. Just as a lion exerts the same force for seizing a great, noble elephant in rut as for small creatures like rabbits and cats, so too the Buddhas, when teaching one, two, many, an assembly of monks, nuns, laymen, laywomen, or even sectarians, teach with respect. However, because the four assemblies listen with faith and conviction, the teaching to them is called a 'respectful teaching' (sakkaccadesanā).

8. Saṅkhadhamasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Saṅkhadhama Sutta

360. Aṭṭhame yaṃbahulaṃ yaṃbahulanti iminā nigaṇṭho attanāva attano vādaṃ bhindati. Tasmā bhagavā evaṃ sante na koci āpāyikotiādimāha. Purimāni pana cattāri padāni diṭṭhiyā paccayā honti. Tasmā tesupi ādīnavaṃ dassento idha, gāmaṇi, ekacco satthā evaṃvādī hotītiādimāha. Tattha ahampamhīti ahampi amhi.

360. In the eighth sutta, by this text, “whatever is abundant, whatever is abundant,” the Nigaṇṭha himself breaks his own doctrine. Therefore, the Blessed One said, “This being so, no one is bound for a state of loss,” and so on. However, the previous four terms are conditions for wrong view. Therefore, showing the disadvantage in them too, he said, “Here, headman, a certain teacher is one who speaks thus,” and so on. Therein, ahampamhi is to be parsed as ahampi amhi.

Mettāsahagatenātiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ sabbaṃ saddhiṃ bhāvanānayena visuddhimagge vuttameva. [V 3.0143] Seyyathāpi, gāmaṇi, balavā saṅkhadhamotiādi pana idha apubbaṃ. Tattha balavāti balasampanno. Saṅkhadhamoti saṅkhadhamako. Appakasirenāti akicchena adukkhena. Dubbalo hi saṅkhadhamo saṅkhaṃ dhamantopi [M 3.0143] na sakkoti catasso disā sarena viññāpetuṃ, nāssa saṅkhasaddo sabbato pharati, balavato pana vipphāriko hoti, tasmā “balavā”ti āha.

Regarding the passages beginning with 'accompanied by loving-kindness,' whatever should be said has all been stated in the Visuddhimagga along with the method of cultivation. However, the passage beginning, “Just as, headman, a strong conch-blower,” is new here. Therein, 'strong' means endowed with strength. 'Conch-blower' means a skilled conch-blower. 'With little effort' means without trouble, without difficulty. For a weak conch-blower, even while blowing the conch, is not able to make it known in the four directions by its sound, nor does his conch-sound pervade everywhere; but for a strong one, it is far-reaching. Therefore, he said, 'strong'.

Mettāya cetovimuttiyāti ettha “mettā”ti vutte upacāropi appanāpi vaṭṭati, “cetovimuttī”ti vutte pana appanāva vaṭṭati. Yaṃ pamāṇakataṃ kammanti pamāṇakataṃ kammaṃ nāma kāmāvacaraṃ vuccati, appamāṇakataṃ kammaṃ nāma rūpāvacaraṃ. Tañhi pamāṇaṃ atikkamitvā odhisakaanodhisakadisāpharaṇavasena vaḍḍhetvā katattā appamāṇakatanti vuccati.

Herein, regarding 'the liberation of mind through loving-kindness' (mettāya cetovimuttiyā): when 'loving-kindness' (mettā) is said, both access and absorption are admissible; but when 'liberation of mind' (cetovimutti) is said, only absorption is admissible. Regarding 'whatever kamma is measured' (yaṃ pamāṇakataṃ kammaṃ), 'measured kamma' refers to sense-sphere kamma; 'unmeasured kamma' refers to form-sphere and formless-sphere kamma. For that latter kamma is called 'unmeasured' because it is performed by developing it, having transcended measure, by way of pervading the directions specifically and unspecifically.

Na taṃ tatrāvasissati, na taṃ tatrāvatiṭṭhatīti taṃ kāmāvacarakammaṃ [P 3.0106] tasmiṃ rūpārūpāvacarakamme na ohīyati na tiṭṭhati. Kiṃ vuttaṃ hoti? Taṃ kāmāvacarakammaṃ tassa rūpārūpāvacarakammassa antarā laggituṃ vā ṭhātuṃ vā rūpārūpāvacarakammaṃ pharitvā pariyādiyitvā attano okāsaṃ gahetvā patiṭṭhātuṃ vā na sakkoti. Atha kho rūpārūpāvacarakammameva kāmāvacaraṃ mahogho viya parittaṃ udakaṃ pharitvā pariyādiyitvā attano okāsaṃ katvā tiṭṭhati, tassa vipākaṃ paṭibāhitvā sayameva brahmasahabyataṃ upanetīti. Iti idaṃ suttaṃ ādimhi kilesavasena vuṭṭhāya avasāne brahmavihāravasena gahitattā yathānusandhināva gataṃ.

‘It is not left behind there, it does not remain there’ means that sense-sphere kamma is not left behind or does not remain within that form-sphere and formless-sphere kamma. What is meant by this? That sense-sphere kamma is not able, in the midst of that form-sphere and formless-sphere kamma, to attach itself or remain; nor is it able, having pervaded and overcome the form-sphere and formless-sphere kamma, to take its own opportunity and establish itself. Rather, the form-sphere and formless-sphere kamma itself—like a great flood pervading and overcoming a small amount of water—makes its own opportunity and remains. Having obstructed the result of the former, it itself leads to companionship with Brahmā. Thus, this sutta, having arisen at the beginning through the power of the defilements and being taken up at the end through the power of the divine abidings, proceeds with its own proper connection.

9. Kulasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Kula Sutta

361. Navame dubbhikkhāti dullabhabhikkhā. Dvīhitikāti “jīvissāma nu kho na nu kho”ti evaṃ pavattaīhitikā. “Duhitikā”tipi pāṭho. Ayameva attho. Dukkhā īhiti ettha na sakkā koci payogo sukhena kātunti duhitikā. Tattha tattha matamanussānaṃ vippakiṇṇāni setāni aṭṭhikāni etthāti setaṭṭhikā. Salākāvuttāti salākamattavuttā, yaṃ tattha vuttaṃ vāpitaṃ, taṃ salākamattameva ahosi, phale na janayatīti attho.

361. In the ninth sutta, 'dubbhikkhā' means 'alms are difficult to obtain'. 'Dvīhitikā' means having a divided mind, thinking, 'Shall we live, or shall we not?' There is also the reading 'duhitikā'; the meaning is the very same. Here, 'duhitikā' means that because there is difficult indecision, no effort can be made with ease. 'Setaṭṭhikā' means that here and there are the scattered white bones of people who have died. 'Salākāvuttā' means that whatever was sown or planted there yielded only stalks without grain; it did not produce fruit. This is the meaning.

Uggilitunti [M 3.0144] dve ante mocetvā kathetuṃ asakkonto uggilituṃ bahi nīharituṃ na sakkhīti[V 3.0144]. Ogilitunti pucchāya dosaṃ disvā hāretuṃ asakkonto ogilituṃ anto pavesetuṃ na sakkhīti.

Regarding 'uggilituṃ': being unable to speak by releasing both ends, he was unable to spit it out, to bring it outside. Regarding 'ogilituṃ': seeing the fault in the question and being unable to dispel it, he was unable to swallow it, to cause it to enter inside.

Ito so gāmaṇi ekanavutikappeti bhagavā kathayamānova yāva nikkhanto nāsikavāto na puna pavisati, tāvatakena kālena ekanavutikappe anussari “atthi nu kho kiñci kule pakkabhikkhādānena upahatapubba”nti parijānanatthaṃ. Athekampi [P 3.0107] apassanto “ito so, gāmaṇī”tiādimāha. Idāni dānādīnaṃ ānisaṃsaṃ kathento atha kho yāni tāni kulāni aḍḍhānīti dhammadesanaṃ ārabhi. Tattha dānasambhūtānīti dānena sambhūtāni nibbattāni. Sesapadadvayepi eseva nayo. Ettha pana saccaṃ nāma saccavāditā. Sāmaññaṃ nāma sesasīlaṃ. Vikiratīti ayogena vaḷañjento vippakirati. Vidhamatīti dhamento viya nāseti. Viddhaṃsetīti nāseti. Aniccatāti hutvā abhāvo bahunāpi kālena saṅgatānaṃ khaṇeneva antaradhānaṃ.

Regarding 'From this, O headman, ninety-one aeons ago...': The Blessed One, while speaking, for as long as an exhaled breath from his nose does not re-enter, recollected back through ninety-one aeons in order to fully understand, 'Has any family ever been ruined by the giving of cooked alms?' And seeing not even one, he spoke the words beginning, 'From this, O headman...' Now, explaining the benefits of giving and so on, he began the Dhamma discourse, 'Then, those families that are wealthy...' There, 'dānasambhūtāni' means 'arisen from giving, produced by giving.' The same method applies to the remaining two terms. Here, 'truth' (sacca) means 'truthfulness' (saccavāditā). 'The state of a recluse' (sāmañña) means 'the remaining morality.' 'Vikirati' means 'he scatters'; using improperly, he squanders. 'Vidhamati' means 'he blows away'; he destroys as if by blowing. 'Viddhaṃseti' means 'he annihilates'; he destroys. 'Impermanence' (aniccatā) is the state of non-existence after having been, and the disappearance in an instant of things accumulated even over a long time.

10. Maṇicūḷakasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Maṇicūḷaka Sutta

362. Dasame taṃ parisaṃ etadavocāti tassa kira evaṃ ahosi “kulaputtā pabbajantā puttadārañceva jātarūparajatañca pahāyeva pabbajanti, na ca sakkā yaṃ pahāya pabbajitā, taṃ tehi gahetu”nti nayaggāhe ṭhatvā “mā ayyo”tiādivacanaṃ avoca. Ekaṃsenetanti etaṃ pañcakāmaguṇakappanaṃ assamaṇadhammo asakyaputtiyadhammoti ekaṃsena dhāreyyāsi.

362. In the tenth discourse, regarding 'he said this to that assembly': It seems this thought occurred to him, 'Sons of good family who go forth do so only after abandoning wife and children, and gold and silver; and it is not possible for them to take what they have abandoned to go forth.' Standing firm on this principle, he spoke the words beginning, 'No, venerable sir...' Regarding 'Ekaṃsenetaṃ': You should hold with certainty that this allowing of the five strands of sensual pleasure is not the practice of a recluse and not the practice of a son of the Sakyans.

Tiṇanti senāsanacchadanatiṇaṃ. Pariyesitabbanti tiṇacchadane vā iṭṭhakacchadane vā gehe palujjante yehi taṃ kāritaṃ, tesaṃ santikaṃ gantvā “tumhehi kāritasenāsanaṃ ovassati, na sakkā tattha vasitu”nti ācikkhitabbaṃ. Manussā sakkontā karissanti, asakkontā “tumhe vaḍḍhakiṃ gahetvā kārāpetha, mayaṃ te saññāpessāmā”ti vakkhanti. Evaṃ vutte kāretvā tesaṃ ācikkhitabbaṃ. Manussā vaḍḍhakīnaṃ dātabbaṃ dassanti. Sace āvāsasāmikā natthi, aññesampi bhikkhācāravattena ārocetvā kāretuṃ vaṭṭati. Idaṃ sandhāya “pariyesitabba”nti vuttaṃ.

'Tiṇaṃ' means grass for thatching a dwelling. 'Pariyesitabbaṃ' (should be sought) means: if a dwelling with a grass roof or a brick roof is decaying, one should go to those by whom it was built and inform them, 'The dwelling you had built is leaking; it is not possible to live there.' If the people are able, they will repair it. If they are unable, they will say, 'You, sirs, take a carpenter and have it repaired; we will arrange for the payment.' When this has been said, one should have it repaired and inform them. The people will give what is to be given to the carpenters. If there are no owners of the dwelling, it is allowable to have it repaired by informing others as well, in accordance with the practice of the alms-round. With reference to this procedure, it is said, 'should be sought'.

Dārunti [V 3.0145][M 3.0145] senāsane gopānasiādīsu palujjamānesu tadatthāya dāruṃ. Sakaṭanti gihivikataṃ katvā tāvakālikasakaṭaṃ. Na kevalañca sakaṭameva, aññampi vāsipharasukuddālādiupakaraṇaṃ evaṃ pariyesituṃ vaṭṭati. Purisoti hatthakammavasena puriso pariyesitabbo. Yaṃkiñci hi purisaṃ “hatthakammaṃ[P 3.0108], āvuso, katvā dassasī”ti vatvā “dassāmi, bhante,”ti vutte “idañcidañca karohī”ti yaṃ icchati, taṃ kāretuṃ vaṭṭati. Na tvevāhaṃ, gāmaṇi, kenaci pariyāyenāti jātarūparajataṃ panāhaṃ kenacipi kāraṇena pariyesitabbanti na vadāmi.

'Dāruṃ' means wood for that purpose, when things like the rafters of a dwelling are decaying. 'Sakaṭaṃ' means a temporary cart, treating it as laypeople's property. And not only a cart, but it is also allowable to seek other implements such as adzes, axes, and hoes in this way. 'Puriso' means a man should be sought for the purpose of manual labor. Indeed, having said to any man, 'Lay supporter, will you offer manual labor?' and when he says, 'I will offer it, venerable sir,' it is allowable to have him do whatever work one wishes by saying, 'Do this and this.' Regarding 'Na tvevāhaṃ, gāmaṇi, kenaci pariyāyena': However, I do not say that gold and silver should be sought for any reason whatsoever.

11. Bhadrakasuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Bhadraka Sutta.

363. Ekādasame mallesūti evaṃnāmake janapade. Vadhenāti māraṇena. Jāniyāti dhanajāniyā. Akālikena pattenāti na kālantarena pattena, kālaṃ anatikkamitvāva pattenāti attho. Ciravāsī nāma kumāroti evaṃnāmako tassa putto. Bahi āvasathe paṭivasatīti bahinagare kiñcideva sippaṃ uggaṇhanto vasati. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭadukkhaṃ kathitaṃ.

363. In the eleventh sutta, 'in the Mallas' (mallesu) means in a country of that name. 'By killing' (vadhena) means by murdering. 'By loss' (jāniyā) means by loss of wealth. 'By timeless attainment' (akālikena pattena) means not by an attainment after a lapse of time, but by an attainment without exceeding the time; this is the meaning. 'A prince named Ciravāsī' means his son of that name. 'He dwells in an outer residence' means he lives outside the city, learning some craft. In this sutta, the suffering of the round of existence is spoken of.

12. Rāsiyasuttavaṇṇanā

12. Commentary on the Rāsiya Sutta.

364. Dvādasame rāsiyoti rāsiṃ katvā pañhassa pucchitattā rāsiyoti evaṃ dhammasaṅgāhakattherehi gahitanāmo. Tapassinti tapanissitakaṃ. Lūkhajīvinti lūkhajīvikaṃ. Antāti koṭṭhāsā. Gāmoti gāmmo. Gammotipi pāṭho, gāmavāsīnaṃ dhammoti attho. Attakilamathānuyogoti attano kilamathānuyogo, sarīradukkhakaraṇanti attho.

364. In the twelfth sutta, 'Rāsiya' (rāsiyo): because the question was asked after making a heap (rāsiṃ katvā), he was thus named 'Rāsiya' by the elders who compiled the Dhamma. 'Ascetic' (tapassī) means one devoted to austerity. 'Living meagerly' (lūkhajīvī) means living a meager life. 'Extremes' (antā) means portions. 'Village' (gāmo) means rustic. There is also a reading 'gammo', meaning the practice of village-dwellers. 'Devotion to self-mortification' (attakilamathānuyogo) means devotion to one's own exhaustion, that is, causing bodily suffering.

Kasmā panettha kāmasukhallikānuyogo gahito, kasmā attakilamathānuyogo, kasmā majjhimā paṭipadāti? Kāmasukhallikānuyogo tāva kāmabhogīnaṃ dassanatthaṃ gahito, attakilamathānuyogo tapanissitakānaṃ, majjhimā paṭipadā tiṇṇaṃ nijjaravatthūnaṃ dassanatthaṃ gahitā. Kiṃ etesaṃ dassane payojananti? Ime dve ante pahāya tathāgato majjhimāya paṭipadāya sammāsambodhiṃ patto. So kāmabhoginopi na sabbe garahati na pasaṃsati, tapanissitakepi na sabbe garahati na [M 3.0146] pasaṃsati, garahitabbayuttakeyeva garahati, pasaṃsitabbayuttake [P 3.0109] pasaṃsatīti imassatthassa pakāsanaṃ etesaṃ dassane payojananti veditabbaṃ.

Why, in this context, is indulgence in sensual pleasures taken up, why self-mortification, and why the Middle Way? Indulgence in sensual pleasures is taken up to show those who indulge in sensual pleasures; self-mortification, for those devoted to austerity; and the Middle Way is taken up to show the three bases for wearing away defilements. What is the purpose in showing these? Having abandoned these two extremes, the Tathāgata attained perfect self-enlightenment by the Middle Way. He neither blames nor praises all those who indulge in sensual pleasures, nor all those devoted to austerity; he blames only those who are fit to be blamed and praises those who are fit to be praised. It should be understood that the purpose in showing these is to reveal this meaning.

Idāni [V 3.0146] tamatthaṃ pakāsento tayo khome, gāmaṇi, kāmabhoginotiādimāha. Tattha sāhasenāti sāhasikakammena. Na saṃvibhajatīti mittasahāyasandiṭṭhasambhattānaṃ saṃvibhāgaṃ na karoti. Na puññāni karotīti anāgatabhavassa paccayabhūtāni puññāni na karoti. Dhammādhammenāti dhammena ca adhammena ca. Ṭhānehīti kāraṇehi. Sacchikarotīti kathaṃ attānaṃ ātāpento paritāpento sacchikaroti? Caturaṅgavīriyavasena ca dhutaṅgavasena ca. Tisso sandiṭṭhikā nijjarāti ettha ekopi maggo tiṇṇaṃ kilesānaṃ nijjaraṇatāya tisso nijjarāti vuttoti.

Now, wishing to clarify that meaning, he speaks the words beginning with 'There are these three, village headman, indulgers in sensual pleasures,' and so on. Herein, 'by force' (sāhasena) means by an act of violence. 'Does not share' (na saṃvibhajati) means he does not share with friends, companions, intimates, or associates. 'Does not perform merits' (na puññāni karoti) means he does not perform meritorious deeds that are conditions for a future existence. 'By Dhamma and Adhamma' (dhammādhammena) means by Dhamma and by Adhamma. 'For reasons' (ṭhānehi) means for causes. 'Realizes' (sacchikaroti) means: how can one who is heating and tormenting oneself realize it? One realizes it by means of the fourfold effort and by means of the ascetic practices. Herein, by 'three immediate eradications' (tisso sandiṭṭhikā nijjarā) it is meant that even a single path is called 'three eradications' because it eradicates the three defilements.

13. Pāṭaliyasuttavaṇṇanā

13. The Explanation of the Pāṭaliya Sutta.

365. Terasame dūteyyānīti dūtakammāni paṇṇāni ceva mukhasāsanāni ca. Pāṇātipātañcāhanti idaṃ kasmā āraddhaṃ? Na kevalaṃ ahaṃ māyaṃ jānāmi, aññampi idañcidañca jānāmīti sabbaññubhāvadassanatthaṃ āraddhaṃ. Santi hi, gāmaṇi, eke samaṇabrāhmaṇāti idaṃ sesasamaṇabrāhmaṇānaṃ laddhiṃ dassetvā tassā pajahāpanatthaṃ āraddhaṃ.

365. In the thirteenth sutta, 'duties of a messenger' (dūteyyāni) means the tasks of messengers, both written messages and oral instructions. Regarding 'and I abstain from killing living beings' (pāṇātipātañcāhaṃ)—why was this stated? It was stated to demonstrate the state of omniscience, thus: 'Not only do I know deceit, but I also know this and that.' Regarding 'For, village headman, there are some ascetics and brahmins' (santi hi, gāmaṇi, eke samaṇabrāhmaṇā)—this was stated after showing the views of the other ascetics and brahmins, for the purpose of causing them to abandon those views.

Mālī kuṇḍalīti mālāya mālī, kuṇḍalehi kuṇḍalī. Itthikāmehīti itthīhi saddhiṃ kāmā itthikāmā, tehi itthikāmehi. Āvasathāgāranti kulagharassa ekasmiṃ ṭhāne ekekasseva sukhanivāsatthāya kataṃ vāsāgāraṃ. Tenāhaṃ yathāsatti yathābalaṃ saṃvibhajāmīti tassāhaṃ attano sattianurūpena ceva balānurūpena ca saṃvibhāgaṃ karomi. Alanti yuttaṃ. Kaṅkhaniye ṭhāneti kaṅkhitabbe kāraṇe. Cittasamādhinti tasmiṃ dhammasamādhismiṃ ṭhito tvaṃ saha vipassanāya catunnaṃ maggānaṃ vasena cittasamādhiṃ sace paṭilabheyyāsīti dasseti. Apaṇṇakatāya [P 3.0110] mayhanti ayaṃ paṭipadā mayhaṃ apaṇṇakatāya anaparādhakatāya eva saṃvattatīti attho. Kaṭaggāhoti jayaggāho.

By 'garlanded and wearing earrings' (mālī kuṇḍalī) is meant: 'garlanded' (mālī) is one who is garlanded by means of a garland; 'wearing earrings' (kuṇḍalī) is one who wears earrings by means of earrings. By 'sensual pleasures with women' (itthikāmehi) is meant: sensual pleasures together with women are 'sensual pleasures with women' (itthikāmā); by those sensual pleasures with women. By 'guest house' (āvasathāgāraṃ) is meant a dwelling place made in one location of a family home for the comfortable lodging of each individual guest. By 'Thus, I share according to my ability and strength' (tenāhaṃ yathāsatti yathābalaṃ saṃvibhajāmi) is meant: I make a distribution for him according to my own capacity and according to my own power. 'Enough' (alaṃ) means fitting. By 'in a matter for doubt' (kaṅkhaniye ṭhāne) is meant in a cause where one should doubt. By 'concentration of mind' (cittasamādhiṃ), it shows that if you, established in that concentration of Dhamma, were to attain concentration of mind along with insight by means of the four paths. By 'for my certainty' (apaṇṇakatāya mayhaṃ) the meaning is: this practice of mine leads only to my certainty and faultlessness. By 'a winning throw' (kaṭaggāho) is meant 'a winning grasp' (jayaggāho).

Ayaṃ kho, gāmaṇi, dhammasamādhi, tatra ce tvaṃ cittasamādhiṃ paṭilabheyyāsīti ettha dhammasamādhīti dasakusalakammapathadhammā, cittasamādhīti saha vipassanāya [M 3.0147] cattāro maggā. Atha vā “pāmojjaṃ jāyati, pamuditassa pīti jāyatī”ti (a. ni. 5.26) evaṃ vuttā pāmojjapītipassaddhisukhasamādhisaṅkhātā pañca dhammā dhammasamādhi nāma, cittasamādhi pana saha vipassanāya [V 3.0147] cattāro maggāva. Atha vā dasakusalakammapathā cattāro brahmavihārā cāti ayaṃ dhammasamādhi nāma, taṃ dhammasamādhiṃ pūrentassa uppannā cittekaggatā cittasamādhi nāma. Evaṃ tvaṃ imaṃ kaṅkhādhammaṃ pajaheyyāsīti evaṃ tvaṃ imasmiṃ vuttappabhede dhammasamādhismiṃ ṭhito sace evaṃ cittasamādhiṃ paṭilabheyyāsi, ekaṃsenetaṃ kaṅkhaṃ pajaheyyāsīti attho. Sesaṃ sabbattha vuttanayamevāti.

In the passage, 'This, village headman, is the concentration of Dhamma; if you were to attain concentration of mind therein' (Ayaṃ kho, gāmaṇi, dhammasamādhi, tatra ce tvaṃ cittasamādhiṃ paṭilabheyyāsi), herein, 'concentration of Dhamma' (dhammasamādhi) means the ten wholesome courses of action; 'concentration of mind' (cittasamādhi) means the four paths together with insight. Alternatively, the five qualities reckoned as joy, rapture, tranquility, happiness, and concentration, as stated thus: 'Joy arises; for one who is joyful, rapture arises' (pāmojjaṃ jāyati, pamuditassa pīti jāyatī), are called 'concentration of Dhamma' (dhammasamādhi), while 'concentration of mind' (cittasamādhi) refers to just the four paths together with insight. Alternatively, the ten wholesome courses of action and the four divine abodes are called 'concentration of Dhamma' (dhammasamādhi); for one fulfilling that concentration of Dhamma, the one-pointedness of mind that arises is called 'concentration of mind' (cittasamādhi). By 'Thus you should abandon this state of doubt' (evaṃ tvaṃ imaṃ kaṅkhādhammaṃ pajaheyyāsi), the meaning is: if you, established in this explained kind of concentration of Dhamma, were to attain concentration of mind in this way, you would undoubtedly abandon that doubt. The rest is as explained everywhere.

Gāmaṇisaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Explanation of the Connected Discourses with Village Headmen is concluded.

9. Asaṅkhatasaṃyuttaṃ

9. The Connected Discourses on the Unconditioned

1. Paṭhamavaggo

1. The First Chapter

1-11. Kāyagatāsatisuttādivaṇṇanā

1-11. Commentary on the Kāyagatāsati Sutta and so forth.

366-376. Asaṅkhatasaṃyutte [V 3.0148][M 3.0148][P 3.0111] asaṅkhatanti akataṃ. Hitesināti hitaṃ esantena. Anukampakenāti anukampamānena. Anukampaṃ upādāyāti anukampaṃ cittena pariggahetvā, paṭiccātipi vuttaṃ hoti. Kataṃ vo taṃ mayāti taṃ mayā imaṃ asaṅkhatañca asaṅkhatamaggañca desentena tumhākaṃ kataṃ. Ettakameva hi anukampakassa satthu kiccaṃ, yadidaṃ aviparītadhammadesanā. Ito paraṃ pana paṭipatti nāma sāvakānaṃ kiccaṃ. Tenāha etāni, bhikkhave, rukkhamūlāni…pe… amhākaṃ anusāsanīti iminā rukkhamūlasenāsanaṃ dasseti. Suññāgārānīti iminā janavivittaṃ ṭhānaṃ. Ubhayena ca yogānurūpaṃ senāsanaṃ ācikkhati, dāyajjaṃ niyyāteti.

366-376. In the Connected Discourses on the Unconditioned, 'unconditioned' (asaṅkhata) means unmade. 'Seeking what is beneficial' (hitesinā) means by one seeking what is beneficial. 'With compassion' (anukampakenā) means by one who is compassionate. 'Out of compassion' (anukampaṃ upādāya) means having taken it up with a compassionate mind; it is also said to mean 'depending on'. 'That has been done by me for you' (kataṃ vo taṃ mayā)—this means: that task has been done for you by me, who teaches this unconditioned and the path to the unconditioned. For only this much is the duty of a compassionate teacher, namely, teaching the Dhamma without error. Beyond this, the practice is the duty of the disciples. Therefore he said, 'These, monks, are the roots of trees... our instruction'—by this he indicates the dwelling at the foot of a tree. By 'empty dwellings' (suññāgārāni) he indicates a secluded place. And by both, he points out a dwelling suitable for the practice, and bestows the inheritance.

Jhāyathāti ārammaṇūpanijjhānena aṭṭhatiṃsārammaṇāni, lakkhaṇūpanijjhānena ca aniccādito khandhāyatanādīni upanijjhāyatha, samathañca vipassanañca vaḍḍhethāti vuttaṃ hoti. Mā pamādatthāti mā pamajjittha. Mā pacchā vippaṭisārino ahuvatthāti ye hi pubbe daharakāle arogakāle sattasappāyādisampattikāle satthu sammukhībhāvakāle ca yonisomanasikārarahitā rattindivaṃ maṅkulabhattaṃ hutvā seyyasukhaṃ middhasukhaṃ anubhontā pamajjanti, te pacchā jarākāle rogakāle maraṇakāle vipattikāle satthu parinibbutakāle ca taṃ pubbe pamādavihāraṃ anussarantā sappaṭisandhikālakiriyañca bhāriyaṃ sampassamānā vippaṭisārino honti. Tumhe pana tādisā mā ahuvatthāti dassento āha [P 3.0112] “mā pacchā vippaṭisārino ahuvatthā”ti.

'Meditate!' (jhāyatha): by means of object-contemplation, contemplate the thirty-eight objects; and by means of characteristic-contemplation, contemplate the aggregates, sense bases, and so forth, beginning with impermanence. This is said to mean: develop both serenity and insight. 'Do not be negligent!' (mā pamādattha): do not be heedless. 'Do not later become regretful!' (mā pacchā vippaṭisārino ahuvattha): for those who, in their early years, in times of health, in times of favorable conditions such as the seven suitable things, and in the presence of the Teacher, lack wise attention and, day and night, having become food for bedbugs, indulge in the pleasures of lying down and torpor, and are thus heedless—they later, in old age, sickness, death, misfortune, and after the Teacher’s final Nibbāna, remembering their former heedless way of life and seeing the heavy burden of a death that leads to rebirth, become regretful. But you should not become like that—showing this, he said: 'Do not later become regretful.'

Ayaṃ [V 3.0149] vo amhākaṃ anusāsanīti ayaṃ amhākaṃ santikā “jhāyatha mā pamādatthā”ti tumhākaṃ anusāsanī, ovādoti vuttaṃ hoti.

'This is our instruction to you' (ayaṃ vo amhākaṃ anusāsanī): this instruction from us to you, 'Meditate, do not be negligent!', is our exhortation—this is what is meant.

2. Dutiyavaggo

2. The Second Chapter

1-33. Asaṅkhatasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-33. Commentary on the Discourses on the Unconditioned and so forth.

377-409. Kāye [M 3.0149] kāyānupassītiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ parato vakkhāma.

377-409. Regarding such texts as 'one contemplates the body in the body,' whatever should be said, that we will state later.

Anatantiādīsu taṇhānatiyā abhāvena anataṃ. Catunnaṃ āsavānaṃ abhāvena anāsavaṃ. Paramatthasaccatāya saccaṃ. Vaṭṭassa parabhāgaṭṭhena pāraṃ. Saṇhaṭṭhena nipuṇaṃ. Suṭṭhu duddasatāya sududdasaṃ. Jarāya ajaritattā ajajjaraṃ. Thiraṭṭhena dhuvaṃ. Apalujjanatāya apalokitaṃ. Cakkhuviññāṇena apassitabbattā anidassanaṃ. Taṇhāmānadiṭṭhipapañcānaṃ abhāvena nippapañcaṃ.

In the texts beginning with ‘anataṃ’: Due to the absence of the bending of craving, it is called ‘anataṃ’ (unbent). Due to the absence of the four taints, it is called ‘anāsavaṃ’ (taintless). Because of being the ultimate truth, it is called ‘saccaṃ’ (truth). Because of the meaning of being the further shore of the round of existence, it is called ‘pāraṃ’ (the beyond). Because of the meaning of being subtle, it is called ‘nipuṇaṃ’ (refined). Because of being very difficult to see, it is called ‘sududdasaṃ’ (very hard to see). Because of not being decayed by aging, it is called ‘ajajjaraṃ’ (unaging). Because of the meaning of being stable, it is called ‘dhuvaṃ’ (permanent). Because of not perishing, it is called ‘apalokitaṃ’ (undecaying). Because of not being seeable by eye-consciousness, it is called ‘anidassanaṃ’ (invisible). Due to the absence of the proliferations of craving, conceit, and views, it is called ‘nippapañcaṃ’ (unproliferated).

Santabhāvaṭṭhena santaṃ. Maraṇābhāvena amataṃ. Uttamaṭṭhena paṇītaṃ. Sassirikaṭṭhena sivaṃ. Nirupaddavatāya khemaṃ. Taṇhākkhayassa paccayattā taṇhakkhayaṃ.

Because of the meaning of being a peaceful state, it is called ‘santaṃ’ (peaceful). Due to the absence of death, it is called ‘amataṃ’ (deathless). Because of the meaning of being supreme, it is called ‘paṇītaṃ’ (excellent). Because of the meaning of being glorious, it is called ‘sivaṃ’ (auspicious). Because of being free from calamities, it is called ‘khemaṃ’ (secure). Because of being a condition for the destruction of craving, it is called ‘taṇhakkhayaṃ’ (the destruction of craving).

Vimhāpanīyaṭṭhena accharaṃ paharitabbayuttakanti acchariyaṃ. Abhūtameva bhūtaṃ ajātaṃ hutvā atthīti vā abbhutaṃ. Niddukkhattā anītikaṃ. Niddukkhasabhāvattā anītikadhammaṃ. Vānābhāvena nibbānaṃ. Byābajjhābhāveneva abyābajjhaṃ. Virāgādhigamassa paccayato virāgaṃ. Paramatthasuddhitāya suddhi. Tīhi bhavehi muttatāya mutti. Kāmālayānaṃ abhāvena anālayaṃ. Patiṭṭhaṭṭhena dīpaṃ. Allīyitabbayuttaṭṭhena leṇaṃ. Tāyanaṭṭhena tāṇaṃ. Bhayasaraṇaṭṭhena saraṇaṃ, bhayanāsananti attho. Paraṃ ayanaṃ gati patiṭṭhāti parāyaṇaṃ. Sesamettha vuttanayamevāti.

Because of the meaning of causing astonishment, and because it is fitting to snap the fingers, it is called ‘acchariyaṃ’ (marvelous). Because what had not occurred before has now occurred, it is called ‘abbhutaṃ’ (wondrous). Because of being without suffering, it is called ‘anītikaṃ’ (trouble-free). Because of having a nature free from suffering, it is called ‘anītikadhammaṃ’ (the trouble-free state). Due to the absence of ‘vāna’ (craving), it is called ‘nibbānaṃ’. Due to the absence of anxiety, it is called ‘abyābajjhaṃ’ (unharassed). Because it is a condition for the attainment of dispassion, it is called ‘virāgaṃ’ (dispassion). Because of its ultimate purity, it is called ‘suddhi’ (purity). Because of liberation from the three existences, it is called ‘mutti’ (liberation). Due to the absence of sensual attachments, it is called ‘anālayaṃ’ (unattached). Because of the meaning of being a support, it is called ‘dīpaṃ’ (island). Because of the meaning of being a place to which one resorts, it is called ‘leṇaṃ’ (shelter). Because of the meaning of protecting, it is called ‘tāṇaṃ’ (protection). Because of the meaning of dispelling fear, it is called ‘saraṇaṃ’ (refuge); the meaning is ‘destroyer of fear.’ Because it is the supreme destination, goal, and support, it is called ‘parāyaṇaṃ’ (the ultimate goal). The rest here is just in the manner already stated.

Asaṅkhatasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on the Unconditioned is concluded.

10. Abyākatasaṃyuttaṃ

10. The Connected Discourses on the Undeclared

1. Khemāsuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Khemā Sutta

410. Abyākatasaṃyuttassa [V 3.0150][P 3.0113][M 3.0150] paṭhame khemāti gihikāle bimbisārassa upāsikā saddhāpabbajitā mahātherī “etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, mama sāvikānaṃ bhikkhunīnaṃ mahāpaññānaṃ yadidaṃ khemā”ti evaṃ bhagavatā mahāpaññatāya etadagge ṭhapitā. Paṇḍitāti paṇḍiccena samannāgatā. Viyattāti veyyattiyena samannāgatā. Medhāvinīti medhāya paññāya samannāgatā. Bahussutāti pariyattibāhusaccenapi paṭivedhabāhusaccenapi samannāgatā.

410. In the first discourse of the Connected Discourses on the Undeclared, regarding Khemā: in her lay life she was a lay follower of King Bimbisāra, and having gone forth out of faith, became a great elder nun. She was placed by the Blessed One in the foremost position for great wisdom with the words: “Monks, among my female disciples who are nuns, Khemā is foremost of those with great wisdom.” ‘Paṇḍitā’ means she was endowed with sagacity. ‘Medhāvinī’ means she was endowed with intelligence and wisdom. ‘Bahussutā’ means she was endowed with great learning in both the scriptures and in penetration.

Gaṇakoti acchiddakagaṇanāya kusalo. Muddikoti aṅgulimuddāya gaṇanāya kusalo. Saṅkhāyakoti piṇḍagaṇanāya kusalo. Gambhīroti caturāsītiyojanasahassagambhīro. Appameyyoti āḷhakagaṇanāya appameyyo. Duppariyogāhoti āḷhakagaṇanāya pamāṇagahaṇatthaṃ durogāho. Yena rūpena tathāgatanti yena rūpena dīgho rasso sāmo odātoti sattasaṅkhātaṃ tathāgataṃ paññapeyya. Taṃ rūpaṃ tathāgatassa pahīnanti taṃ vuttappakārarūpaṃ samudayappahānena sabbaññutathāgatassa pahīnaṃ. Rūpasaṅkhāya vimuttoti āyatiṃ rūpassa anuppattiyā rūpārūpakoṭṭhāsenapi evarūpo nāma bhavissatīti vohārassapi paṭipassaddhattā rūpapaṇṇattiyāpi vimutto. Gambhīroti ajjhāsaya gambhīratāya ca guṇagambhīratāya ca gambhīro. Tassa evaṃ guṇagambhīrassa sato sabbaññutathāgatassa yaṃ upādāya sattasaṅkhāto tathāgatoti paññatti hoti, tadabhāvena tassā paññattiyā abhāvaṃ passantassa ayaṃ sattasaṅkhāto hoti tathāgato paraṃ maraṇāti idaṃ vacanaṃ na upeti na yujjati, na hoti tathāgato paraṃ maraṇātiādivacanampi na upeti na yujjatīti attho.

‘Gaṇaka’ means one skilled in flawless counting. ‘Muddika’ means one skilled in counting by finger-signs. ‘Saṅkhāyaka’ means one skilled in counting in heaps. ‘Gambhīra’ means profound, as deep as eighty-four thousand yojanas. ‘Appameyya’ means immeasurable, not to be measured by counting with an āḷaka. ‘Duppariyogāha’ means difficult to fathom, difficult to penetrate for the purpose of grasping the measure by counting with an āḷaka. ‘By what form the Tathāgata…’: By whatever form—long, short, dark, or fair—one might designate the Tathāgata, who is designated as a ‘being.’ ‘That form is abandoned for the Tathāgata…’: That form of the kind described has been abandoned by the all-knowing Tathāgata through the abandoning of its arising. ‘Freed from the designation of form’: Due to the non-arising of form in the future, and because even in the division of form and formlessness the convention ‘such a form will be’ has been stilled, one is also freed from the designation of form. ‘Profound’: He is profound due to the profundity of his disposition and the profundity of his qualities. For that all-knowing Tathāgata who is thus profound in qualities, based on which the designation ‘the Tathāgata, designated as a being’ occurs—due to the absence of that basis, for one who sees the absence of that designation, this statement: ‘This one, designated as a being, the Tathāgata, exists after death’ does not fit, is not suitable. And the statement ‘The Tathāgata does not exist after death,’ etc., also does not fit, is not suitable. This is the meaning.

Saṃsandissatīti [P 3.0114] ekaṃ bhavissati. Samessatīti nirantaraṃ bhavissati. Na virodhayissatīti [V 3.0151] na viruddhaṃ padaṃ bhavissati. Aggapadasminti desanāya. Desanā hi idha aggapadanti adhippetā.

‘Saṃsandissati’ means it will be the same. ‘Samessati’ means it will be without difference. ‘Na virodhayissati’ means there will not be a contradictory phrase. ‘In the foremost term’: in the teaching. For here, the teaching is intended as the ‘foremost term.’

2. Anurādhasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Anurādha Sutta

411. Dutiyaṃ [M 3.0151] khandhiyavagge vitthāritameva, abyākatādhikārato pana idha vuttaṃ.

411. The second sutta has indeed been explained in detail in the Khandhiyavagga; however, due to its being the section on the undeclared, it is stated here.

3-8. Paṭhamasāriputtakoṭṭhikasuttādivaṇṇanā

3-8. Commentary on the First Sāriputta-Koṭṭhika Sutta and Others

412-417. Tatiye rūpagatametanti rūpamattametaṃ. Ettha rūpato añño koci satto nāma na upalabbhati, rūpe pana sati nāmamattaṃ etaṃ hotīti dasseti. Vedanāgatametantiādīsupi eseva nayo. Ayaṃ kho āvuso hetūti ayaṃ rūpādīni muñcitvā anupalabbhiyasabhāvo hetu, yenetaṃ abyākataṃ bhagavatāti. Catutthādīni uttānatthāneva.

In the third sutta, as for the phrase 'this has gone to form,' it means: this Tathāgata is merely form. Here, no being whatsoever other than form is found. When form exists, this term 'Tathāgata' is merely a name; thus it is shown. In 'this has gone to feeling' and so on, this same method applies. As for 'This, friends, is the reason,' it means: this state of being unfindable after having relinquished form and so on is the reason why this was left undeclared by the Blessed One. The fourth sutta and so on have an obvious meaning indeed.

9. Kutūhalasālāsuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Kutūhalasālā Sutta

418. Navame kutūhalasālāyanti kutūhalasālā nāma paccekasālā natthi, yattha pana nānātitthiyā samaṇabrāhmaṇā nānāvidhaṃ kathaṃ pavattenti, sā bahūnaṃ “ayaṃ kiṃ vadati, ayaṃ kiṃ vadatī”ti kutūhaluppavattiṭṭhānato kutūhalasālāti vuccati. Dūrampi gacchatīti yāva ābhassarabrahmalokā gacchati. Imañca kāyaṃ nikkhipatīti cuticittena nikkhipati. Anupapanno hotīti cutikkhaṇeyeva paṭisandhicittassa anuppannattā anupapanno hoti.

418. In the ninth sutta, as for 'in the hall of curiosity': there is no separate hall named 'Kutuhalasālā' (Hall of Curiosity). However, a place where various ascetics and brahmins of different doctrines engage in diverse discussions is called a 'Kutuhalasālā' because it is a place where a clamor arises for many people, thinking, 'What is this one saying? What is that one saying?'. As for 'goes far,' it means it goes as far as the Ābhassara Brahma-world. As for 'and casts off this body,' it means he casts it off with the death-consciousness. As for 'he is not reborn,' it means that at the very moment of death, because of the non-arising of the rebirth-linking consciousness, he is not reborn.

10. Ānandasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Ānanda Sutta

419. Dasame [P 3.0115] tesametaṃ saddhiṃ abhavissāti tesaṃ laddhiyā saddhiṃ etaṃ abhavissa. Anulomaṃ abhavissa ñāṇassa uppādāya sabbe dhammā anattāti yaṃ etaṃ “sabbe dhammā anattā”ti vipassanāñāṇaṃ uppajjati, api nu me tassa anulomaṃ abhavissāti attho.

419. In the tenth sutta, as for 'this would have been in accordance with them,' it means: this would have been in accordance with their doctrine. The full passage means: The insight-knowledge that 'all phenomena are not-self' arises; would that other view have been suitable for the arising of that knowledge for me? This is the meaning.

11. Sabhiyakaccānasuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Sabhiya Kaccāna Sutta

420. Ekādasame [V 3.0152][M 3.0152] etamettakena ettakamevāti āvuso yassāpi etaṃ ettakena kālena “hetumhi sati rūpītiādi paññāpanā hoti, asati na hotī”ti byākaraṇaṃ bhaveyya, tassa ettakameva bahu. Ko pana vādo atikkanteti atikkante pana atimanāpe dhammadesanānaye vādoyeva ko, natthi vādo, chinnā kathāti.

420. In the eleventh sutta, as for the phrase 'with just this much, just this much': Friends, for any person for whom, in just this much time, the explanation, 'When a cause exists, the designation "form," etc., occurs; when it does not exist, it does not occur,' would be possible, for that person, just this much is a great deal. As for 'what further talk when this is surpassed?': in a method of Dhamma teaching that is more excellent and exceedingly pleasing, what is there to be said? There is nothing to be said; the discussion is cut off.

Abyākatasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Abyākatasaṃyutta is completed.

Iti sāratthappakāsiniyā saṃyuttanikāya-aṭṭhakathāya

Thus, in the Sāratthappakāsinī, the commentary on the Saṃyutta Nikāya,

Saḷāyatanavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on the Six Sense Bases is completed.
